# Interfaces

What a study (or a platform) implements to plug into the core, with the signature as the code has it, what the core guarantees, what the implementer must guarantee, and the closed vocabularies. [`architecture.md`](architecture.md) says why the interfaces are shaped this way; this file says what they are in v0.1. The runnable example is [`examples/tiny_study/`](../examples/tiny_study/).

All of them are `typing.Protocol`s, so a test double needs no inheritance. Signatures below are copied from the code; where a docstring in the code says more, the code wins.

## What v0.1 has

| Architecture name | In v0.1 |
|---|---|
| `Study` | the study directory (`study.toml` and its siblings, which may declare the [side files](#side-files-of-a-record) of its records) plus one plugin module whose `build(context)` returns a `StudyPlugin` ([below](#the-study-plugin)) |
| `Platform`, `Sandbox` | not in the core. The platform layer (`expcore_onedata`, steps 4a and 4b) supplies a `HealthCheck`, a `ResourceProbe` (leased platform users), its own `ToolProvider`s and four `Instrument`s (the platform probes, the bytes the agent read through its data path, a progressive ingest that makes part of a dataset arrive during a run, and the mount's declared and effective I/O mode) through the same `StudyPlugin`; its sandbox is [described below](#the-platform-layers-sandbox), and its fakes for a study's own tests are [its testing module](#the-platform-layers-testing-module). A platform of another kind is a distribution of its own that plugs in through the same `StudyPlugin` and the same protocols: [the study guide's section 10](study-guide.md#10-a-platform-of-your-own) lists what it needs, and `examples/sparql_platform/` is one |
| `ToolProvider`, `Scorer`, `Instrument` | protocols: [`ToolProvider`](#toolprovider-and-callcontext), [`Scorer`](#scorer) and [`Instrument`](#instrument) (a protocol since step 4b2) |
| `ModelBackend`, `RunExecutor`, `HealthCheck`, `ResourceProbe` | protocols, below |

## The study plugin

A study directory holds `study.toml`, `models.toml`, `campaign.toml`, `preregistration.toml` and the plugin, the Python file `[study] plugin` names, with any modules it imports from beside it; `[study] requires` names the distributions beyond the core that the study's code imports. `expcore.study.config.load_study(directory)` validates all of them against each other. `expcore.study.plugin.load_plugin` imports the file and calls

```python
def build(context: StudyContext) -> StudyPlugin: ...
```

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class StudyContext:
    study: StudyConfig  # the loaded configuration
    code: Mapping[str, Any]  # the code identity every record carries


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class StudyPlugin:
    executor: RunExecutor
    scorers: Sequence[Scorer]
    prompts: Mapping[str, str]  # per condition: SHA-256 of its prompt template
    tools: Mapping[str, str]  # per condition: SHA-256 of its tool set (tools_sha256, below)
    health: HealthCheck | None = None
    probe: ResourceProbe | None = None
    lease_group: Callable[[Mapping[str, Any]], str | None] = lambda factors: None
    lease_keys: Callable[[str], Sequence[str]] = lambda group: ()
    admission: RunAdmission | None = None  # (treatment_kinds, treatment_targets, cancel, exclusive) -> context manager
    platform: Mapping[str, Any] = field(default_factory=dict)  # the platform's settings that make one experiment
    backend_factory: BackendFactory | None = None  # None: the core's default_backend_factory
    environment_identity: Callable[[], Mapping[str, str]] | None = None  # a shard's identity, below
    reset_settings: Mapping[str, Any] = field(default_factory=dict)  # the platform's settings in no fingerprint, below
    platform_legacy: Mapping[str, Any] | None = None  # an earlier form of `platform` a store may have recorded (C06)
    data_path_factor: str | None = None  # the factor the platform reads a run's data path by; None: `data_path` (O75)
```

`admission` is the platform's hold on a resource every run shares (`expcore.campaign.attempt.RunAdmission`). The core enters it after the lease and the second look at the gates, before the attempt's backend, timer and executor exist, so its wait is outside the agent's clock, and leaves it after the post-run check and the store's record; a refusal (`InfraError`) leaves the run unstarted, with no attempt used. Every health check runs in its caller's context, so the post-run check sees what the run's admission holds. Each try of a run has a note of its own (`expcore.campaign.prerun`: `opened(checked=True)`, `current()`, `note(key, value)`), open from its pre-run check to the end of the try; the check, run in a copy of the worker's context, writes into the same note, so a platform's admission reads what that try's pre-run check noted. With no health check the note holds only `prerun.UNCHECKED`, since nothing will write to it; `current()` is None only outside a try (the dispatcher's own check, a caller that is no campaign). `expcore_onedata.admission.run_admission(config, schema)` is the one for a platform with `[faults]`, and it also declares, with a method `declared_deferral_s()`, the longest the platform's health check may stay deferred, which the core holds the health check to ([HealthCheck](#healthcheck)); **an admission may declare that its platform needs a health check** (`expcore.campaign.health_required`: `requiring_health_check(admit, reason)` returns the admission with the platform's reason in the attribute `requires_health_check`, which an admission of a platform's own may also set; `required_reason` reads it, and a declaration that is not a non-empty text is refused, never read as none), and `Scheduler.__init__` refuses a campaign that has such an admission and no health check with a `ConfigError`, before the dispatcher's lock is taken or the store opened, saying why in the platform's words and to pass the platform's health check as the plugin's `health`; `run_admission` declares it for every platform with `[faults]` (`expcore_onedata.admission_recheck.HEALTH_REQUIRED`), so no run of an outage-capable federation is admitted with no check behind it, while an admission with no declaration, and a campaign with no admission, run with no health check as before; an admission a study wraps in a function of its own must keep the attribute (`functools.wraps` does), and when it does not, the platform refuses at run time what the refusal at load never saw (a try whose note holds `prerun.UNCHECKED`: both `run_admission`'s admission and `require_admission`, which the sandbox and the tool server call before they start anything under any admission, an executor's own included, raise `AdmissionRefused`, a `ConfigError`, so a run admission's refusal stops the campaign loudly, a start in the run's own try (an executor that took the federation itself) ends the attempt as a `harness_error` naming the refusal, and nothing is scored; the note travels in the try's context, so work run in a context of its own reads none); the declaring admission is called as the one it wraps, keywords included, and an attribute set to anything but a non-empty text, `None` included, is refused, while one left out declares none; given the study's factor schema, it refuses at once a condition that declares `exclusive = true` on a platform without `[faults]`. `exclusive` is whether the run's condition declares `exclusive = true` in study.toml (`FactorSchema.exclusive`): a condition with no fault whose runs the platform holds exclusively, as it does an outage run's, so a control can run under the outage's isolation; a campaign with such a condition and no run admission is refused at its start, naming the file and the key. The context manager yields what it holds, `shared` or `exclusive`; the core stores it in the attempt's row as it is admitted (`none` without an admission) and the export carries it as `admission` in every analysed and excluded entry and as `admissions`, one per charged attempt, in every exhausted run.

The core guarantees: the code identity is computed once, from the study directory and the `[study] code` roots, and refused if it is unknown, stale or (without `--allow-dirty`) dirty; the plugin is rejected at load if a condition has no prompt or tool hash, two scorers share a name, or `platform` is not a mapping; `prompts`, `tools`, the scorers' names and versions, the hash of each configuration file and, when not empty, `platform` go into the campaign fingerprint, so a resume under any change is refused (C06). A study on the Onedata layer passes `platform=config.fingerprint()`: the platform file's TLS mode with the CA bundle's SHA-256; with a `[sandbox]`, each level's data path and listing mode and, when `[sandbox.served_bytes]` is declared, the caps of the tables of the bytes served; and, with `[faults]`, the fault injector's sanction and each target's label with the SHA-256 of its Compose project and service names. So a resume under another mode, bundle, data path or caps, or with `[faults]` added, dropped, unsanctioned or taking another container down, is refused. `[faults]` names its Compose `project` explicitly; Compose would otherwise take one from the environment, the Compose file or its directory.

**The core's own identity (C06, P01).** The campaign's environment (`provenance.environment`) names every distribution the study's code imports with its version, and the core by its code: `sources["papers-experiments-core"]` is `{"kind": "core", "commit", "tree", "dirty", "python_sha256"}`, its commit, its git tree, whether that tree was dirty, and a digest of the `*.py` files of `expcore` and `expcore_onedata` by path below the directory holding them (the example under `expcore/examples/` left out). One commit gives one entry however the core is installed: editable, from a git URL, from a directory, from a wheel, or from the wheel of its sdist. `expcore.provenance.core_identity.current()` reads it: live from git when the imported `expcore` is the `src/expcore` of a checkout whose top is the core's own project, else from `expcore/_build_identity.json` (`expcore-core-identity/1`: `commit`, `tree`, `dirty`, `python_sha256`), which the project's build hook (`hatch_build.py`) writes into the wheel and the sdist and a wheel built from an sdist takes from it. Dirty is a change to a file a build reads (`core_identity.builds_from`: `src/`, `examples/tiny_study/`, `pyproject.toml`, `hatch.toml`, `hatch_build.py`, `README.md`, `.gitignore`, `.hgignore`, `tests/data/study_tokens.sha256`) that is not HEAD's, compared file by file with HEAD's tree (changed, new whether git ignores it or not, removed, its mode changed unless `core.fileMode` is off; bytecode caches aside), or a link there or on the way there (the digest follows links, a cycle once); a change elsewhere in the repository is not; an installed core whose source no longer matches its build's digest is stale, which is dirty; a core with neither has the commit `unknown`. `run`, `check`, `merge` and `analyze` call `core_check.require_core` beside the study's `require_known`, with the same `--allow-dirty`: an unknown or stale core is refused always, a dirty one unless dirt is allowed (`CoreVersionUnknown`, a `CodeVersionUnknown`). A replication package's check runs on the core its wheels pin and asks nothing of this; its build refuses a check recorded under a dirty or unknown core, and an anonymised build replaces the core wheel's `_build_identity.json` with a neutral member and searches for the commit and tree it held. A store an earlier core recorded, whose environment names the core by how it was installed (`environment.is_earlier_form`), is resumed, checked, analysed and packaged against the core in use read in that earlier form (`environment(requires, earlier_core=True)`, `study.core_legacy`), so it resumes, checks and analyses exactly where the earlier core would have: a store of an editable or git-URL install only under the same code, one of a wheel, directory or index install under any core of its version, since that form never named the code, as the earlier core compared it, and the start says so on stderr.

**Settings outside the fingerprint (`reset_settings`, C35).** A platform has settings that change how it works and not what an agent sees: how the reset finds what it removes, how long it waits, how many requests it has in flight. They are in no fingerprint, so a resume under other ones is not refused, and the later resets and placements then run differently with nothing to say so. The platform gives the core them as `StudyPlugin.reset_settings`, after its defaults: a flat mapping of names to text, numbers, booleans or None, with no secret and no name that identifies a machine or a private space, since it travels with the export and the replication package. The core reads no meaning into it. A mapping that is no mapping, or has a nested value, a key that is not text or empty, or a number that is NaN or infinite, is refused when the plugin is loaded, naming the file and the key.

- **A first start records it** in the store's meta table under `reset_settings`, as JSON with sorted keys and no spaces, in the same transaction as the pins and the fingerprint, and writes no event and no log line.
- **A resume compares it, key by key,** over the union of the keys, and in the same transaction, after the checks that can refuse the start, so a refused start leaves the record, the events and the log as they were. `2` and `2.0` are one value; `True` and `1` are not. Equal: nothing is written and nothing said. Different: the record is replaced; one event `reset_settings_changed` holds `{"changes": [{"key", "old", "new"}, ...]}` in key order, a side that lacked the key having no entry for it; and one warning on the logger `expcore.campaign.reset_settings` names each changed key as `key: old -> new`, the values as JSON and `absent` for a missing side (the command line shows it on stderr). The resume is never refused for it. The next resume under the new values is quiet.
- **A store with no record** (made before the settings were recorded, or by a platform that supplied none) is left as it was while the plugin supplies none. When it supplies some, the resume records them, writes one `reset_settings_unknown_before` event and warns once that the settings its earlier starts ran under are unknown. A platform that stops supplying them is a change like another: every recorded key is `-> absent`.
- **The export** has an optional top-level key `reset_settings`, after `deployment_shares` (and so after `kinds`), only for a store that has a record: `{"changes": [{"at", "key", "old", "new"}, ...], "unknown_before": [{"at"}, ...]}`, both lists written out when empty, `at` the time of the start that found it, `old` and `new` omitted for a side that lacked the key. It is not one of `REQUIRED_KEYS`, and an export without it is what it was. In an export joined from shards (`join_exports`) each shard's entries follow in shard order, each with `"shard"` added last, since each shard's store records its own platform's settings; a shard that has no record adds none.
- **The report** shows it as "Platform settings outside the fingerprint", among the infrastructure notes after the failures table: a row per change (`Recorded`, `Key`, `Before`, `After`, with a `Shard` column in a merged batch), a line for each start whose earlier settings are unknown, or one line saying that no change was recorded; nothing for an export without the key.

The Onedata layer's is `Placement.reset_settings()` (`expcore_onedata.rangemap`, what `load_placement` returns; those of its fields a run's own instruments read during the run, `runsettings.PLACEMENT_IN_RUN`, and the map's digest are in the fingerprint as well, so a resume under another of them is refused first): every field of `[placement]` after its default, as `placement.<field>` (`placement.qos_scan`, `placement.poll_s`, `placement.concurrency`, ... that is `PlacementSettings.reset_settings()`), and the declared range map as one more setting, `placement.range_map_sha256`. It leaves out `holder`, a provider's name, and it gives the map by its digest alone, never its rows, since a row names a file and a provider. A range map decides which bytes a state places, so a resume after it changed starts from another state, which is what the warning is for. The digest is `RangeMap.digest()`, the SHA-256 in lower-case hexadecimal of the UTF-8 bytes of `RangeMap.canonical()`: a JSON array of the rows sorted by space, file and receiving provider, each an object with `file`, `provider`, `ranges` (the merged `[offset, length]` pairs) and `space`, keys sorted and no spaces (nothing else of the platform hashes the map). The order of the rows in the file, and ranges written unmerged, do not change it; the map written inline or in the study's own file gives the same digest. A study passes the settings beside the platform's fingerprint: `StudyPlugin(..., platform=config.fingerprint(), reset_settings=load_placement(path, config).reset_settings())`. A study that never passes it has no record and no note, and its campaigns behave as they did. `open_campaign` and `Scheduler` take the same mapping as `reset_settings`.

The implementer must guarantee: `build` has no side effects beyond constructing objects; the hashes describe what the model sees under each condition; nothing is named by the core, so every name lives in the study.

**A plugin over several files.** `import_plugin_module` imports the plugin as a package whose path is the plugin's own directory, named `expcore_study_plugin_` plus a hash of the plugin's path. A module beside it is imported relatively (`from .scoring import Scorer`, or `from . import scoring`) and is named below that package, so two studies' `scoring.py` never meet in one process. Every module of the package is compiled from its file's bytes on each load, never from cached bytecode, so what runs is what the code identity hashed, and no bytecode is written into the study. `import scoring` finds nothing, since the study directory is not on the module search path; the refusal names the fix.

The core holds the code that runs to the code identity by where it comes from, not by its module name (`expcore.study.plugin_import`). While the plugin is imported and while `build` runs, it records every file whose code is executed, and it then finds every module in `sys.modules` whose file lies in the study directory; code the recorded environment covers does not count: the core's own packages, the running environment (a virtual environment inside the study directory, for one) and any installed distribution's code, editable or not, found as the environment record finds it (its `RECORD`, or for an editable install where it imports from), as does code below a directory the identity skips. It refuses the load, naming `study.toml` and the file relative to the study, when such a file is one `[study] code` does not cover, and when it is not a module of the plugin's package: a file imported through the module search path, or loaded by path (`spec_from_file_location`), would be cached under a name another study could be served, or run from stale bytecode. Before every import, and after a refused one, it drops every module of the study's own code, whatever its name, and every module of the package, so no study is served another's; it never drops a module of the core or of an installed distribution. The replay check's import is held to the same rules.

The implementer must guarantee that every file the plugin imports is in `[study] code` (and so in `package.toml`, whose build requires every file the identity covers), and that it imports each at the plugin's top level or in `build`: a module first imported while a run is under way escapes the check. The check sees code only: a data file the plugin reads by path (a prompt, a table) is not checked, and the identity covers it only if `[study] code` lists it, as the example lists `prompt.txt`. A backend factory in a module beside the plugin enters the fingerprint as `scoring.Factory`, without the hashed name.

**Running a study built in code.** `expcore.study.pipeline.run_built_study(study, *, allow_dirty=False, code_root=None, crash_hook=None, shard=None, after=()) -> RunSummary` is the one public way into a campaign: `run_study(path, ...)`, and so `run`, loads the study and calls it. A study that cuts a loaded one (a smoke over fewer models or seeds, made with `dataclasses.replace` of its campaign, tasks or schema) calls it with the `StudyConfig` it built, since it has no directory to name. A selection (`shard` a `SelectionSpec`, with `after` the earlier sequential selections' stores, C38) goes through the same call, so a selection of a study built in code keeps the study's kinds in scope as `run --select` does. The call first holds the config to what the loader holds a study to (`expcore.study.config.check_study`, public too), raising a `ConfigError` that names the key before any store, lease or code identity is touched. Each kind in `kinds` must be one a study may declare. The factor schema is built again from its own parts (`FactorSchema.rebuilt`), so a treatment kind the config does not know is refused, and so is a kind that is a fault on one target with no target (C34), even where the schema was edited after it was built; a schema with a part edited, swapped or deleted after it was built is refused too: every attribute is compared with what the constructor makes of the others, and the digest with the one taken, since the lookup of a run key to its condition and the digest were derived from what the parts were. The `[scheduler]` health and identity kinds must be known, every model of the campaign must be in the model table, and each contrast's cluster must be an attribute of every task. A campaign that shards by model and has a budget must share it, and the plan must expand, so a cut of the tasks that leaves a condition naming one it dropped is refused. The call then holds the study's own kinds in scope (`expcore.errors.study_kinds`) for the whole run, so they are known in the executor's thread, in the threads the executor starts and in the scheduler's. The private `_run_campaign` does neither, and a study that calls it gets its own `InfraError` kinds refused as unknown (C37). Two checks of the loader are not repeated, because a `StudyConfig` does not carry what they read: importing `[study] platforms` (a config can name a platform's kind only in a process that has imported the platform, as the loading of the study it was cut from did) and the preregistration against the schema (the analysis loads the study from its files). The campaign store is `<study.root>/out` and its fingerprint covers the plan, so a cut run in the real study directory neither resumes the whole campaign nor can be resumed by it: run a smoke from a copy of the directory, with `code_root` the real one, as [the guide](study-guide.md#a-smoke-built-in-code) shows.

**A campaign's fingerprint without a start.** `expcore.study.pipeline.campaign_fingerprint(path) -> dict` is the campaign fingerprint a start of the study at `path` pins (C06), without starting it or opening a store: the configuration checked and the code vouched for as `run` holds them (`require_known`, no dirty code), the plugin loaded with the study's kinds in scope, and `fingerprint_of` of both, as the build of `run_built_study` makes it. The HPC shard driver's `resume-line` compares it, computed on a copy of the study beside a federation's `platform.toml`, with what a shard's snapshot pins before it takes a node for the resume.

**An analysis without the platform (`build_analysis`, R17).** `build` often needs the study's platform (the rendered federation, the site's variables), which no longer exists when an HPC batch is analysed after copy-back, and analysis needs only the scorers. The plugin module may also define

```python
def build_analysis(context: StudyContext) -> StudyPlugin: ...
```

with the same context. `analyse_study`, so `analyze`, `report`, `regenerate` and a replication package's check, builds its plugin with it when the module defines it (`expcore.study.analysis_plugin.load_analysis_plugin`, called from `_analyse_loaded` alone) and with `build`, exactly as before, when it does not; `check` builds nothing. **It is never used for a run:** `load_plugin`, which every start, resume and shard and `campaign_fingerprint` build with, does not look for it, and a test fails on any other reference to the loader. Only the `scorers` of what it returns are read, and only they are checked (at least one, names that differ), so it gives no prompt or tool hash, no executor and no backend factory that would need the platform.

*The guard.* The campaign fingerprint records the run plugin's `scorer_digest`, the SHA-256 of its scorers' names and versions (`StudyPlugin.scorer_digest()`), in the store and in every snapshot. An analysis built by `build_analysis` is refused, before any record is scored, unless its scorers have that digest: `analyze refused: <plugin file>: build_analysis gives the scorers 'a', 'b', but the campaign was run with other scorers`, and says that `--rescore` re-scores on purpose. It names the analysis plugin's scorers, never a digest or a version (a digest cannot say which scorer differs). A merged batch compares each shard's snapshot. A snapshot that records no valid digest (none, or not 64 lower-case hex digits) is refused as well, naming how many: every fingerprint the core writes holds one (`build_fingerprint` requires it), so such a store was changed or is damaged, and the scorers cannot be shown to be the run's. Nothing is written by a refused analysis, and `export.json`, `scores.json`, `analysis.json` and `report.md` are the same bytes whichever builder served an analysis that is not refused and re-scored nothing (a re-score, below, adds the key and the line it records). An analysis built by `build` is not compared, as before: it is the run's own builder, and re-scoring after a scorer changed is what it is for (R13).

*Re-scoring with a scorer corrected after the run (`--rescore`, R17).* The refusal stands unless the analysis is asked, on purpose, for the other scorers: `analyze --rescore` and `report --rescore` (`allow_rescore=True` of `analyse_study` and `regenerate`). The flag relaxes one branch only, a digest that differs. A store with no snapshot, or with a snapshot that records no valid digest, is refused with the flag as without it, also beside a snapshot that differs. When the flag lets a differing digest through, the re-score is recorded and never silent. R13's own record of a re-score holds the corrected scorers' versions: the `version` of every score in `scores.json`, `scorers` in `analysis.json` and the report's `- Scorers:` line. The flag adds the digests, to `analysis.json` as `"rescore": {"run_scorer_digests": [...], "analysis_scorer_digest": "..."}` (the distinct digests the campaign's snapshots hold, sorted, and the digest of the scorers the analysis used) and to the report's Provenance as one line after the scorers', ``- Re-scored (`--rescore`): the campaign ran with scorers of digest `…`; this analysis scored with `…` (the scorers above)``. Which scorer changed is not recorded and cannot be said: the store holds one hash of all the run's scorers' names and versions, and the fingerprint is not changed to hold more. When the analysis plugin's scorers have the digests the snapshots hold, or the module defines no `build_analysis`, nothing is recorded and every output is the same bytes with the flag as without it. The scorer is code, so `check` is run again first (it builds nothing, so it runs where the federation is gone), as for any re-score (R13). `regenerate` derives what a re-scored analysis wrote only with `rescore=True` or with `honour_shipped_rescore=True`. The second is what a replication package's build and check pass instead of a flag (and the anonymised package's analysis passes `shipped_rescore` as `analyse_study`'s argument): the decision was recorded at `analyze`, so the `rescore` entry of the analysis.json that ships is read once and a differing digest is let through exactly when it equals what the guard records now (`require_run_scorers(..., shipped=...)`: the digests the snapshots hold, sorted, and the digest of the analysis plugin's scorers). Any other entry or none, a store with no valid digest or no snapshot, is the refusal of an analysis that was not asked to re-score; one whose entry is present says that the shipped analysis records a re-score that is not of these scorers on this store. The byte comparison of the shipped outputs stays the guard: an entry on a batch that re-scored nothing derives no entry and the file fails as differing. `analyze` and `report` never read the entry; they need the flag.

The implementer must guarantee that `build_analysis` gives the scorers `build` gives (one function both call), and that a scorer's `version` changes whenever its behaviour does, as for any scorer: the digest sees names and versions, not code or constructor arguments. A scorer's identity, its name and the `version` it declares (`scorer_version`: the declared one, else the hash of the file its code is in), must therefore cover everything that decides its scores: its code and its reference inputs, such as an answer key or a table of expected values. The scorer takes the reference when it is built, since it opens no file by path (R13). If the version names the code alone, `build_analysis` can build the scorer from another reference and the digest still matches: nothing is refused and no re-score is recorded, though the scores are not the run's. Fold a hash of the reference into the version string, taken from the bytes the scorer parses: `version = f"2+key-{hashlib.sha256(key_bytes).hexdigest()[:12]}"` (the [study guide](study-guide.md#analysing-without-the-platform) runs one). A reference corrected after the run then changes the version, and the analysis is refused until it is asked to `--rescore`. A scorer built from the platform's settings (paths, a table of expected values) must be built from the same values in `build_analysis`, so keep them out of the platform or in a file of the study. A scorer that declares no `version` is versioned by the hash of the file its code is in, so adding `build_analysis` to that file changes its version and the analysis is refused: declare `version`. With `build_analysis` defined the campaign's scorers are the reference, so a changed scorer is refused at `analyze` unless the analysis is asked to re-score with `--rescore`, which records it.

The command line is `python -m expcore run|merge|check|analyze|report|package|package-check|example` ([`cli.py`](../src/expcore/cli.py)); `run` also takes `--shard i/N [--shard-label L]` ([below](#shards-of-a-campaign)), or `--select FACTOR=L1,L2 --shard-label L [--sequential [--after EARLIER_OUT]]` ([below](#selections-of-a-campaign)), and `--spend-journal FILE` ([below](#shards-of-a-campaign)). Output goes under `<study>/out/`; `merge` writes the batch of a campaign's shards where its first argument says (normally `<study>/out/`), `package` writes a replication package where `--out` says, and `example` writes the example study into a new directory. How to use them is in [`study-guide.md`](study-guide.md).

What the analysis may use. `check` replays every record and also copies the campaign store into the batch (`campaign-snapshot.sqlite`, made with SQLite's online backup, from a private copy of a finished store or over a read-only connection to one a dispatcher holds, as below; the backup copies pages and so needs no particular SQLite version; then each attempt's directory is written relative to the batch and the copy is rebuilt with a plain `VACUUM`, so it holds no path of the machine the campaign ran on; and integrity-checked) and hashes the copy with the records. It lists the records it replayed (`replayed`) and refuses a run record named by a hashed-only pattern, so a check cannot replay nothing and still vouch for the records beside it. **A record is known by its name (R04):** `expcore.records.record.is_record_name(name)` is true for a name `record_filename` gives, `<SHA-256 of the run key>.<attempt>.json`, the one form the core has written a record under in every version, and nothing else is read as a record, whatever its suffix or content. Every other file of the batch is a hashed-only input (an outcome marker, the campaign's snapshot, a [declared side file](#side-files-of-a-record)), the check file, or a dot-name, which the check never reads; anything else fails the check at stage `unexpected`, against its own path, with neither a hash nor a replay, and is counted apart from the records. So does a link to a directory, whatever its name (the walk does not enter it, and a copy of the batch, the merge's, would follow it), and any other entry that is neither a directory nor a regular file (a dangling link, a FIFO), which is never opened. `expcore.replay.check.batch_files(batch, hashed_only) -> BatchFiles(records, hashed, unexpected)` is that walk (`input_files` returns its first two lists, as before), `UNEXPECTED_STAGE` the stage, `unexpected_in(check)` the files a check failed so, `describe_unexpected(files)` the text that names them and what to do, and `outcome_lines(check)` what `check` prints. `require_current_check` walks the batch again and refuses an unexpected file added since a passing check, which changes no input's hash; `archive_batch`, an anonymised package's build (`package.identity.verify_inputs`) and `merge` refuse one too, and `rescore_files` and `archived_records` take records by name only. It refuses a store with attempts still running, and one a dispatcher holds, unless `--partial`, which the check records. `analyze` and `report` then use only those bytes: the snapshot, never the live store, and the replayed records, each read once and compared with the check's hash. They cross-validate the two: every record maps to an attempt of the snapshot, and every attempt scored (state `done`, a scored treatment failure included) has a replayed record that agrees with it on run key, attempt, outcome and treatment flag. Any mismatch is refused, naming it. With `--partial`, attempts still running at the check are tolerated, a final attempt no replayed record covers is left out. Every saved output (`export.json`, `analysis.json`, `report.md`) says whether the analysis is partial and lists the attempts left out (run key, attempt, reason); `analysed_attempts` holds only what was scored, and every count in the report is of that population, with the left-out runs' attempts in a column of their own.

How a reader opens a store (R16). The campaign keeps its store (`out/state.sqlite`) in WAL mode for good: nothing checkpoints it into a rollback journal when the campaign ends. A finished store is still a WAL database, and after a kill a `-wal` holding committed transactions stays beside it.

An open of a WAL database in place, a read-only one included, makes its `-shm` and `-wal` beside it, cannot remove them, and fails with `attempt to write a readonly database` in a read-only directory. So no reader of a finished store opens it in place:

- **A finished store is read from a private copy** (`campaign.storecopy`). Its file and its `-wal` are copied as they are, the `-shm` never (SQLite rebuilds it from the `-wal`). Each file's signature is taken before and after the copy: device, inode, size, modification and change times, and the SHA-256 of its contents, read in chunks; a missing `-wal` is part of it. A copy is kept only when both signatures of each file are equal and the SHA-256 of the bytes copied equals both. File times move only on the kernel's coarse tick, on tmpfs too, so a write in place can leave size, times and inode unchanged, and only the contents show it. What makes the copy one state is the reader's lock, below. The signatures catch a writer that ignores the lock and leaves a trace in what they sample, but no copy of files is a snapshot against one that puts both files back between the reads. A copy across which either file changed is made again, at most 3 times, 0.25 s apart, and then refused (`StoreChanged`, a `SnapshotError` in the snapshot), naming the file that changed. The copy is opened read-write in a temporary directory, so its `-wal` replays, and that directory is removed after. A killed dispatcher's last transactions, which only its `-wal` holds, are read this way.
- **Three readers read a store so:** `check` (the fingerprint, then the snapshot), `merge` (each shard's store) and `run --after` (each earlier selection's store). Each holds the store's lock as a reader (`lock.ReaderLock`) across its copy. The existing `campaign.lock` is opened read-only, never created, and locked with a shared, non-blocking `flock`. That is refused while the dispatcher holds its exclusive one, and keeps a dispatcher from starting while the reader holds it. A store a dispatcher holds is refused by `merge` and `--after`. A store whose lock cannot be held is never read, as a finished store or in place, by any of the three, `check --partial` included:
  - a dispatcher makes the lock file before it opens the store, so an `out/` with none never had one, or was copied without it, and nothing would keep a dispatcher off: refused, "no dispatcher ever ran there" (copy `out/` whole);
  - a lock file that cannot be opened (no read permission) or locked (a file system without `flock`) is refused, naming it and the reason (`LockUnavailable`). Only a lock a dispatcher holds sends `check --partial` in place. `check` refuses it unless `--partial`, which backs the live store up in place over a read-only connection (`take_snapshot(..., in_place=True)`, `read_fingerprint(..., in_place=True)`), the one consistent read of a store being written. That leaves the `-shm` and `-wal` a live store has anyway, and a package drops every `out/state.sqlite-*`. A read-only `out/` is read as it is, and the store's directory keeps every byte.
- **The sealed store is the snapshot**, a single rollback-journal file that never has a `-wal` or `-shm`. Every later reader reads it once as bytes, compares them with the hash and opens them in memory, never the file in place: `analyze`, `report`, `regenerate`, the check of a merged batch, a package's build and its `make check`. The anonymised build rewrites a copy in a temporary directory. No read changes a hashed batch or a package by a byte.
- **Never open a finished store with `immutable=1`.** It ignores the `-wal`, so a killed campaign's store would read as it was before its last transactions.

The campaign fingerprint in the export (#243, C36). `export.json` has one more top-level key, `campaign_fingerprint`, after every other key and before a shard's own `shard`: `{"sha256": <hex>, "fingerprint": {...}}`. The `fingerprint` is the one the store records, exactly as the store keeps it (the export reads the store's, or its snapshot's, `meta` row and never computes one again), and `sha256` is the SHA-256 of its canonical JSON (`manifest.fingerprint_sha256`), which is the `fingerprint` that `merge.json` names for a merged batch. A report can so read a setting that holds for the whole campaign (each mount level's declared `io_mode`, `tool_call_bound_s`, the caps, the platform's data paths) from the export, without opening `state.sqlite`, and can tell which campaign it reads. The fingerprint holds digests of the study and its configuration and the environment's names, versions and, for an install from a URL, its source; it names no host, path or secret (O12), and what it holds is what the snapshot a package ships holds. A store that records no fingerprint (it was never started as a campaign) exports without the key. In the export of a merged batch the key holds the fingerprint every shard shares, once: a merge refuses shards of different fingerprints (C29), and the join of their exports (`campaign.export_join`) refuses them again, and refuses exports of which only some carry the key. An anonymised package's export holds the fingerprint without the environment's `sources`, as its snapshot does, and the hash of that.

The key is not one of the keys `read_export` requires: an export from before it reads as it did. `read_export` refuses an entry whose form is not `{sha256, fingerprint}` or whose `sha256` is not the SHA-256 of the fingerprint beside it, so a hand-edited export does not read. To check which campaign an export is of, `expcore.campaign.export.verify_export_fingerprint(export, expected_sha256)` takes the hash of the store or snapshot the report is read with (`stored_fingerprint_sha256(store_or_snapshot)`) or merge.json's `fingerprint`, and returns a line saying which hash it found, or `fingerprint not recorded` for an export without the key (it does not refuse that one); it raises `ExportError` for an export of another campaign. `analyze` makes the same check on the export it builds, against every snapshot it read and, for a merged batch, merge.json's hash, and refuses with `Refused` on a mismatch. `package-check` (`study.pipeline.regenerate`) names a saved `export.json` of another campaign as `export.json: the export is of another campaign: its campaign fingerprint hash is ..., but the one it is read with is ...`, and one without the key as `export.json: differs from what the records give (fingerprint not recorded)`. `check` makes no snapshot-versus-export comparison: it does not read `export.json`, which is an output of `analyze`, and one left from an earlier campaign must not stop the check that makes a new analysis possible.

A merged batch (`python -m expcore merge OUT SHARD_OUT...`, #154) is the batch of a campaign run as shards, each with its own store, `out/` and platform. The merge takes each shard's `out/` and writes `OUT/runs/` (the union of the shards' runs), `OUT/shards/<index>/campaign-snapshot.sqlite` (each shard's snapshot of its store, taken with the same online backup) and `OUT/merge.json`: `{"schema": "expcore-merge/1", "fingerprint", "rule", "count", "environment_identity", "shards": [{"index", "label", "started_utc", "shares", "runs", "snapshot_sha256"}]}`, where `fingerprint` is the SHA-256 of the campaign fingerprint every shard shares, `runs` is how many runs the shard planned, and `shares` is what the shard recorded. `OUT` must not exist or must be empty; a refused merge leaves nothing behind, and writes nothing into a shard (an `OUT` that is a shard's `out/` or lies below one, by its path or through a link, is refused before anything is made). It refuses, naming the shard (`shard <index> (<label>)`) and the reason, shards that are not one campaign run against one environment (C29): a core at another commit, which it names with the first shard's (`merge.check_core`, first of the fingerprint checks), or recorded in the other form, by how an earlier core was installed beside one recorded by its identity, and a shard of that earlier form even among its own kind (`study.merge.refuse_earlier_form`: it does not say which core ran it, so its shards merge with the core that ran them; a batch an earlier core merged is still checked and analysed), another rule or count, an index missing or given twice, a label used twice, another campaign fingerprint, a run in two shards, a run that the shards' rule over the whole plan (`assign()`, or `assign_by_model()` with the layout of their fingerprint) gives to another shard, a run of the plan no shard holds, a shard with attempts still running, a shard whose `out/` holds `NOT-FINAL.json` (a driver's check of its results did not pass: the HPC shard driver's post-run script, O63), a record that fails the replay check `check` makes, or another environment identity. `check` on `OUT/runs` makes no snapshot: it compares each snapshot with the SHA-256 merge.json names, refuses anything under `OUT/shards/` that merge.json does not list, rebuilds merge.json from the snapshots and the study's plan through the merge's own checks and refuses a file that is not that document (a merged batch has at least 2 shards, each snapshot under its own index), replays the records as one batch, and records merge.json's SHA-256 under `merge` (a check of an unmerged batch has `snapshot`, never `merge`). Rebuilding needs the plan, so the check of a merged batch loads the study's configuration, which imports the platforms `[study]` names. `analyze` and `report` read merge.json and each snapshot once, compared with those hashes, rebuild merge.json from those snapshots in the same way, and cross-validate the records against all the snapshots. The export they build joins the shards' exports: each run list in the order of the whole plan, every entry (those of `excluded_attempts` too) with `"shard": <index>`, the figures that are sums summed (`planned_runs`, `running_attempts`, and the rows of `discarded_usage`, `retries_by_kind`, `infrastructure_by_condition` and `budget_holds` that share a key), `deployment_shares` summed per model and fingerprint with every share computed again from the sums (a fingerprint is confirmed when any shard confirmed it, by the witness of the first shard that did; a shard whose plan has no model with a set exports none, which counts as no entries, and the joined export has the key exactly when any shard's does), a top-level `"shard_retries_by_kind"` (every row of each shard's own `retries_by_kind` with `"shard": <index>` added, in shard order, so a set-aside is still readable per shard once `retries_by_kind` is summed; an empty list says they were recorded and none was set aside) and a top-level `"shards"` that repeats merge.json's list. Each analysed entry also gets `"wall_s"` and `"turn_latency_s"` (set out below, with the report that shows them). The joined run lists must hold every run of the plan exactly once. An unmerged batch's export, and the export of one shard on its own, has none of these keys and is byte for byte what it was before shards existed. `dispatch_seq`, `started_at` and `lease_wait_s` stay each shard's own, so the report's median dispatch position pools the positions of every shard's own sequence; every other figure, and the scores, equal what one driver of the whole campaign gives, except the keys a merged export alone carries and the witness of a deployment fingerprint two shards both confirmed, which is the first shard's where one driver names the earliest. A package of a merged batch lists `out/merge.json` and `out/shards` beside `out/runs`. Every build, plain or anonymised, first compares merge.json with the SHA-256 the check names and each snapshot with the SHA-256 merge.json names, and refuses any difference before it reads or rewrites either; an anonymised build rewrites each snapshot, then merge.json as the merge writes it from the rewritten snapshots (their new hashes, and the hash of the campaign fingerprint without the environment's sources).

The report of a merged batch (#154). The export of a batch merged from shards has `shards` (the list `merge.json` keeps: index, label, start, shares, runs and snapshot hash of each shard) and `shard: <index>` in each run entry. Only then does `report.md` have a section `## Shards`, after the conditions: one row per shard and condition, with the shard's label and start, the condition's planned, analysed, exhausted, unfinished and left-out runs in that shard, and the shares of its planned runs that were analysed, exhausted and unfinished, `n/a` (never 0%) where the shard ran none of the condition's runs. The shards are a check that the hosts are interchangeable, not a factor of the design: every figure is descriptive, no test across shards is made, every contrast pools all of them, and no score is shown by shard (a pair never crosses shards, so a host effect meets both arms alike, and a score by shard would show which instances landed where). A run entry whose shard `shards` does not list is refused. `expcore.accounting.shard_accounting` holds the counts. An export without `shards` gives the report an unsharded batch has, byte for byte.

Two more tables follow in the section (`expcore.report_shards`), each figure with its N or `not recorded`, never 0. *Infrastructure attempts by shard and kind*: the attempts set aside in all the shard's runs, the charged attempts of its exhausted runs by kind (read from each reason, `<kind>: <detail>`) and the attempts scored as treatment (an analysed entry with `treatment` and its `infra_kind`). *Time by shard*: median and 90th percentile, with N, of the wall time of the analysed runs and of each model turn's latency, and the median reset time. The run lists already carry what the exhausted and treatment columns need. The rest is what a merge's sums lose or what no export holds. The export of a merged batch, and only it, carries the first three of the following; a figure whose source an export lacks reads `not recorded`:

- Attempts set aside by kind per shard: `retries_by_kind` and `infrastructure_by_condition` are summed over the shards, and the counts live in each shard's store (`attempts`: `state`, `infra_kind`, `outage_kind`, `charged`). The merged export has a top-level `shard_retries_by_kind`: those rows of each shard's own `retries_by_kind`, each with `shard` added last, in shard order (an empty list is recorded, so a shard with none reads 0).
- Wall time per run: the analysed entry's `wall_s` is the store's `attempts.wall_s` of its analysed attempt, read from the shard's snapshot (`null` for a store from before the column: unknown, never 0). Elsewhere an analysed entry keeps only `started_at`, and `discarded_usage` only sums of the discarded attempts' `wall_s`.
- Latency per model turn: the analysed entry's `turn_latency_s` is a list of `latency_s`, one per turn of the attempt's record in turn order (`TurnRecord.latency_s`; the store keeps each turn's token counts and no time), read from the verified records the analysis reads anyway; `[]` for an attempt that never got a reply.
- Reset time per run: no store column, record field or export key holds it, and nothing in the core times it. The report reads `reset_s` in each `analysed_attempts` entry if an export carries one, and the core's merged export does not, so it shows `not recorded`.

`expcore.study.merged_times` adds `wall_s` and `turn_latency_s` to each shard's export before the join, so each analysed entry of a merged export is `run_key`, `digest`, `condition`, `factors`, `attempt`, `run_dir`, `treatment`, `infra_kind`, `dispatch_seq`, `started_at`, `lease_wait_s`, `lease_outcome`, `admission`, `wall_s`, `turn_latency_s`, `shard`, in that order, and the last two top-level keys are `shard_retries_by_kind` and `shards`. The export of an unmerged batch, and of a shard on its own, is made by `campaign.export` alone and has none of the five. The values are exact copies (a wall time and a latency are not rounded), and the report rounds them to four significant figures. An export without `shards` ignores all of these.

Side files are verified with the records. A file a run writes beside its record, which the record names by its SHA-256, is declared in `study.toml` ([below](#side-files-of-a-record)). `check` hashes every file of a declared name in the batch it parses into its inputs, never replays one, and pairs each with the one record in its directory; the check records the declaration (`side_files`) and each side file's hash in `inputs`. `analyze`, `report`, `regenerate` and a package's `make check` refuse a check made under another declaration, refuse a side file changed, removed or added since the check, and read each side file once to pair it with its record again, whatever the check file claims; the bytes so read are what a [scorer](#scorer) that takes `side_files` is handed. A study that declares none is checked exactly as before, and its check file holds no `side_files`.

### Shards of a campaign

`run STUDY --shard i/N [--shard-label L [--shard-label-wide]]` runs shard i of N of one campaign on one host, with its own store, `out/` and platform ([`campaign/shard.py`](../src/expcore/campaign/shard.py), [`campaign/shard_run.py`](../src/expcore/campaign/shard_run.py)). It is given on the command line only, never in `campaign.toml` (which may declare only how many shards serve each model, below), so every shard has the campaign fingerprint of the whole plan. N is at least 2. The label is a plain lower-case name of 1 to 64 characters (`[a-z0-9-]`, starting with a letter or digit), `shard-i` by default, and never a host name or an address: it has no dot, so a fully qualified name or an IPv4 address is refused. `--shard-label-wide` (with `--shard-label`) allows `.` and `_` as well, for `ShardSpec.parse(..., wide_label=True)`; `ShardSpec.from_json` always reads the wide form, so a store or an export that pinned such a label reads back, and a resume of it gives `--shard-label-wide` again. Without `--shard` and without a `[shards]` table nothing here is used: the store, the records, the export and the report are byte for byte what they are without this feature. With a `[shards]` table, loading the study still runs the budget split (`shard_budgets_of`), whether or not the run is sharded, and so can refuse an unsharded run, for the budget reasons listed under sharding by model below.

- **Its plan** is the whole plan's runs of the pairs (task, model, seed) assigned to the shard by `shard.assign`, balanced per model, each run keeping its seq in the whole plan and the order of the order seed (C28). `manifest.shard_manifest` filters the manifest; `plan_problem` and the check of the run set compare a start against that part of the plan its fingerprint declares. `shard.partition(pairs, spec, layout)` picks the assignment by the spec's rule, and the merge checks each run with it.
- **Sharding by model** (C32): `[shards] per_model = { model = count, ... }` in `campaign.toml` (`CampaignConfig.shards`, a `shard.ModelShards` that keeps the declared order) puts `{"per_model": [[model, count], ...]}` under `caps.shards` of the campaign fingerprint, only when declared, and makes the spec's rule `shard.RULE_BY_MODEL` (`expcore-shard/3`; `shard.by_model` converts the command line's spec). N must be the sum (`--shard i/N` with another N is refused at start); a count of the models of one unit above its `[scheduler.unit_caps]` cap, a model of the plan without a count, and a sum below 2 are refused at load. `ModelShards.at(i)` gives shard i's model and its place k among that model's shards, the models in declared order; `shard.assign_by_model` deals each model's pairs among its own shards as `assign` deals them. Such a shard's shares are `{"unit_caps": {its model's unit: 1}, "budget": ...}` (`shard_run.share_settings_by_model`), its budget part from `campaign.shard_budget.shard_budgets`: its model's `per_model_usd` split over the model's shards, and `max_usd` over the priced models' shards in proportion to planned runs times `shard_budget.reservation_usd` (the larger of `bound_usd` with and without the reply maximum), in micro-dollars rounded down. A shard of a model that costs nothing records `max_usd` 0 and runs without a `Budget`. `load_study` refuses, as a `ConfigError` naming `campaign.toml`, a budget too small for one run's reservation in every priced shard, a priced shard whose part of `max_usd` or of a ceiling above 0 is below one reservation, and a model with no price that is not free. Stores pinned under `expcore-shard/2` resume and merge as before.
- **A shard that could never run its pairs** is refused at start, before the store is opened (`shard_run.refuse_unrunnable`), naming the model: one holding pairs of a model whose concurrency share is 0, which under `expcore-shard/2` is a unit cap below N for the shards past it, and under `expcore-shard/3` any model but the shard's own.
- **Its store** pins four facts in the meta table at the first start, inside the transaction that pins the order seed, as canonical JSON (sorted keys, no spaces): `shard_spec` (`ShardSpec.to_json()`: rule, index, count, label), `shard_started_utc`, `environment_identity` (the checked identity) and `shard_shares` (`{"unit_caps": {unit: n}, "budget": {"max_usd": x, "per_model_usd": {model: x}} or null}`).
- **A resume refuses**, as a `ShardMismatch`, naming the difference and leaving the store as it was: another spec, label or rule; a sharded store resumed without a shard, and an unsharded store resumed with one; another environment identity; other shares. The plan of a sharded store never grows on resume, whatever `allow_growth` says (the assignment depends on the whole plan): the `FingerprintMismatch` for the grown plan says so.
- **Shares** (C30) are applied after the fingerprint is computed from the totals: each unit's cap becomes `caps_shares(...)` and `max_usd` and `per_model_usd` become `budget_shares(...)`, by `dataclasses.replace` on the scheduler settings and the budget; the release step and file and `workers` stay the host's (a study's `campaign.toml` sets no release step or file, so a study's budget hold does not end). A total below N is refused at start, naming its key, before the store is opened; so is a shard that has no run. A `per_model_usd` ceiling of 0 holds its model entirely and shares as 0 for every shard (`share_usd(..., allow_zero=True)`); a total of 0 anywhere else is still refused.
- **The environment identity** is `StudyPlugin.environment_identity`, a function with no arguments that returns names mapped to SHA-256 hex digests (`shard.check_identity`). The core calls it once, when a shard starts, never in an unsharded campaign, and never at plugin load; the plugin is refused at load only if the field is set and not callable. Absent, the identity is an empty mapping. A function that raises, or one that returns names that are not text, or anything else that is not names mapped to digests, is a refusal at start that names the plugin's file, before the store is opened. Names and digests only: no host name, path or label that identifies a machine (C29).
- **Its start says its environment** (C29): as a shard starts, after the refusals above and before the store is opened, `run` prints one block on stderr (`shard_run.environment_block`): the SHA-256 of the fingerprint's environment section (which every shard host of the campaign must print alike), the Python, and the distributions, each with its version up to `LIST_LIMIT` (60) of them and each with 12 characters of the SHA-256 of its version and source beyond that. No source URL, commit or path is printed. A host that would not merge shows before it runs, and a resume the store refuses for another environment has already said which one this host has. An unsharded start prints nothing.
- **The export** of a shard has a top-level `shard` object, last: `{spec, started_utc, environment_identity, shares}`, read from the store's meta. Per-run entries are the ones every export has. An unsharded export has no such key.
- **The spend journal, and spend lost in a kill** (O62; [`campaign/spend_journal.py`](../src/expcore/campaign/spend_journal.py), [`campaign/lost_spend.py`](../src/expcore/campaign/lost_spend.py), [`campaign/lost_ledger.py`](../src/expcore/campaign/lost_ledger.py)). `run STUDY ... --spend-journal FILE` (any campaign, sharded or not; `run_study(spend_journal=)`, `Scheduler(spend_journal=)`) creates FILE new (`O_EXCL`, mode 0600) with a header line and attaches it to the store (`Store.spend_journal`). Every transaction that changes what was spent notes a line, written once it commits and dropped when it rolls back, each flushed and fsynced before the spend it allows (`Store.transact`): `begin_attempt`, `add_topup` and `finish_attempt` an `attempt` line (the row's `id`, `run_key`, `model`, `pid`, `started_utc`, `state`, `reserved_capped`, the four counts, `turn_usage`, `usage_complete`, `replies_checked`, `topups`, `topup_usd`, and how many call lines of it the journal holds so far, `calls_sent` and `calls_closed`, None for an attempt that keeps no call log), `note_call_sent` a `call` line (`id`, `run_key`, `phase` `sent`) before the request goes out, `note_call_answered` a `call` line (`phase` `closed`, `entry`: the reply's four counts or null), and `add_topup(close_call=True)` an `attempt` line that also closes the request (`closes` `"failed"`, counted in its `calls_closed`), one line so a write cut short loses the top-up and the close together (C05: a line per call, so the journal grows linearly in the calls; `spend_journal.read_journal` sums an attempt's call lines into `Journal.calls`, `CallLines(sent, closed, consistent)`, refusing a call line of an attempt the journal never began or of another run, and marking inconsistent the lines of an attempt whose attempt lines name other counts or that keeps no log), `probe_spend.add` a `probe` line (`event_id`, `model`, `usd`), `reply_cap.mark_unheld` an `unheld` line (`model`). Every line carries `campaign` (the hash of the fingerprint the store records, or, after the ledger's first start, the one it kept, so a grown plan keeps it) and `shard` (the shard's whole spec as its store pins it, `shard_spec`'s canonical JSON, so the same label in another partition is another shard; null when unsharded). A line that cannot be written raises `SpendJournalError` after the commit and before the spend (no run starts, no top-up or probe is sent; a worker's error stops dispatch, C17), and so does every later write. Before its probes the start counts every other `*.jsonl` of FILE's directory (`lost_spend.open_journal`): a journal of another campaign or shard, a line before the last that is not whole, or a store whose own journal (the ledger's `journal`) is missing refuses the start, as `run refused:`, leaving no journal of its own; what the store lacks is recorded in its meta under `lost_in_kill` (`{"campaign", "journal", "counted": {name: {"counted_utc", "attempts", "charges"}}}`) with one `lost_in_kill` event per journal that lost anything, each journal once. Of the store's own journal, an attempt is matched to the row of the same id, run key, start and pid, extra top-ups past the row's are charges, and a probe charge whose event the store holds is the store's; every other journal not yet counted is lost whole; a model any counted journal took off the reply maximum is taken off it in the store, in the same transaction (`reply_cap_lost_in_kill`). The budget gate (`BudgetGate._costs`) and `selection_ledger.spend_of` add each lost attempt as an `AttemptCost` of state `lost_in_kill` (`attempt_bounds.LOST_STATE`), charged as its row would be (when the journal never saw it end, `attempt_bounds.journaled_cost`: by its journaled call log, else its reserved bound, C05; else `attempt_bounds.row_cost`: measured or its bound; its top-ups on top), and each charge at its dollars; a ledger that cannot be read holds every model. The export has an optional `lost_in_kill` after `reset_settings`: one entry per lost attempt (`journal`, `kind` `attempt`, `attempt_id`, the journaled fields but `turn_usage` and the call log, `ended`, and `usd`, its charge when counted, and `basis` when its end was not journaled, counted by a core with the call log) and per charge (`journal`, `kind` `topup` or `probe`, `model`, `usd`, a top-up's `run_key`); `join_exports` concatenates the shards' with `"shard"` added; `report.md` gains "### Spend lost in kills" after the operator holds (`expcore.report_lost`), with a Basis column when any entry has `basis`. Without the key both are what they were.
- **The call log, and what an interrupted attempt is charged** (C05; [`campaign/interrupted_spend.py`](../src/expcore/campaign/interrupted_spend.py)). Three attempt columns: `calls_sent` (requests sent, NULL when no log is kept), `calls_usage` (JSON list of each closed request's `[input, output, cache writes, cache reads]`, `null` for a reply billed and not read, `"failed"` for a request closed by its last failed send, which `Store.add_topup(..., close_call=True)` writes in the transaction of that send's top-up, from `Ledger.charge(..., close_call=True)`) and `spend_basis` (`measured`, `measured_plus_inflight` or `bound`; NULL when no log was kept). `Store.begin_attempt(..., calls_logged=True)` starts an empty log (the attempt runner passes it with a budget); `Store.note_call_sent(attempt_id)` and `Store.note_call_answered(attempt_id, counts)` each update a running attempt's log in a transaction of their own and journal a `call` line, refusing (`AttemptRefused`) an attempt that is not running or keeps no log. The campaign's guard (`topup.AttemptSpend(..., calls_logged=True)`) calls them before each cleared request and after each reply; `SpendGuard.after_reply` and `models.spend.after_reply` take `cache_creation_tokens`, `cache_read_tokens` and `unread` keyword arguments (all optional), which `RecordingBackend` passes. `recovery.recover` sets `spend_basis` for each attempt with a log it interrupts (from the log when the store's `lost_in_kill` ledger names no journal and its meta has no `copied_back_utc`, which `snapshot.rebase_run_dirs` writes, else `bound`), with the counts in its `recovered_interrupted` event; `lost_spend.reconcile` sets the log and basis of each interrupted row its own journal lists from the merged log (`interrupted_spend.merged` of the row's and the journal's call lines summed, `interrupted_spend.log_of`), and keeps a lost attempt's summed log in the ledger as `calls_sent` and `calls_usage`, with a `spend_basis_confirmed` event. `attempt_bounds.attempt_costs` charges an interrupted row with a basis by `interrupted_spend.trusted_log` (`bound` unless its log still says its basis) and `AttemptCost` has a trailing `basis` (None for any other attempt); `mean_run_usd` leaves interrupted attempts out. The export has an optional `interrupted_spend` after `lost_in_kill`, present once any interrupted attempt records a basis: per interrupted attempt in id order `attempt_id`, `run_key`, `model`, `basis` (`bound` for one without), `calls_sent`, `calls_answered` (None without a log); `join_exports` concatenates the shards' with `"shard"` added; `report.md` gains "### Interrupted attempts' spend" after the spend lost in kills (`expcore.report_interrupted`), a row per model (and shard) counting each basis. Without the key both are what they were. **An attempt that raises after its model loop** is charged by the same log (`campaign/after_loop.py`). `models.spend.after_loop()`, which `records.build` calls as `run_agent` returns, sets `AttemptSpend.loop_ended` (a guard without an `after_loop` method is not asked); the attempt runner then settles the result of an executor that raised with `after_loop.settle_usage(result, calls_sent, calls_usage)` from the attempt's own row. A complete log (at least one request, every request closed with its counts, or by a failed last send) gives the attempt the replies' totals, the per-turn usage, `usage_complete` true and `AttemptResult.spend_basis` `measured`; a log that is unreadable, empty, or holds a request with no end entry, a reply not read or one with a count missing leaves its usage unknown and sets `bound`, so it is charged its bound. `finish_attempt` writes `spend_basis` on the finished row (NULL for every other finished attempt: one with a record, a raise during the loop that sent a request, an attempt without a budget), and the outcome marker carries it as an optional field of schema 3, written only when there is one. This changes what such an attempt is charged: the replies' cost where it was the bound, in the budget gate, the export's usage totals and a done run's measured cost (`mean_run_usd`). **An attempt that raised before its loop ended, with no request in its complete log, is charged nothing** (C05; [`campaign/no_request.py`](../src/expcore/campaign/no_request.py)). Whatever raised (an `InfraError` of any kind, or any other exception: a fault that could not be induced, a state error, a failure of the executor before its loop), the attempt runner settles the result with `no_request.settle_unsent(result, calls_sent, calls_usage, topup_usd, backend)` from the attempt's own row, and `no_request.no_request_in` is the one place that decides "complete and no request": the log is readable and consistent with `calls_sent` 0 and no entry (a request begun and not closed, a send with no reply, one closed by a failed last send and a log that cannot be read are not), the row's `topup_usd` is 0, and the backend's own `requests_sent`, an optional attribute that the core's adapters keep, is exactly 0 (the log sees only the requests the core's recording makes, so an empty log alone cannot be told from that of an executor that sends another way; a backend with no count, or a count that is None, above 0, no whole number or unreadable, confirms nothing, and its attempt keeps its bound). Then the attempt keeps zero tokens and no turn, `usage_complete` true and `spend_basis` `measured` on its row and in its marker, so the budget gate's spent figure, the export's usage totals and the report count 0 USD for it, and a `usage_measured_zero` event gives the reason ("no model request in a complete journal"), written in the result's transaction (a kill between the marker and the store keeps the counts and the basis, not the event). Anything else keeps its usage unknown and its bound, with no basis, as before. An attempt past its loop is `settle_usage`'s (an empty log there contradicts the loop having run: the bound), one killed before its result was stored is recovery's, and an attempt without a budget keeps no log and nothing changes. A study's own backend that keeps no `requests_sent` keeps the bound for such an attempt, as before; it should keep a count, as the core's adapters do.
- **Snapshots of a running `out/`** (O62; [`campaign/out_snapshot.py`](../src/expcore/campaign/out_snapshot.py)), for a store on a host that can die outright: `take(out, into, scratch=, facts=, live=, now=)` backs the store up first (`snapshot.take_snapshot`, in place while `live`, from a private copy otherwise), copies the rest of `out/` after it (`runs/`, the lease notes, the logs, the lock file; not the live store's own files), leaving out every `runs/<digest>/try<N>` no row of the copy names and every dot-name, checks every ended attempt's marker is there and reads every file back against its hash, writes `snapshot.json` (`format` `expcore-out-snapshot/1`, `number`, `taken_utc`, `facts`, `campaign`, `store`, `files`, paths relative to `out/`) last and renames `.snap-<n>.part` to `snap-<n>`, keeping the newest `KEEP` (2). `restore(snapshot, out)` copies one into a new `out/`, every file hashed against the manifest (one missing, added or changed fails it), the store's integrity checked, every ended attempt's marker present (as `take` checked it), its run directories pointed at `out/runs` (`snapshot.rebase_run_dirs`), and its lease directory marked rolled back (`lease.mark_rolled_back`: every note that says released names no holder, and the file `ROLLED_BACK` makes every lock file created there start so, so each key goes to a new holder only once `resource_gone` answers True, and `sweep_dead_holders` looks at a key with no file there too, counting it in `may_be_live` until then, C19); `OutSnapshotError` (also for a manifest that is no object, or a file that cannot be read or written) leaves nothing behind. `snapshots(into)` lists them newest first. `deploy/hpc-federation/shards.py` drives them (its README), and refuses a resume whose every snapshot fails and that has no archive.

### Selections of a campaign

`run STUDY --select FACTOR=L1,L2 [--select ...] --shard-label L [--shard-label-wide] [--sequential [--after EARLIER_OUT ...]]` runs one batch of a campaign: the runs whose declared factors have the given levels, every factor given matching (C38; [`campaign/selection.py`](../src/expcore/campaign/selection.py), [`study/selection_start.py`](../src/expcore/study/selection_start.py)). It is a shard of the same campaign, on the command line only, so the fingerprint is the whole plan's, and everything a shard pins and exports applies, with these differences:

- **The spec** is `selection.SelectionSpec` under `selection.RULE_SELECTION` (`expcore-shard/4: a declared selection of factor levels`), pinned as `shard_spec`: `{"rule", "label", "select": {factor: [levels in the schema's order]}, "sequential": bool}`. `selection.spec_from_json` reads a stored spec of either kind by its rule; `ShardSpec` refuses the rule. A selection has no index: a merge numbers the selections 1..N by their canonical selection text (`merge_selection.numbered`), `SelectionSpec.at(i, N)`, never stored.
- **Its plan** is `manifest.selection_manifest`: the whole plan's runs the selection selects, each keeping its seq and the order; `plan_problem` filters the fingerprint's plan the same way. Refused at start, before the store is opened (a `Refused` naming it): a factor the schema does not declare (`task`, `model` and `seed` are not selected on), a level the factor lacks, a selection of no run or of every run, `[shards] per_model` in `campaign.toml`, `--shard` with `--select`, `--select` without `--shard-label`, `--sequential` or `--after` without `--select`, and `--after` without `--sequential` (the last four as `run refused:`, since a usage error's message is never shown).
- **Its shares** (`campaign.selection_budget`), recorded as `shard_shares`. Concurrent (the default): `unit_caps` each `cap * selected runs on the unit // the plan's runs on it` (0 for a unit it runs nothing on, left out of the scheduler's settings; 0 on a unit it runs on is refused, naming the unit), and `budget` `{"max_usd": floor(max_usd * the selection's reserved bound / the plan's), "per_model_usd": {model: floor(ceiling * selected runs of the model / its planned runs)}}`, the reserved bound being the planned runs of each priced model times `shard_budget.reservation_usd`, all in whole micro-dollars. Sequential: the full `unit_caps`; `budget` the totals minus what the earlier selections spent (`remaining_budget`: the spend rounded up, what is left down, never below 0); and `"after"`: the earlier selections, in the order they ran, each `{"label", "select", "started_utc", "spent_usd", "per_model_spent_usd"}`. In both modes a part of `max_usd`, or of a ceiling above 0, below one run's reservation of a priced model the selection runs is refused, naming the model (`refuse_short`); a part of `max_usd` of 0 (a selection of free models) runs without a `Budget`. A model with no price that is not free is refused when the campaign has a budget.
- **`--after`** (`campaign.selection_after.earlier_selections`) takes each earlier selection's `out/`, its study directory or its store, takes the store's `DispatchLock` and a snapshot (a held lock or a running attempt is refused), and refuses one that is not a sequential selection, has another fingerprint or environment identity, selects a run this one selects, or has an unknown spend; and refuses a set of earlier selections that is not one chain (each ran after exactly those before it), that leaves out one an earlier selection ran after, or whose record of each other no longer matches its store. The spend is `selection_ledger.spend_of`: `attempt_bounds.attempt_costs` (bounds as recorded at dispatch, top-ups included) plus `probe_spend`, per model and in total, the budget gate's figure.
- **A resume refuses** another selection, label, mode or rule, a selection store resumed without `--select` (the message gives the options that resume it), and other shares, which includes other `--after` selections or a change in what they spent.
- **The merge** (`merge_selection.check_selections`, from `merge.check_shards` when any store is a selection) refuses: a shard among selections, fewer than 2, concurrent with sequential, a label twice, another fingerprint or environment identity, a study whose plan is not every condition x task x model x seed the fingerprint declares, a run of the plan selected by two selections or by none, a store holding a run its selection does not select (judged by the plan's factors for the run key, and a store giving a key other factors is refused) or missing one it does, prices (`models.toml` entries or `[agent]` caps) other than the fingerprint's; for sequential selections, a chain that is not exactly each one after all the earlier ones (by the length of `after`), a record in `after` whose label, levels, start or spend differs from that selection's store, and windows that overlap (`selection_ledger.window_of`: from `shard_started_utc` to the latest `started_utc` or `ended_utc` of its attempts; the next must not start before it ends); and, with a budget and the study's prices (`selection_ledger.Pricing`), a total spend over `max_usd` or a `per_model_usd` ceiling plus what releases added (`budget_released`), compared exactly, with one nano-dollar allowed for adding the gate's floats. merge.json's `rule` is `expcore-shard/4` and each entry of `shards` adds `"selection"`, `"sequential"` and `"window"`; `read_merge` takes those keys for that rule only. `check` and `analyze` rebuild merge.json through the same checks, prices included, and refuse a snapshot that does not lie under the index the merge gives it.
- **The merged export** adds `"selection": <label>` to every run entry, after `"shard"`, and its `shards` list carries each selection; the report's `## Shards` table heads its first column `Selection` and names each with its levels, and its note says pairs may cross selections. Scores and analysis equal one driver's of the whole campaign byte for byte (pairs by task, model and seed, across selections).

### Release files

A campaign built in code may name a release file for its harness-error stops (`HarnessStopConfig.release_file`) and one for its budget holds (`Budget.release_file`, with `release_step_usd`); `campaign.toml` takes neither, so a study's campaign has none. A release file belongs to one store: its serials are that store's.

- **Each stop has a serial**, `<count>.<random>` (`3.k7f2qd9a`): a model's stop and the all-stop on harness errors, and a budget hold on a model's ceiling or on the total. It is given in the transaction that records the stop (`release.next_serial`): the count is one more than the last the store gave (meta `stop_serial`), the random part eight base-32 characters, 40 bits from `secrets`, so a store copied back from a snapshot never gives a lost stop's serial to the next. The stop's record keeps it (`serial` in `harness_error_stops` and `budget_holds`), its event (`harness_error_stop`, `harness_error_stop_all`, `budget_hold`) and its hold say `(stop <serial>)`, and `HarnessStops.status()` lists them under `serials`.
- **A line is `<name> <serial>`**, one per line: a model key or `ALL` (the all-stop, or the campaign's total), a space, and the serial. With a release file the hold gives the exact line: `dispatch of m1 is stopped by harness errors (stop 3.k7f2qd9a; to end it, write the line 'm1 3.k7f2qd9a' in the harness stop's release file)`. A line ends the stop in force for its name only when it is that stop's line (runs of blanks count as one space); it is removed when it does, and the file goes with its last line. A file that cannot be rewritten ends nothing.
- **The file's modification time decides nothing.** A line written before or after the stop, by a writer or a file server whose clock is behind or ahead, ends the stop exactly when it names its serial. The time is kept only in the event of the release, with the file's owner.
