- **A line that ends nothing:** a bare name (the form before serials) or another stop's serial for a name with a stop in force stays in the file, where it never matches again, and is said once per process: a warning naming the file and the line that would end the stop, and an event `harness_error_release_refused` or `budget_release_refused` without the file's path. A line whose name has no stop in force is left alone and says nothing.
- **A store recorded before serials**: a stop in force with no serial is given one at the start (`HarnessStops.reconcile`) or at the budget gate's next assessment, in a transaction with an event (`harness_error_stop_numbered`, `budget_hold_numbered`), and its bare name is refused like any other. No release is ever judged by the file's time.

## ModelBackend

`expcore.models.adapters.ModelBackend`; built by the core, never by the executor. The core builds three backends, named by `backend` in `models.toml`: `openai_compat`, `anthropic` and `stub` (`expcore.config.models.BACKENDS`). A study plugs in a backend of its own without editing the core: its model entries name it with a plain lower-case `backend` value, and the plugin supplies the `backend_factory` that builds it ([BackendFactory](#backendfactory)).

```python
class ModelBackend(Protocol):
    @property
    def output_limit(self) -> int | None
    @property
    def context_tokens(self) -> int | None
    def info(self) -> dict[str, Any]
    def begin(self, system: str, user: str, tools: list[dict]) -> None
    async def step(self) -> ModelTurn
    def add_tool_results(self, results: list[ToolResult]) -> None
    def transcript(self) -> list[dict]
    async def probe(self) -> ProbeReply | None
    async def aclose(self) -> None
```

Core guarantees:
- The scheduler builds the backend of a run from the endpoint the campaign admitted (`probe` succeeded once, B07), through a `BackendFactory` (below); a run never resolves a URL of its own (C14). `probe` returns what its one-token reply reported (`ProbeReply`: the `max_tokens` it carried, the input and output counts, the stop reason); a reply without both counts, or a probe that returns `None`, is not admitted, because the token budget is held from the counts each reply reports (B07). A backend that wraps another passes the inner probe's reply on. With a budget, each send of a probe is paid from it, reserved before it goes out (C26); a model whose probe the budget cannot cover is not admitted. A model that is not frozen (B08), whose settings cannot be sent together (B16) or, with a budget, has no price (C12) or no `context_tokens` (C25) is refused before a run starts.
- With a budget, each run is reserved the most its caps let it cost (C25). For a model held to its reply maximum that counts at most the rounds times the reply maximum as output; for any other, the whole token cap and, for the request a run can make past it, the whole context window at the dearest rate. A model is held to it only when the core's own factory builds an `openai_compat` or `anthropic` adapter for it, which sends the maximum as `max_tokens`, and admission's probe showed the endpoint cuts a long reply at a small `max_tokens`; never a model a plugin's factory builds, whatever its `backend`, and no longer once one of its replies reports more than the maximum (C27). A backend of the study's own whose request outgrows the window is outside that bound.
- With a budget, a send the token cap cannot account for reserves its own bound before it goes out (C26): a resend after a connection error or a 5xx from the core's retrier, and every request after a reply that left a count out. The guard reaches the core's adapters through a context variable the attempt runner installs (`expcore.models.spend`), so a core adapter a plugin's backend wraps is covered; a backend of the study's own that resends on its own is not. A top-up that does not fit ends the attempt as infrastructure of kind `budget_hold`.
- The backend is closed (`aclose`) after the executor returns, whatever happens.
- Retries of transient errors, the served-model check and usage totals are the adapters' (B01 to B16); the stub adapter takes a script (`StubAdapter.use(script)`, items: text, a list of `(tool, arguments)`, `{"text", "stop"}`, a ready-made `ModelTurn`, or an exception it raises).
- A model whose entry declares `accepted_fingerprints` (B18) is admitted only when its probe was served by the model (its `api_id` or an alias) under a fingerprint of the set. Admission reads what the probe's reply said it ran from the admission backend's `info()`, as `served_model_ids` and `system_fingerprints`, which the core's OpenAI-compatible adapter fills from the probe; a backend whose `info()` has neither confirms nothing at admission and is not refused for it. Each run's replies are then checked against the set instead of a fingerprint baseline, the served model id against its baseline as before (C16). A declared fingerprint is confirmed by the probe or by the first run whose reply shows it under the model's id, which is a record and not a gate; each attempt records the turns each fingerprint served, and the export's `deployment_shares` and the report's "Deployments" section give the shares. A model that declares no set is admitted, checked and fingerprinted as before.
- **The served-model check of a preflight takes the same set** (B06, B18). `expcore.models.served.judge(served_ids, served_settings, expected_id, requested, accepted_ids=(), *, served_fingerprints=(), expected_fingerprint=None, accepted_fingerprints=())` returns `{"state": "match" | "mismatch" | "unreported", "reasons", "not_reported"}`. A study's preflight passes `accepted_fingerprints=spec.accepted_fingerprints` for each model, and `judge` then judges the fingerprints exactly as the campaign does, by the one rule both call (`expcore.models.served.outside_accepted`, whose split of a mixed reply is `fingerprint_parts`): a reply from any member of the set is the model, so replies from two declared deployments are a `match`; a reply from a fingerprint the set does not list is a `mismatch` whose reason names it and the set; a reply that carried several fingerprints (joined with `MIXED_FINGERPRINTS`) counts only when the set lists each; a reply without a fingerprint is no change. The set replaces the fingerprint baseline, as it does in the campaign (which never consults a baseline fingerprint of a model that declares a set, even one stored before it did), so an `expected_fingerprint` given with a non-empty set is ignored and a preflight may pass both; a bare string for the set is a `TypeError`. An empty set, or none, leaves every result as it was: a baseline (`expected_fingerprint`) or, without one, a run whose replies carried two fingerprints is a `mismatch`. The served model id is judged against `expected_id` and `accepted_ids` as before, whatever the set says. `judge` is not called by the core's own campaign, which checks identity through `ServedIdentities`; a preflight that leaves the set out reports a mismatch for replies the campaign accepts.

Implementer must guarantee:
- `step` returns one reply per call. A token count the reply did not report is `None`, never 0 (R10).
- A failure of the endpoint that outlasts the retries is raised as `InfraError("endpoint", ...)`; a refused key as `InfraError("key_rejected", ...)`. A reply cut at the output limit sets `stop_reason` to a limit stop.
- `info()` and `transcript()` never contain a credential; the base URL has none (R12).
- A backend of a study's own that serves a model declaring `accepted_fingerprints` sets `ModelTurn.system_fingerprint` on each reply that reports one, and after `probe` gives the probe's served model id and fingerprint in `info()` (`served_model_ids`, `system_fingerprints`, each a list); without them no declared fingerprint is confirmed at admission.
- An executor that needs more than the protocol (the example's executor scripts the stub with `use`) checks for that method, never for a class, so a backend that wraps or replaces the stub serves it too.
- A backend may have a `requests_sent` count, the requests it has started, a resend after a failure included; the core's backends have it. The scheduler reads it to tell an attempt that sent no request: after an instrument's first-turn refusal ([below](#refusing-an-attempt-the-first-turn-hook-and-the-post-run-check)) a backend of a study's own without it is taken at the refusal's word, and for any other failure at start (C05, `campaign/no_request.py`) a count of exactly 0 is what confirms that an empty call log means none, so a backend without it keeps its attempt's bound.

### Retries of a reply

`ModelTurn.retries` holds the retries the reply took before it came, one object per retry, oldest first; `()` when it took none. The core's adapters fill it through `expcore.models.retry.Retrier`, and a backend of a study's own fills it the same way or leaves it empty. The keys of an entry are a closed set, `expcore.records.schema.RETRY_FIELDS`, and every one may be left out. Any other key makes the record invalid: writing it raises `RecordError`, and the attempt is recorded as a harness defect, which names the exception's class and not its message, so check the keys when you write the backend.

| Key | Holds |
|---|---|
| `retry` | the retry's number within this reply, from 1 |
| `kind` | the class of error that was retried (below) |
| `error` | the class name of the error |
| `status` | the HTTP status, or `null` for an error that has none (a timeout, a lost connection) |
| `retry_after_s` | the wait the server asked for (`Retry-After`, `Retry-After-Ms`), or `null` |
| `wait_s` | the wait before the retry, never less than `retry_after_s` |
| `message` | the provider's message, cut to 300 characters; empty when the error has none |

Only a transient error is retried, so `kind` is one of five: `rate_limit` (429), `timeout` (408, or a timeout while connecting or sending), `conflict` (409), `server` (5xx) and `connection` (the endpoint could not be reached). The retrier's other kinds never become an entry: `auth` (401 or 403) raises `InfraError("key_rejected", ...)`, `read_timeout` (the request went out and the reply is late) is never resent and abandons the request, `context_overflow` (a 400 or 413 whose code or message names the context) ends the run as the agent's outcome, and `not_found` and `bad_request` are raised unchanged. The record checks the keys and not the words, so a backend of its own uses these five for the same errors, and a reader can then count retries by kind across backends.

### The request given up

When the loop gives up a request already in flight (the run's time ran out and the call was cancelled, or the client's read timeout fired, `RequestAbandoned`), no reply comes back and its usage is unknown. The record's `route.abandoned_request` then holds `reason` (`deadline` or `read_timeout`), `usage` (`"unknown"`) and the adapters' bound on the request, which is **recorded only**: no gate reads it, and the reservation of C25 and C27 is unchanged.

| Field | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `prompt_bound_tokens` | an upper bound on the request's prompt tokens, or `null` when `prompt_bound_by` is `none` |
| `prompt_bound_by` | `bytes` (the bound below), `window` (the model's `context_tokens`) or `none` (no window and nothing to anchor on) |
| `reply_max_tokens` | the `max_tokens` the request carried (`ModelSpec.output_tokens(limits.max_output_tokens)`) |
| `window_tokens` | the model's `context_tokens`, or `null` |
| `bound_checked`, `bound_exceeded` | the completed requests of the conversation that were bounded by bytes, and those whose reported prompt was above the bound |
| `bound_unreported` | the replies of the conversation that left a prompt or completion count out |
| `bound_overlapped` | the requests built while another was still in flight (the adapters send one at a time, so 0 in a run of the loop) |

An entry made with no request in flight (an admission probe, `abandon` after a reply) is bounded by the window. A record written before this, or by a backend of a study's own, has `reason` and `usage` alone, and is priced by the window.

**Which request.** Exactly one send is in flight when a request is abandoned, and it is the request built last. Both SDK clients are built with `max_retries=0`, so the SDK never resends; `Retrier.call` resends the same body only after a transient error (`RetryPolicy.max_retries`, 8 by default), so a resend has the bound of its step. Each failed send before it in the same step (`retry_events` of kind `server` or `connection`) may have been billed too, and each is a further request of the same bound, charged as a top-up when it failed (C26). A read timeout is never resent (B03), and the client's read timeout, `max(ClientSettings.request_timeout_s, time_cap_s + 60)` from the request's start, is never shorter than the run's time cap, so it fires only when the run's time is gone. A cancel while the retrier waits to resend has no request in flight and abandons nothing.

**The bound on its prompt.** Let request k be the last whose reply reported both counts, `in_k` the whole prompt with its cached part (`prompt_tokens` for an OpenAI-compatible endpoint; the uncached remainder and both cache counts for the Messages API) and `out_k` its completion, and n the request in flight:

    P(n) = in_k + out_k + bytes(messages appended after k) + bytes(system prompt, tools and extras, if they changed)
           + FRAMING_MARGIN_TOKENS x (1 + messages appended)

where `bytes` is the length of each message as the adapter sends it, written as a chat template writes it (`message_bytes`: ASCII JSON, every non-ASCII character as its `\uXXXX` escapes, 6 bytes and 12 past the Basic Multilingual Plane, and 5 more for each `<`, `>`, `&` and `'`, which Jinja's `tojson` writes as 6): the reply as it goes back (its reasoning when the entry echoes it, thinking blocks with their signatures, a malformed call wrapped as `{"_raw": ...}`) and each tool result. `FRAMING_MARGIN_TOKENS` is 32. The completion count and the bytes of the reply are both added, though they describe one message, because the count includes tokens its text does not show (reasoning that comes back as a signature) and the bytes cover the reply as it is written again; only one reply is added twice, since the anchor moves with each reply. The prompt bound is the window instead when no reply has reported both counts (the first request: a provider adds text its bytes do not show, such as a tool-use preamble), for the request after a reply that left a count out, when the bound is the window or more, when the history is no extension of the anchor's (its first messages are not the anchor's, compared by digest: a replaced, reordered or shortened history), and for the rest of the conversation once a reply has reported a prompt above its bound or a request was built while another was in flight. A change to the system prompt, the tools or the extras counts all of their bytes as appended.

**The assumptions**, the same for both adapters, are these. A token stands for at least one byte of the text it was read from: true of byte-level BPE and of tokenizers whose tokens cover whole characters, and a token that stands for no text is framing, which the margin covers beyond the 30 bytes or more of JSON structure each message already pays for. The text is the text the template writes, and for a tool call's arguments it is not the text the adapter sent: an OpenAI-compatible server parses the arguments and the template writes them again with `tojson`, which is why `message_bytes` counts escapes and not UTF-8. A template that writes arguments in more bytes than that (indented JSON of a large nested object) breaks the assumption. A reply's reported prompt is the whole prompt it was billed, which the token cap already assumes (C25). The history only grows, every message appended and none changed, which the tracker checks by digest and answers with the window when it fails; a template that drops earlier reasoning only makes the real prompt smaller. Requests are sent one at a time, as the retrier sends them, and an overlap is counted in `bound_overlapped` and costs the conversation its byte bound. Every completed request bounded by bytes tests the first: a reply that reports a prompt above its bound ends the byte bound for the conversation and is counted in `bound_exceeded`. That is a tripwire and not a proof: an endpoint that is wrong only on the request that hangs is not seen. A backend of a study's own has none of this, and its entry keeps `reason` and `usage`.

**The reply, and the dollars.** The reply is at most `reply_max_tokens` where the campaign showed the endpoint applies the `max_tokens` it is sent (C27), and otherwise only the window holds the prompt and the reply together, so the entry holds no total. `expcore.campaign.abandoned_bound.abandoned_request_bound(spec, limits, entry, reply_capped=held_to_reply_maximum(record.totals))` returns `request_bound_tokens` and `request_bound_usd` with `prompt_by` and `reply_by` naming the bounds used: `P x p_in + R x p_out` for a held attempt, `W x p_out + P x max(0, p_in - p_out)` for any other, at the dearest rates of the tiers a prompt of P tokens can fall in (C24), with P never above W. For an entry bounded by the window it is the loose bound, `attempt_bounds.overshoot_usd` for a flat price and a model held to R.

**What the tight bound rests on.** `abandoned_request_bound` also returns the entry's account of the tripwire, for the model and the run the entry belongs to (the adapters keep one tracker per backend and start it over with each conversation, so another model's requests are never in it): `tripwire_checked` (`bound_checked`: the earlier requests bounded by bytes whose reply was tested against the bound) and three counts kept apart, `tripwire_exceeded` (`bound_exceeded`: replies that reported a prompt above their bound), `tripwire_unreported` (`bound_unreported`: replies that left a count out) and `tripwire_overlapped` (`bound_overlapped`: requests built while another was in flight, the abandoned one included). `tripwire_fired_before` is true when any of the three is above zero, false when all three are recorded as zero, and None when none is above zero and one is not recorded. A count the entry lacks (a record from before the bound, a backend of a study's own) is None, never 0, so a run that was never tested does not read as one that passed. An entry with `bound_exceeded` or `bound_overlapped` above zero is bounded by the window, so there the counts say why. They are read from the entry as it stands and add nothing to the record: the schema stays `expcore-record/3`, and a study that reports a tight bound can report beside it what it rests on.

### Usage fields beyond the counts

A provider may report more in its usage object than input and output tokens: an endpoint's own credit count, cache reads and writes, reasoning tokens. `ModelTurn.usage_extra` keeps the numeric ones, per turn, under the provider's own names, and the record holds them in the turn's usage as `transcript[i].usage.extra` (a mapping of name to number) with `extra_cut` beside it (an integer). They are recorded only: the token budget, the totals and the prices read the counts above and nothing here, and no price is looked up for a field until a model's price table names one.

- **Taken as reported.** The reply's usage object is read as a mapping, an SDK model or a plain object. A field is kept when its value is an integer of 64 bits or a finite float and its name is a plain ASCII identifier (at most 80 characters). An object one level down is read the same way under `parent.child`: `completion_tokens_details.reasoning_tokens`, `prompt_tokens_details.cached_tokens` and `cache_creation.ephemeral_5m_input_tokens` are fields. Text, flags, null, lists, non-finite numbers and other names are left out. A zero the provider reported is kept as a zero.
- **Absent, never 0.** A field the reply leaves out is not in the mapping, so a sum of a named field over a run's turns is "not reported" for a turn that lacks it, never a short total. An adapter that read a usage object with no extra field gives `{}` and `extra_cut` 0. A backend that reads none (the stub, or one of a study's own) leaves both at `None` ("not recorded"), and the record then has no `extra` and no `extra_cut` for the turn.
- **Not repeated.** The counts a turn already holds are not among them: `prompt_tokens`, `completion_tokens` and `total_tokens` (their sum) of an OpenAI-compatible reply, and `input_tokens`, `output_tokens` and the two cache counts of an Anthropic reply, which stay in their own fields.
- **Bounded.** At most `expcore.models.usage.USAGE_EXTRA_CAP` (32) fields are kept per turn: the first by name, so the same reply always keeps the same ones, and `extra_cut` is the number of numeric fields the cap left out. A field left out for its type or name is not counted as cut.
- **Replay.** A replay serves the recorded mapping and count exactly, and the replayed record has the same bytes.
- **Record version.** This is record schema version 3 (`expcore-record/3`), which adds `extra` and `extra_cut` to the usage of each turn, both optional ([the schema](#the-run-records-schema)). A record of version 1 or 2 lacks both and stays valid: it loads, writes back to its own bytes and replays, and a replay of it never writes them. A record of version 3 written before this change lacks them too, and is valid. Every new record is version 3 and keeps version 2's first-turn rule. Version 3 is also the first under the reply-count rule (C26, `expcore.records.schema.reply_count_rule`): when the backend counted a billed reply that became no turn, the record's `totals.usage_incomplete` is true, while a record of version 1 or 2 keeps the totals it was written with. The merge-point fixtures pin both: `tests/data/record-v3/01-usage-extra.json` the usage fields, and `02-billed-unread-reply.json` a billed reply the adapter could not read. The third, `03-fault-target.json`, pins the target of a fault ([the schema](#the-run-records-schema)), and a fourth, `04-instrument-refusal.json`, pins the optional `instrument_refusal` ([below](#refusing-an-attempt-the-first-turn-hook-and-the-post-run-check)). A fifth, `05-reserved-capped.json`, pins the optional `totals.reserved_capped` and `totals.reply_cap_unheld` ([the schema](#the-run-records-schema)). A sixth, `06-tool-call-bound.json`, pins the optional `agent_config.tool_server_call_bound_s`, the optional `agent_config.tool_server_tools` beside it, and the optional `elapsed_s` of a tool call ([the per-call bound](#a-per-call-bound-on-a-tool-server-call)).

A backend of a study's own fills `usage_extra` and `usage_extra_cut` with `expcore.models.usage.extra_usage(usage, skip=...)`, which applies these rules, or leaves them at `None`. A value outside the rules (a flag, text, more than the cap, a name that is not a plain identifier) makes the record invalid: writing it raises `RecordError`, as for a retry entry.

### BackendFactory

```python
class BackendFactory(Protocol):
    def __call__(self, spec: ModelSpec, *, seed: int, limits: Limits, base_url: str | None,
                 time_left: Callable[[], float | None] | None) -> ModelBackend
```

The scheduler calls it for admission and for every run with the same `base_url`. The default, `expcore.campaign.executor.default_backend_factory`, builds the adapter the model entry names and refuses a backend the core does not build. A study replaces it with `StudyPlugin.backend_factory`, which `run_study` passes to the scheduler.

The core guarantees: a model entry whose `backend` the core does not build is accepted by `models.toml` only as a plain lower-case name, and only the core's rules that are not about its own adapters apply to it (its `base_url`, if given, its key reference, its limits, its sampling); the plugin is refused at load, naming `models.toml` and the key, when such a model is in the table and the plugin supplies no factory. A plugin's factory is in the campaign fingerprint by its qualified name and its `version`, so a resume under another factory or another version is refused (C06); a study on the core's factory has no such section and keeps its fingerprint. A factory in the plugin file goes by its name alone, since a plugin's module name holds the study's path. The code identity covers a factory whose module's file is among the files `[study] code` lists, and its `version` is taken as declared. Otherwise the `version` speaks for its code, so it is held to the installed distribution that owns the module's file (the one whose `RECORD` lists it, or, for an editable install, the one whose top-level package holds it where it imports from, not a loose file below its project; never a namespace sibling that shares its top-level name): the campaign refuses to start or resume unless the two are equal as versions (`1.0+Build_1` is `1.0+build.1`), naming both. A factory whose file nothing owns and the identity does not cover is refused, naming its module.

The implementer must guarantee: a factory a plugin supplies has a `version` attribute, a non-empty string changed whenever its behaviour changes (the plugin is refused at load without one); the factory decides which models it serves and passes every other model to `default_backend_factory`, so the core's backends keep their own rules (B08, B16); the backend it returns meets the `ModelBackend` contract above; a backend whose transcript is not in a core format registers its parser with `expcore.replay.native.register_native_parser`, under the name its `info()` gives, at the top level of the plugin module or of a module in `[study] platforms`, or the replay check cannot read its records. `check` imports both only when a record names a backend no parser is registered for, says so in one line, and does not call `build`, so it needs no model or key and a parser registered only inside `build` is not there. A record that names an infrastructure kind neither the core nor `[study.kinds]` holds makes it import the `[study] platforms` modules alone, and a batch that needs neither imports nothing from the study.

## ToolProvider and CallContext

`expcore.plugin`. The core ships an MCP stdio client (`expcore.tools.mcp_client.McpToolProvider`) and a wrapper for plain functions (`expcore.tools.function_provider.FunctionProvider`); it has no tool of its own.

```python
class ToolProvider(Protocol):
    def tools(self) -> Sequence[ToolSpec]
    async def execute(self, name: str, arguments: dict[str, Any], *, timeout_s: float, ctx: CallContext) -> ToolOutput

class CallContext(Protocol):
    def infrastructure_wait(self, kind: str) -> AbstractAsyncContextManager[None]
```

`ToolSpec(name, description, input_schema, terminal=False)` is one tool the agent sees: the name is 1 to 64 letters, digits, underscores or dashes and the schema is a JSON schema of type object, both checked when the spec is built. A successful call to a `terminal` tool ends the attempt as `finished` (L09). What a call returns is `ToolOutput(text, is_error=False, error_class=None, source_truncated=None)`:

- `text` is the result, or for an error the message. `is_error` makes the call an error: the model reads its text as a failed call and the record gives the call the status `error`.
- `error_class` names the kind of failure and is allowed only with `is_error`; an error without one is `ToolError`. The model and the record see an error as `<error_class>: <text>` (the class alone when `text` is empty), and the record keeps the class in the call's `error_class` as well. So `text` should not repeat the class: a message that starts `QuerySyntax: ...` under the class `QuerySyntax` reaches the model as `QuerySyntax: QuerySyntax: ...`.
- `source_truncated` is the platform's word on a cut it made itself (L07). `True`: the source hit its own row or size limit, so the result may be cut. `False`: the provider checked, and the source did not cut. `None`, the default: the provider says nothing, and the record keeps `null`. A value that is not one of the three is refused. The core never sets or reads it: it copies it into the call's record, an error's included.

A platform's own cut is told apart from the core's cap by these two flags. The core caps what the model sees at `output_cap_chars` (`[agent]` in `campaign.toml`) after the provider returns: a longer text is cut, a notice that counts inside the cap ends it (a cap shorter than the notice cuts plain, with none), the full text stays in the call's `raw_text`, and `truncated` in the record is `True` (L06). A platform that cuts at its own limit returns text that is already short, so the core cuts nothing: `truncated` stays `False`, the platform's text is the call's `raw_text`, and only `source_truncated=True` tells the platform's cut from a whole result. The core adds nothing to the text of the platform's cut, so the platform says in the text that it cut, as `examples/sparql_platform` does with a last line. Both flags are `True` on one call when the platform's result is still longer than the cap, and the cap, which counts the whole text, may cut the platform's own last line, off or in the middle: the code tool's note of a cut stream is one (the Onedata layer, below, with the edges). A platform that wants the model to meet its own cut and never the core's sets `output_cap_chars` above what its capped result can hold, as the example's `campaign.toml` does.

Core guarantees:
- `timeout_s` is the agent's time left (L05). For a call to a tool server (a provider with a true `tool_server` attribute, which `McpToolProvider` has) under a declared per-call bound it is the bound when that is shorter (`[agent] tool_server_call_bound_s`, [below](#a-per-call-bound-on-a-tool-server-call), L17); any other provider, a code tool among them, is always handed the time left. The loop cuts a call that outlasts the agent's time left and ends the run as a time cap (L05). A provider that can bound its own call honours `timeout_s` too: `McpToolProvider` ends a call at `timeout_s` (L16, [below](#mcptoolprovider)). A provider that does not honour it is cut only at the agent's time left, so a study that declares a bound uses providers that do. The tool box caps what the model sees (L06) and records the full result beside the capped text.
- `ctx.infrastructure_wait(kind)` pauses the agent's clock while it is open and measures the wait live, per kind. A kind outside `WAIT_KINDS` raises at once.
- A malformed call is not executed: the model gets the error and the raw arguments are recorded (L01).
- An `InfraError` raised by the provider ends the attempt as infrastructure of that kind (C02), naming the target it carries (`InfraError(kind, detail, target="providers[1]")`, a position label, never a configured name); any other exception is a harness defect: the attempt is set aside (L08).

Implementer must guarantee:
- `tools()` does not change during a run.
- Everything the model should see, errors included, is a `ToolOutput`. Raising is for infrastructure (`InfraError`) and for defects.
- Blocking work does not run on the event loop (`asyncio.to_thread`, or `FunctionProvider`), and bounds itself with `timeout_s`: a thread cannot be killed (L15).
- Every wait on infrastructure is wrapped in `ctx.infrastructure_wait`. What the agent's own call does (executing, reading data, a federation operation it triggers) is never a wait.

### FunctionProvider

`expcore.tools.function_provider.FunctionProvider` makes a provider of plain functions, the usual way for a study or a platform to give the agent a tool that is Python code.

```python
class FunctionProvider:  # expcore.tools.function_provider
    def __init__(self, tools: Sequence[tuple[ToolSpec, ToolFunction]])  # ValueError: a tool name twice, a function that cannot take timeout_s
    def tools(self) -> Sequence[ToolSpec]  # the specs, in the order given
    def abandoned_threads(self) -> int     # cancelled calls whose thread may still be running
```

The calling convention:
- It takes `(ToolSpec, function)` pairs. A function serves the tool of its spec, and a tool name appears once in a provider.
- A plain function is `f(arguments, *, timeout_s)`. `arguments` is the call's arguments as a dict, checked against `input_schema` before the call (L02); the loop hands the provider a copy, so a function may change it. `timeout_s` is the time the run has left (L05). The function must accept the keyword (a `timeout_s` parameter, or `**kwargs`), or the provider refuses it when it is built, naming the tool: a thread cannot be killed, so a function bounds its own work with it (L15).
- A plain function runs in a worker thread (`asyncio.to_thread`), so it may block. A coroutine function, `async def f(arguments)`, runs on the event loop instead, takes no `timeout_s` and must not block.
- `ctx` is passed only to a function that declares a `ctx` parameter. A coroutine function gets the call context and uses `async with ctx.infrastructure_wait(kind)`. A plain function, in its thread, gets a stand-in that it uses with a plain `with ctx.infrastructure_wait(kind)`.
- It returns a `str` (the result text) or a `ToolOutput`. Anything else is turned into text with `str()`, so return one of the two. An error the model should read is a `ToolOutput` with `is_error`; a function that raises is a defect, or infrastructure if it raises `InfraError` (see above).
- A call the loop cancels while its thread runs (the run's time ran out) returns at once, and the thread goes on until the function returns. Until then `abandoned_threads()` counts it, the loop records the count, and every new call to the provider is refused with the tool error `AbandonedThread`.

`SparqlTools.functions()` in `examples/sparql_platform` builds the pairs of a platform's tools, and the tiny example's plugin has two plain functions.

### Several providers, and the hash of a tool set

`record_run(request, providers, ...)` takes a sequence of providers of any kind (`FunctionProvider`, `McpToolProvider`, a platform's own) and builds one tool box from them:
- The box offers the tools of every provider, and a call goes to the provider that offered its name.
- The model is told the tools sorted by name. The record's `tools` lists the specs in the order of the sequence, each provider's in the order of its `tools()`. No order changes a hash.
- A name offered by two providers is refused: `record_run` raises `ValueError("two providers offer a tool named ...")` before the loop starts, and nothing picks a winner. It surfaces from the executor, so it is a harness defect (C10): the attempt is set aside, keeping the exception's class and a stack hash, and its run is pending again. It shows at the first run and is never a scored result. One `FunctionProvider` refuses a name twice when it is built.

```python
def tools_sha256(specs: Sequence[ToolSpec]) -> str  # expcore.study.plugin
```

`tools_sha256` is the SHA-256 of a tool set: each tool's name, description, `input_schema` and `terminal` flag, in name order. A study gives one per condition as `StudyPlugin.tools`, over the specs its executor passes to `record_run` under that condition, and it goes into the campaign fingerprint: a changed description or schema, an added tool or a changed `terminal` flag refuses a resume (C06). It does not see a name clash, which shows at the first run as above. It is not the hash a record carries. `RunRecord.prompt["tools_sha256"]` is the tool box's, taken over the descriptions as the model saw them, each with the output-cap sentence appended, so it also changes with `output_cap_chars` (L06). The core compares neither with the other, nor the declared hash with the tools the executor then passes: the plugin guarantees that the hash describes what the model sees under the condition.

### Two tool hashes

Two hashes describe a run's tools, over different text, so they differ even for the same tools, by design:

| Hash | What it covers | Where it goes |
|---|---|---|
| `expcore.study.plugin.tools_sha256(specs)` | the tools as the study declares them: each `ToolSpec`'s name, description, input schema and terminal flag, in name order | the plugin's `StudyPlugin.tools`, one per condition, which the campaign fingerprint holds (C06) |
| `ToolBox.manifest_sha256` (`expcore.loop.toolbox`) | the definitions the model is sent: each tool's name, input schema and description with the output-cap sentence the tool box appends (`describe_cap`, which states `output_cap_chars`), plus each tool's terminal flag, which the model is not told | the record, as `prompt.tools_sha256` despite its name; the replay check rebuilds the tool box from the record's `tools` and the cap and refuses a record whose hash differs (R03) |

The record also keeps `tools`, each tool as its provider gave it, before the cap sentence: exactly what `tools_sha256` hashes. The cap itself is in the fingerprint through the loop's caps, so neither hash leaves it unguarded.

To check that conditions differ only in which tools they expose, compare `tools_sha256` values: the plugin's per condition before the runs (conditions meant to differ only in tools have equal `prompts` hashes and different `tools` hashes), and, after them, the hash recomputed from each record's `tools` (`tools_sha256([ToolSpec(**t) for t in record.tools])`) against the plugin's hash for the run's condition, which shows that the executor exposed what the plugin declared. That value does not depend on the output cap, so it compares across campaigns too. The records' `prompt.tools_sha256` values may be compared with each other within one campaign, where every run has the same cap, but never with a `tools_sha256`. An MCP server's frozen manifest has a third hash, `FrozenManifest.sha256` ([below](#the-frozen-manifest)), over the server's own tool listing.

### McpToolProvider

A study or platform whose tools come from an MCP server speaks to it through `McpToolProvider`, one server session per run over stdio. The Onedata layer builds it for its tool server (`SandboxPlatform.tool_server`); any other platform builds it the same way.

```python
class McpToolProvider:  # expcore.tools.mcp_client
    def __init__(self, command: str, args: Iterable[str] = (), *, frozen: FrozenManifest | Path,
                 env: dict[str, str] | None = None, cwd: str | Path | None = None,
                 allowed_tools: Iterable[str] = (), start_timeout_s: float = 120.0)
    async def __aenter__(self) -> McpToolProvider  # start the server and check its tools
    async def __aexit__(self, *exc) -> None        # end the session and stop the server
    manifest_sha256: str | None                    # the frozen manifest's sha256, once the start succeeded
```

- `command` and `args` start the server as a child process, in `cwd`. `env` holds the variables the server gets beyond the few the MCP SDK passes on by default (`HOME`, `PATH`, `USER` and the like on POSIX); nothing else of the caller's environment reaches it, so a token the server needs goes in `env`.
- `frozen` is the condition's frozen manifest, an object or the path of its file, read when the provider is made. The server's tools must equal it.
- `allowed_tools` is the allow-list the caller gave the server's launcher; the server enforces it, and the client only checks that the server shows exactly those tools. Empty, there is no such check.
- `start_timeout_s` bounds the whole start (spawn, initialize, list the tools); it must be positive (L14).
- **The Onedata layer's tool server reads no `.env` file and starts in an empty directory (O84).** `toolserver.server_environment` puts `ONEDATA_MCP_DOTENV=0` in the server's environment on every platform, the HPC resolve launcher's included, and the word is one of the managed settings: a caller can neither give nor clear it. `ToolServerLauncher.launch` passes `cwd`: a new directory for each start, empty, mode 0700, the driver's user's (`tempfile.mkdtemp`, beside the run's other private files), removed once the server has stopped. onedata-mcp read a `.env` when its config module was imported, searching from the directory of its installed package up to `/`, so a `.env` in any ancestor of the package (on a cluster account, a home directory several studies share) could set any `ONEDATA_*` setting the launch left unset, with nothing in the launch saying so; given the word, a server of a version that has the guard reads none. FastMCP separately reads a `.env` in the working directory for its own `FASTMCP_*` settings, which the empty directory covers. What a study sees: a relative path in `[toolserver] command` or `args` no longer resolves against the driver's working directory, so `load_platform` refuses one when the file is read, naming the key (`command`, or `args` with the argument's position, never its value): a command with a directory part that does not begin at the root, an argument that is `.`, `..` or begins `./`, `../` or `~/` (also as the value of `--option=`), and the script of a Python server (`expcore_onedata.toolpaths`, #382); name files by absolute path, or find the command on `PATH`, and know that the check is lexical and does not see `conf/server.toml` or a path inside code given to `-c`, which fail at the first launch; the pinned server must be a commit that carries the guard, since an older one ignores the word; and the manifests are frozen and checked from the same start: `python -m expcore_onedata.toolmanifest freeze` and `check` ([the frozen manifest](#the-frozen-manifest)) start the server through `toolserver.server_start`, which the launch uses too, so the server has the command, the environment and the empty directory a run's server has. The server also writes no bytecode: `server_environment` puts `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1` in its environment on every platform, a managed setting too, so a start (a launch, or `freeze` and `check`) leaves no `__pycache__` and no `.pyc` in the server's clone, its virtualenv or the interpreter's own library. A platform whose server starts through the resolve launcher ran it `-I -B` already, which ignores the variable and writes none; a platform without it started the server with neither, so a `check` of its manifest wrote over a thousand files into the trees it certifies, which changes the digest of a study's frozen artifacts. A study whose `[toolserver] args` run Python with `-E` or `-I` and no `-B` makes the interpreter ignore the variable: it passes `-B` itself.

Use it as an async context manager around `record_run`, started before the loop, so its start is outside the agent's time, and pass it in the providers. `tools()` returns the server's tools, none of them terminal. What fails where:
- A server that cannot be spawned, does not start within the timeout or closes while starting raises `InfraError("knowledge", ...)` from `__aenter__`.
- A server whose tools differ from the frozen manifest in any way (an extra or a missing tool, a changed description or schema), that lacks a tool of the allow-list or shows one outside it, raises `ManifestError` from `__aenter__`, listing every difference in `problems`. That is a fault of the study's set-up, not of the infrastructure, so the attempt is a harness defect (L13, C10).
- During the run, a closed connection or an internal server error raises `InfraError("knowledge", ...)`; any other MCP error, and a result the server marks as an error, is a `ToolOutput` with `is_error` for the model to read (L12). The round trip to the server is the agent's time: the client marks no wait.
- `execute(name, arguments, *, timeout_s, ctx)` ends within `timeout_s`, in the loop and outside it (L16). A server that is up and does not answer in time raises `CallTimedOut`, a subclass of `InfraError` of kind `knowledge` (`expcore.tools.mcp_client`), whose `detail` names the tool and the bound and whose `tool` and `bound_s` hold them. The call is abandoned and the session stays usable for the next one. A bound that is not positive raises `ValueError` before the call. Outside the loop (a pre-run check, a probe) this is the call's only bound, so a study that makes such a call chooses `timeout_s` itself; a caller that also bounds the call with its own wait should treat `CallTimedOut` as the same expiry, as the day-0 outage check does (`blocked`, timed out). Inside the loop `timeout_s` is the agent's time left, or the study's declared per-call bound (`tool_server_call_bound_s`) when that is shorter (L17): this provider is a tool server, which is what the bound is for. So the call's bound is never longer than the loop's own: when it is the time left, the loop's cut and the expiry meet, and the loop judges the expiry as the cut it stands for (a time cap, or the ceiling's infrastructure, the call recorded as `cut`), whichever of the two the event loop runs first. An expiry of a shorter bound than the one the loop handed over, the declared per-call bound among them, or one that arrives with the agent's time and the ceiling both intact, is not the loop's cut: the call is `failed` as `knowledge` infrastructure.

### A per-call bound on a tool-server call

Inside the loop, a tool call's bound used to be the agent's time left alone. A tool server that hangs mid-run then waited out the whole cap, and since the watch judges that expiry as the loop's cut (L16) the run was scored as the agent's time cap. In a study whose conditions differ in how many tools they call, platform hangs would be charged to the agent unevenly. A study can declare a bound on one call to a tool server so that a hang is the platform's, not the agent's (L17).

```toml
# my-study/campaign.toml
[agent]
tool_server_call_bound_s = 300   # optional: positive and finite, in seconds
```

`AgentConfig.tool_server_call_bound_s` (`expcore.loop.config`) is the same value, `None` when the study declares none. The key was first called `tool_call_bound_s` and capped every call; it is refused at load now, with a message naming the new key and why, and is never read under the new meaning.

- **The bound is for tool servers only.** A provider says it serves a tool server with a true `tool_server` attribute (`McpToolProvider` does; the attribute is optional and not part of the `ToolProvider` protocol, so a test double needs none). The calls to its tools are the only ones the bound reaches. A code cell, which the agent's own code runs, and any other tool that is no tool server's (a `FunctionProvider` function, the code tool of the platform layer) are handed the agent's time left as `timeout_s`, with or without the key, and are never judged by the bound: what the model is told when a cell runs long, "the code did not finish within the time limit", is the text it has without the key. A cell's time is the agent's.
- **A call to a tool server runs under the shorter of the bound and the agent's time left.** The loop hands the provider `min(tool_server_call_bound_s, time left)` as its `timeout_s`, at every such call. A provider that bounds its own call ends it there.
- **When the per-call bound expires first,** the call is `failed` as `knowledge` infrastructure (`CallTimedOut`, the detail naming the tool and the bound), the attempt ends as infrastructure and is set aside like any other infrastructure ending, and it is not the agent's time: the watch does not judge it as a cut. A condition that declares `knowledge` as its treatment kind scores such an attempt as treatment, as for any `knowledge` failure.
- **When the time left is the shorter,** or the two are equal, nothing changes from L16: the provider's expiry at the time left is the loop's own cut, a time cap, or the ceiling's infrastructure when that was passed with the agent under its cap, the call recorded as `cut`.
- **A cut that comes after the bound is the bound's, for a tool server.** The bound's deadline is earlier than the cap's whenever the bound is the shorter, and earlier than the ceiling's when it is shorter than the ceiling left at the call's start. When the loop cuts a call to a tool server at the cap or the ceiling at a reading at or after the bound's deadline, the call had hung first: the ending is the same `knowledge` infrastructure (the same detail, naming the tool and the bound), the call recorded as `cut`, and never the agent's time cap. This closes the race in which a provider is still unwinding its own expiry when the cap arrives a moment after it, and it holds for a tool server that ignores `timeout_s`, whenever the loop has to cut it. A call to any other tool cut at the same reading is the cap's or the ceiling's, as it is without the key. The ending is derived from the call's two clock readings, the configuration and the tool's kind (`expcore.loop.endings.hung_past_the_bound`), so a replay reaches it from the record.
- **The bound is the tool server's to keep.** The loop cuts a call only at the agent's time left and the ceiling; the declared bound reaches the provider as `timeout_s`. `McpToolProvider` honours it. A provider that is a tool server and ignores `timeout_s` is not stopped at the bound: a call of it that returns after the bound and before the time left is an ordinary result, and one that is still running at the time left is cut and judged by the bound, as above. The bound is wall time: a wait on infrastructure that a provider marks is inside it, though the agent's clock is paused in it.
- **Each call's elapsed time is recorded.** The call's record in the run carries `elapsed_s`, the seconds from the reading before the call to the one that ended it (`ended_s - started_s`), for a call that started: an ordinary result, an error, a failed call and a cut one. A call that never started (malformed, skipped) has no such field. It is optional in `expcore-record/3` (the schema, [below](#the-run-records-schema)), so a study can see hangs, and what they cost, per condition, from the records.
- **The record says which tools the bound reached.** Beside a declared bound, and only with it, `agent_config.tool_server_tools` lists the names of the tools of the tool servers, sorted: empty when the run had none. A reader can see that the code tool was not among them. A replay, whose one provider serves every recorded call and cannot say which tool was a tool server's, judges a cut call by this list.
- **Replay reproduces both orders.** A record whose call to a tool server failed at the bound replays through the recorded failure, with no clock reading for it; one cut at the time left replays through the recorded `watch` reading as in L16. The bound and the list are values of the record's `agent_config`, so changing either is a divergence at `agent_config_sha256`, and a list edited and sealed again diverges at the call, which the replay judges the other way.
- **A study that declares no bound keeps L16's behaviour exactly,** and its configuration is what it was: `tool_server_call_bound_s` and `tool_server_tools` are left out of `agent_config` (never a null), so the configuration hash, the record's `agent_config` and the campaign's caps are the same bytes. Its new records differ from the old only by the optional `elapsed_s` of each call that started. A declared bound is part of the campaign's caps in the fingerprint, as the other caps are, and campaign.toml's own hash changes with it, so a store started without it is not resumed with it: start a new store.

### The frozen manifest

`expcore.tools.manifest` reads a server's tools, freezes them into a file per condition and compares a server with that file.

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class FrozenManifest:
    tools: list[dict]  # sorted by name: name, description, input_schema, output_schema, annotations
    sha256: str        # SHA-256 of the canonical JSON of tools
    server_name: str | None = None
    server_version: str | None = None
    protocol_version: str | None = None
    @classmethod
    def load(cls, path: Path) -> FrozenManifest  # ManifestError for a file that is not one, or was edited
    def dump(self) -> str                        # the file's text: sorted keys, one trailing newline
```

Two manifests are equal when their names, descriptions, schemas and annotations are equal, byte for byte. `load` refuses a file whose stored `sha256` is not the hash of its tools, so a hand-edited manifest is refused rather than trusted. Freeze each condition's manifest from the server it will run, and check it again whenever the server is upgraded:

```sh
python -m expcore.tools.manifest freeze [--pass-env NAME ...] [--timeout SECONDS] OUT -- COMMAND [ARG ...]
python -m expcore.tools.manifest check [--pass-env NAME ...] [--timeout SECONDS] FROZEN -- COMMAND [ARG ...]
```

Both start the server with `COMMAND [ARG ...]`, as the provider will, in a new, empty directory of mode 0700 for each start, removed once the server has stopped (O84), so a relative path in the command no longer resolves against your working directory and a `.env` there is not found, and read its tools within `--timeout` seconds (120 by default). This command line knows nothing of a platform: use it for a server you start yourself, and the one below for the Onedata layer's tool server. `--pass-env NAME`, repeatable, copies that variable of your environment to the server by name, so no value is on the command line. `freeze` writes the frozen manifest to `OUT` and prints its tool count and hash; `check` compares the live server with `FROZEN`. Each exits 0 on success, 1 on a mismatch (listed on standard error) and 2 on an error. For example, with a server installed as `notes-server`:

```sh
python -m expcore.tools.manifest freeze --pass-env NOTES_TOKEN manifests/basic.json -- notes-server --read-only
python -m expcore.tools.manifest check --pass-env NOTES_TOKEN manifests/basic.json -- notes-server --read-only
```

**A condition of the Onedata layer's tool server** is frozen and checked with the command line of `expcore_onedata.toolmanifest`, which starts the server exactly as the launch does (O84):

```sh
python -m expcore_onedata.toolmanifest freeze PLATFORM CONDITION [--pass-env NAME ...] [--token-env NAME] [--timeout SECONDS]
python -m expcore_onedata.toolmanifest check PLATFORM CONDITION [--pass-env NAME ...] [--token-env NAME] [--timeout SECONDS]
```

`PLATFORM` is the `platform.toml` and `CONDITION` a name `[toolserver.conditions]` declares. A manifest hashes what the server shows, and the server shows what it is configured with, so the command builds nothing of its own: `toolserver.server_start`, which the launch uses, gives the command and arguments of `[toolserver]` (the resolve launcher's included), the environment `server_environment` builds for the condition (its allow-list, its hosts, its TLS settings with the run's private copy of the CA bundle, `ONEDATA_MCP_DOTENV=0` and `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1`) and a new, empty directory of mode 0700 for each start, removed once the server has stopped. `freeze` writes the file the condition's `manifest` declares, which need not exist yet (the command loads the platform without requiring its manifests, which every other loader does require, so one condition is frozen or checked before the others are frozen); `check` compares the live server with that file, and says so when it is missing. When `[toolserver]` pins a checkout (`clone` and `commit`), both actions verify it first, as a launch does, and refuse a clone at another commit or with a local change in a tracked file, naming which, before anything is started or written: a manifest is never frozen from code the runs do not use. The platform's credentials are not read and no federation is contacted.

`--pass-env NAME`, repeatable, gives the server a variable of your environment, as the caller's `extra_env` of a launch does. It refuses, naming them and before anything starts, every variable the launch sets itself (`toolserver.MANAGED_ENV`, the word `ONEDATA_MCP_DOTENV` and `PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE` among them, and `toolserver.OVERRIDDEN_ENV`), and a name that is not set. The tool list is taken not to depend on the server's token (the stand-in server shows it; try it with the real one): without `--token-env` both token settings hold a fixed placeholder (`toolmanifest.FREEZE_TOKEN`, no token of any run), and `--token-env NAME` takes the value from that variable, for a server that checks its token when it starts. `--timeout` defaults to the platform's `[toolserver] start_timeout_s`. The exit codes are the core command line's.

A manifest you froze for this server with the core command line was read from the environment and the directory that command ran in. Freeze it again with this one: when the tools are the same the file is unchanged, and when they differ the condition's hash in the campaign fingerprint changes, so a store started with the earlier manifest is not resumed with it (start a new store).

## Scorer

`expcore.records.rescore.Scorer`, and `SideFileScorer` for a scorer that reads the record's [side files](#side-files-of-a-record).

```python
class Scorer(Protocol):
    name: str
    version: str
    def score(self, record: RunRecord) -> Mapping[str, Any]

class SideFileScorer(Protocol):
    name: str
    version: str
    def score(self, record: RunRecord, side_files: Mapping[str, bytes]) -> Mapping[str, Any]

def rescore(record: RunRecord, scorers: Iterable[Any], *, side_files: Mapping[str, bytes] | None = None) -> RunRecord
def rescore_files(source, scorers, *, out_dir=None, side_files: Sequence[SideFile] | None = None) -> list[Path]
```

A scorer, or a plain function used as one, that declares a parameter named `side_files` (positional or keyword-only) is passed it by keyword; one that does not is called with the record alone, exactly as before. The parameter is chosen over a `StudyContext` accessor because a scorer is built once in `build`, before any record exists, while the parameter is scoped to the one call that scores one record.

Core guarantees:
- A scorer receives a deep copy of the record. The result is kept in `record.scores[name] = {"version", "result"}`, apart from instrument output, and a scorer without `version` is versioned by the hash of its source file.
- A scorer that takes `side_files` gets the record's side files as a read-only mapping of its own from declared pattern to bytes: the bytes the loader read once, within the cap, whose SHA-256 it compared with the hash the record names, never a path or a handle to reopen. They are bytes rather than a handle because the loader must hold them to hash them, and bytes cannot be read twice differently or moved between one scorer and the next. `analyze`, `report`, `regenerate` and a package's `make check` hand over the bytes `study.verified.load_verified` read against the replay check, all of them read and paired before the plugin is loaded, so a side file that no longer matches is refused before any scorer runs. `rescore_files` with the study's declaration reads them beside the record of an attempt directory and pairs them as the check does. The same batch therefore gives the same scores, byte for byte, through each. `check` scores nothing. A record with no side file, or a study that declares none, gives an empty mapping; a caller that hands over nothing (`rescore` without `side_files`, `rescore_files` without the declaration) refuses such a scorer, naming it, rather than give it an empty mapping for a record that has a side file.
- A side file holds at most `expcore.replay.side_files.MAX_SIDE_FILE_BYTES`, 64 MiB. Every side file of a batch is held in memory while it is analysed, as every record is, so a larger one is refused with an error naming the file and the cap: `check` fails it at stage `side_file`, and the loader stops reading one byte past the cap. Neither the check nor the comparison of the batch with it reads a side file whole: a side file is never looked into for a record, and it is hashed in chunks.
- An attempt that is set aside (infrastructure not declared as treatment, or a harness defect) is never scored. An attempt that ends in a declared treatment kind, after the model completed at least one turn, is, so the scorer must handle a partial record; the same kind before the first turn is set aside. Where the condition declares the kind with a target (record version 3), only a failure of that target while the fault injector held it down is scored; any other is set aside.
- The analysis reads `result["outcomes"]` and nothing else (`expcore.study.pipeline`). The pipeline scores in memory and never writes into a checked batch. `analyze` derives every score anew from the verified records and reads no stored score, so it is the re-scoring command (R13): after a scorer changes, `check` again (the scorer is code, so the code identity changed), then `analyze` and `report`. `scores.json` and the report name each scorer's version.

Implementer must guarantee:
- The score is a pure function of the record, and of `side_files` for a scorer that takes them: no model, no platform, no clock, and no file opened by path (R13). The platform axis comes from run-end snapshots in the record.
- The result is JSON. By convention: `axes` (two named axes, what the agent did and what the platform's state shows), `verdict` (`fulfilled`, `violated` or `not_determined`; not enforced by the core) and `outcomes`, a mapping from a name to a finite number or `None`.
- An outcome that could not be computed is `None`, never 0, and a `not_determined` verdict leaves every outcome that depends on the unobserved state `None` (R10, S05).
- A treatment attempt (`record.outcome["treatment"]` is true) gets values: what the agent achieved under the manipulation (the failure itself, for example no answer and zero coverage) and what it did before it. `None` drops the run's pair from every contrast on that outcome; the analysis counts those drops per contrast, and refuses a contrast in the Holm family that has any, unless its preregistration declares `treatment_outcomes = "excluded"`; `"scored"` is a claim the analysis checks, so such a contrast is refused too, past its preregistered `max_unscored_treatment` (0 unless set) (S10).

## Instrument

`expcore.plugin.Instrument`, defined in step 4b2. Hooks at the start and the end of a run that write into the record under their own namespace.

```python
class Instrument(Protocol):
    namespace: str
    def start(self, identity: Mapping[str, Any]) -> None
    def finish(self) -> Mapping[str, Any]
```

The study's executor calls them; nothing in the loop does. Two more methods are optional, `at_first_turn` and `check`, and let an instrument refuse the attempt ([below](#refusing-an-attempt-the-first-turn-hook-and-the-post-run-check)); an instrument without them is called exactly as before. The core finds them by name, so a class that wraps an instrument [passes them on](#a-class-that-wraps-an-instrument). `expcore.records.instruments` has the two helpers an executor uses:

```python
def start_instruments(instruments: Sequence[Instrument], identity: Mapping[str, Any]) -> None
def finish_instruments(record: RunRecord, instruments: Sequence[Instrument]) -> RunRecord
```

An executor runs them around `record_run`, as the example's does (`FactsServed` in `examples/tiny_study/plugin.py`):

```python
instruments = [FactsServed(shelf)]  # new objects for every run
identity = {"run_key": request.run_key, "factors": dict(request.identity.factors), "attempt": request.identity.attempt}
await asyncio.to_thread(start_instruments, instruments, identity)
record = await record_run(request, providers, system, user, config, code=code)
record = await asyncio.to_thread(finish_instruments, record, instruments)
record.write(request.run_dir)  # the record finish_instruments returned, which holds the snapshots
```

- `identity` is the request's [`RunIdentity`](#runexecutor) as a plain mapping: `run_key`, `factors` and `attempt`. It is there for an instrument that labels its reading or looks something up by run; one that reads only the platform's state, such as the platform probes, ignores it.
- One fresh instrument per run: an instrument holds one run's reading between `start` and `finish`, and the scheduler can run attempts at the same time, so an instrument shared between runs would mix their readings.
- Which attempts get a snapshot: `record_run` returns a record however the loop ended, an infrastructure failure included (a tool's `InfraError`, an endpoint that outlasted its retries), so `finish` runs and the snapshot is kept: a treatment attempt's scorer reads it, and an attempt set aside keeps it for diagnosis. When the executor itself raises, before the loop or instead of returning, there is no record and so no snapshot. An exception from `start` is the executor's: an `InfraError` is recorded as infrastructure of its kind, anything else as a harness defect (C10).

Core guarantees:
- `check_instruments` runs first on both: a namespace that is not lower-case letters, digits and underscores, and two instruments with one namespace, raise before anything runs.
- **Instruments nest (C31).** `start_instruments` starts the instruments in list order and `finish_instruments` calls their `finish` in the reverse of it, so an instrument listed earlier finishes later: the first listed starts first and finishes last, and one listed after another starts after it and finishes before it. List an instrument whose `finish` undoes something first (a fault injector's restore, a progressive ingest's), and the probes that must read the state before the undo after it. Only the calls are reversed: the record keeps its instruments in list order, so the keys of `instruments` keep the order the list gives them. What an instrument reads can differ with the order of the calls, which is the point of the order. `at_first_turn` and `check` stay in list order ([below](#refusing-an-attempt-the-first-turn-hook-and-the-post-run-check)).
- `finish_instruments` returns a new record with each snapshot in `RunRecord.instruments[namespace]`, a JSON object (NaN and values JSON cannot hold are refused). An instrument whose `finish` raises, or returns anything but a JSON object, is recorded as `{"ok": false, "error": <class name>}` and the instruments listed before it still finish: the attempt keeps its record and a scorer that needs the snapshot sees that it failed (O08).
- The snapshot is the only platform state a scorer reads (R13); replay serves it and asks the platform nothing.

Implementer must guarantee:
- `start` and `finish` run outside the agent's time: before the loop starts and after it ends. They do blocking I/O, so an async executor calls them with `asyncio.to_thread`. The platform's waits for the federation to settle (`expcore_onedata.consistency`) are not a declared wait kind and refuse to run on the thread of a running event loop.
- `finish` records what it read and never guesses: a reading that failed is `None` (with the failure beside it), not zero, and anything derived from it is `None` too (R10).
- The snapshot holds no secret and no identifier of the deployment beyond the labels the study's configuration gives.

The platform has four instruments. The first is `expcore_onedata.probes.PlatformProbes` (namespace `platform_probes`): the block map of each tracked file on each tracked provider before and after the run, the records of the transfers the provisioner scheduled, the record of every transfer of the space that started in the run's window whoever started it (`space_transfers`), the bytes moved between provider pairs, attributed to a source only where exactly one other provider held those bytes at the start, and per receiving provider the transfer bytes into it and a lower bound on the bytes fetched on demand (`on_demand`). Every provider in the snapshot (version 3) is named by its position, `providers[i]`, never by its configured name, since records are published. A provider's `gained_bytes` is placement gained: the bytes of the blocks a provider's block map shows at run end that it did not show at run start; what it showed at the start and no longer does is `lost_bytes`, kept apart, so it is not a net change, and it is not bytes on the wire. See its module docstring and `expcore_onedata.transferlog` for the snapshot's fields.

**The transfer log reads the run's window, never the space's history (O32).** Ended transfers are never removed, so a space's ended listing grows with every run and reset. The waiting and ongoing listings are read to their end; the ended one is read newest first (its order on the federation's 25.0 build, from the source: `expcore_onedata.transferorder`) down to the first job whose record says it ended before `space_transfers.ended_since`, the window's start less `[placement] clock_skew_allowance_s` and `transferorder.STOP_MARGIN_S` (10 s), on this host's clock. So `MAX_LISTED` (2000 distinct ids) bounds the jobs read down to `ended_since`, and `listed` counts them; the window places each against itself as before, so a job read in the margin below it is listed and never one of `records`: a long history never makes `probe_ok` false, and a window that truly holds more than the bound is still unknown, never cut. The order is checked as the listing is read, to the end of the page that holds the stop and across it: when that page is not the listing's last, the walk reads the next page and every record on it (one page and up to a page's `limit` of records more), keeps none of them, and each must have finished no later than the one before it, the last finish read on the stop's page first. A finish time later than the one before it makes the listing unknown (`probe_ok` false, the assumption named), and so does the global clock running more than the margin behind this host's, since the window would then open before the cutoff. No page token is built by hand. **Residual:** a job of the window listed two or more pages past the stop is not seen, and nothing reports it, since catching it would take reading the whole history. The walk rests there on the order holding by construction: on op-worker `f118ed9` an ended job's link key is `9999999999` less its finish time, then the start of its id (`transfer_links.erl:83-95,160-164`); the page past the stop guards that assumption where its failure would cost a job of the window. A job cancelled while still scheduled, before it was enqueued, gets no finish time (its `finishTime` stays 0), so its link key is the largest and the ended listing puts it after every job that has one: the walk stops at the first such record whose own record says it ended, and neither it nor what follows is read as part of the window or counted against the bound, however many such cancellations the history holds, so `records` and `listed` never hold it. A job with a finish time listed after one with none is an order the listing cannot have, and makes the listing unknown (`probe_ok` false, the order named), on the stop's page and the page past it. A record whose `finishTime` is 0 but that does not say it ended may lag its listing entry and is kept, as any record that gives no readable time is. Neither `records` nor `listed` counts every transfer that ended in the window, only those with a finish time; the job moved no bytes, so no byte figure misses it. **The clock a job's times are on:** the provider that runs a job stamps its times on the platform's global clock, Onezone's cluster time (ctool `global_clock`), to which the providers synchronise theirs, while a provider's Date header gives its system clock, which places nothing, and neither does Onezone's own: a multi-node Onezone's answering node can have a system clock that differs from the cluster's global clock by its bias (a Date of +8 s against a bias of -8 s dates an answer like a node 8 s ahead while the stamps are on a clock that agrees with this host). So the probes read the global clock itself, from the public endpoint the providers synchronise to, once per observation: `GET /api/v3/onezone/provider/public/get_current_time` (`OnezoneClient.get_dated(..., anonymous=True)`, which sends no credential header; answer `{"timeMillis": N}`, epoch milliseconds; oz-worker `14b5bda`, `provider_routes.erl:192-198`, `provider_logic_plugin.erl:304-305`, `provider_rest_translator.erl:128-129`, `provider_logic.erl:548-554`; no live run has made the call yet, so it is in `client.UNVERIFIED_ENDPOINTS`). The answer less the midpoint of this host's clock around the read is the clock's offset from this host, and half the round trip is how far that may be off. A job is placed by its schedule time in the window widened by the least offset the readings allow (each reading's offset less its half round trip) less the allowance and the greatest (each one's plus it) plus it, the allowance covering a provider's synchronisation error to Onezone's clock, and a job with a time in the band at either edge, that width on either side of it, has `near_window_edge` true and leaves its target's bound unknown (`transfer_near_window_edge`). `space_transfers.clock` gives the readings under `zone` (`samples`, `unreadable`, `min_s`, `max_s`, `half_trip_s`, and `date_offsets_s`, the Date headers of the same answers, a cross-check) and the providers' Date offsets under `offsets`, a diagnostic; `known` and `clock_offset_unknown` say whether the global clock was read.

**A harness transfer that ended before the window flags nothing (O08, O32).** A transfer the provisioner scheduled that the accepted listing does not show leaves every bound unknown (`harness_transfer_not_listed`, counted in `space_transfers.harness_unlisted`), since the listing is then provably incomplete. The provisioner's log holds the jobs it scheduled before the run as well, a reset's evictions among them, which the walk never keeps: for each harness job the listing lacks, its record is read on every tracked provider, and the job is absent by design and flags nothing (`space_transfers.harness_before_window` counts them, a key present only when one is) when a record says it ended (a final state) with a finish time after 0 and before `ended_since` and no readable record contradicts that: every final record gives that same finish time. A copy that has not ended lags and contradicts nothing, and a record that cannot be read neither confirms nor contradicts. With no such record, or a final one that gives another finish time, none, 0, or one at or after `ended_since`, it still flags.

**A record's state is read per kind (O32).** Every record of the federation's 25.0 build carries both halves, `replicationStatus` and `evictionStatus`, beside its summary `transferStatus`, and the half a job does not do reads `skipped` (op-worker f118ed9, `transfer.erl:157-166`): an ended replication reads `completed`, `completed` and `skipped`. A replication's state (`space_transfers.records[].state`, `transfers[].state`, and what `expcore_onedata.consistency.transfer_state` and `expcore_onedata.transferrecord.state_of` return) is its replication half's, an eviction's its eviction half's, and a migration's that of the half it is in: its replication's until that ends, then its eviction's, so it is final (`completed`, `failed`, `cancelled`) only once both halves are, and one whose replication completed and whose eviction failed is `failed`. `skipped` on the half a job does not do contradicts nothing. Fields that do contradict each other read `conflicting`, never a final state: a summary other than the one op-worker derives from the halves (`transfer.erl:474-495`, `active` read as `replicating` or `evicting`), a half the job does not do that is not `skipped`, the half it does marked `skipped`, a migration whose replication failed and whose eviction completed, a migration half its state rests on missing, provider ids that name another kind than the record's `type` (op-worker derives the type from which of `replicatingProviderId` and `evictingProviderId` it names, `transfer.erl:397-403`), or a status field that is neither a string nor null (a null one counts as absent). `effectiveJobStatus`, the state of the newest rerun of the job, is not read. Before this rule every ended replication and eviction read `conflicting`: a poll for its end never ended, the window carried every earlier job over into the run, and an `on_demand` bound into its target was unknown.

The second is `expcore_onedata.sandbox.served.ServedBytes(sandbox)` (namespace `served_bytes`): the bytes the run's agent read through its data path (O22), for a measure such as bytes moved but never consumed. Build it once `SandboxPlatform.launch` has yielded the run's sandbox, and call `sandbox.collect(...)` before `finish_instruments`: the collection stops the data gate and counts its log once, and `finish` records that count, so replay serves the recorded value and nothing measures it again. The snapshot is `{"version": 1, "data_path", "listing", "run_start_marked", "served_bytes", "content_bytes", "listing_bytes", "other_bytes", "gate_answer_bytes", "files", "files_total", "files_cut", "listings", "listings_total", "listings_cut", "caps", "before_run_start", "unknown"}`:
- what it counts: the body bytes of the platform's answers that the gate's socket accepted for the agent, after any cap or cut; `content_bytes` are the 200 and 206 answers to reads of a file's content (a cut transfer's part included), `listing_bytes` the 200 answers to listings as they were served (each page in the paged mode, the merged answer in the complete one), `other_bytes` every other answer of the platform's (the token's scope, a looked-up file id, a file's attributes, the platform's errors), and `served_bytes` the three together. `gate_answer_bytes`, the gate's own refusals and errors, is kept out of `served_bytes`;
- per file and per directory, so a study can check an agent's claimed reads file by file, which a run's total cannot (a read log that moves reads between files keeps the total):
  - `files` holds a row for every file whose content the platform served, keyed by the file id the request's path names (`/api/v3/oneprovider/data/<file id>/content`, as sent, never decoded): `path`, that path as the gate logged it, which names the file by its id and never by a name; `requests`, its content answers; `ranges`, the byte ranges they served, merged where they overlap or touch, lowest first, each `[start, stop]` with `stop` excluded; `range_bytes`, the bytes in those ranges, each counted once; `ranges_cut`; and `body_bytes`, every body byte of its answers, so a file read twice counts twice here and once in `range_bytes`. The rows' `body_bytes` add up to `content_bytes` when the table is not cut;
  - a range begins where the platform's answer says, never where the agent's `Range` header asked: at the file's start for a 200, at the first byte of the `Content-Range` the gate logs on a 206 (`content_range`), and it runs for the bytes the gate's socket accepted. A 206 whose `Content-Range` is not one range holding those bytes (a multipart answer, say), or a line without the platform's status, leaves that file's `ranges`, `range_bytes` and `ranges_cut` `None`, never a part of them; its `requests` and `body_bytes` stay known;
  - a file whose attributes alone were read, or whose content read was refused or answered with the platform's error, served no content and has no row;
  - `listings` holds a row for every directory whose listing the platform served, keyed by the directory's id as the request's path names it (`/api/v3/oneprovider/data/<directory id>/children`): `path`, `requests` and `body_bytes`, which add up to `listing_bytes` when the table is not cut. A listing answered with the platform's error counts as another answer, not under its directory;
