An executor with such instruments calls the first just before `record_run` and the second where it called `finish_instruments`:

```python
await asyncio.to_thread(start_instruments, instruments, identity)
# ... the executor's own steps before the loop, such as sandbox.mark_run_start() ...
await asyncio.to_thread(first_turn_instruments, instruments, request.timer)
record = await record_run(request, providers, system, user, config, code=code)
# ... sandbox.collect(...), when the platform needs it ...
record = await asyncio.to_thread(finish_instruments, record, instruments)  # snapshots, then the checks
record.write(request.run_dir)  # the record that holds the snapshots and, if a check refused, the refusal
```

- **When the hook fires.** `first_turn_instruments` runs after the executor's last step of its own and just before `record_run`, so before the loop starts the agent's clock and before the first request is sent. The agent's time is not charged for it, and it is no wait kind. The loop is not involved, so a little work of the core's own (building the tool box, starting the clock) still lies between the hook and the request. It takes one reading of the run's clock and logs it in the record's clock under `instrument_first_turn`; each hook gets it as `moment["clock_s"]`, the same number, so what the instrument reads is labelled with the clock that the loop's own readings (`start`, `before_model`, ...) are on. With no instrument that has `at_first_turn` it takes no reading and returns. It refuses to run once the timer has started. The hooks run in list order: the first turn is one moment, not a nest, so it is not reversed like the finishes, and the first listed instrument that refuses is the refusal that reaches the caller.
- **What the hook may do.** Read the state, keep the reading for `finish` to record (the hook returns nothing; the snapshot is the record of it), and refuse. It runs blocking I/O on the executor's thread, so the executor calls it through `asyncio.to_thread`, and it bounds itself: the loop's watch does not cover it. It must not change the manipulation it measures.
- **How the hook's failure is classified.** An `InfraError` of a kind no condition can declare as its treatment (a core kind such as `tool_plane_integrity`, a platform's registered with `treatable=False`, or a study's own declared with `treatable = false` in `[study.kinds]`) is the instrument's refusal. It leaves `first_turn_instruments` as an `InfraError` of that kind whose detail names the instrument, the executor lets it reach its caller, and the attempt is recorded as infrastructure of that kind: set aside, charged, run again like any infrastructure failure. No request was sent, so it completed no turn and is never treatment, whatever a condition declares. It has no record, as an executor that raised has none, but its usage is known: it is a `FirstTurnRefusal`, raised before the loop's first request, so the attempt holds zero tokens and no turn and costs nothing at any price, as the attempt of a run that found an outage before it began does. It is still the run's charged attempt. If the backend's `requests_sent` says a request was started before the hook ran, the usage is unknown as for any executor that raised, and the attempt is charged its bound. A refusal the executor wraps in another error is no `FirstTurnRefusal`, but with a budget its call log still shows no request, so the attempt is charged nothing like any attempt that failed before a request (C05, `campaign/no_request.py`); without a budget there is no log and no charge to settle. An `InfraError` of a kind a condition may declare, and any other exception, is a defect of the instrument: `InstrumentRefusalError` for the first, the exception itself for the second, and the attempt is a harness defect (C10).
- **When the post-run check runs.** `finish_instruments` takes every snapshot first, the last listed instrument's first, and then asks each instrument that has `check`, in list order, not in the finishes' reverse: a check is a verdict taken once every snapshot is in and every restore has run, not a release that nests, and a refusal of a hold kind (C33) stops the rest while a charged refusal does not, so list order records the refusal of the instrument listed first, the outer one (the injector, the ingest), over a symptom read by a probe listed after it. The executor calls it after the platform's collection (`sandbox.collect(...)`) and before the teardown, so a check can still read what the run left; a check that needs a reading `finish` failed to take is still called, and refuses, since unknown is not a pass. A check that passes changes nothing. A check that raises a kind a condition may declare, or anything but a refusal, raises out of `finish_instruments`: a defect, as for the hook, and with no record.
- **What the check may refuse, and how that is recorded.** It may refuse any attempt, one the loop scored (a cap, an answer) and one that ended in the fault its condition declares as its treatment alike. A refusal of a hold kind (C33) stops the rest and is the record's refusal; a charged refusal does not stop them, and the first one listed is the record's refusal only when no later check holds, so a held start is never charged because another instrument refused first. The refusal goes into the record as `instrument_refusal`, `{"namespace", "infra_kind", "detail"}` (the detail cut at 500 characters), beside every snapshot, so the attempt keeps its record and its usage and is not charged as unknown. The record's outcome stays what the loop saw; `RunRecord.set_aside` is true, so re-scoring skips it.
- **Where the scheduler sets it aside.** In the one place a finished attempt can still be set aside (`AttemptRunner._judge`): the post-run health check first (C11), then the refusal, then the endpoint's identity (C16). An attempt the store would score, a cap or an answer or a fault its condition declares as its treatment, becomes infrastructure of the refusal's kind with the instrument and the detail, and an event `instrument_refused` says so; the run is tried again. That holds when an outage flag is on the attempt too: the treatment rule is checked before the outage flag (C01), so such an attempt is scored until the refusal rewrites it, and once rewritten to a kind that is never treatment it is not. A result that still carries the outage's kind is then classified `outage_discarded` and not charged, as any attempt an outage overlapped is; any other is `infra_fail` and charged. The result no longer claims the treatment the record flagged, so the store and the record do not disagree about it. An attempt the store would not score, one set aside by the health check, an outage, or its own infrastructure failure or defect, keeps its outcome, and the event says the outcome stands, except for a refusal of a hold kind (`register_infra_kind(..., charged=False)`, C33): an instrument whose start was held back, so nothing the run did counts, always decides, and the attempt is set aside as that hold, uncharged, whatever set it aside first, a charged post-run failure and a fault injector's unverified restore included (O11: it is still never scored).
- **Replay and fixtures.** Replay runs no instrument. It serves the recorded `instrument_first_turn` reading and `instrument_refusal` back, so the replayed record has the stored bytes; the reading is not the loop's, so the replay clock does not serve it to the loop. `tests/data/record-v3/04-instrument-refusal.json` pins both.
- **Without hooks.** A study whose instruments have neither method takes no reading, writes no `instrument_refusal` (the key is absent, never null) and calls `finish_instruments` as before: the example study's records are what they were.

#### A class that wraps an instrument

The core looks for the two optional methods by name, with `getattr` on the object it is given, and calls one when it is callable. A study's class that wraps an instrument (to add a label, a retry, a log) and passes `start` and `finish` on but not `at_first_turn` or `check` drops them without a word: the wrapped instrument's refusal is never asked for, and the attempt is scored as if the manipulation had held. The names are one list, and a helper checks a wrapper against it:

```python
OPTIONAL_HOOKS: tuple[str, ...] = ("at_first_turn", "check")  # expcore.records.instruments
def missing_hooks(wrapper: Instrument, wrapped: Instrument) -> tuple[str, ...]
```

- **The wrapper rule.** A wrapper passes each name in `OPTIONAL_HOOKS` on, as a method that calls the wrapped instrument's or through `__getattr__`, or leaves one out on purpose and says so in its test. `namespace`, `start` and `finish` are not optional, are not in the list, and a wrapper must have them.
- **The list is the core's discovery.** `_method`, the one lookup of an optional hook, refuses a name that is not in `OPTIONAL_HOOKS`, and a test compares the list with every name the module looks an instrument up by, and fails on a lookup outside `_method` whose name it cannot read, so a hook the core gains later is in the list, and so in the rule, or its tests fail.
- **`missing_hooks` is for a study's own test.** It returns the names the wrapped instrument has and the wrapper does not, in the list's order: `assert missing_hooks(wrapper, wrapped) == ()` for a wrapper that passes everything on, or the tuple a wrapper that drops one on purpose expects. A hook counts as the core counts it when it looks it up: an attribute that is callable. A property that answers None is no hook (the progressive ingest's `at_first_turn` is one outside its preallocated mode), so a wrapper around it misses nothing then, and a hook only the wrapper has is not missing. Test the wrapper around each instrument it wraps, in each mode that changes which hooks the instrument has.


### Side files of a record

An instrument whose reading is too large for the record, a compressed log of the byte ranges a run read for instance, can write it to a file beside the record and keep its SHA-256 in the snapshot. The study declares each such file in `study.toml` (`expcore.replay.side_files`, validated by `expcore.study.config` when the study loads):

```toml
[[study.side_files]]
pattern = "extent_ranges.json.gz"               # one file name, looked for beside each record
hash_field = "instruments.extent.side_sha256"   # the record field, a dotted path, that holds the file's SHA-256
```

- `pattern` is one literal file name, never a glob or a path, because each side file is paired with the one record in its own directory: the attempt directory `request.run_dir`, which holds one record. A name that starts with a dot is refused (the check never reads such a file), and so is a name the campaign or the check writes: a record's, `attempt-marker.json`, `campaign-snapshot.sqlite`, `replay-check.json`. A name ending in `.json` is a side file, never taken for a record. A side file is the only way a study's own file enters a run's directory: one that is neither a record nor declared fails the check as unexpected (R04).
- `hash_field` is record keys joined by dots, looked up in the record's stored JSON. Each pattern and each field is declared once.
- The declaration is part of `study.toml`, whose hash is in the campaign fingerprint, so a campaign resumed under another declaration is refused (C06).

Core guarantees, in `check` and again wherever the analysis reads the batch (R14), each refusal naming the file:
- a record whose field holds a hash with no such file beside it is refused;
- a side file whose bytes do not have the hash its own record names is refused, two records' side files swapped included;
- a file of a declared name in a directory that holds no record (left from an earlier campaign or attempt) is refused against its own path, wherever it lies in the batch, and never hashed into a passing check;
- a side file beside a record whose field is absent or `null` is refused; a record with neither the field nor the file is fine, since that run made no side file;
- a field that holds anything but 64 lower-case hexadecimal digits is refused;
- a hash or a side file in a directory that holds more than one record is refused, since it cannot be paired;
- the side files are found in exactly the batch directory the check parses, however the command names the study and the batch.

Implementer must guarantee: the executor writes the side file into `request.run_dir` with the record, and the record names it, in the order [below](#placing-a-side-file-beside-its-record) (`stage_side_file`, then the record, then `commit`), so that a kill leaves a state `check` settles and never one it refuses for a reason the study did not cause. A side file holds at most 64 MiB (`MAX_SIDE_FILE_BYTES`). A measure computed from a side file's content is a [scorer](#scorer) that takes `side_files`, which is handed the bytes the analysis read once and verified; the scorer never opens the file.

#### Placing a side file beside its record

`expcore.replay.side_file_placement`. One helper takes a declared side file out of the sandbox's `/work` and puts it beside the record, in two calls, because the record that names its hash is written between them:

```python
def stage_side_file(sandbox, request, side_files, name, *, source=None) -> StagedSideFile | None
def settle_side_files(batch, side_files, *, remove_unnamed=False) -> list[Settled]    # what `check`, `merge` and a merged batch's check run first
def temporary_name(pattern: str, attempt: int) -> str                                 # ".<pattern>.tmp-<attempt>"

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class StagedSideFile:
    declaration: SideFile; sha256: str; size: int
    path: Path                         # where the file lies once committed: request.run_dir / pattern
    def commit(self) -> Path           # rename into place; refused until the record on disk names sha256
    def discard(self) -> None          # remove the staged file, for an attempt that fails before its record
```

- **`stage_side_file`** calls `sandbox.collect(scratch, outputs=[source or name])` itself, so the file reaches the driver through the copy of O19 (never a link, a special file or a hard link out of the output). The sandbox must not have stopped, and a second collection is as safe as the executor's own. The scratch directory is outside the batch and is removed. The file is then streamed into `request.run_dir` under the temporary name, hashed as it goes, fsynced, with the directory fsynced too. `side_files` is the study's declaration (`context.study.side_files`) and `name` one of its patterns; `source` is the file's relative path in `/work` when that is not `name`.
- **It refuses** a `name` no declaration holds (`UndeclaredSideFile`, a `ValueError`) and a collected file over `MAX_SIDE_FILE_BYTES` (`SideFileTooLarge`), each before anything is written into the attempt directory, and a `source` that is absolute or holds `..`. Raised in an executor they are harness defects (C10); an executor that wants the oversized file to cost the run only its side file catches `SideFileTooLarge` and leaves the record's field out.
- **It returns `None`** when the sandbox copied nothing: the file is not in `/work`, or the agent made it a link or the like, which the collection refuses and records for the scorers (`refused_output`). The run made no side file, and the record then holds no hash for it.
- **The order is the temporary file, then the record with its hash, then the rename.** The executor puts `staged.sha256` in the record's `hash_field` (inside an instrument's snapshot, typically), writes the record, and only then calls `staged.commit()`, which renames the file to its declared name and refuses, renaming nothing, while the record on disk is missing or names another hash, and never overwrites a file that lies at the declared name with other bytes. Nothing but `commit` and `check` ever gives the file its declared name.

**The rule `check` keeps, by the state a kill leaves.** A kill at each step is a state that `check` settles (`settle_side_files`, before it parses the batch, printed on stderr as `check: finished ...` or `check: removed ...`), and `check` never refuses one for a reason the study did not cause:

| Killed | The attempt directory holds | `check` |
|---|---|---|
| after the temporary file, before the record | `.<pattern>.tmp-<attempt>`, no record | passes it as it stands (a dot-name is never read), then removes the file, since no record names it, once it holds the campaign's lock |
| after the record, before the rename | the record naming `H`, and the temporary file | finds that the file's SHA-256 is `H`, the one the record names at the declared field, and renames it; the pairing then hashes it and compares it with the record like any side file. No lock is needed for this |
| after the rename | the record and the file | nothing to settle |

- **A file is renamed only when** its directory holds exactly one record, that record names its SHA-256 at the declared field, and nothing lies at the declared name. The pairing of the check re-verifies every file in place, so the check never accepts a wrong file: a temporary file with another hash than the record's is removed, never renamed, and the record's hash with no file is refused as before; a temporary file that one of several records in its directory names is left as it is, and the directory refused as before; a file already at the declared name is never overwritten (the same bytes make the temporary copy redundant and it is removed, any other file is refused as changed).
- **A temporary file no record names is removed** (no record beside it, or a record that names another hash or none), **only while `check` holds the lock of the campaign the batch belongs to** (`campaign.lock`, the dispatcher's, `expcore.campaign.lock.DispatchLock`, taken on the study's `out/`; the batch must lie under it, so a copy named with `--study` is never settled this way): with no dispatcher alive no attempt can be staging a file whose record is not written yet, and none can start before the lock is released. A check made while the campaign runs (`check --partial`, or a dispatcher between two attempts) cannot take it, and removes nothing; the file is a dot-name no check reads, and the next check made with the campaign stopped removes it. A rename that a record names is finished either way, since the hash is the record's, and the record is written only after the file is complete; the attempt's own `commit`, coming after, finds the file in place.
- **A check of a running campaign is a snapshot of a batch that is changing.** An attempt it finds between its record and its rename is settled when the check lists it; one that reaches that point after the listing, and not its rename before the check pairs it, is refused by that check, and passes the next. The rule above is for a batch no attempt is writing in.
- **What it never touches:** a dot-file of another name (a record's own interrupted write), a temporary name of no declared pattern, one without an attempt number, and anything that is not a regular file. A second `check` finds nothing to do. A study that declares no side file is not touched at all.
- **Three paths settle, each as far as it can prove** (#304). `check` of a study with a store settles as above. **`merge` settles in a private copy and never writes into a shard.** It copies each shard's `runs/` (its own `replay-check.json` and snapshot left behind) into a staging directory inside the one it writes the batch in, beside `OUT`, whose directories are made writable whatever the shard's modes are (a shard fetched read-only is merged as it is); settles the copy as `check` does (`settle_side_files`, said on stderr as `merge: shard <i> (<label>): finished ...`, with the note that it was done in the merge's copy); replays the copy; and, once every shard has passed, moves the verified copies into `OUT/runs` by rename, so what was replayed is what the batch holds. A rename that a record names is finished in the copy, so shards copied back from another host with a kill's leftovers are merged, not refused as missing a file. A partial file no record names is removed from the copy (`removed ...`): no attempt can be writing in a directory that only this merge holds, so the lock `check` takes is not needed, and the shard keeps the file as the evidence it is. The shard's `out/` stays byte for byte what it was, whatever the merge finds, refused or not. **The check of a merged batch** (`check` on `OUT/runs`) settles that batch in place, said as `check: finished ...`, and takes no lock and makes no `campaign.lock` (R16): a merged batch holds no store, so it removes nothing that no record names, since nothing proves that no campaign is started in its `out/` later; the batch a merge wrote holds no such file. It reads and writes only `OUT/runs`, never merge.json, the snapshots under `OUT/shards`, or a shard. A directory that cannot be written is never written to: the result is `left`, said as `check: left <path>: its directory is read-only, so it was not finished (...); make the directory writable and run the command again`; the merge's own copy is always writable, so a merge refuses a copy it could not settle (`N side file(s) that a kill left under a temporary name cannot be settled`) only if the staging directory beside `OUT` cannot be written. A temporary file of other bytes than its record's is never renamed, in any of the three, so the replay still fails it as a missing side file. `settle_side_files` returns each `Settled` with `left` set for a write it could not make (the write was refused for permission or by a read-only file system; any other error is raised).

One namespace is read by the core: a fault injector writes under `fault_injector` (`expcore.records.injections.FAULT_NAMESPACE`) a snapshot holding the run key, the attempt and a list of `injections`, each with its `target` and `injected` (true only once the injector verified the target was down). Under a condition that declares a kind with a target, the treatment flag needs that target in this list for this run and attempt; a missing, failed or stale snapshot gives none, so the attempt is set aside. Once the injector has taken a target down, the snapshot also holds `restore_verified` (an optional, additive field of the reading: `true` when the restore was verified, `false`, with `how`, `identity_changed` and `serving`, when it was not). The core refuses to score a record that carries it as anything but `true`, in any instrument's reading (`expcore.records.injections.restore_refusal`): `RunRecord.set_aside` is true for it, so `rescore` hands it back unscored, and the scheduler sets its attempt aside as infrastructure of kind `injector_failure`, whatever its outcome was, whether or not an instrument's own check refused it, and whatever the post-run health check decided, and runs it again (an attempt the health check or an outage already set aside keeps that outcome, and is not scored either). The platform that writes the reading registers the kind; a process that has not registered it, the core alone with a reading filed by hand, sets the attempt aside as `post_check_unverified`, the core's own untreatable kind for a result nobody verified, and never fails for an unknown kind.

The platform's fault injector is `expcore_onedata.faults.FaultInjector`. It has two kinds, registered by `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds`, the module a study lists in `[study] platforms` ([below](#infrastructure-kinds-of-a-platform-or-a-study)): `provider_outage`, which a condition may declare with a provider's label as its target, and `injector_failure`, its own failure to inject, clear or verify, which no condition may declare. Built only with `sanctioned = true` in `[faults]` of platform.toml and `sanctioned=True` from its caller (O10), it takes its target down in `start` and brings it back in `finish` (or as `session` or `running` end, whatever they end with: `running` is the async form that an executor uses, which keeps the start's thread and still clears when the run is cancelled), both outside the agent's time, so no wait kind covers either. A restore counts only when the provider's identity changed and, after that, only when the provider serves: a restarted provider's REST endpoint refused connections right after the restart and its container's health check passed 48 to 52 s later (the live run of 12 Nov 2026), so the injection is journalled cleared and the federation's exclusive admission released only once the driver says the provider serves (its container healthy, when it has a health check, and the platform's authenticated read of a space through that provider answered; `compose_driver(settings, probe=provider_serves(config, settings, client))`), polled every `poll_s` for at most `ready_timeout_s` of `[faults]` (180 s by default). A provider that does not serve in time is an unverified restore (`not_serving`): the injection stays open, the admission held and the fault gate failing, as for a failed restore, and a later `clear`, `finish`, `start` or the gate's recovery waits again. **An unverified restore is never passed silently (O11).** `clear` and `finish` report it as data (`clear()` is `False`, the snapshot's `ok` is false), which a caller that handles only a raising exit never reads, so the way out of an injector that cannot be read as data does not pass it: the exit of `session` raises `RestoreNotVerified`, an `InfraError` of kind `injector_failure` (untreatable, so a caller keyed on that kind sees what it saw for a failed start) that names the provider by its label, how the restore ended (`how`, a word such as `not_serving`, `identity_unchanged` or `start_failed`) and what was checked (`identity_changed`: the provider runs with an identity other than the one read before the injection; `serving`: it then served), and never a service, a path or a token. When the start or the body raised too, it is raised from that exception (`__cause__`), so neither is lost; an interrupt, an exit or a cancellation is raised as it was, with the restore failure in its notes, and so is a platform's hold (an `InfraError` of a kind that costs no attempt, C33, such as a progressive ingest's `ingest_held`), so a held start costs its run no attempt while the journal keeps the injection open for the health gate (O45). **`running` returns instead**, when its body returned: an executor that does `record = await record_run(...)` inside the `async with` keeps the run's record, and the record carries the failure itself, in the injector's reading (`restore_verified` false, with `how`, `identity_changed` and `serving`; a verified restore records `restore_verified` true), so the exit's silence never makes it fail-open: the campaign never scores a record that carries it (above), the instrument's post-run check refuses it as well, and the journal still blocks the next start. A body that raised leaves no record to carry the failure, so `running` then raises as `session` does. The injection stays open in the journal (`clear_failed`), so the health gate fails for it, naming the label, and every other injector's start is refused until a later `clear`, `finish` or `start` verifies the restore. The post-run check of the injector as an instrument, `FaultInjector.check`, raises the same failure, so an executor that starts the injector with `start_instruments` and clears it itself (ignoring `clear`'s `False`) gets the refusal from `finish_instruments`: the record keeps its snapshot and holds `instrument_refusal` of kind `injector_failure`, and the scheduler sets the attempt aside and runs it again. `finish` itself never raises for it, as `finish_instruments` would replace a raised snapshot with a bare failure and lose the injections. A `start` that fails already raises `injector_failure` whatever its own clear did. The snapshot records the wait as `ready_after_s`, the seconds from the verified identity change to the first reading that showed the provider serving, in the `verified` step and in the injection entry, and the `recovered` reading of a crashed run's injection carries it too. The injection is journalled before it happens, and the next start settles one a crash left open (O11). Outage runs are exclusive with every other run: with `[faults]` configured, every run holds the federation's reader-writer lock (`expcore_onedata.admission.FederationLock`) from before its infrastructure starts until after its post-run check, taken by the campaign through the plugin's `admission` (`run_admission(config, schema)`), shared for a baseline run and exclusive for an outage run, whose injector refuses a shared admission; a post-run check runs under the run's own admission, so no outage run can take the federation between a run's teardown and the check that judges it; the sandbox (`SandboxPlatform.launch`) and the tool server refuse to start without one, before they mint or start anything, and a sandbox start thread that a cancelled launch stops waiting for keeps a share of the admission until it has ended, so the federation stays held while it creates or removes anything. The tool server keeps the same guarantee: each of its check threads, the reads of Onezone before the spawn and after the stop, takes a share before it starts (`expcore_onedata.admission_share.to_thread_holding_admission`) and gives it up when it ends, on success, on an error and on a cancellation, so a launch cancelled while one runs may leave its admission but the federation stays held until the thread has ended, and nothing of a run acts on the federation after its admission ends. An admission names its federation (the lock file's resolved path and the digest of platform.toml), and one held for another federation is refused as none. The wait is admission, before the agent's clock starts, and a turnstile (`federation.intent`) queues later runs behind a waiting outage run so it is not starved. **A wait that runs out is a hold, never a failure (O11):** past `admission_timeout_s` (3,600 s by default) while another run holds the federation, an outage run through its restore and heal say, it raises `FederationBusy`, of the kind `federation_busy` (registered by `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds`, untreatable and `charged=False`, as `ingest_held` is). The campaign's run admission leaves such a run unstarted, with no attempt used, and asks again; a wait inside an attempt (the in-call `FederationLock.admitted`, or an injector whose run holds no admission and waits for the federation itself, which raises the hold as it is) sets the attempt aside uncharged. So waits piled up behind a long heal never exhaust `max_attempts`. A wait that is cancelled, a holder record that cannot be written and an unsettled injection under a shared admission are still `injector_failure`, as is every failure of the injector's own. The platform's standard health check (`PlatformHealth`) runs the fault gate around the whole check, built in and with no opt-out: it recovers what a crashed run left open whenever no run is on the federation, then takes the federation shared, waiting at most `health_wait_s`, and holds it until the check has answered, so no injection starts mid-check and the content type is never measured through a provider an outage holds down; a check whose wait runs out because an outage run holds the federation answers deferred, which charges no run (see HealthCheck), and charging no one (`charge=False`), so a deferral that outlasts `max_defer_s`, as an outage run with a heal can, closes the gate blocking without charging the run that found it; a heal's own marker (`clear_failed` with `healing` as the injection's latest line) is such a holder at work, never a failed restore; to tell that wait from a fault, whoever holds the federation exclusively keeps a holder record beside the lock (pid, boot id, purpose, target label, start time), so a wait that runs out is deferred for a live holder, fails naming the target for a restore that failed, and fails as stuck, after recovering what it left open, for a holder that is gone, keeping the record marked `stuck_since` until an acquisition of the lock clears it; and the check fails while an injection stays open (recovery failed or its lock is stuck), so the scheduler's gate holds back every run, baseline conditions included. `provider_outage(config, name, detail)` builds the failure the platform attributes to a provider, named by its label (O12).

**A baseline before the stop (`FaultInjector.prepared`, O11).** `start(identity)` admits the run, recovers what an earlier run left open, waits out the drain that recovery owes and the federation's drain record, and only then locks, journals and stops the target. A study whose probes read a baseline reads it between the two halves: `with injector.prepared(identity) as prepared: <baseline>; prepared.inject()` (`PreparedInjection.inject`), and `start` is that block with nothing in it but `inject`: the same journal lines, snapshot steps, driver calls and clock and sleep reads. Its own block has no body between the halves and re-reads nothing, so `start` is what it was, down to every clock read. In the pilot's shape the body is `start_instruments(probes, identity)`, with the injector left out of that list and first in `finish_instruments(record, [injector, *probes])`, so the probes finish before the restore. The block holds the federation exclusively for its whole body (a share of the run's admission) and touches nothing of the target before `inject`. Its exit keeps `start`'s rule: the admission goes only when nothing is down (an exception or an interrupt in the body, a body that never injects, an `inject` that failed before its stop) and stays after a stop that took effect, until the restore. **It never restores**: the caller's `finish` or `clear` does, as after `start`. The injector's lock is held while the block prepares and while `inject` runs, never across the body. While a block is open, `start` (so `session` and `running`), a second `prepared` and `recover` raise `RuntimeError` at once from any thread (a block when it is entered), even while the block's `inject` holds the lock (a `start`'s own block is waited for, as before). A `clear` or `finish` during the body returns at once and makes the block's `inject` refuse as `injector_failure`, and one that comes while `inject` runs waits for it and restores what it took down, so nothing is stopped after a clear has returned. `inject` raises `RuntimeError` outside its block, after it ended or a second time. Before it touches anything, a caller's block's `inject` re-checks what the window may have made stale, with no reading of the injector's clock and no step: the exclusive admission must be held where it is called (`asyncio.to_thread` carries the run's context; else `injector_failure`), the journal must still hold no open injection (`injector_failure`, naming its label), the drain record must still have passed, and the link epoch must be the same `id` and count as at the preparation (which makes the record when there is none, as the gate's check does), read once every link check under way has ended (`LinkEpoch.settled`, within `admission_timeout_s`): a record raised, missing, made anew or unreadable at either end refuses (`federation_busy`). No other run, and no check or heal of the gate that holds the federation, can move them while the exclusive admission is held; what can is named in `faults` ("A baseline before the stop"): a health check in the run's own context, a second injector under the run's admission, and the health gate's stuck-holder path acting on a holder record it read before this block's admission, a race `start` has too. The re-checks refuse whatever of these lands before `inject` reads. **The window is the caller's and unbounded.** In the documented order it lies inside the run's token window, and the least `[tokens] margin_s` counts no baseline: the injector's start after the mint falls inside `TOKEN_SLACK_S` (300 s), apart from the start's stop on the HPC backend, a term of its own. So a study whose baseline may take longer than the slack leaves declares a larger margin or reads its baseline before its launches mint a token; an overrun fails the run at the tool server's stop as `tool_plane_integrity`, never scored. It also lengthens the exclusive hold that other runs' health checks defer behind, a deferral no setting bounds already. On the HPC backend a fault relay that stops answering in the window makes `inject`'s first request, the read of the target's identity, fail: never sent when the heartbeat is already missing or stale, withdrawn when a relay that has just died never takes it, a failed reading when it took it and never answered. `inject` then fails as `injector_failure` with the target's lock let go and nothing journalled or stopped: an attempt, where the admission's refusal of a dead relay is an uncharged hold.

**An HPC federation (O58).** With `[faults] backend = "hpc"` (deploy/hpc-federation, `DESIGN.md` section 7) the injector, its journal, the restore and its settle, the recovery, the heal and the fault gate are the same; the driver is `hpc_driver(settings, probe=provider_serves(config, settings, client))` (`expcore_onedata.fault_hpc.HpcDriver`), which `PlatformHealth` and the day-0 runner build for that backend. It asks for four verbs on a provider's service, by its layout name: `status` (one JSON line: `running`, `identity` `<launch>:<pid>:<start tick>`, which every start of a stopped service changes, and `ready`, the service script's own checks for this launch, read as a container's health check, so a restore is never verified before them), `stop` (the service stopped with its work directory, data and registration kept, bounded by `settle_timeout_s`, not the command timeout), `start` (the same service started again from them) and `exec` of one command of a fixed set inside the running Apptainer service (`ping` and `restart_link`, the Compose driver's very commands; never a free-form command). The container fallback is that service's stop and start. A campaign that runs on a node cannot launch a Slurm step, so every request goes through **the fault relay** (`expcore_onedata.fault_relay`): a process on the login node, started by the federation's `up` and stopped by its `down`, which takes each request from a 0600 file in `<root>/fault-relay/` (0700), runs only its own federation's verbs for the services its state file lists (`hpcfed.py <verb> <service>`, with the prefix it was started with and an environment of a few plain names; it refuses to start with a secret-like name in its own), and answers in a file, which the client finds by listing `answers/` on every poll (never by looking the answer's path up first, which a compute node's NFS client would cache as missing for about 30 s), so the file system adds at most one poll to a verb's time (the relay runs one request at a time, in the order they were issued). A request names the relay it was made for, so a restarted relay never replays an old one, and an id the relay has taken before is refused (`replayed`); anything else is refused with a word and nothing run, and no malformed request stops the relay. **A verb that outlives its bound is ended with its whole process group, and its late steps are cancelled and journalled:** the relay stamps each verb's control steps with a tag of its own (`hpcfed.py --step-tag`), and after a timed-out verb a detached watch (`hpcfed.py late-steps`, 400 s) cancels a step of those exact names in the federation's own job by its exact id and writes `<root>/fault-relay/late-steps.jsonl`; the answer stays `timed_out`. **Each answer also carries `timing`:** from one accounting call, the time from the verb's issue to each of its steps' accounted Start and from its answer to each End (null with the reason where it cannot be given; a start's steps include its service step, the one it launched or the one an earlier attempt launched that it found listed and took over, whose Start figure is then negative), which the driver keeps (`HpcDriver.last_timing`) and the injector journals with the stop (`stop.timing`), the start's restore (`start_timing`) and the heal's link restart (`timing`). **A request with no answer in its bound and `relay_wait_s` (30 s) is unknown, never done:** withdrawn and read as not sent when the relay never took it, otherwise a command with no answer, so a stop is journalled `stop_unknown` and waited out, a start counts only once a changed identity is read, and a link restart is `command_timeout`, never retried. The relay beats every 5 s; the fault gate's pre-run check notes whether it saw a heartbeat no older than 30 s, and the run admission refuses an outage run as the hold `federation_busy`, uncharged, when that check saw none or none beats at the admission; a baseline run is admitted. **The settings:** `compose_file`, `project`, `command` and `docker` are refused; `stop_bound_s` (140 s: the layout's `stop_seconds` 50, supervise.py's kill 30 s, the step's end 60 s) is the longest a service's stop takes; `step_launch_wait_s` (the layout's `[hpc]`, rendered into `[faults]`, 240 s by default, 0 or more) is how long a control step may wait to start; `settle_timeout_s` (its default is the stop verb's own worst case and 30 s, `stop_bound_s` + `step_launch_wait_s` + 70 s, 450 s) is refused at or below the first two together; `poll_s` defaults to 10 s (each reading is a Slurm step); `command_timeout_s`, the floor of every relay verb's bound, defaults on this backend to the launch wait and 60 s of work, 300 s (120 s on Compose); `up` declares both in the platform file. **Each relay verb's own bound is derived** (`fault_hpc.verb_bounds`) from its inner worst case, every step at its whole bound with the launch wait in each, and 30 s, never below the floor: a status 600 s (its step, then a restarted shared-import owner's mount check), a start 630 s (a status step, then the wait to see its service step listed), a ping and a link restart 570 s, a stop 450 s. One bound of 300 s was below a start after an unrecorded stop and a restarted owner's status, and would have timed out a verb about to complete. Every one of these, and each verb's bound, is in the faults fingerprint after its default. **The launch wait widens timeouts only,** how long the harness waits before it fails closed: it never lengthens an outage or a hold. It is added to every bound that waits on a step's client from its start (the deploy driver's stop, status, exec and mount-check steps, `down`'s teardown step and a start's wait to see its service step listed; here each relay verb's bound), and the heal's reserves, the restore's bound and the platform's declared deferral are counted from those, so a study's `max_defer_s` check sees it. The 50 s is measured: three provider outages on one HPC federation (17 Nov) ended a service 26.1 to 33.1 s after its SIGTERM. The 240 s is a step's creation waiting on the scheduler's controller up to its message timeout (measured 47 to 103 s live, up to 200 s possible on the site, plus 20 %), and one round trip took 104.6 s. The heal's reserves count the slowest command verb's bound and the relay's wait for each command, a link restart's own bound and a reader's stop at its own bound, and the least `[tokens] margin_s` counts the relay's wait in each start and the injector's start's stop (its verb's bound + `relay_wait_s`): **5,370 s with the defaults, against 2,134 s on Compose, so an HPC run's tokens live that long past its ceiling.** **The stop's evidence:** the stop verb reports whether the stop was confirmed (`stopped`), how it ended (`exit`, `kill` at the bound, or `none`), its `seconds` from the request to its verdict and the service's own `service_seconds` from its SIGTERM to its end, measured on the node, and the injector journals them with the stop's outcome (key `stop` of the `injected` line, or of the `stop_unknown` line for a stop that answered unconfirmed) and its `injected` step, as does a heal's reader restart, so a live slot's journal gives every stop's duration, the figure `[hpc] stop_seconds` is set from. A stop with no answer has none. **The start's evidence (O58):** a restarted provider waits, inside its readiness budget, for op_worker's domain, which op_worker says null until its Onezone connection is up, before its identity is checked, and that wait is part of its down time; the ready service's `status` carries it as `domain_wait_s` (seconds as the node measured them, 0 when no wait was needed), the HPC driver keeps it (`last_domain_wait_s`), and the restore records it as `domain_wait_s` beside `ready_after_s`, in the snapshot's `verified` step and the injection (and in a recovery's `recovered` reading). It is optional: absent when the status carries none or a bad one, and on Compose, whose restore has no such wait; no snapshot holds a 0 that nobody measured. Nothing of it has run live.

**The session gate and the link heal of a restore (O35).** Once a restored provider serves, and before every ordered pair's link is checked, the restore passes a **session gate** (`expcore_onedata.fault_sessions`). The gate is polled every `poll_s` for at most `gate_timeout_s` of `[faults]`, 240 s by default: after a long outage a peer's session renewal toward the restored provider backs off to 180 s (inferred from op-worker's source), plus a margin. It passes when two things hold:

- Onezone shows every provider of `[link_check].providers` online (`GET /providers/{id}`, `online`).
- The block map of the gate's probe file, read through each peer of the restored provider, holds an answer for it, not an error. A provider answers `/distribution` by asking each provider of the space through its outgoing session to it, the same session the rtransfer callback `get_connection_secret` uses, and it keeps an error (`success` false) as that provider's entry. A fetch through a peer whose session is not up yet would raise there. The gate never reads through the restored provider itself.

The probe file, `expcore-session-probe` in the probe space, predates the injection. Before it journals or stops anything, `start` finds or writes the file through the local provider, waits until every provider of the probe space sees it, and journals its id with the injection (`gate_probe`). If the probe cannot be made, the start fails with nothing stopped. An injection journalled without one fails the gate at once (`unarmed`). A gate that does not pass leaves the restore unverified as `sessions_not_ready`, its links unchecked. A reading that passes only after `gate_timeout_s` counts as no pass. Without `[link_check]` nothing is read and the gate passes.

A pair that then does not pass is `links_stuck`, and `[faults] heal` says what follows (`expcore_onedata.fault_heal`). The heal runs only while the injector holds the federation exclusively at that moment, between runs. That hold is either the admission the injector took itself or its run's exclusive admission, still held. So a clear after the run's admission has ended does not heal, and neither does the health check's recovery of an open injection; the fault gate heals stuck pairs of its own check under an exclusive admission of its own (the fault gate's heal, below). Before its first step the heal journals that it heals the injection: a `clear_failed` line marked `healing`, which the journal reads back as `healed`. After that, neither a later clear nor another process's recovery heals the same injection again; they only check. The modes:

- `"off"`, the default, fails the restore closed.
- `"all_links"` is the primary mode. It takes a fresh matrix of every pair, then pings every provider with a declared service (`ComposeDriver.ping`: `docker exec <container> /usr/sbin/op_worker ping`, which must print `pong`); a provider with no pong fails it before any restart. It then waits for no active transfer (below), and restarts the rtransfer link of every provider in label order with nothing fetched in between, the restored provider and the crashed source included (`ComposeDriver.restart_link`: `docker exec <container> bash -c '. /usr/lib/op_worker/lib/env.sh && $NODETOOL -rpctimeout 120000 rpcterms rtransfer_config restart_link ""'`, bounded at 150 s). Each restart's result is recorded as a word: `ok`, `badrpc_timeout`, `badrpc`, `unexpected`, `command_timeout`, `no_node` or `not_run`. The command's output, which names the node, is never kept, and no restart is ever issued twice. Then comes one settle of `heal_settle_s` (180 s by default) with no probe, no fetch and no run, then no active transfer again, the restored provider's session gate, and a check of every pair with fresh probes. If a pair still fails, the heal falls back to restarting the containers of the stuck pairs' readers, unless `[faults] heal_container_fallback = false` (default true): then no container is ever restarted, and a pair the link restarts do not mend fails the restore closed (`links_stuck`), the gate closed, for an operator to decide. That is the setting for a study whose authority covers `restart_link` on any provider but not an automatic container restart of a provider other than the outage's target. `"link_then_container"` with the fallback off is refused at load.
- `"link_then_container"` is the per-reader mode (and `"link"`, the same without containers, kept because it costs nothing). Each stuck pair's reader in turn is pinged and re-read; a reader that is now the source of a failing pair, or whose pairs pass, is left alone. Then comes no active transfer, its link restart, the same enforced settle, no active transfer, its gate and a check of every pair; `"link_then_container"` then also restarts that reader's container, enforced the same way.

**The enforced settle.** The rule the live tests settled: after `restart_link` on a provider R, nothing may fetch through R until a settle has ended. A restart followed by a clean 3-minute settle with no fetch healed R's own pair and left every pair with R as its source healthy, including one whose connection was minutes old. The one reverse pair left stale came from a check made 23 s after R's restart; why a fetch so soon wedges the pair is in the link's private code. So every link or container restart is followed by a settle in which the heal issues no probe, no gate read and no check, and no run is admitted (the injector holds the federation exclusively), with no active transfer before the restart or at the settle's end. `"all_links"` restarts every link in one sequence so that one settle covers them all.

**Active transfers.** A replication could dial a link while it restarts. So the heal reads the waiting and ongoing transfer listings of every declared space and of the probe space, through each provider that supports it, one page and one job each, read only (`ComposeDriver.active_transfers`). Before the first restart of a step it waits, at most `gate_timeout_s`, for no listing to show a job not yet ended. Before each later restart of the all-links sequence, and at the end of every settle, it reads once and does not wait, since a transfer active then may already have fetched through a restarting link. A job still active, a listing that cannot be read, and a driver that cannot tell all fail it closed (`transfers_active`). The settle is enforced after every restart that was sent, including an interrupted sequence and an unverified container restart. A restart that raises settles before the failure leaves the heal, while the injector still holds the federation. Each restart is also held before it is sent (`Recovery.hold`): the settle's end goes into the journal on the wall clock (`settle_until`, on a `clear_failed` line of the healed injection), and every gate read and link check, by any recovery in any process, first waits for the latest one an open injection holds. So even a heal interrupted mid-step, or a process that exits, leaves no read before the settle could have ended.

**The container fallback.** The heal restarts the containers of the stuck pairs' readers one at a time, through the injector's own stop and start on the Compose project's containers. Each restart is followed by the settle, that reader's gate and a full check. A container restart is an injection of its own: it is journalled before the stop (`heal: true`, with the healed injection's `gate_probe`) and cleared only when the heal ends with every pair passing. Otherwise it stays open, journalled `clear_failed`, and recovery settles such an entry before the injection it healed, which cannot pass its gate while a reader is down.

**The heal's rules and bounds:**

- It never restarts the container of a provider that is the source of a failing pair, and never the restored provider's container: that would be the outage again.
- It does at most one link restart and one container restart per provider per restore, in at most 2 passes of per-reader steps.
- It runs within `heal_timeout_s`, provisional, and derived when undeclared (below). No step starts unless its declared bounds fit in what is left, and each wait for transfers or the gate is cut to what is left; the link check's own time is outside that reserve. The bounds are counted as follows:
  - `fault_settings.heal_all_links_reserve`: per provider, a ping and a link restart (three commands at `command_timeout_s`, plus the RPC's 150 s), plus one settle.
  - `heal_link_reserve`: one provider's ping and link restart, plus one settle.
  - `heal_container_reserve`: a container restart (four commands, `settle_timeout_s` twice, `ready_timeout_s`), a margin of three commands, and one settle. The restart's own waits and start attempts end by what its reserve leaves (the start is always sent, so no reader is left stopped by the bound); one cut short is unverified, its entry left open for recovery.
- A declared heal whose `heal_timeout_s` cannot hold one round of its steps is refused at load, naming the least it may be and the value declared. With the other defaults, four providers in `"all_links"` need 3,780 s and five need 4,290 s; with `heal_container_fallback = false` (no container reserve), 2,220 s and 2,730 s.
- **An undeclared `heal_timeout_s` is derived from that same least, never fixed** (`fault_settings.default_heal_timeout_s`): `heal_round_s` for the declared providers, mode, fallback, `command_timeout_s`, `settle_timeout_s`, `ready_timeout_s` and `heal_settle_s`, plus 10 % (`HEAL_TIMEOUT_HEADROOM_PCT`), rounded up to a whole minute (`HEAL_TIMEOUT_STEP_S`). The load check and the default use one function, so they cannot drift: a fixed 4,200 s was refused by its own check for five providers. With the other defaults, `"all_links"` derives 4,200 s for four providers and 4,740 s for five; without the container fallback 2,460 s and 3,060 s; `"link_then_container"` 2,520 s and `"link"` 780 s. With the heal off there is no round, and `heal_timeout_s` is 0. A declared value is kept as declared, and refused below the least.
- **The run's token outlives the restore, and no heal runs while a step that examines it may follow (`faults`, "The token's window").** The restore runs inside the outage run's attempt, at `running`'s exit, which the executor's pattern places inside the tool server's launch: after it come the collection, `finish_instruments` and the tool server's stop, whose re-verification of the tool plane examines the run's token and fails the run as `tool_plane_integrity` once it has expired (`toolserver._recheck`).
  - **The restore always runs** (its container start and its session gate use no run token). A token's lifetime is the study's choice, declared and never implied: a platform.toml with `[faults]` is refused at load, heal declared or not, unless it declares `[tokens] margin_s` and that is at least `fault_settings.restore_token_margin_s`, rounded up to a whole second. A missing margin (no `[tokens]` table, or one without the key) and a short one are refused alike, and the refusal names the least, its derivation term by term with each declared value and the sum (`fault_settings.restore_margin_derivation`), and the line to write, such as `[tokens] margin_s = 2134`. No default is derived. A platform without `[faults]` is unchanged and still gets 900 s when it declares nothing. That is the restore's declared bounds and pauses (`restore_bound_s`: a stop of unknown outcome waited out, `settle_timeout_s`; `clear_attempts` starts, each with `command_timeout_s` and `settle_timeout_s`, and a pause between two; `ready_timeout_s`; each of those waits one pause, `poll_s`, past its bound, which it checks only after a reading; and `gate_timeout_s`), 1,514 s with the defaults, plus `TOKEN_SLACK_S`, 300 s, for the readings' own time and the link check after the gate, plus the session drain a clear may wait (`restore_drain_s`): 2,134 s, so a platform with `[faults]` declares at least that.
    - **The drain is counted because the injector can always hold an exclusive admission of its own**, whatever its run holds. A clear that finds none held takes one (`FaultInjector._restoring`), and that acquisition waits out a drain record still standing (`federation.drain`), at most `session_drain_s` after the release that wrote it, before anything is restored: `session_drain_s` in full, named in the derivation as "a drain record still standing, which a clear that takes the federation itself waits out before it restores". An injector that holds an admission of its own, taken at its start or for the clear, then waits out its own drain before it lets go (`FaultInjector._drain`). That drain runs from the restore's start, so the derivation adds only what outlasts the restore's bound, as "its own drain past the restore's bound": nothing with the defaults, 486 s with `session_drain_s = 2000`. `session_drain_s = 0` removes both terms, since a clear then takes no admission and waits for no drain (1,814 s at the other defaults).
    - **Not counted:** a start's recovery of what an earlier run left, which the pre-run health check settles before an attempt starts; a start's waits for the federation (its admission, the drain record, and the drain a recovery owes, `_wait_owed`), which a run's admission has passed before its attempt starts, and which an injector taking an admission of its own waits before any token of its run exists (below); in the clear's own admission, the wait for the lock itself and a drain record another holder raises during it, bounded by `admission_timeout_s` and brief while an injection is open; and the drain of the run's own admission, published after the post-run check.
    - **No second restore inside the window, so the one counted is exact.** A restore that failed is tried again (by `finish`, a later `clear`, or a reused injector's next `start`) only when no step of the run that uses its tokens still runs, decided at once and never waited for (`FaultInjector._retry_now`). An executor that stops its sandbox and tool server before `running`'s exit has `finish` retry at once and get the provider back. While a step still runs, as at `finish` in the documented order, the injection is handed to recovery instead (`_hand_over`): it stays open in the journal (its `clear_failed` says how the restore failed), the record keeps `restore_verified` false with the injection open and is never scored (#245), a later `clear` returns False, and its target's lock goes with the run's admission (one of `Admission.endings`, which now all run even when one is interrupted, the interrupt raised after), so nothing of the run restores it inside the window: a recovery finds it held by a live injector and a second start fails closed. Once the run's admission has ended the next start's recovery, or the health gate's, restores it. The snapshot's `restore_retry` step records the path, `after_steps` or `left_open`, with `why` and the `steps` that ran.
    - **Every step that uses a token runs under the run's admission.** On a platform with `[faults]`, the tool server's two checks and the sandbox's mint and start start only under the run's admission held then (`admission.begin_token_step(name, config, what)`); without it the step is refused before it starts with `admission.TokenStepRefused` (an `AdmissionRefused` naming the step and what it was about to do, a harness defect that stops the campaign). So every such step is counted where the injector reads, a token is minted only after the run's admission and its waits, and an injector's own admission, with its wait for the lock, never follows a mint of its run. The launches' own spans (`admission.token_step`) and the sandbox's stop (`admission.count_token_step`) count without refusing: the launches refuse an unadmitted run already (`require_admission`), and a stop must always run.
  - **A heal is skipped while a step of the run that uses its tokens runs.** The tool server's launch (`ToolServerLauncher.launch`, up to the end of that re-verification) and the sandbox's (`SandboxPlatform.launch`, from its mint to the end of its stop) count themselves on the run's admission while they run (`admission.token_step`, read with `Admission.open_steps`: `TOOL_SERVER_STEP`, `SANDBOX_STEP`), and each of their worker threads that uses a token (the tool server's two checks, the sandbox's mint and start) stays counted until the thread itself has ended (`admission.begin_token_step`, refused without the run's admission, above). At a heal's start, the restore's or the start's recovery's (`fault_restore.LinkSettling`, asking the injector's `heal_skip`), a step still running without the long margin skips the heal. The skip is recorded: the `heal_skipped` step, or `heal_skipped` in the start's `recovered` step, with `steps`, `margin_s` and `long_margin_s`. The restore then fails `links_stuck`, the record kept and never scored, the injection open and not marked healed, so a later settling with no such step may heal it.
  - **In the documented order that later settling is no outage run's start**, whose launches also run before it, and the health gate's recovery never heals, so the gate holds the campaign: a stuck pair is healed only by a start with no step of its run running (an executor that starts the injector before its launches, or a bare injector start under a fresh exclusive admission), by the long margin, or by hand.
  - **The long margin**, `[tokens] margin_s` of at least `fault_settings.heal_token_margin_s` (the restore's margin, two heals of `heal_timeout_s`, the restore's and the start's recovery's, as the injector's `cancel_wait_s` counts them, and the session drain the start waits out after it restored what an earlier run left down; it counts the effective `heal_timeout_s`, declared or derived: 10,854 s for four providers in `"all_links"` with the other defaults and 11,934 s for five at their derived 4,740 s), heals as before whatever still runs. An executor that stops its sandbox and tool server before `running`'s exit heals with the default margin. A tool server or sandbox the study starts itself, which no launch counts, is not seen.
- No other step after the restore needs the run's token: the sandbox's collection and stop, the mount sidecar's and the data gate's teardown and the lease's release make no check that its validity decides, the post-run check and the restore's own reads use the platform's principals, and the slot's revocation sends with each user's own credential (its confirmation of an expired token may fail, which is logged and never fails the run).
- Listing a provider's service in `[faults.services]` lets the heal reach it (`ping`, `restart_link`, and a container restart only where declared). It never makes that provider an injection target: the injector takes down only the label its run's condition declares as its `provider_outage` target (#263), and journals that one alone.
- It fails closed:
  - a step after which a pair that passed before it fails stops it at once (`heal_broke_pair`);
  - so does a gate that does not pass (`sessions_not_ready`), a transfer that stays active (`transfers_active`), and a container restart that is not verified (`heal_restart_failed`);
  - a heal that ends with a pair failing is `links_stuck`.

A restore the heal mends is verified, with the heal in its snapshot. One it cannot mend is `RestoreNotVerified` with that word, and its detail names what the heal tried. Until the restore is verified, the injector holds the federation exclusively, so no run is admitted before the final all-pairs check passes.

**The link-check record (O35, `expcore_onedata.linkrecord`).** Every all-pairs check a restore and a heal make, through the driver's probe (`fault_serves.ServingProbe.matrix`, which `links` and the heal's reads of the pairs use alike), and the health gate's routine check (`PlatformHealth`), writes `link-check.json`, the record `deploy/compose-federation`'s `up` writes, so that after a heal or a restore the record on disk is the latest check's verdict and time, and a pre-check that wants a check under an hour old finds it instead of re-running `up`. The file is the platform layer's own report (`LinkReport.to_json`: `ok`, `detail`, `window` with `start_epoch_s` and `end_epoch_s`, `transfers`, and each pair with its seconds and bytes), in the bytes `up` always wrote (sorted keys, indent 2, a final newline), and `up` writes it through the same function (`write_record`), which is atomic: a draft of the writer's own in the directory, fsynced, renamed over the record, the directory fsynced, so a concurrent reader sees the old record or the new one, whole, and a write that fails leaves the old record as it was.

- **Where: the federation's root, whichever platform file the check ran through.** The record describes the federation's links, so it lies where `up` writes it and a pre-check reads it, in the root (`linkrecord.record_path`), not beside whatever file a check ran through. A platform file names its federation by `[faults] journal`, which every file of one federation names alike (it is how they share the federation's lock) and which `up` makes as `<root>/fault-journal`; the root is the journal's parent (`linkrecord.federation_root`). A file derived in another directory, as the outage check's example derives one, states the root's journal in full and writes the root's record, with its own `[link_check]` (its probe space's name, id and prefixes are the check's own and are not in the record: the report holds pairs by position, seconds, bytes, windows and transfer ids), and nothing lands beside it. `load_faults` refuses a `journal` whose parent holds no `platform.toml`, naming the key, rather than guess a path. It refuses a relative `journal` too in a platform file that lies in a study directory (one that holds `study.toml`, as a copy of the federation's file kept with the study does; the HPC federation's rendered file already states its journal in full): the journal would resolve into the study, whose parent holds that very copy, so the check above would pass vacuously and the lock and the record would land in the study. The message names the key and the absolute form to write, `<absolute path of the federation's root>/fault-journal`. The root's own file, and a file derived in a directory with no `study.toml`, may still state it relative. The pairs are labelled `providers[i]` by position in the file the check ran through, so a derived file that orders its providers differently labels them differently from the root's own.
- **Every check writes its own verdict.** A heal's sequence leaves, in turn, the failing verdict of its first read and the passing one of its re-check; a restore whose check finds a pair stuck, and a heal whose re-check still does, leave a failing record that names the pair, never the pass of the check before. The latest verdict on disk is always the latest check's.
- **One check at a time makes the record.** The check and the writing of its record run under `link-check.lock` beside it (`linkrecord.recording`, held exclusively; `up` takes it around its own check too), so a check that starts while another is under way waits for it and writes after it, and a check that finishes late never replaces the record of one that started after it. No clock decides the order. Under a restore's or a heal's exclusive admission the lock is never contended, and a heal's checks never meet the gate's routine check, which holds the federation shared; it orders what the admission does not, such as `up` run on a federation another process is checking, or the routine checks of two campaigns that hold the federation shared together. It is a leaf of the lock order (`admission`): its holder waits for nothing but the platform's bounded answers to the check. The lock file is opened for writing (an exclusive `flock` on NFS, which implements it as a POSIX lock, needs a write descriptor: opened read-only it fails with EBADF there, and no check on an NFS root would be ordered) and its owner makes it 0644; a file the process cannot write (another user's 0644 file, a read-only mount, an immutable file) is opened read-only instead, which a local filesystem locks all the same, so any process that can write the directory takes part there (the residuals: a lock file made some other way and unreadable to a process, and, on NFS, one the process cannot write, while another process holds it mid-write; the first then publishes nothing and removes the older record, which the second can still replace, and the file's mode or owner is the operator's to fix). A lock that still cannot be taken is logged: the restore's or heal's check goes on and answers as made, but publishes no verdict and removes the older record (an unordered write could leave an old pass standing), and `up`, which alone runs while nothing else does, writes its record and says in its output that the lock was not held.
- **A check that cannot answer leaves no older verdict standing.** One that raises removes the record before the error goes on (the restore fails closed, as before), and so does a record that cannot be written: the matrix is returned as made, a warning names the error's type, and the older record is removed, so a pre-check finds none and runs `up`. When the directory refuses the removal (a read-only one, with the record's own file still writable) the old record is emptied in place, which is no record (it is not JSON), and dated to the epoch for a reader that goes by the file's time. Only when it can be neither removed nor emptied does the old record stay, logged (the same permissions stop `up` and the injector's journal as well). The record is advisory: no restore or heal fails or passes because of it.
- **A record that was not written is a fact of the run's record, not only a log line.** A check that could not be serialized (the record's lock not taken) or whose record could not be written answers with a report whose `not_written` is `lock_not_taken` or `write_failed` (`linkrecord`, fixed words: no path, no error text), which `fault_serves.recorded` sets and the record file never holds. The matrix of the restore's and the heal's reads carries it (`LinkMatrix.not_written`), and the run whose check it was has the optional fact `link_record: "not_written"` with `link_record_reason` in its record (`linkrecord.fact`):
  - **a restore and a heal:** the injector's snapshot (`instruments.fault_injector`) holds both keys at its top level, for the latest check that was not written, and a `link_record` step with the same two keys (`ok` false); a `recovered` step, a start's recovery of an earlier run's injection, holds them flat as it holds `gate` and `heal`; the heal's own matrices (`first`, `before`, `after` and each action's `matrix_after`) say it of their own check. The day-0 outage check's `restore` facts hold them too, so its report has one line for each;
  - **a routine check:** `PlatformHealth.link_record` holds the fact for the latest check (None when it was written, and before the first), and the try's note (`expcore.campaign.prerun`, key `linkrecord.LINK_RECORD_NOTE`) holds it for the run whose pre-run check it was, which an executor reads with `prerun.current()` to put in that run's record. A study's record carries it only if its executor writes it: the core's record schema is unchanged;
  - **the fault gate's heal** has no run: its `ended` record (`PlatformHealth.gate_heal`) holds it in the matrices it read, as above.

  Both keys are absent, never null, when the record was written, so every reading is the one it was before. The restore, the heal and the gate's verdict are as they were: the record is advisory.
- **It is not the link epoch.** The record is in the root and not the fault journal's directory, takes none of the federation's locks or the journal's, and is read by no admission: a run's admission (`admission_recheck`) compares `link-epoch.json` and `gate-heals.jsonl` only. Writing it never raises or lowers the epoch, and a passing re-check leaves the epoch as the heal's raise left it, so a run checked before the heal is refused as before.
- **The gate's routine check writes it too.** `PlatformHealth`'s all-pairs check (at its first call and every `every_s`, and again after a failure), which runs under the health gate's shared hold, goes through the same function and rules as the restore's and the heal's (`fault_serves.recorded`): its verdict is the record, a failing one over an older pass, a check that raises or a record that cannot be written leaving no older record standing, one check at a time under `link-check.lock`. It never changes its own verdict or the gate's. **Not written:** a check run through a platform file with no `[faults]` (the root's own rendered `platform.toml` has no such table): it declares no federation root, and the record is not guessed beside the file, so it writes nothing.

**The snapshot** gains two steps (and the `drain` step below):

- `gate`: `ok`; `how`, which is `ready`, `not_ready`, `unarmed` or `none`; `waited_s`; and `detail`.
- `heal`:
  - `ok`, `mode`, `outcome`, `why`, `seconds`;
  - three matrices of every pair: `first` (the restore's own check), `before` (the heal's fresh reading) and `after`. Each lists every pair by label (`reader`, `source`, `ok`, `seconds`, `stuck`), with `failing_sources` and `shared_source`, the source every failing pair shares, which names a crashed provider;
  - `shared_source` again, and `window`, the all-links settle's start, end and seconds;
  - `actions`, one per step. Each has the provider by label (`all` for the transfer readings and the all-pairs check), the step (`ping`, `restart_link`, `transfers`, `check` or `container_restart`), its `result` word, `start_utc` and `end_utc`. Where they apply, it also has its `settle_s`, the `gate` reading, the `matrix_after` it, the transfer reading's `phase`, `active` count and `waited_s`, and a container restart's `serves_after_s`;
  - `skipped`, and `tried`, the steps in words.

A `recovered` reading carries the same two records.

A link check whose record was not written adds, to the snapshot's top level and as a step of its own, `link_record: "not_written"` and `link_record_reason` (`lock_not_taken` or `write_failed`; the bullet above), and nowhere else; with every record written the snapshot is what it was.

Every link restart exposes a provider to the failure it mends. `rtransfer_worker_sup` restarts its child `rtransfer_link_sup` at most 10 times in 3,600 s, and it sits under op-worker's top supervisor `cluster_worker_sup`, which allows 0 restarts in 10 s. So an 11th death of `rtransfer_link_sup` within an hour may take the node down (inferred from source, not measured), and a long outage with repeated fetches toward the down owner could approach that bound. The heal's own restarts count against it too, which is why it makes at most one per provider per restore.

**The fault gate's heal of stuck pairs (O35, `expcore_onedata.fault_gate_heal`).** With the executor's documented order, a run's tokens are already in play when its injector's start recovery runs, so that recovery's heal is skipped, and a stuck pair no restore heals would wait for an operator. The platform's health check runs before any attempt launches anything, so the fault gate heals such pairs there. `PlatformHealth` gives the gate the link report its check made in the calling thread (`links`, taken once, so a call never acts on a report another call made), a way to make its next call check the links again (`relink`) and the providers' services by label (`FaultGate(settings, driver, inner, labels=..., links=..., relink=...)`); a gate built without all three heals nothing.

- **When it acts.** Only when the check's own all-pairs link check, made in the call that is answering, found a stuck pair (a fetch that failed: `PairState.stuck`), the check failed, `[faults] heal` is not `"off"` and `sanctioned = true`. A check whose pairs pass, one whose only failure is an untested pair, one that failed before its link check (the space) while an earlier report shows a stuck pair, and one that fails on an open injection start nothing; so does a heal whose own fresh matrix shows no stuck pair (it recovered meanwhile, or only an untested pair fails). A check that runs under a run's admission (a post-run check, while the run's tokens may still be in play) never heals, shared or exclusive, and keeps a share of that admission until it ends (`Admission.try_retain`), so a check that outlives its caller's wait still holds the federation while it reads.
- **Its admission.** The gate lets its shared hold go and takes the federation exclusively (`RECOVERY` in the holder record), waiting at most `health_wait_s` for every shared holder (a campaign run, and a tool outside the campaign, which takes the same shared lock) and then for the drain record, as any admission does. When either outlasts the wait the check fails closed, not deferred, with nothing done and nothing recorded, as the hold `federation_busy`, which charges no run (`charge=False`); the next check of the closed gate tries again. It takes no reset lock (`federation.reset`): the exclusive hold of `federation.lock` already keeps out every shared holder, each with its share of the reset lock, and every shortcut reset, whose run keeps its share of `federation.lock` for the whole section, and no holder of the reset lock waits for what the heal holds. Holding the admission, it reads the latch again, so a heal another check ran while this one waited keeps it out. While the gate holds the federation no run is admitted, so no run's tokens exist during the heal, and another check that waits for it is deferred (`the recovery of the health check holds the federation`).
- **The heal.** The restore's (`Recovery.heal_stuck` over `fault_heal.LinkHeal`, no provider restored): the declared mode, one `restart_link` and one container restart per provider, 2 passes, `heal_timeout_s`, the enforced no-fetch settle, a fresh matrix before and after each action, and the container fallback under `heal_container_fallback`. A restarted reader's container is journalled as an injection of its own and cleared by the heal only when every pair passes, as in a restore; once its heal is over (ended, or cut short), the gate's recovery clears it when it has the container back, settled, and its session gate passes, and leaves every pair to the gate's own link check, made next in the same check, so an open entry never keeps a later heal out. A mode that may restart a container first arms the session gate's probe (`ComposeDriver.arm_gate`), so the reader's gate reads a file that predates its restart; one that cannot be armed fails the check with nothing done. The all-links sequence passes no session gate after its restarts, since no provider was down.
