- **The settle holds in every process.** Each restart is held before it is sent (its command, the RPC's bound and the settle) and again from its return, in the process (`Recovery.hold`) and in the federation's drain record (`FederationLock.hold_for`), so no admission in any process is granted before the settle could have ended, a crash of the healing process included. The record can outlast the settle by the restart's unused bound (about 5 min with the defaults): the next run waits for it before its agent's clock starts, and a check meanwhile defers (`closed ... while an outage run's sessions drain or a heal settles`).
- **Never.** The container of a provider that was the source of a stuck pair, in the gate's check or any matrix the heal read, even once that pair passes (skipped, with why); a gate that opens before its own link check, made afresh under the exclusive admission after a heal that ended `healed` (`relink`: a recent pass another call made before the heal is not reused), passes; a second heal before the gate's link check has passed again.
- **Once per stuck episode, durably.** `gate-heals.jsonl`, beside the fault journal, gets a `healing` line before the first action and an `ended` line when the heal ends. Once a heal has begun, no heal runs until a link check of the gate passes (`links_passed`), in this process or a later one, so a heal that left a pair failing keeps the gate closed for an operator, and the link restarts never pile up against the supervisor's bound. A record that cannot be read or written heals nothing.
- **A heal cut short is forgiven once.** A heal whose process ended before it did (a kill, a crash, or a clean exit that stopped it between two steps, which writes no `ended` line either) leaves a `healing` line with no `ended` line and a `process` (`liveness.this_process`: boot id, pid and start time) that is gone. The first such line of an episode does not latch: the next check that finds a pair stuck heals again, within the same bounds. A second one latches, so a process killed again and again gives each provider at most two link restarts per episode. A `healing` line whose process lives, or cannot be told gone (none recorded, or no process table to read), latches. A passing link check ends the episode, a heal cut short in it included.
- **Fail closed.** A heal that ends in any other word than `healed` (`links_stuck`, `heal_broke_pair`, `sessions_not_ready`, `transfers_active`, `heal_restart_failed`, or a heal that raised) is logged with why, each pair still failing at the latest check that answered by its reader, and what it tried, and leaves the pairs to the next check: that one finds the pair still stuck and fails, the latch keeping a second heal out, so the core's gate closes, as for any stuck link, and the campaign holds, so nothing is scored.
- **The record.** By label only (O12). `healing`: `id`, `start_utc`, `mode`, `trigger` (the gate's check as a matrix of every pair), `process` (the healing process, as `liveness.this_process` names it). `ended`: the heal's record (`ok`, `mode`, `outcome`, `why`, `seconds`, `before`, `after`, `shared_source`, `window`, `actions`, `skipped`, `tried`, as in the injector's `heal` step) with `id`, `start_utc`, `end_utc`, `trigger` and `failing` (each pair still failing, `reader` and `source`). `links_passed`: `utc`. `PlatformHealth.gate_heal` (and `FaultGate.last_heal`) is the latest `ended` record, None before one.
- **Its time: a wait that charges no run.** The heal can take up to `heal_timeout_s`, longer than the campaign's `health_timeout_s`, so it runs in the background (a daemon thread that owns the exclusive admission and lets it go at its end; `FaultGate.wait_for_heal` and `PlatformHealth.wait_for_gate_heal` wait for it). At a clean exit of the process (`atexit`, `FaultGate.stop_heal`) the heal starts no further step and is waited for, at most the longest step's reserve, so the step under way finishes, its settle included; it then ends `heal_interrupted` and writes no `ended` line, leaving what a kill leaves (above). A process killed mid-step leaves that step to the next process. An all-links sequence cut between two restarts leaves the settle of the restart in flight in the drain record, and pairs no worse than a fresh heal mends: the next check waits, uncharged, until the settle has passed, then finds the pairs stuck (or none) and heals again, the latch forgiving the heal cut short. A reader's container stopped and not started is journalled as an injection of its own (`heal: true`): once the hold the restart wrote before its stop has passed (until then the check waits, uncharged, and admits no run), the next check restores it under its own exclusive admission (`RECOVERY`), verified (a new identity, serving), holds the enforced settle (`heal_settle_s`, in the drain record and on the entry, a `clear_failed` line `settling` marked `healing`, which no check takes for a failed restore) and answers the uncharged wait, reading nothing through the links; every check inside the settle waits the same way, one that a tool's shared hold keeps from recovering included. Only a check after the settle reads the reader's session gate, with the journalled probe, and clears the entry when it passes; the gate's own link check of every pair, made next in the same check, opens the gate only when they all pass, so a run is admitted only then, and otherwise heals again (the heal cut short forgiven once) or keeps the gate closed. A container that cannot be started fails the check closed, the entry open. A container found back with no settle journalled on its entry (a recovery killed after its start and before its hold, or a heal's own restart) gets one held the same way before its gate is read. A recovery reads a heal's container restart only after every hold that heal left (one reading then decides), so a reader whose stop was journalled and never sent is found never down and cleared. A check that meets a heal's container restarts left open (`clear_failed`) while that heal's settle still closes the federation (its drain record) waits, uncharged, like the recovery that settles them. The gate's recovery never sleeps out a held settle inside its check (`Recovery.recover(defer=True)` raises `RestoreSettling`, which the gate answers as the uncharged wait); the injector's start recovery, which may wait, sleeps it out. The check that began it answers the uncharged deferral (`Health(..., deferred=True, charge=False)`), and so does every check of the gate while it runs, at once: the core holds the run without charging it and keeps the gate as it is, so a pair the heal mends charges no run. Once the heal has run longer than its own bound (`heal_timeout_s` plus `heal_settle_s` since it began), a check answers failed, not deferred and uncharged: the gate closes, so a hung heal never defers for ever. A heal that mended every pair makes the gate's own link check afresh under the admission, and a pass lifts the latch; whatever the heal did, it then expires the last link report (`relink`) before it lets go, so the next check makes the link check afresh and no pass from before the heal's end, by any check, opens the gate (after a heal that failed, that check finds the pair still stuck and closes the gate). Until a check made after the expiry has answered, a check that cannot make it at the same time waits, uncharged, instead of answering with the old report. The settle's drain record may still defer the next check, uncharged, for a few minutes. A check that found the pair too and meets a heal under way (this gate's, or another check's holding the federation) defers the same way, never taking the latch the running heal set for a closed episode; another process's check that meets the heal's admission defers too (`the recovery of the health check holds the federation`), even while one of the heal's container restarts is journalled `clear_failed` during its settle. Such a wait for another check's heal or recovery is bounded the same way, from the holder record's `since_utc`: past `heal_timeout_s` plus `heal_settle_s` (or with no readable time) the check closes the gate, uncharged, even while that holder's process lives. The core's own bound on a deferral (`max_defer_s`) still applies, so the gate declares its longest deferral to the core: `FaultGate.max_deferral_s()` and `PlatformHealth.max_deferral_s()` answer `DeferralBound(session_drain_s + heal_timeout_s + heal_settle_s, detail, rechecks, checks)` with a declared heal (`fault_settings.heal_deferral_s`: after an outage run the session drain comes first, with no check between them that passes; 4,700 s with the defaults, 4,790 s for five providers at the 4,290 s they need and 5,240 s at the 4,740 s derived when `heal_timeout_s` is left out), naming the keys and how to shorten it, and None with `heal = "off"` or without `[faults]`. That is the platform's own time. The heal begins at a check, and a call that meets the fresh link check after the heal waits for it, so the deferral also holds waits whose lengths are the core's settings: after a drain one recheck (`recheck_s` and up to `poll_interval_s`) and the check that starts the heal (`health_timeout_s`), and after the heal, whose last deferred answer can come just before it ends, one more recheck and the check that makes the link check afresh, declared as `rechecks=2, checks=2` (`rechecks=1, checks=1` with no drain, where the first deferral is the check that starts the heal), and the core adds its own figures: **4,882 s with the defaults (`recheck_s` 30 s, `poll_interval_s` 1 s, `health_timeout_s` 60 s, `session_drain_s` 320 s, `heal_settle_s` 180 s) for four providers at their derived `heal_timeout_s` of 4,200 s (4,700 s and 182 s), and 5,422 s for five at their derived 4,740 s (5,240 s and 182 s); for five at a declared 4,290 s, the least they need, 4,972 s (4,790 s and 182 s). With `session_drain_s = 0` (the core adds 91 s) they are 4,471 s, 5,011 s and 4,561 s, in that order, and with a `health_timeout_s` of 150 s, which the link check needs, 5,062 s, 5,602 s and 5,152 s with a drain, and 4,561 s, 5,101 s and 4,651 s with none (the study guide's table gives all of them).** Each is the total the scheduler's refusal prints for a `max_defer_s` shorter than it. The figure assumes the heal ends within its own bound (one that overruns it closes the gate, and the settle's hold it leaves can defer a check later still) and every check ends within `health_timeout_s`, as a check must (the link check too: keep it above `visible_timeout_s + timeout_s + evict_timeout_s`): the core gives up on one that outlives it, but its thread runs on and can still start the heal later than the figure counts. It is also for a deferral that starts with the drain: one that began earlier (an outage run still running, another worker's check deferred on it) or for another reason (a run on an ingest baseline, which `PlatformHealth` checks first and which declares no bound, or a run's shortcut reset holding the reset lock, `federation.reset`), a heal that waits for another holder of the federation (the hold `federation_busy`), a heal cut short by its process's end, which the next process settles and heals once more on the same deferral (the store keeps its age across the restart), and a heal that fails with a container restart left open, which the next check recovers, owing a session drain and holding a settle when none is journalled, before the gate closes on the stuck pair, count from the deferral's first answer, so its age holds that wait too. The scheduler refuses a campaign whose `max_defer_s` is shorter when it starts ([HealthCheck](#healthcheck)), so a heal that mends its pairs never turns into a blocking hold that stops a campaign which exits when blocked; a failed heal leaves the gate closed for an operator, and its recovery's drain can run the deferral past the bound, which then closes blocking. Every answer the gate gives in a failed heal's aftermath (the waits of the recovery of the container restart it left open, and the closing on the pair it left stuck) leads with `a failed heal: operator action needed (stuck pairs: providers[2] reading from providers[0], ...)` (`fault_gate_heal.failed_heal_reason`, from the `ended` line of the current stuck episode in `gate-heals.jsonl`, the first two pairs failing at the heal's latest check by label and how many more, never a provider's name or id), ahead of the rest of its words and separated from them by a semicolon, since the core keeps 200 characters of a detail and a status shows 160; a heal under way, one cut short, one that mended its pairs, a wait for an outage run and a record that cannot be read leave their answers as they were. The run admission's refusal of a run whose pre-run check the heal outdated (`admission_recheck`: the epoch moved, a heal begun since the link check last passed) leads with the same reason, then says what the epoch did, so the detail of the run's `run_unstarted` event, which keeps the refusal's after the run's key, says first that an operator is needed; it is the same uncharged hold `federation_busy`, with no reason added to the vocabulary, and a failed heal the gate's link check has since seen mended, a heal under way or cut short and an `ended` line that names no pair by a label leave it as it was. The core words such a hold as one ([HealthCheck](#healthcheck)). A study that wraps `PlatformHealth` in a check of its own must pass `max_deferral_s` on: the platform's run admission (`run_admission`) declares the same bound from the same `[faults]` settings (`declared_deferral_s`), and a check that exposes no `max_deferral_s`, or a smaller bound, is refused when the campaign starts ([HealthCheck](#healthcheck)).
- **A run checked before a heal or a failing pair is refused at its admission (`expcore_onedata.admission_recheck`).** A run's pre-run check can pass, and the run then wait for its lease and the federation's admission while a heal runs, or a link check finds a pair failing; it would then be admitted onto a stuck link once the heal let go. So the federation keeps a link epoch (`link-epoch.json` beside the fault journal, `fault_epoch.LinkEpoch`: an `id` set when the record is made, the count `epoch`, and the `event`, `heal` or `links_failed`, and `utc` of its latest raise), written whole under the journal-wide lock and raised before any heal's first action (the gate's, and a restore's; a heal that cannot raise it does not begin) and whenever the gate's link check, in any call, finds a pair failing. A raise that cannot be written spoils the record instead of leaving the old count readable: it removes it, or, when the directory refuses that, empties it in place (an empty record reads as missing); the next check makes it anew under a new `id`. When even the overwrite fails (`RaiseLost` with `spoiled` false), the gate keeps `link-epoch.lock` held shared (`LinkEpoch.hold_shared`) until a later check of it records the raise, so every admission's wait for that lock runs out and refuses the run meanwhile. The gate reads it (making it when there is none) under the shared hold of a check that holds no run's admission and notes its `id` and count in the try's note (`GATE_NOTE`, `noted_of`). A link check of the gate holds `link-epoch.lock` shared from before it reads the pairs until it has raised the count (`LinkEpoch.checking`). `run_admission` compares the note with the record, holding the federation's lock it took (so no heal can begin meanwhile) and `link-epoch.lock` exclusively (`LinkEpoch.settled`, waiting within `admission_timeout_s` for the checks under way to end, so it never reads between a check's finding and its raise), and refuses the run as `FederationBusy` (the hold `federation_busy`, no attempt used, nothing scored) when the try noted no epoch (its check never reached the gate: unknown, so checked again, never admitted blind), when the count has moved since the note or the record is missing or has another `id`, when `gate-heals.jsonl` holds a heal begun since the gate's link check last passed (`GateHeals.unpassed`), when either record cannot be read, or when a link check under way outlasts the bound or the campaign ends meanwhile. The refusal says which, and the core puts it in the run's `run_unstarted` event, leading with the failed heal's reason (`a failed heal: operator action needed (stuck pairs: ...)`, as the gate's answers do) when the heal record's current stuck episode holds a heal that ended with a pair still failing; the campaign asks again, and the run's next pre-run check sees the federation as it is. A try with no note at all is admitted, which is a caller that is no campaign (a tool outside one): a campaign with `[faults]` and no health check does not start, since `run_admission` declares that it needs one (`HEALTH_REQUIRED`) and the scheduler refuses the campaign when it is built, before its lock or store, and, for a declaration that refusal never saw (an admission wrapped without it, a runner built by hand, an executor that takes the federation itself), a try of a campaign with no health check has a note that says so (`prerun.UNCHECKED`), which `run_admission` and `require_admission` refuse as `AdmissionRefused` and so stop the campaign loudly, so no run is admitted or started blind; a health check that never reaches the gate still has a note, with no epoch, so its runs are refused as above. A link report made before the epoch last moved is no answer: the gate's next check makes the link check afresh (`relink`), so a failing pair or a heal another process saw is seen at this process's next check. A link epoch that cannot be read fails the check (`injector_failure`).

**The session drain of an outage run (O11, `[faults] session_drain_s`, `expcore_onedata.fault_drain`).** During an outage an agent's read of a file only the stopped provider holds, through the local provider, leaves an on-demand sync job on the local provider's per-file synchronizer that nothing calls back; a later read of the same file and range, from any session, joins it and hangs. The job is cancelled only when the run's callers end: a REST read's when its HTTP request process dies (seconds after the client's bound, at most about 5 min), a mount's at the file's release or about 300 s after oneclient's last connection closes (`fuse_session_grace_period_seconds`), plus about 15 s (op-worker f118ed9, source-read, not measured). Eviction, a transfer cancel, deleting the file and a link restart do not cancel it; only a container restart does. So the federation admits no run before `session_drain_s` (320 s by default, a number of seconds, 0 or more) after an outage run's mount disconnected and its REST gate stopped, whichever is later.

- **The times.** The sandbox records when its gate's stop was confirmed (the collection stops it, else the removal of its container) and when its mount's oneclient sidecar was shown gone (`DockerSandbox.session_kinds`, `session_ends`, and `session_ends` in its metadata, UTC). `SandboxPlatform.launch` begins them with the drain of the exclusive admission the run holds, and reports each end it showed as the launch ends, whatever the stop did. A stop that failed, was cut at its deadline or left the mount live shows no end, and that end stays unknown.
- **The wait is a deadline, so it overlaps the restore.** `until` is the later end plus `session_drain_s`; only what is left of it after the restore, a heal's settle included, is waited. Times that are missing (an end never reported, a sandbox that reported nothing) are unknown, and unknown is never zero: `until` is the restore's start plus `session_drain_s`, and never earlier than a known end plus it. A sandbox with no mount and no gate has no session to drain. `session_drain_s = 0` declares no drain.
- **Where it is enforced: by the federation, not by a process.** The last release of an armed exclusive admission (`Admission._release`) writes the end of the drain, on the wall clock, to the journal's drain record (`federation.drain`, only ever later: `InjectionJournal.raise_drain`), and returns at once. Every admission checks that record once it holds the lock: `FederationLock.acquire` waits for it, holding the lock it took, inside the bound of its wait and under its cancellation (`FederationBusy` when the bound ends first), and `try_acquire` refuses. So no run, outage or baseline, is admitted inside the drain, whichever way the federation was let go (the campaign's run admission at the end of its attempt, after the sandbox's stop and the post-run check; the injector's own admission; the health gate's recovery), whether the process that let it go was interrupted afterwards, and whether a restore held an admission at all. The next run waits before its agent's clock starts, like any wait for the federation; the health check meanwhile reports the federation closed and defers, charging no run. A drain record that cannot be written is waited out in process, the lock held, before the lock is let go, and a wait that is interrupted keeps the lock for the life of the process, with its holder record, rather than let it go; one that cannot be read refuses every admission (`injector_failure`). The injector arms the drain before it restores a provider it took down (`clear`), so no interrupt between the restore and its report leaves the federation open, and takes the arming back when the provider never went down (`never_down`); a clear that holds no admission (the retry after the run's admission was let go) takes the federation exclusively for its restore, as does `FaultInjector.recover()` with none held (a recovery that cannot get it inside `admission_timeout_s` fails as `injector_failure` and restores nothing), so no process is admitted while it restores, and it waits the drain out before it lets go. A recovery that restored a provider a crashed run left (the start's, the health gate's, `FaultInjector.recover`) owes the full drain from its own start: that run's sessions are unknown, whatever the current run's sandbox says (`SessionDrain.owe`; the latest recovery's start counts), decided against the injections the recovery has just read and before it settles any (`Recovery.recover(before=...)`); the health gate's recovery of a stuck holder's injection takes the lock when the holder has gone since its wait ran out and recovers under it, and, while the lock is still held by a holder that is gone (so that it admits nobody), closes the federation by the record alone (`FederationLock.hold_for`), written before it restores anything. Since that record is written without the lock, every admission reads it again after it read the journal (`held` and `admitted`, which now settle the journal for an exclusive control as they do for a shared run, and the injector's start after its recovery), so one that finds the journal settled finds the record too. A start whose recovery restored a provider an earlier run left down waits out the drain that recovery owes before it takes anything down, and records a `recovery_drain` step. A restore keeps a share of the admission it runs under until it has ended (`Admission.try_retain`), and one whose admission cannot be kept takes the federation itself. An admission nothing armed (every shared admission, an exclusive run with no fault, an outage run whose provider never went down) writes nothing and is let go at once.
- **The order in a run.** The gate stops in the sandbox's collection, the injector's restore follows, and the mount ends at the launch's exit, after it. The restore therefore cannot wait for a mount still connected: it records its plan with the mount unknown and `deferred` true, and the run's admission publishes the drain when it ends, every end known. An injector that holds the federation by an admission of its own (a run that holds none) has no sandbox of its run: it waits in `clear`, before it lets go, and counts the drain in `cancel_wait_s`.
- **The snapshot** gains a `drain` step after `verified`, when `session_drain_s` is above 0 and a provider that went down was restored: `ok` (false only when no exclusive admission was held, with `why`), `deferred`, `drain_s`, `basis` (`session_end`, `restore_start`, `no_session`), `why`, `mount_end_s`, `gate_stop_s`, `restore_start_s` and `until_s` (seconds on the injector's clock since its start, None when not recorded) and `waited_s` (0 when deferred). What a run's admission closes the federation for at its own end comes after the snapshot is taken and is not in it; it is the drain record's time.
- **Not covered.** A process killed before it let its admission go writes no record, and the kernel drops the lock: the next start finds the injection settled, or recovers it and owes the full drain from the recovery's start. A crashed run's orphaned sandbox keeps its mount connected until the next sandbox start reaps it, which is after the drain such a recovery owes, so that drain is a floor and not the sessions' end. The drain record is on the wall clock, so a clock set back reopens the federation early.

**A tool outside a campaign holds the federation's shared admission (O48).** A study's smoke driver, warm-up or `verify` is no campaign attempt and holds no run admission, so nothing keeps an outage from taking a provider down or bringing it back while it reads and writes through it. `expcore_onedata.tool_admission.shared_admission(config, *, reason, timeout_s=None, ingest_journals=())` is the hold such a tool takes around all it does on the federation (`shared_admission_async`, an `async with`, for a driver in an event loop, taking `waits` as `FederationLock.admitted` does): the same lock a campaign attempt takes, shared, through the same turnstile, set as the running context's admission, so the sandbox and the tool server accept it (`require_admission`) and an outage run, which needs the lock exclusively, waits for it and never overlaps it. It is admitted the way a campaign run is (`FederationLock.held`, `admitted` for the async form): it waits for an outage run in progress, and then for an outage run's session drain (`federation.drain`), at most `timeout_s` (`[faults] admission_timeout_s` when None, 0 to try once) and is then refused as `FederationBusy`, an `InfraError` of kind `federation_busy`, the uncharged hold a campaign run's wait ends in. Under the lock it is refused, as `InfraError` of that kind, naming why and never a host or a name (O12): an injection the fault journal holds open (the target by its label), a fault journal that cannot be read, a record of the holds that cannot be read or written, and, for each journal in `ingest_journals`, a preallocated ingest's baseline that is unhealthy, orphaned by a run that is gone, or held open by a live run (`baseline_health`; the campaign's own check defers there, but a tool has no clock to wait on, and a reader would see the baseline in flux). It validates `reason` and `timeout_s` (finite, 0 or more) first, as `ValueError`, before it takes anything. `reason` goes into the published record of the holds, so it is a short code (`expcore_onedata.shortcode.checked_code`, the rule a full reset's reason follows too): a lower-case letter, then up to 63 lower-case letters, digits or underscores, holding no name the configuration gives (the zone's name and host, each provider's name, id and host, each space's label, name, id and prefixes), compared lower-cased with every separator dropped; the refusal names the argument, never the value. A platform without `[faults]` has no lock: it yields None, its ingest journals still checked. Inside a shared admission of the same federation (a warm-up called by a smoke driver) it yields that one and records nothing more; inside an exclusive one, or one of another federation, it raises `AdmissionRefused`. **The hold is recorded** in `shared-holds.jsonl` (`expcore_onedata.shared_holds.SharedHolds`) beside the fault journal, in the directory the exclusive admission reads, durably and under the journal-wide lock: a `shared_held` line once the hold is taken and the journals allow it, with the `reason`, the writer (`holder`: pid and boot id), the `owner` (`expcore.campaign.liveness.owner`: the process as the lease pool names a holder, `boot id:pid:start time`) and `since_utc`, and a `shared_released` line with `until_utc` and `how` when the lock goes: on exit, on an exception, and, for a worker that took a share (`retain_admission`) and outlives the body, only when it has given the share up, so the record never ends before the lock does. `SharedHolds.holds()` lists every hold and `open_holds()` the open ones with `live` (whether the process runs). The kernel drops a dead tool's lock with its process, so an outage run is never kept out by one; its record is ended as `holder_gone` by the next tool's hold, when its process is gone, and by any exclusive admission, whatever the record says, since holding the lock exclusively proves no tool holds it. An exclusive wait that runs out (`FederationBusy`) says that N tools hold the federation shared and since when, never a reason, which is the tool's own text and stays in the record. The hold covers the body of the `with`: a thread, a task, a forked child or a subprocess that goes on after it has ended is not held back from an outage, so the tool's whole use of the federation belongs inside it.

**Every shared admission holds the reset lock shared (O11, `expcore_onedata.resetlock`).** A campaign run's shared admission, a tool's (`shared_admission`) and the health check's hold `federation.reset` shared beside `federation.lock`, taken after it and outside the turnstile, within the same bound (`FederationBusy` past it), for as long as they hold the admission (`Admission.reset`, a `ResetShare`; `Admission.may_act` is false while it is given up). Each hold is recorded in `reset-holds.jsonl` beside the fault journal, in the format of `shared-holds.jsonl` (`SharedHolds(directory, name=RESET_HOLDS)`; `reason` is `a campaign attempt` or `outside a campaign`), and a hold that cannot be recorded is refused, `injector_failure`, both locks let go. An exclusive admission takes no reset lock. A reset's exclusive hold (`resetgrant.exclusive`, above under the reset; every reset takes one, O66) is the only exclusive holder, and while it holds it `require_admission` refuses the resetting run's infrastructure (`require_reset_share`), a tool may not reuse its admission, and a health check is deferred, uncharged. The lock order (`federation.intent`, `federation.lock`, `federation.reset`), what each holder waits for holding what, and why none of it deadlocks, are in `admission.py`'s docstring. Every lock file is opened `O_CLOEXEC`, every held lock is closed in a forked child, and no `subprocess` call of the package passes descriptors, so no lock outlives its holder.

## RunExecutor

`expcore.campaign.executor.RunExecutor`.

```python
class RunExecutor(Protocol):
    async def __call__(self, request: RunRequest) -> RunRecord

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RunRequest:
    identity: RunIdentity
    run_dir: Path
    endpoint: Endpoint
    backend: ModelBackend
    timer: AgentTimer
    clock: Callable[[], float]
    treatment_kinds: tuple[str, ...]
    lease: Lease | None = None
    treatment_targets: Mapping[str, str] = {}  # kind -> the target the condition declares for it
    models: ModelTable | None = None
    defect_log: DefectSink | None = None
    reserved_capped: bool | None = None  # the bound the budget gate reserved the run by; None: no gate decided it
    reply_cap_unheld: Callable[[], bool | None] | None = None  # with a budget: has the model left the reply maximum

    @property
    def run_key(self) -> str  # identity.run_key

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RunIdentity:  # expcore.records.build
    run_key: str
    factors: Mapping[str, Any]
    attempt: int
```

`RunIdentity` says which attempt of which run the request is, and goes into the record as it is:
- `run_key` is the run's key text, the factor values joined in the order of `[identity] order` (`task=lake|access=direct|tools=basic|model=alpha-stub|seed=1`). It is the same for every attempt of the run, and `RunRequest.run_key` returns it.
- `factors` maps every factor of the run key to the run's level: the study's declared factors and the built-in `task`, `model` and `seed` (an integer). The condition is not among them; `study.schema.condition_named(factors, run_key)` names it.
- `attempt` is 1 for a run's first attempt and one more for each attempt of the run that was charged before it, an infrastructure failure set aside for instance, so a stub that should fail on the first try and succeed on the next tests `attempt`. An attempt that was interrupted (a killed process) is not charged, and its run is tried again under the same number. The record's file is named by `run_key` and `attempt` (`record_filename`), in `request.run_dir`, the directory of this try (`try<N>`, where N counts every try of the run, interrupted ones included, so it can be larger than `attempt`).

`record_run(request, providers, system, user, config, *, code, **options)` runs the loop with the request's backend, timer and treatment kinds and returns the record. It records with the request's model table, which the scheduler fills in for every run, so the key of each model in `models.toml` is redacted whatever the backend (R12). It also hands on the request's `defect_log`, so the traceback of a crash the loop catches goes to the local harness-error log, redacted with every secret the executor gave `record_run` (L08). A sink should not raise; if one does (the log cannot be written, or an executor passed a `defect_log` of its own), the record is still built and written, and the attempt stops dispatch once its result is stored, naming the failure (C17). It writes what the campaign decided about the run's reservation into the record's totals, so a scorer reads it from the record and never opens the store (C27): `totals.reserved_capped`, the request's `reserved_capped` (True: the run was reserved by the reply maximum R, False: by the window), which is the value the attempt's row in the store keeps; and `totals.reply_cap_unheld`, whether the campaign had taken the run's model off the reply maximum when the loop ended, read from the store then. False means the model was not marked when the loop ended, not that it was shown to hold the reply maximum: a model never probed (a stub, or a plugin's own factory) is also False. Each is left out when it is None, as it is for a campaign without a budget, and an executor that builds its record without `record_run` has to pass both to `record_attempt` itself.

Core guarantees:
- The gates (health, harness stop, budget, unit pause) were passed, the lease the platform declared for the run is held for the whole attempt, and the timer is not started: the loop starts it. The attempt has a row (`running`) before the executor is called, so a kill leaves a run that the next start settles (C04).
- `InfraError` raised by the executor is recorded as infrastructure of its kind; any other exception as a harness defect (C10). The result is written as a marker, then to the store in one transaction.
- The record's treatment kinds and targets must equal the condition's, and its treatment flag the store's own decision from the outcome, the kinds and targets, the completed turns and the targets the fault injector recorded as injected (`AttemptResult.model_turns`, `recorded_treatment`, `first_turn_rule`, `target_rule` and `injected_targets`, all set by `result_from_record` from the record and its schema version), or the store refuses the result (C01). Record schema 2 added the first-turn rule and no field; schema 3 added the target of a fault and the target rule. Older records and outcome markers stay readable and are decided under their own rules.

Implementer must guarantee:
- Return the record `record_run` produced, and **write it to `request.run_dir`** (`record.write(request.run_dir)`) before returning: the core reads, checks and exports records from disk and does not write them for you.
- A run that writes a declared [side file](#side-files-of-a-record) places it with `stage_side_file` and `StagedSideFile.commit` ([order and recovery](#placing-a-side-file-beside-its-record)): stage, put `sha256` in the record, write the record, commit, and call `discard` when the attempt fails before its record.
- Write nothing else into `request.run_dir`: the record (under its own name, which `record.write` gives it) and the declared side files are the only files of the study's there. Any other file, evidence the plugin collects included, fails `check` and `merge` as unexpected (R04); keep it outside `out/runs`, or declare it as a side file.
- Script or configure the backend through the request; never build one.
- Pass `request.treatment_kinds` and `request.treatment_targets` unchanged (`record_run` does) and the code identity from `StudyContext.code`.
- Add the instruments' snapshots with `finish_instruments` before writing the record: it derives the treatment flag again, because under a declared target the fault injector's reading decides it.
- Close nothing the core owns (backend, timer, lease).
- With instruments that can refuse an attempt, call `first_turn_instruments(instruments, request.timer)` just before `record_run`, and write the record `finish_instruments` returned, which holds a refusal. Nothing else calls the hook, so an instrument whose executor never does has no first-turn reading, and its `check` should refuse for want of one ([instruments](#refusing-an-attempt-the-first-turn-hook-and-the-post-run-check)).

### What an executor reads

An executor keeps the `StudyContext` that `build` received (the example's keeps it as `self.context`) and reads the loaded configuration from `context.study`, an `expcore.study.config.StudyConfig`, beside what the request carries. All of it is read-only, and every field comes from the study's configuration files, whose hashes are in the campaign fingerprint, so nothing an executor reads there can change under a resumed campaign (C06).

| To get | Read | What it is |
|---|---|---|
| the run's levels | `request.identity.factors` | every factor of the run key: the declared ones and the built-in `task`, `model` and `seed` |
| the run's condition | `study.schema.condition_named(factors, request.run_key)` | the condition's name |
| a task's attributes | `study.tasks[task_id].attributes` | the text keys of `[tasks.<id>]` in `study.toml`; `study.tasks[task_id].id` is the id |
| the loop's caps | `study.campaign.agent.limits` | `max_tool_rounds`, `time_cap_s`, `token_budget`, `output_cap_chars` and `max_output_tokens` (`expcore.config.models.Limits`); `study.campaign.agent`, the whole loop configuration, goes to `record_run` |
| the loop's whole-time ceiling | `study.campaign.agent.ceiling_s` | the bound on a run's elapsed time, waits on infrastructure included, which ends the run as infrastructure of kind `ceiling` (L04). `[agent]` of `campaign.toml` sets it with `ceiling_s` (seconds) or `ceiling_factor` (a multiple of `time_cap_s`, at least 1), at most one of them and never below the cap; with neither it is 3 times the cap. It is in `study.campaign.caps()`, so in the fingerprint, and the record keeps the value in force. A platform that mints tokens for a run takes this value (`launch(..., ceiling_s=agent.ceiling_s)`, [below](#the-platform-layers-sandbox)) |
| the plan | `study.campaign.models`, `.seeds`, `.order_seed`; `study.manifest` | the models and seeds of the plan, its order seed, and every run in the seeded order |
| a model's entry | `study.models.resolve(key)` with the run's `factors["model"]`; `request.models` is the same table (`ModelTable \| None`) | a `ModelSpec`: backend, API id, limits, price, status; the run's backend itself is `request.backend` |
| a condition's settings | `study.schema.conditions[name]`, `.condition_tasks[name]`, `.treatment_kinds[name]` | its levels, its task list (`None`: every task) and its treatment kinds; the run's own kinds are `request.treatment_kinds`, passed on unchanged |
| the study's name, its own kinds, its distributions | `study.name`, `study.kinds`, `study.requires` | |
| the code identity | `context.code` | passed to `record_run` unchanged |

The rest has no place in a run: `study.prereg` is the analysis plan, and `study.root`, `study.plugin_file` and `study.scope` hold paths of the machine, which must reach neither a prompt nor a record (the plugin finds its own files from `__file__`, as the example's `HERE` does). A prompt filled per task takes its values from these fields, as the example's does with the task's `region` (a value that is not text is rendered with `str`):

```python
task, limits = study.tasks[factors["task"]], study.campaign.agent.limits
slots = {"TASK": task.id, "REGION": task.attributes["region"], "ROUNDS": limits.max_tool_rounds}
user = template.render(slots, study_run=True)  # for a template with a {ROUNDS} slot, which the example's has not
model = study.models.resolve(factors["model"])  # the run's ModelSpec, looked up by the model key of its run key
```

## PromptTemplate

`expcore.prompt.templates`. A template is text with named slots. The executor renders it for each run and passes the result to `record_run`, which records the text with the template's hash and the values of its slots.

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class PromptTemplate:
    text: str
    slot_names: tuple[str, ...]  # a property: the template's slots, sorted, each once
    sha256: str                  # a property: the SHA-256 of the template's text
    @classmethod
    def from_file(cls, path: Path, *, expected_sha256: str | None = None) -> PromptTemplate
    def render(self, values: Mapping[str, Any], *, study_run: bool = False) -> RenderedPrompt

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RenderedPrompt:
    text: str
    template_sha256: str
    rendered_sha256: str
    slots: dict[str, str]  # the values inserted, as text
```

- **Slots.** A slot is `{NAME}`: an opening brace, a capital letter, any capital letters, digits and underscores, and a closing brace. Every other brace is text and is sent as it is, so a JSON example in a prompt (`{"answer": "...", "sources": [{"id": 1}]}`), `{}` or `{lower}` needs no escape. The slot form itself has no escape: to show `{NAME}` to the model, give it as the value of a slot.
- **Values.** `render` needs a value for every slot, and refuses a value for a name the template does not have, or for a name that cannot be a slot. A value is made text with `str`, and one that is `None` or blank (empty, or only whitespace) is refused, so a condition with nothing to add in a slot still says something short (the example's `HINTS`). A value is inserted as it is and never searched for slots again.
- **`study_run=True`** refuses the draft marker `PLACEHOLDER`, in any case, in the template or in any value, so a draft prompt never reaches a model of a study run; it changes nothing else. The example's executor passes it. A pilot or a test that renders a draft leaves it off.
- **`from_file(path, expected_sha256=...)`** reads a template as UTF-8 and, given `expected_sha256`, refuses a file whose text has another hash, to pin the template to the hash a protocol froze.
- **Refusals** raise `PromptError`, a `ValueError` that names the slot. Raised in an executor, it makes the attempt a harness defect (C10).
- **The record.** Pass the `RenderedPrompt` itself to `record_run` as `system` or `user`: the record keeps its `text`, the text's `sha256`, `template_sha256` and `slots`. A plain string is recorded with neither a template hash nor slots. The hash a plugin gives for a condition (`StudyPlugin.prompts`) is the template rendered with what the condition decides and stand-ins for what each task fills in, as the example's `build` does; the tasks' values come from `study.toml`, whose hash the fingerprint holds.
- **Nesting.** To compose templates, render the inner one first and pass its `text` as the value of a slot of the outer one, as in `outer.render({"RULES": inner.render(rules).text, ...})`. A value is never searched again, so the inner text's braces stay as they are. **Known limit:** the record then holds the outer template's hash and the inner text as that slot's value, and neither the inner template's hash nor its slot values. A study that needs them pins the inner template with `expected_sha256`, or records its hash and slots itself, in an instrument's snapshot for instance.

## HealthCheck

`expcore.campaign.healthgate.HealthCheck`; `Health` is a named tuple `(ok: bool, detail: str, deferred: bool = False, charge: bool = True)`.

```python
class HealthCheck(Protocol):
    def __call__(self) -> Health

class ContextHealthCheck(Protocol):      # a check with a parameter named `context` is told the attempt it judges (O82)
    def __call__(self, *, context: CheckContext | None) -> Health

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class CheckContext:                      # `expcore.campaign.healthgate.CheckContext`, defined in `campaign/health_context.py`
    run_key: str
    attempt: int                         # the number the store gives the attempt (`RunIdentity.attempt`); a pre-run check's is the number its try will have
    phase: str                           # "pre_run" (`PRE_RUN`) or "post_run" (`POST_RUN`)
    attempt_id: int | None = None        # the store's id of the attempt's row, which `liveness.owner()` names; None before the row exists, so in a pre-run check

class DeferralBound(NamedTuple):   # what an optional `max_deferral_s()` of a check answers
    seconds: float                 # the platform's own time of the longest the check may stay deferred, with no pass in between
    detail: str                    # what defers it that long and how to shorten it, in the platform's words
    rechecks: int = 0              # waits between two of the core's checks the wait includes (recheck_s, poll_interval_s each)
    checks: int = 0                # checks' own time it includes beyond `seconds` (health_timeout_s each)
    platform: str = ""             # the platform module the bound comes from, as `[study] platforms` lists it ("": not said)


# the platform's run admission (`StudyPlugin.admission`) may also declare the same bound from its settings:
#     def declared_deferral_s(self) -> DeferralBound | None
# and so may the platform's own module, one `[study] platforms` lists, from the platform configuration the study points at:
#     def declared_deferral_from_config(path: Path) -> DeferralBound | None
```

Core guarantees: it is asked before an attempt starts and after it ends, and again at an interval while the gate is closed. A check that raises, or answers anything other than a `Health` (or an `(ok, detail)` pair with a real boolean), counts as a failed check, never as a pass or a crash. A failed pre-run check closes the gate, holds dispatch, and the run that found it is recorded as the outage's attempt, an infrastructure failure of `SchedulerConfig.health_kind` (C11): the `health_kind` key under `[scheduler]` in `campaign.toml`, `sandbox` unless the study names another, as [the study guide](study-guide.md#the-schedulers-kinds) shows. A failed post-run check turns an attempt that would have been scored into an infrastructure failure of that kind, or discards it as the open outage's. A deferred check (`Health(False, detail, deferred=True)`: the platform is held on purpose, by a run, and could not be looked at now) is a wait, never a pass or an outage: a deferred pre-run check holds its run for `recheck_s` without closing the gate or charging an attempt (event `run_deferred`), a deferred post-run check verified nothing, so any result `classify` would score, a treatment failure included, is set aside as `post_check_unverified`, a core kind no condition may declare (`expcore.campaign.attempts.unverified`) (event `post_run_check_deferred`), and only a passing check opens a closed gate. A deferral is bounded: its start is kept in the store's meta `health_deferral` (a restart keeps its age; `healthgate.fact` and `alarms` report it), a pass before or after a run ends it, and a pre-run check deferred longer than `SchedulerConfig.max_defer_s` (campaign.toml `[scheduler] max_defer_s`, default 3600) fails with the deferred detail, which names the holder's purpose and the target, closing the gate as a `blocking` outage: the all-dispatch hold is then not temporary, so `exit_when_blocked` stops the campaign. A platform whose own wait is bounded, and must not be cut off by that bound (a repair it runs between runs), declares the longest its check may stay deferred with an optional method `max_deferral_s()` answering a `DeferralBound` or None (`healthgate.deferral_bound`); the scheduler refuses, when it is built and before it takes the lock or opens the store, a campaign whose `max_defer_s` is shorter than `seconds` plus the core's waits the bound declares (`rechecks` x (`recheck_s` + `poll_interval_s`) and `checks` x `health_timeout_s`: `SchedulerConfig.deferral_needed`), with a `ConfigError` naming the campaign's figure, the platform's own seconds and detail, each of the core's waits with its value, the total and the fix (`SchedulerConfig.check_deferral_bound`; a total equal to `max_defer_s` starts, since only an age past it fails). It refuses whatever `exit_when_blocked` says. A check without the method, or one answering None, is held to nothing where the platform declares nothing, and an answer that is not None or a `DeferralBound` of a finite, non-negative number of seconds, a string and non-negative whole counts is refused. **A wrapper must pass `max_deferral_s` on.** A study's own check around the platform's (a closure, a class that calls it) hides the method, so the platform declares the same bound a second time, without the health object: its run admission (`StudyPlugin.admission`) may have a method `declared_deferral_s()` answering the same `DeferralBound` or None, read from the platform's settings when the scheduler is built (`expcore_onedata.admission.run_admission` answers it from `[faults]`, the figure `PlatformHealth.max_deferral_s` answers). With a health check and a platform that declares a deferral, the scheduler refuses the campaign when it is built, before it takes the lock or opens the store, if the check exposes no `max_deferral_s` ("the health check passed to the scheduler doesn't expose max_deferral_s, but the platform declares a deferral of N s; pass the method on"), answers None, or declares a smaller one than the platform's (`SchedulerConfig.check_declared_deferral`): each is compared as the `max_defer_s` it needs under the campaign's own waits, and the refusal names the platform's figure and words and what the check declares. A check that declares the same or more agrees, and is then held to `max_defer_s` as above. A platform that declares none (no `[faults]`, or `heal = "off"`), an admission without the method, and a scheduler with no health check are unaffected. A study that wraps the admission in a callable of its own hides `declared_deferral_s` the same way, so the platform declares the bound a third time, where no object of the study's plugin sits between its settings and the comparison: **through the platform registry, from the platform's own configuration** (C11, O35). A platform module that `[study] platforms` lists may expose a function `declared_deferral_from_config(path)` (`expcore.study.platform_deferral.PLATFORM_HOOK`), answering a `DeferralBound` or None for the platform configuration at `path`. When a campaign starts, before any study code runs, the core calls it for each listed platform that has it, with the file the study points at (`StudyConfig.platform_config`: `[study] platform_config`, a path inside the study directory, or without the key the `platform.toml` beside study.toml; the file's being there is checked only when a listed platform reads it, so a replication package that ships the study without its platform.toml still loads), validates the answer as it validates a check's (`healthgate.checked_bound`) and hands the answers to the scheduler (`Scheduler(platform_deferrals=...)`, a tuple of `healthgate.PlatformDeferral(platform, bound)`). The scheduler holds the health check to each exactly as above, before the lock and the store, with the same refusal naming the platform ("... but the platform 'expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds' (its configuration, read through [study] platforms) declares a deferral of N s; pass the method on"), whatever the admission declares or hides, and then still compares the admission's own declaration. **Listing the module is required whenever a platform with a deferral is used.** A bound names the module it comes from (`DeferralBound.platform`; the Onedata layer's is `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds`), and when the campaign starts the scheduler refuses one, declared by the run admission (`declared_deferral_s`) or by the health check (`max_deferral_s`), that names a module the study's `[study] platforms` does not list (`Scheduler(listed_platforms=...)`, which the pipeline passes; a scheduler built in code with no list reads none): "the platform's run admission declares a deferral of N s that comes from the platform module 'X', which [study] platforms does not list: list it". It is refused before the lock, the store and the comparisons above, with or without a health check, because a platform the study leaves out is never asked for its declaration, so a study that wraps both objects would hide it. A listed module speaks for the platforms its own answers name (a module of the study's that re-exports a platform's function answers bounds that name the platform's module, which is then covered); one that answers nothing covers nothing. A bound that names no module (a platform that does not say, a study's own check), one that waits for nothing, and a platform that declares none are unaffected. **The core also sees the platform built, so a plugin that hides both declarations and lists nothing is still refused.** The platform's own loaders record that they ran: `expcore.config.platform_loads.record_platform_load(module, path)`, which `expcore_onedata`'s `load_platform` and `load_faults` call at their entry with `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds` and the file (a load that then refuses its file still ran), and which a platform layer other than the Onedata one calls from its own loader with the module name its study lists in `[study] platforms` for it. The pipeline opens a record around the study's build, from its plugin's import to the scheduler's start (`watching_platform_loads`, empty at each build, so a second study in the process, or a test, inherits nothing), and passes it as `Scheduler(platform_loads=...)`; when the scheduler is built it refuses, before the lock and the store, a record of a module `[study] platforms` does not list (`check_loads_listed`): "the study's build loaded the platform configuration 'platform.toml' through the platform module 'X', which [study] platforms does not list: list it". A listed module covers its own platform and the platforms its function ran a loader for to answer (`PlatformDeferral.loaded`: a study's module that re-exports a platform's function and answers None for a platform with no heal still speaks for it); one whose function loads nothing covers nothing. A loader run outside a study build (a platform's own tooling: a day-0 tool, a federation script) records nothing and is never refused, a study that builds no platform is unaffected, and a scheduler built with no list checks none. The record holds the module and the path only, never a value, a secret or the file's directory in a refusal, and no record, fingerprint or state file depends on it. One study build is open in a process at a time (a second thread's build is refused at once with a RuntimeError, never made to wait, and the pipeline's watch ends before the scheduler starts, so no campaign holds it), so every loader that runs while a build is open, a worker thread of the plugin's included, belongs to that build, and a build that loads nothing is never refused for another's platform. It does not see a platform loaded after the study's build returned (a health check that loads its configuration at its first call), a `PlatformConfig` made by hand without the loader, or one that an installed helper outside the study's code loaded in an earlier build of the same process and still holds (the later build runs no loader). A platform without the function is not asked and declares nothing; a platform that answers None (no `[faults]`, `heal = "off"`) asks nothing of the check; a study that lists a platform with the function and points at no configuration is refused, naming `[study] platform_config`; a function that raises a `ConfigError` reaches the study as the platform worded it, one that fails in any other way is refused naming the platform and the exception's type and never its text, and an answer that is not None or a `DeferralBound`, or a function that is not callable, is refused naming `declared_deferral_from_config`. A scheduler built with no `platform_deferrals`, or with no health check, checks nothing of the registry's (nothing can defer without a health check, and a study run through `run_study` or `run_built_study` always passes what its listed platforms declare). The Onedata layer's module `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds` answers it from the platform.toml (`fault_deferral.declared_from_file`: `load_platform` with no secret read, `load_faults`, `heal_deferral_bound`), so the figure is that of the file's own backend and the file is held to every check the platform makes of it, the least `[tokens] margin_s` of its backend included. A study keeps no copy of the figure: a health check, an admission or a `max_defer_s` that disagrees with the platform.toml the study points at is refused or held to it. A gate already closed for another failure, whose own re-checks stay deferred past the bound, becomes blocking the same way. On a resume, a restored blocking outage holds dispatch without blocking it until the restored gate's first check has answered (bounded by `health_timeout_s`), so a platform that is healthy again reopens the campaign. A detail that leads with a reason only an operator can end (`healthgate.OPERATOR_ACTION`, "operator action needed", in its first clause, up to the first semicolon: `operator_reason(detail)`; the fault gate's after a failed heal does) is worded as that hold, never as a wait that ends by itself (C11): the status's alarm for the deferral (`deferral_alarms`) and for the closed gate (`alarms`) lead with the reason and say a check passes only once an operator has mended it, instead of a run holding the platform or a gate that resumes by itself; the hold the run says (`all`, `health_gate`), a deferral past `max_defer_s` or a closed gate, carries the reason after its own words, and `HealthGate.operator_reason` gives it; the export lists each such outage under `operator_holds` (`start_utc`, `end_utc`, `blocking`, `charged`, `checks`, `reason`; the key is there only when there is one, a merged export joins them with the shard that met each, and the reason is the latest an answer of the outage led with, which the gate keeps in the outage's record as `operator_reason`, apart from its details, since the check that passes overwrites `last_detail` and an ordinary failure can be followed by one), and `report.md` has a section "Holds that needed an operator" after the budget holds. Any other detail, one that mentions the words later included, reads exactly as before. A failure an earlier attempt left behind, and was charged for, charges no one (C33): a check answers `Health(False, detail, charge=False)`, and a pre-run check that fails so closes the gate as any failure does, holding every run until a check passes, but holds the run that found it too instead of charging it the outage's attempt; the outage's record has `"charged": false` and the status says no attempt was charged. A deferral may carry the flag as well, so one past `max_defer_s` closes the gate blocking without charging its finder. A post-run check that fails with `charge=False` sets its attempt aside as one an outage overlapped (`outage_discarded`, with `SchedulerConfig.health_kind` as its `outage_kind`, never charged; event `post_run_check_failed`), whatever the attempt's own outcome, since the failure is not its own; a platform answers `charge=True` for a failure the judged attempt left itself, which is judged as before. A check, like every callback of the attempt, runs in its caller's context: the post-run check inside its attempt, which `expcore.campaign.liveness.current()` names, and the pre-run check and the dispatcher's own outside any.

The attempt's liveness (`expcore.campaign.liveness`, C33). An attempt is live from its 'running' row until the store holds its result, finished or set aside: the scheduler registers its store when it opens (`liveness.register`) and takes it back as it closes, and the attempt runner enters `liveness.running(campaign, attempt_id)` around the executor, the post-run check and the store's record. A platform that takes a hold for a run writes `liveness.owner()`: `{"process", "campaign", "attempt_id"}` inside an attempt, `{"process"}` outside one, the process named as the lease pool names a holder (`lease.process_owner`: boot id, pid and start time); no run key, path or name. `liveness.state(token)` answers `ended` when the process is gone, or when it is this process and the store says the attempt is not running (or this process runs that campaign no more); `live` when the store says it runs, or the token names another process that still runs, whose store this process cannot read, or names no attempt and its process runs; None when the process's liveness cannot be read. Since this process cannot read another's store, a hold any process must be able to judge also takes a lock of its own and asks `liveness.on_end(callback)` inside the attempt: the callback runs as the attempt ends, last given first, one that raises logged and the others still run; outside an attempt `on_end` keeps nothing and returns False.

**A failure an earlier attempt left charges no run that meets it** (`Health.charge`, O45, O80). `Health(False, detail, charge=False)` closes the gate and holds every run without charging any, the one that found it included; the default is `True`, and the core does not change it. A check that reads a marker an attempt leaves behind must therefore answer `charge=False` for a marker an earlier attempt left, and `True` only for the one the judged attempt left itself: compare the marker's owner (`liveness.owner()`, written when the marker was) with `liveness.current()`, the attempt this context runs, which is None in a pre-run check and the dispatcher's own, so those never charge for a marker (`ingesthealth._owned_here`, `fault_owner.charge_of`). A marker that records no owner cannot be told apart, so the marker should record one: the core's own checks charge such a marker as before, and say so, rather than guess. The core's own checks do this: the baseline marks charge only their owner, and the fault journal's `injecting` line carries `owner` (an optional field of the journal line, absent from lines written before it, which are charged as before) with the verdicts of `SettledInjections` and the fault gate charging by it.

**A check can be told which attempt it judges** (O82; `expcore.campaign.health_context`). The gate runs the pre-run checks of some runs, the post-run checks of others and its own in the one shared check at the same moment, so a check that decides whom a failure is charged to cannot tell whose check it is in from where it is called. The gate hands it the attempt as an argument, a frozen `CheckContext(run_key, attempt, phase, attempt_id)`, passed down each call and never kept on the gate:

- **One rule decides whether a check receives it:** its call signature has a parameter named `context` (positional-or-keyword or keyword-only, with a default or not), and the gate calls it `check(context=ctx)`. Every other check is called with no argument, exactly as before: a check with `*args` or `**kwargs`, one with a parameter of another name even when it has a default (`timeout=30`), a mock, and a callable whose signature cannot be read. Only the name opts in, so no check that works today is handed something it does not expect. The signature is the callable's own, read once when the gate is built, and not that of a function a `functools.wraps` wrapper wraps: **a decorated check opts in through its wrapper's signature**, and a wrapper that takes no argument is called with none, whatever the function it wraps declares (a `__signature__` attribute is honoured, as `inspect.signature` does, and `functools.wraps` copies one).
- **An ambiguous signature is refused when the scheduler is built** (`ConfigError`, before the lock and the store, naming the parameter; `healthgate.takes_context` is the same rule for a study's own wrapper to ask): a `context` that cannot be passed by keyword (positional-only, `*context`, `**context`), and a required parameter the gate would never fill (`def check(ctx)`, `def check(context, other)`). Such a check used to read as a failed check at every call (`health check raised TypeError`) and is now refused at the start; a check with no required parameter is unaffected.
- **The fields.** `attempt` is the number the store gives the attempt (`BegunAttempt.number`, the `RunIdentity`'s `attempt`): one more than the run's charged attempts when it starts, so a pre-run check is given the number its try will have if it goes, and the attempt that follows is the same number. An attempt set aside uncharged, or interrupted, is run again under the number it had, so **two attempts of one run can share a number**: `attempt_id`, the store's id of the attempt's row (the `attempt_id` of `liveness.owner()`), tells them apart. It is set in a post-run check, which judges a stored attempt, and None in a pre-run check, which runs before the row exists. `phase` is `PRE_RUN` (`"pre_run"`) or `POST_RUN` (`"post_run"`).
- **None is the context of a check that judges no attempt**: the dispatcher's own check (the gate re-checking while it is closed), and a call of `HealthGate.before_run` or `after_run` that names no attempt (a caller outside the scheduler; the scheduler always names it). A check that takes the context takes the None, and answers for no attempt.
- **A wrapper passes it on.** A study's check around another that takes the context declares `context` too and calls `inner(context=context)`. The core's own wrappers of the platform's checks (`PreCampaignHealth`, `FaultGate`, `SettledInjections`) call their inner check with no argument, so a check that takes the context goes outermost, as the study's check around the platform's does.

An owner-based check, a marker that charges only the attempt that left it, then needs no stack inspection. The executor (or the platform tool that leaves the marker) writes `liveness.owner()` into it, and the check compares the owner with the context:

```python
from expcore.campaign.healthgate import POST_RUN, CheckContext, Health, HealthCheck


class MarkedHealth:
    """The platform's health, and a marker an attempt may leave; charges the attempt that left it, and no other."""

    def __init__(self, platform: HealthCheck, read_marker) -> None:
        self.platform, self.read_marker = platform, read_marker

    def __call__(self, context: CheckContext | None) -> Health:
        health = self.platform()
        left = self.read_marker()  # None, or what the attempt wrote: {"owner": liveness.owner(), ...}
        if not health.ok or left is None:
            return health
        mine = (
            context is not None and context.phase == POST_RUN and left["owner"].get("attempt_id") == context.attempt_id
        )
        # only the post-run check of the attempt that left it is charged; a pre-run check or the dispatcher's own
        # (context None) that meets the marker holds every run without charging the one that found it
        return Health(False, "a marker the study leaves is open", charge=mine)
```

A pre-run check knows the run and the number of the attempt it precedes (`run_key`, `attempt`), never an id; the attempt's own post-run check is told apart from every other by `attempt_id`, not by the number, which a retried run reuses.

Implementer must guarantee: it is quick (bounded by `health_timeout_s`), side-effect free, and `detail` holds no secret. `health_kind` must not be a kind any condition declares as its treatment, and, like `identity_kind` (the kind of a run whose endpoint changed its served identity, C16, `endpoint` by default), it must be a kind known when the study is loaded, or the load is refused. Each infrastructure kind is treatable or not: the core's `tool_plane_integrity`, `budget_hold` and `post_check_unverified`, any kind a platform registers with `treatable=False` (the Onedata layer's `data_gate_integrity`, `output_collection`, `injector_failure`, `ingest_failure` and `ingest_held`) and any kind a study declares with `treatable = false` cannot be declared in `infrastructure_is_treatment`, and the factor schema refuses to load a condition that does, naming the file and the key; the scheduler refuses, at its start, a campaign whose health or identity kind a condition declares.

## ResourceProbe

`expcore.campaign.lease.ResourceProbe`. Both questions must fail safe.

```python
class ResourceProbe(Protocol):
    def holder_gone(self, owner: str) -> bool | None
    def resource_gone(self, key: str, note: Mapping[str, Any]) -> bool
```

Core guarantees: a lease is an exclusive file lock per key, dropped by the kernel when its holder dies; a key whose last holder did not release it goes to nobody until `resource_gone` answers `True` for it (C19). `holder_gone` returning `None` or raising is treated like gone; `resource_gone` frees a key only by returning exactly `True` (an identity test: `1`, `"unknown"` or any other truthy value does not count), on every path: at the end of a run, when a quarantine is settled and for a dead holder's key. Anything else, or raising, keeps the key quarantined and counted against its unit's cap.

Implementer must guarantee: `True` from `resource_gone` means everything the holder started with that key (its sandbox, processes, mounts) is shown gone, never "probably". `lease_group` and `lease_keys` in the plugin name the keys; a key is letters, digits, dot, dash and underscore.

## The platform layer's sandbox

`expcore_onedata.sandbox`. Nothing in `expcore` knows it; a study reaches it through the platform layer's objects, which take their names from `platform.toml` (`[sandbox]`, loaded with the rest of the file by `load_platform`).

```python
class Sandbox(Protocol):            # api.py
    def exec(self, code: str, timeout_s: float, *, record: bool = True) -> ExecResult  # record=False: a harness step
    def collect(self, dest_dir: str | Path) -> dict[str, Any]
    def stop(self) -> None
    def metadata(self) -> dict[str, Any]

class ExecResult:                   # api.py: what `exec` returns
    stdout: str; stderr: str; exit_code: int; duration_s: float
    timed_out: bool                 # a bound of the sandbox's own ended the cell, `record` or not (O17, O39)
    bound: str | None               # which: "cell_timer", "container_timeout" or "host_call"; None exactly when not timed_out
    kill: dict | None               # the cause of a kill, from the kernel's counters; gate_refusals: tuple[str, ...]
    status: str                     # "ran", or "not_started" for a cell too little time was left to start

class InfraReadings(Protocol):      # api.py: a run's infrastructure as a study reads it, on every backend (O17, O18)
    def infra_check(self) -> dict      # {"containers": {role: {start, end, problems}}, "deaths": [...], "death": [...]}
    def mount_log(self) -> dict | None     # {"text": oneclient's INFO log, the token as <token>} or {"unavailable": ...}
    def mount_errors(self) -> dict | None  # oneclient_log.summarize of that text, or the same {"unavailable": ...}

class DockerSandbox:                # sandbox.py; an InfraReadings
    @classmethod
    def start(cls, host: HostOps, settings: SandboxSettings, spec: SandboxSpec, **options) -> DockerSandbox
    def collect(self, dest_dir, outputs: Sequence[str] = ()) -> dict      # outputs: files of /work to copy
    def served_bytes(self) -> dict                                        # what ServedBytes records (O22)
    def mark_run_start(self) -> dict      # where the agent's run begins, before its loop (O24)
    def infra_container_check(self) -> dict    # deprecated: infra_check(), the same answer, with a DeprecationWarning
    def sidecar_errors(self) -> dict | None    # deprecated: mount_errors(), likewise

class HostOps(Protocol):            # hostops.py: every effect on the host, so a test supplies a fake
    def docker(self, *args, check=True, timeout=None, env=None, input=None,
               output_cap: int | None = None,                    # output_cap: bytes kept of each stream (O43)
               marker: bytes | None = None) -> CmdResult         # marker: lines taken out of both streams (O55)
    def run(self, argv, timeout_s, *, sudo=False, input=None) -> CmdResult

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class CmdResult:                    # returncode, stdout, stderr, and, under an output cap, stdout_count, stderr_count;
                                    # with a marker, stdout_marks, stderr_marks (O55); input_error (O55)
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Counted:                      # produced: int, kept: int (a stream's bytes written, and kept); .cut
    def read_text(self, path) -> str      # also readlink, exists, process_alive, make_quota_dir, make_ca,
    ...                                   # unmount, image_id, host_facts

class SandboxPlatform:              # provider.py
    def __init__(self, config, issuer, host, *, events: EventSink, slots: Slots | None = None,
                 journal: TokenJournal | None = None,                   # both or neither: neither is a lease-less platform (O38)
                 tool_condition=lambda factors: None, tool_server: ToolServerLauncher | None = None, ...)
    def mode(self, factors) -> DataPathMode
    def lease_group(self, factors) -> str | None                     # its condition's group, users.GROUP, or None
    def condition_group(self, condition: str) -> str                 # users.group_of(its rights); ConfigError if none
    def lease_keys(self, group: str) -> Sequence[str]                # the names of the group's [slots]
    def probe(self) -> SlotProbe                                     # the ResourceProbe (see below)
    def sandbox_probe(self) -> SandboxProbe                          # its sandbox half
    def launch(self, ctx, *, run_key, level, ceiling_s, lease=None, condition=None, inputs=(), inputs_record=None)
    def tool_server(self, ctx, sandbox, *, ceiling_s=None, extra_env=None)  # yields the run's McpToolProvider
                                    # where the sandbox names it (tool_server_process()), the launcher gets `process`

class ReadOnlyInput:                # api.py: files a study supplies, read-only in the agent (O20)
    source: Path                    # a directory on the host: regular files and directories only
    target: str                     # the absolute path the agent sees it at
    sha256: str | None = None       # its content's hash when it was declared (inputs.declare)

class CodeExecutionProvider:        # codetool.py: a ToolProvider with one tool that runs Python
    def __init__(self, sandbox: Sandbox, *, name="python", description=...)
```

**Data paths.** The study's data-path factor, `data_path` unless `[study] data_path_factor` names another (O75), names its levels in its own words; `[sandbox.data_paths]` maps each to one of three kinds, `mount`, `rest` or `none`, written as the kind alone (`level = "rest"`) or as a table that also names a REST level's option (`level = { kind = "rest", listing = "complete" }`). A study selects a data path and its option per condition, through the level the condition gives its data-path factor; `SandboxPlatform(..., data_path_factor=...)` is the name `mode` reads it by (`SandboxPlatform.data_path_factor`, `data_path` by default), which a study passes from `StudyConfig.data_path_factor`, and hands back to the core as `StudyPlugin.data_path_factor` (`platform.data_path_factor`): **a campaign refuses to start when the two names differ** (`expcore.study.plugin.check_data_path_factor`, at the study's build, before any run and not in analysis, which builds no platform), a `ConfigError` naming `study.toml`, `[study]`, the key and both names, so a platform built without the name its study declares, or with another, is never left to read the default; a plugin that says nothing is the default `data_path`, so a study with no key and a platform at the default starts as it always did. A `mount` run has a read-only FUSE mount made by a oneclient sidecar that holds the data-path token; the agent holds no token and no FUSE. The sidecar takes the token from a file bound into it alone, read-only, never from docker's configuration of the container, so `docker inspect` does not show it, and the host's copy is removed once the mount is up and again at teardown. A `rest` run reaches the zone and its pinned local provider only through the data gate, which holds the token and adds it to the requests it forwards (a token the agent sends is dropped); the agent holds no token in its environment, its files or its process arguments, only the placeholder described below. The gate, like the sidecar, takes the token from a file bound into it alone, read-only, never from docker's configuration of its container. A `none` run has neither.

**The mount's I/O mode (O36).** `[sandbox.mount] io_mode` is required, `"proxy"` or `"direct"`, with no default: a mount table without it, or with another word, is refused at load, and `extra_args` may carry neither force option (nor an abbreviation of one). The sidecar's oneclient is always started with the matching option, `--force-proxy-io` or `--force-direct-io`, so the storage's access type is never left to oneclient's own detection. oneclient 25.0 (tag `bb8a63e3`) started with neither option detects it at the first read of each space (`src/cache/helpersCache.h`, `performAutoIOStorageDetection`, lines 463-615), and for a second space on a storage already detected as proxied it takes `m_accessTypeMutex`, a plain `std::mutex` (line 265), and calls itself again while still holding it (lines 596-599); the call locks the mutex a second time on the same thread (line 478). The helper's promise is never fulfilled, no `SynchronizeBlock` is sent, and the read hangs with nothing logged. A study saw it live on 13 Nov: the first space a oneclient process read worked, and the first read of any other space hung. With `--force-proxy-io`, `get()` sets `forceProxyIO` up front (line 427) and detection never enters that branch. `"direct"` takes `performForcedDirectIOStorageDetection` (lines 619-691) instead, which has no such pattern: it locks the mutex once, in a block of its own that ends before anything else is called (lines 626-629), and the helpers it calls lock it only in scopes that call nothing else (`requestStorageTestFileCreation`, lines 721-722; `handleStorageTestFile`, lines 757 and 784; `handleStorageTestFileVerification`, line 847). It reads the local provider's storage itself, so it loads only with that storage mapped into the sidecar and a check file the start reads ("A direct mount (O40)", below).
