A platform file whose mount table had no `io_mode`, which before this release left the detection to oneclient, must now declare `"proxy"`. `"proxy"` is the mode that works in a sandbox as it is. Each run's metadata records the effective mode (`mount.io_mode`), the options the sandbox itself passed (`mount.options`, for example `["-o", "ro,allow_other", "--force-proxy-io", "--log-dir", "/var/log/oneclient", "--config", "/dev/null", "--disable-log-buffering"]`) and the study's `extra_args` by the names of the options oneclient has, with `<value>` for any other word that starts with a dash (it may be a value), and the SHA-256 of the whole list (`mount.extra_args`), never a value. The settings are checked where they are built (`MountSettings`), where the file is loaded and where the command is made: an `io_mode` that is not `"proxy"` or `"direct"`, and an extra argument that is, or abbreviates, a force-I/O option, the TLS flag, `--config`, the log directory's option (below) or `--disable-log-buffering` (O54, below), or that is an `ONECLIENT_...` assignment, raise (one check, `oneclient_args.extra_arg_problem`), so settings built in code never give oneclient two force options or a second configuration. The I/O mode is in the campaign fingerprint, for each mount level, as a REST level's listing mode is (`PlatformConfig.fingerprint`, from `data_path_record`: `{"kind": "mount", "io_mode": "proxy"}`): a resume under another mode is refused, naming `platform.data_paths.<level>.io_mode`. So is the resume of a store started before this release, whose mount levels recorded no mode: the leaf is "absent when the campaign started", the store ran under oneclient's own detection, and no declared mode stands for that. Start a new store; the old runs keep their records, whose `mount.io_mode` is null. `extra_args` is not in the fingerprint, but the platform file's SHA-256 (`platform_config_sha256`) changes with it, and the metadata names the options. `mount.io_mode` is the declared mode; the mode oneclient's own log shows it ran in is read when the sandbox collects and kept as `mount.effective` (`declared`, `effective`, `agree`, the start values, the contradicting lines by name and the problems), and `MountIoMode`, the platform's fourth instrument ([above](#instrument), O49), puts it in the run's record and refuses a run whose two modes differ.

**A direct mount (O40).** `io_mode = "direct"` loads only with two more keys of `[sandbox.mount]`, and a `"proxy"` mount is refused if it has either. `[sandbox.mount.storages]` maps each storage the sidecar reads directly, by its storage id (letters, digits, `.`, `_` and `-`: oneclient splits an override at every `:`), to the directory that holds it on the host (one absolute, normalised path, not the root, with no comma, quote or control character, since it is bound with `--mount`); no two may nest, and none may be, hold or lie under `[sandbox] work_root`. `direct_check_file` is a file the local provider holds in full, as a path under the mount: its space's directory, then the path in the space. A message names a storage by its position, never by its id or path. The sidecar's oneclient then gets `--force-direct-io`, `--disable-log-buffering` and one `--override <storage id>:mountPoint:/mnt/expcore-storage/<n>` per storage, `n` its position, and each host directory is a `--mount type=bind,...,readonly` into the sidecar alone at that path, so the POSIX helper reads the storage there (oneclient 25.0, `performForcedDirectIOStorageDetection`, `helpersCache.h` lines 666-683) and never scans the sidecar's mounts for it. The agent's container never gets a storage, in any argument, and a read-only input whose source is, holds or lies under one is refused by its position. `extra_args` may not carry `--override` or `-r` in either mode, nor `--disable-log-buffering` in either mode (O54). A start checks that each host directory is there before anything exists. Once the agent's container runs, the start shows direct I/O in effect or refuses the sandbox (`SandboxError`, "direct I/O is not in effect for the mount", with each reason and no id or path; `directio.py`). The agent reads the check file's size and its `org.onedata.storage_id` and `org.onedata.replication_progress` attributes through the mount; only when the local provider holds all of it (`100%`) does it read the whole file, in a child process killed after 60 s, so the check never places a block. Then the sidecar's oneclient INFO log is read in the sidecar. The start is refused unless the file is not empty, its storage is one of the mapped ones, every byte was read, the start lines say `Forced direct IO: 1` and `Forced proxy IO: 0`, and the log holds no line of automatic detection, of a fallback to the provider, of a storage with no direct helper or no override, of an override's path under no mount, or of a missing helper. A read that returned every byte is not enough: oneclient started with `--force-direct-io` still reads a file through the provider when its direct read fails with EPERM or EACCES (`fsLogic.cc`, `readInternal`, lines 1176-1203), logging only the ERROR line, and forced detection marks the storage's access type `direct` before it has tried anything, so that attribute is no evidence either. `--disable-log-buffering` makes the INFO lines the check reads land as they are logged (glog holds them up to 30 s otherwise). Each run's metadata records `mount.direct`: `storages`, the count, and `check`: the two start lines' values, the file's `size`, the bytes `read`, the `seconds`, the share `held`, the check file's `storage` by its position in the table, and the names of the `lines` found. A file of the study's data whose direct read is refused later in the run falls back the same way, after the check passed, so the collection reads the log again (`DockerSandbox.direct_io_lines`, which a harness may call too): a line of these since the start, or a log that cannot be read, sets the run aside (`InfraError` of kind `output_collection`), and `mount.direct.during_run` holds the names of the lines found. The fingerprint holds the I/O mode, never a storage id or path, which the platform file's hash covers.

What direct I/O changes for provider-side measurements: the provider no longer serves the reads of data its storage holds, so a measurement of the provider's own I/O, its proxied reads, or an audit of them, loses those reads. The client still reports each read to the provider as a file-read event unless `--disable-read-events` is given (`fsLogic.cc`, lines 1095-1099), and those events are the client's report, not reads the provider served. A block the local provider lacks is still fetched through it: oneclient sends `SynchronizeBlock`, the provider places the block on its storage (the same 1 MiB minimum), and the client then reads it from the storage, so block maps and transfers record what is fetched as they do under proxy. The data gate's byte counting is the REST path's and is unaffected. A study validated under proxy keeps proxy unless direct I/O gives the same placed bytes per read sequence: `deploy/compose-federation/measure_direct_vs_proxy.py` replays the same read sequences on the same files under both modes, each pass from the same starting state (the files evicted from the local provider, a fresh oneclient), and compares the local provider's gains read by read and the ranges placed at the end, a file being the same only when both agree; a direct pass whose sidecar log shows a read through the provider after its reads makes its files incomplete, every file is compared in every round even when both passes failed, and the verdict is yes only when every file compared and none differed, and `--control` adds a proxy-against-proxy pass for the noise floor. Direct I/O is not live-verified until its comparison run passes: until a live run of `measure_direct_vs_proxy.py` gives a verdict of yes, `"direct"` is unverified.

What the mount leaves to oneclient's defaults, and why none of it is a detection: every other option of oneclient 25.0's client takes a constant at start (`src/options/options.h`, lines 40-68) or the switch's off state, whatever the host: the buffer sizes and prefetch thresholds and mode (`async`), the metadata cache (5,000,000 entries), `readdir` prefetch (2,500), the thread counts (1, 25, 1 and 10), the provider and storage timeouts (120 s each), the directory cache's 300 s, the buffered I/O and extended attributes (both on), file read events (on), `--emulate-available-space` (0) and monitoring (none); the table in `deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-11-federation-facts-mount.md` lists the values `oneclient --help` printed. `std::thread::hardware_concurrency()` is read only for the OneS3 server's threads, never by the client. A change to any of them is the study's, through `extra_args`, and shows in the run's metadata by name. Two inputs outside the argv are closed (O36). The sidecar passes `--config /dev/null`, so the image's own configuration file is never parsed: oneclient's parser opens the path it is given and reads options from the stream (`optionsParser.cc`, `parseConfigFile`), `/dev/null` opens and reads as empty, and a path that did not open would give no options either, since the oneclient binary checks for a missing file nowhere (only OneS3 warns, `Options::parse`), so there is no spelling of the option that fails; an option the parser did not know would end the start with its message, which the start-up check reports as the sidecar's exit. And `env -u` removes `ONECLIENT_INSECURE` (the image's own flag), `ONECLIENT_OVERRIDE` and `ONECLIENT_CONFIG`. `ONECLIENT_ACCESS_TOKEN` stays: the token arrives that way (`--ignore-env` would drop it too). The command line is stored first, so it wins over the environment and the file, which fill only what it leaves unset. The effective options are the argv and the token, and the gated live test (`tests/live/test_mount_spaces.py`, `mount_effective.py`) checks it: each option oneclient logs at its start (`src/logging.cc`, `startLogging`: the force switches, the certificate check, the provider, buffering, monitoring, the mount point) must say what the argv sets or the client's own default, and the only variable of the sidecar's environment that oneclient reads an option from (the `withEnvName` set of `options.cc`) must be the token's. A variable of the image that sets another option is not removed by `env -u`, which names three; the check fails on it. The image's own configuration file is recorded though never parsed: `mount.image_config` holds its path in the image and its SHA-256, or `unread` with the path when the sidecar could not hash it and with none when the binary named none. The sidecar's entrypoint reads it before oneclient starts: it asks the binary where its default file is (`oneclient --help` prints it after `--config`; the 25.0 source builds the default of the oneclient binary from `ones3.conf` and OneS3's from `oneclient.conf`, `options.cc` lines 30-39, which look swapped, so the path is read from the binary and never assumed), hashes it with `sha256sum` and prints one line, which the sandbox reads from the container's output. No container is created for it and no file is copied out of the image.

**The REST agent's environment and trust (O23).** The agent's environment is what the published client, `onedatarestfsspec`, reads: `ONEDATA_ONEZONE_HOST` (the zone's host name, which resolves to the gate, on port 443), `ONEDATA_VERIFY_SSL=true`, the CA variables `SSL_CERT_FILE`, `REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE` and `CURL_CA_BUNDLE` (the run's CA, mounted read-only), and `ONEDATA_TOKEN`, set to `[sandbox] placeholder_token` (`placeholder` by default; 1 to 128 printable ASCII characters without white space). The placeholder is no credential: the client refuses to start without a token, and the gate drops it as it drops any token the agent sends, from every header in which the platform reads one (`X-Auth-Token`, `Authorization`, the deprecated `Macaroon`, `X-Onedata-Consumer-Token`, `X-Onedata-Service-Token`) and from a scope inference's body, where the gate puts its own token; the inference then goes to the zone with no token in any header, since Onezone serves it to anyone but authenticates a header's token first, and a data-path token's data-access caveats fail that with 401 (O30, live 13 Nov); the platform reads no token from a query string, and a request whose header block folds a line (one that starts with a space or a tab) or holds a CR or LF inside one is answered 400 and forwarded nowhere. A placeholder equal to the run's token refuses the start. The client verifies TLS against the certifi bundle of the agent's image and reads none of the CA variables, so the start asks the image's interpreter (`[sandbox] python`), in a one-shot container with no network and as the agent's user, where certifi keeps its bundle, and treats that path as one more `trust_paths` entry: the image's bundle with the run's CA appended is mounted over it read-only. `metadata()["certifi"]` is `run_ca_added`, `not_in_image` when the image has no certifi (the start goes on, and certifi-based clients cannot verify the gate), or None without a REST data path; an interpreter that fails, or an answer that is not one absolute path the sandbox can mount over, refuses the start. A client that verifies against another bundle needs that bundle's path in `trust_paths`. The data path is read-only in every condition at four points: the token (4a: read-only, scoped to the space, one interface), the gate (only the five read calls pass), the mount, and the agent's root filesystem. `start` proves the last two by trying to write from inside the agent and requiring the refusal.

**A REST data path's listing (O21).** `listing = "paged"`, the default, passes the server's page of a listing as it comes, as the official client's `ls` reads it: a directory of more than 1000 entries shows its first 1000. `listing = "complete"` has the gate answer every listing with all of the directory's entries, in the server's order, as one page that is the last (`isLast` true, `nextPageToken` null). The gate follows the pages upstream itself, with its own page size, and the agent's other options go with every page. It goes on while a page says it is not the last, however short. For a directory that does not change while it is read (the known limit below), it never passes a part of a listing as if it were the whole:
- a page after the first that is not a 200, or any page that neither ends nor continues the listing (no boolean `isLast`, `isLast` false without a token, a token given before, not an object with a list of children, not JSON with each key once), or that gives an entry the listing gave before (by its file id, else its name), is the platform's 502 (`error_origin: upstream`); the first page's own error, a 404 for a directory that does not exist say, is passed on as the paged mode passes it;
- a listing whose merged answer, with every continuation token it gave, would pass 64 MiB, or that needs more than 10,000 pages (`gatepolicy.COMPLETE_LISTING_BYTES`, `COMPLETE_LISTING_PAGES`), is refused for the gate's integrity (`gate_refusal: listing_too_large`, with `listing_bound` on the line), so the run ends as `data_gate_integrity` and is set aside. Of each token and entry the gate keeps only a 16-byte digest, to find a repeat;
- a listing that names where it starts (`token`, `index` or `offset`, in the query or the body) is refused with a 400 the agent reads, a policy refusal; its `limit` and `tuneForLargeContinuousListing` are dropped, the gate's to set;
- every page passes the response boundary as any answer that is no file's content does, and the merged answer is checked once more before any byte of it goes.

A known limit: Onedata gives a listing no snapshot, so a complete listing is complete only for a directory that does not change while the gate reads it, as a study's data should not. An entry another client adds meanwhile shifts the ones after it, one of them comes twice, and the listing is refused; an entry removed meanwhile can shift another out of the pages unseen, and the answer then lacks it with nothing to show. Entries that carry neither a file id nor a name (a listing that asks for neither attribute) cannot be told apart, so a repeat among them is not found.

The option is refused on a mount or no data path, where no gate lists, and a mode the gate does not know refuses the load, the start and the gate itself. `metadata()["listing"]` records it (None without a gate), and `PlatformConfig.fingerprint()` holds every level's kind and listing mode (`data_paths`), so a campaign is refused a resume after a level changed either: two modes are never one experiment.

**The gate's image.** `[sandbox.images.gate]` names the image the data gate runs in, by `name` and the `id` it is pinned to, like every image of the sandbox (O13); without it the gate runs in the agent's image (`[sandbox.images.agent]`). The gate's code is not in the image: `gate.py` and its two siblings are mounted read-only from the installed package at `/opt/gate` and started as `python /opt/gate/gate.py /etc/gate/gate.json`, the entrypoint replaced. So the image must hold:

- an executable named `python` on its `PATH` (the entrypoint is that word, not `[sandbox] python`, and not `python3`), a CPython of at least 3.12, the oldest the core supports (K08);
- its standard library, with `ssl` built against OpenSSL, and nothing more: the gate imports no third-party package (`tests/onedata/sandbox/test_gate.py` checks it), so no `pip install` is needed;
- with the platform's default `[tls]`, no `ca_file`, a system CA store that the federation's certificate chains to; with `ca_file` the gate trusts the run's copy of that bundle alone, and with `insecure_tls` it checks nothing;
- nothing that must write to its root filesystem or run as another user: the gate runs as root with every capability but `NET_BIND_SERVICE` dropped, on a read-only root, writing only its log directory, which is mounted in.

`python:3.12-slim` meets all four, and is what the live tests run. In an image without `python` on its `PATH` (only `python3`, or a distroless image) the gate's `docker run` fails; in one whose interpreter lacks `ssl` the gate exits before it says it is ready; an interpreter older than 3.12 is not supported. Either way the start raises `SandboxError`. An agent image slimmed for the agent is the usual way to meet this by accident, so a study whose agent image is not a full Python 3.12 or later names a gate image of its own. The core publishes no gate image: a study pins one by id, one for all its conditions.

**One pool of leased users (C19).** A run that needs a data-path token or a tool server leases one slot of `[slots.<name>]` (`users.py`), and one that needs neither takes none (`lease_group` is None). The slot gives the run both planes' users: `launch` mints the run's tokens through `users.SlotTokens` at once, with one journalled expiry, the data path's on the slot's data-path user and, when `condition` names the run's tool-server condition, the tool server's on its tool-server user; `tool_server` then starts the server with that token, and the launcher refuses a token whose subject is not that slot user. `launch`'s `ceiling_s` is the run's whole-time ceiling, the loop's `AgentConfig.ceiling_s`, which a study may set in `[agent]` (O06): the tokens are minted to live that long plus the margins of `[tokens]` (`ceiling_s + margin_s + mint_headroom_s`, 900 s and 300 s by default; with `[faults]`, the declared `margin_s`, at least `fault_settings.restore_token_margin_s`, added after whatever ceiling, so a lower ceiling shortens the tokens and never the margin), the sandbox's metadata records each as `data_path_token` or `tool_server_token` with `slot`, `expires_at`, `ttl_s` (the lifetime it was minted for) and `ceiling_s`, and every line a mint writes to the journal carries `ttl_s` and `ceiling_s`, beside its `expires_at` (an indeterminate line, of a dropped token whose expiry is unknown, has none). `tool_server` re-examines its token against a ceiling at its start and after its stop, which has to be the one the token was minted for: `ceiling_s` is left out and defaults to it, and another value raises `ValueError` before anything starts. A run with a tool server and no lease is refused; a run without a lease and with a data path (a live check) mints as the fixed `[principals.data_path]` user. The platform's `ResourceProbe` is `SlotProbe(journal, sandbox_gone=SandboxProbe(...).resource_gone)`: a key is freed only when the sandbox is proven gone and the slot's tokens have ended, by their journalled expiry plus a margin (60 s) or by a revocation that is confirmed and settled. A launch that held a lease revokes the slot's tokens on its way out, inside the stop's wait (`users.SlotTokens.revoke`): each slot user's own revocation of its temporary tokens, the rise of each generation and `tokenRevoked` for every token the run held are required before the journal records the settle end, `[tokens] revocation_settle_s` (330 s) later; a settle shorter than 315 s, the propagation bound (a provider's cache purge 300 s after a lost connection to Onezone, plus an open session's 15 s recheck), is refused in the file and when passed to `SlotTokens.revoke` directly. The journal records the revocation's start line before the first bump, and the settle end caps only the slot's lines before that line, so a mint journalled after it, which may hold a token of the new generation, keeps its own end. So the lease's hold is the run, its teardown and about 330 s, plus up to the pool's 60 s between looks at a quarantine; without a confirmed revocation (it failed, the run's tool server had not stopped, or a mint or start thread of the key still ran) it is the tokens' whole lifetime plus 60 s, the run's ceiling plus 1,260 s with the default `[tokens]`. A slot held from before is revoked by `python -m expcore_onedata.lease revoke --platform platform.toml --leases DIR --slot NAME`, which holds the slot's lease lock from before its first check to after its revoked line, so no pool can lease, reap or free the slot and no mint of it can run meanwhile. It refuses a slot whose lock any live process holds, a run under way or a running campaign that keeps it quarantined (a pool frees a quarantine by letting the lock go, at a moment the command cannot know), so stop that campaign first; it also refuses a slot whose sandbox is not shown gone. A `[sandbox.users]` table, the second pool of earlier versions, is refused at load with a message that points to `[slots]`.

**A platform with no leases (O38).** `slots` and `journal` (the token-expiry journal beside the leases, `users.TokenJournal`; not the fault injector's) are optional, for a run that takes no lease, a one-off check of the data path say: build `SandboxPlatform(config, issuer, host, events=...)` and nothing else needs inventing. Such a platform leases nothing and never uses a slot's users or tokens: a run with a data path mints its token as the fixed `[principals.data_path]` user, as a run launched with `lease=None` on a platform with slots always did, records the user's place as `principals.data_path` and journals and revokes nothing; a run with no data path mints nothing. `lease_group` is `None` for every run that needs no tool server. What needs a slot or the journal is refused with a `ConfigError` that names what is missing (`without `slots` and `journal` (a lease-less platform), so it cannot ...`), before anything is minted or created: `lease_keys`, `condition_group`, `probe`, a `lease_group` of a run with a tool-server condition (so the scheduler refuses such a plan when it is built), and a `launch` with a `lease` or a `condition`. Giving one of `slots` and `journal` without the other is a `ValueError` naming the one missing. A study's plugin for such a platform leaves `probe`, `lease_keys` and `lease_group` out (no lease pool is built) or passes `lease_group` alone (always `None`). A platform built with both is unchanged: its data-path runs still lease from every slot.

**A lease group per set of tool-server rights (O31).** A slot's tool-server user must hold exactly the rights of the condition its token is minted for (`TokenIssuer.check_user`), so every slot declares them: `[slots.<name>.toolserver] rights = [...]`, required, by a condition's rules (known rights, each once, the baseline among them). Each set of rights the slots declare is a lease group, `users.group_of(rights)` (`platform_users:` and the rights, sorted and joined by `+`), whose keys are the slots that declare it in the order of the file; how many slots a group has is how many declare its rights. A run with a tool server leases from its condition's group (`SandboxPlatform.condition_group`), and conditions with the same rights share one. So one campaign can run a read-only condition and a writing one interleaved, in its randomised order: each run gets a slot whose tool-server user holds its condition's rights, two runs of different groups hold a slot each at once, and a run waits only for a slot of its own group. A run with a data path and no tool server needs only a data-path user, which every slot has with the baseline rights, so it leases from `users.GROUP`, every slot (it can hold a slot another group would have used). A condition whose rights no slot declares has no group: `lease_group` raises `ConfigError`, naming the condition by its position and the rights, and the scheduler asks it of every run of the plan when the campaign is built (`Scheduler.plan_groups`), before the store is opened or a model probed. `SlotTokens.mint` refuses a slot that declares other rights than the condition's before it journals or mints anything. The day-0 outage agent leases from its condition's group. Neither the record nor the fingerprint changes: a run's group follows from its condition, which its run key holds, and from the slots of platform.toml, and the slot a run held is recorded as before, by its position (`slots[i]`). The group is written where the one group was: the lease waits (`waits.jsonl`), the `lease:<group>` hold subjects and their events.

**A condition's tools and rights agree (O31, #182).** `load_platform` refuses a `[toolserver.conditions.<name>]` whose `allow` and `rights` disagree, naming the condition by its position and the tool or the right, never a value (`toolrights.agreement_problem`, called by the loader), because the agent would see a tool that fails on every call, or the token would be wider than its tools. Three things are refused. A write tool whose right `rights` lacks: `schedule_file_replication` needs `space_schedule_replication`, and `add_file_qos_requirement` and `remove_qos_requirement` need `space_manage_qos` (the message names the key `rights`, the right and the tools). A file-writing tool (`create_file`, `create_directory`, `delete_file`, `set_file_metadata`, `move_file`; key `allow`), since a tool-server user is never granted `space_write_data` (`tokencheck`). And a write right of `WRITE_RIGHTS` that no tool of `allow` needs, which is always the case for `space_cancel_replication`, `space_schedule_eviction` and `space_cancel_eviction`, the pinned server having no tool for them. Nothing else is refused: a view tool without its view right loads, and so does a view right with no view tool, because the view rows of the table (`get_transfer` and `list_space_transfers` need `space_view_transfers`; `get_qos_requirement` and `get_file_qos_summary` need `space_view_qos`) are inferred from the privilege names and not enforced until a live run records each; the other tools need the baseline alone. `toolrights.TOOL_NEEDS` holds every one of the pinned onedata-mcp server's 27 tools, read at `toolrights.OMCP_PIN` (the OMCP row of `inventory.md`), and `tests/data/omcp-pinned-tools.json` records their names and the server's own list of its eight mutating tools, which are exactly the tools whose rows hold a write right or `space_write_data`. A tool the table does not know (a stand-in server's, or another server's) is not judged, and a condition holding one is not refused for an unused write right, since the tool may use it; a name the server does not show still fails at its start, against the frozen manifest. Only names from the table and from `KNOWN_RIGHTS` appear in a message. The table belongs to the pinned commit: a test fails when the inventory's pin moves, or the table and the recorded names differ, until all three are reviewed together.

**Reading a run's infrastructure after its loop (O17, O18).** A study's post-run check (was the gate, the mount or the rest of the run's infrastructure healthy while the agent read?) calls the sandbox's `infra_check()`, `mount_errors()` and `mount_log()` (`api.InfraReadings`), which both backends answer in the same shapes, while the sandbox is up, before or after its collection; never `docker exec` of its own, nor a backend's attributes (`host`, `names`).
- `infra_check()`: `containers` holds each of the run's other components by role, `{"start", "end", "problems"}`, and `"stopped_by_harness": true` for one the harness stopped itself, judged by its state just before that stop (the harness notes it before the stop, and on an HPC node the check reads the live state before it looks for the note, so a stop that lands while it reads is never a death); `deaths` holds a death per component with a problem, with `role`, `condition`, `cause` (`infradeath.CAUSES`), `category` (`infrastructure` or, for Docker's sidecar at its own memory limit, `agent`), `basis` and `problems`; `death` is the problems of the infrastructure's deaths, the lines a study names. `kinds.death_error(check["deaths"])` is the run's `InfraError`, or None. On Docker the roles are `gate` and `sidecar`, and `start` and `end` the containers' states (`infradeath`); on an HPC node they are `collector`, `gate`, `mount` (oneclient's process and its mount: `end` adds `mount`, None when it is as the host mount made it) and `mount_watch` (the mount guard's watcher), and `start` and `end` a process's (`pid` and start time, then `running` and `exit_status`; `apptainer.infra`). A state that cannot be read is a problem, never a clean one.
- `mount_log()`: oneclient's INFO log, `{"text": ...}` with the data-path token replaced by `<token>`, or `{"unavailable": reason}` (on Docker with `stderr`, the digest of `docker exec`'s); None without a mount. On Docker it is the `docker exec <sidecar> cat` of the log that `sidecar_errors` always made; on an HPC node a read of the host mount's log directory, in which no link is followed on the way to the log: each directory from the run's down is opened from the one before it without following a link, glog's link at the log's own name is followed once, to a bare file name of that directory alone, and a link anywhere else (in a directory above it, or one that leaves it) gives `unavailable` (#362). It may be read during the run, before the agent's first turn say: the mount guard does not watch that directory, so the read is no access of the agent's (O60). The I/O mode the core takes from that log is `mount_io_mode()`, one shape on both backends (`iomode.judge`'s, `effective` unknown, `not_collected`, until the collection); `iomode.judge(declared, mount_log()["text"])` reads it earlier. `metadata()["mount"]` is each backend's own record and differs between them (Docker's `io_mode` is the declared word, the Apptainer helper's the judged reading).
- `mount_errors()`: `oneclient_log.summarize` of that text (which `oneclient_log.platform_errors` counts), the same `unavailable` answer, or None.

On Docker the three wrap what the sandbox always read, byte for byte; the old names, `infra_container_check()` and `sidecar_errors()`, still answer on both backends, and warn (`DeprecationWarning`) naming the new one. The collection keeps the check in the metadata as it did: `infra_check`, `container_deaths` and `infra_death` once the collection has read it (on an HPC node a collection refused before it, when the agent's processes cannot be shown stopped, has none), and, while the sandbox is up, `metadata()`'s `container_deaths` and `infra_death` are read live.

**Slots of a deployment (O83).** `[slots]` is the operator's: this layer creates no user. A federation brought up from `deploy/compose-federation` or `deploy/hpc-federation` makes the slots' users as ordinary principals of its layout, and its platform template declares `[slots]` and `[toolserver]` (the ids as `@user_id.<username>@`, the paths under `@study_artifacts@`). A slot user that already exists is verified and never changed: exactly as declared it is recorded `matched`, otherwise refused naming the user and the attribute. The written file is loaded as a campaign loads it and checked against the layout's principals, so each tool-server user's `rights` here are the privileges it was given, as the lease groups above need.

Core guarantees: `ctx.infrastructure_wait` is given a kind of `WAIT_KINDS`; the lease the platform declared for the run is held for the whole attempt and freed through the platform's probe, `SlotProbe.resource_gone`, whose sandbox half is `SandboxProbe.resource_gone`.

Implementer (the platform layer) guarantees:
- **The teardown has a deadline, and says how it ended** (O42). `SandboxPlatform(..., teardown_deadline_s=600.0)` (`provider.TEARDOWN_DEADLINE_S`; a positive, finite number of seconds, or the constructor raises `ValueError`) is the longest `launch` waits for the sandbox's stop, whatever the host is doing: a `docker rm` of a container whose FUSE mount's provider is gone can wait for ever. The outcome is in `metadata()["teardown"]`: `outcome` `completed`, `failed` (the stop raised; the run is `InfraError("sandbox")` as before) or `cut`, `deadline_s`, `elapsed_s`, and for `cut` also `left`, `{"step", "containers", "network", "mounts"}`, the phase the stop was in (`token files`, `fuse teardown`, `containers`, `network and mounts` or `run directory`) and how many containers and mounts, and whether the network, it had not shown removed: counts and a phase name, never a name from the configuration (O12). `containers` is what the stop had not yet shown removed as of its last look: every container the start registered that it had not looked for (one that went by itself, a one-shot, is counted until it has), and not one that a start still in flight creates after the stop looked. This deadline covers the stop of a sandbox that started, not the cleanup of a start that failed: `DockerSandbox.begin` cleans up in its own start thread, which an uncancelled launch waits for without a bound, and a cancelled launch for at most `cancel_wait_s`. A cut raises `InfraError("sandbox")` with the same words, so the run is infrastructure and never the agent's outcome, except when the run's body had raised, whose error stands (the metadata still says `cut`); a cancellation that was pending is raised after the cut. The teardown itself is not stopped, since a thread cannot be: it goes on in a daemon thread of its own that never holds a normal exit of the process, and what it removes later is not recorded. A SIGTERM after a cut still waits for it: the SIGTERM stopper (`termination.py`) calls `stop()` too, behind the same lock, as it always waited for a teardown. The lease's probe keeps the key quarantined until the sandbox is shown gone, whatever the cut says, and the slot's tokens are revoked after the cut as after any stop, while the stop may still be running (the revocation waits only for the mint and start threads). `DockerSandbox.teardown_left()` is the `left` of a stop under way; it asks the host nothing.
- **What is kept of a cell's output is capped, and the cut is recorded** (O43). The sandbox keeps the first `cells.OUTPUT_CAP_BYTES` (1 MiB) of each of a cell's stdout and stderr (`DockerSandbox(..., output_cap_bytes=)` changes it), a whole number of characters, and reads and drops the rest: the host's command runner (`hostops.run_bounded`, which every `DockerHost` command goes through) reads the pipes in a thread each and keeps only the kept part, so a cell that writes without end never fills a pipe and the harness holds no more than the cap. `ExecResult.output_cut` is `None` when nothing was cut, and otherwise `{"stdout": {"produced", "kept"}, "stderr": {...}}` for the streams it cut, in bytes, and the cut is on the run's record, `metadata()["cell_output_cuts"]` (one `{"cell": n, "stdout": {...}, "stderr": {...}}` per cell it cut) and, for a harness step, as the step's `output_cut` under `harness_steps` (only a step that was cut has one); a run with no cut has no `cell_output_cuts`. The code tool (`CodeExecutionProvider`) ends the text it returns with `[output cut by the sandbox: the first K of the P bytes of stdout were kept]` after a `\n` (one clause per cut stream, stdout's first, joined by `; `, K and P as plain digits) and returns `source_truncated=True`, which stays the data source's own limit (L07). The loop's cap (`output_cap_chars`, L06) then applies to that whole text, the notice included, so the two cuts compose in one fixed order: the sandbox's cut first, the tool's notice, the loop's cut last. A cap at least as long as the text with its notice (2 MiB and 1 KiB is above the longest the default cap can return) cuts nothing, and the notice is the last line; a smaller cap cuts the text from its end, so the notice goes with the tail and the model sees the loop's `[output truncated: D of T characters dropped by the harness cap]` as the last line and no sandbox notice (T counts the text the tool returned, not the bytes the cell wrote, which only the record's `output_cut` and the call's `raw_text` tell); a cap just below the text, because the loop's notice takes room from the cap too (for a 3 MiB print of stdout, the caps 1,048,650 to 1,048,663, which are 1 to 14 characters below the text of 1,048,664), leaves a piece of the notice before the loop's, and a cap too short to hold the loop's notice (77 characters there) cuts the text plain, with no notice. Both flags are True in the last two cases, and `raw_text` always holds the text with its notice whole. A study that wants its model to read the sandbox's notice sets `output_cap_chars` above that length; `output_cap_chars` of 8,000, as in the guide's example, tells the model of a 3 MiB print by the loop's notice alone. A `HostOps` of a test takes `output_cap` and cuts a result as `hostops.apply_output_cap` does.
- **A cell's exec is verified, and one that did not complete raises** (O55). Every cell, the agent's and a harness step alike, runs under a bootstrap (`completion.BOOTSTRAP`) that `timeout` starts in place of the interpreter, isolated (`-I`): it reads the program whole, checks its size and SHA-256 against its arguments and runs nothing when they differ, runs a whole program as before (`<python> -`, the program on its stdin, the exec's streams its own), and after that interpreter exits writes one completion record on each stream (a line starting with `\x1eexpcore-exec <nonce> `, then JSON with `source_bytes`, `source_whole` and `exit_status`) and ends as the program did, by the same exit code or signal. The runner takes those lines out of the streams before it counts and caps them (`hostops.run_bounded(..., marker=)`), so the program's output is its own byte for byte, and says when the program was not written whole to `docker exec` (`CmdResult.input_error`). `ExecResult.exec_check` is `{"source_whole", "exit_status", "nonce_matched"}`, also on the cell's record and a harness step's (`exec_check`). An exec that no timeout of the harness (`timed_out`) and no kill (`kill`) explains, and whose record is missing, partial or says another status than docker reported, raises `completion.ExecIncomplete`, an `InfraError` of kind `sandbox` and a `SandboxError`, with its cause (`cause`) and the result (`result`): the source not written whole to `docker exec`, the source not arrived whole in the container ("its program did not run"), no record ("the exec's exit was not recorded": `docker exec` reports exit 0 for an exit the daemon never recorded), a record on one stream only ("its output was lost"), or a status docker reported other than the recorded one. The cell's record gains `incomplete` with the cause, and the run's metadata `exec_incomplete` (`{"cell", "cause"}` per cell); a harness step's record gains `incomplete` before it raises. The code tool passes `exec_check` on (`ToolOutput.exec_check`), and the run record keeps it on the call. A program that exits 0 and prints nothing, `os._exit(0)` included, is verified and a success as before. The bootstrap is a second interpreter in the agent's container for the cell's life, a few MB in its memory limit, and the cell's parent process is the bootstrap, not `timeout`. The nonce guards against another exec's record and against an accident, not against a program that forges one: the bootstrap's arguments are readable from the agent's processes.
- A command the host runs is bounded in time, and the wait after its kill is too (O42). `hostops.run_bounded` kills a command past its limit and waits `KILL_WAIT_S` (10 s) more for it to end: a process in an uninterruptible sleep (a read of a FUSE mount whose provider is gone) cannot die, and `subprocess.run` waited for it for ever. A command given up on is the timeout all the same (return code 124, `timed out after Ns`, the output it had written), and it is left behind. The limit covers the end of the command's output as well as the command: a process the command left running that holds the pipes open past the limit is a timeout, never a success with a short output, and the readers of those pipes drop what they read from then on.
- `launch` opens `ctx.infrastructure_wait("sandbox_lifecycle")` around the start (the token mint, the image checks, the containers) and around the stop with the slot's revocation after it, and nowhere else (a start that fails before its sandbox exists revokes inside one more). Executing the agent's code and its reads of data are never waits.
- Every image is started by the id it is pinned to in `platform.toml`; a tag that now resolves elsewhere raises `StartRefused` before anything exists (O13). Each sandbox gets its own small network from the configured pool, whose ranges are strict networks checked at load (O14). The agent is on that network alone. The mount sidecar and the data gate are on Docker's default bridge, dialling the hosts of the URLs of the local provider and, the gate, of `[zone]`; or, when `[sandbox.federation]` names the federation's own network, on that network instead, dialling the local provider and, the gate, the zone by their names there (`providers.<local>`, `zone`) on port 443, the names the federation's certificates hold, which the gate's upstream checks as `[tls]` says. The gate is then connected to the sandbox's network too, and serves the agent the names it dials, each with a certificate of the run's CA; the agent resolves them to the gate and never joins the federation's network. A mount or REST run whose federation network is not on the host raises `StartRefused` before anything exists, and the sandbox never changes or removes that network (O03, O15, O30). [`study-guide.md`](study-guide.md#how-the-sandbox-reaches-the-federation) shows the whole path.
- The data gate runs as root (`--user 0`) with every capability dropped but `NET_BIND_SERVICE`, on a read-only root, so it reads only files root owns or anyone may read. Each TLS key it serves a name with is made owner-only and given to root (`sudo chown 0:0`) before it starts, as its token file is, and a key that cannot be given to root raises `SandboxError` before any container exists; the certificates, the directory they are mounted from and its configuration (`gate.json`) are made readable to anyone, whatever the driver's umask, and hold no secret (O05, O30).
- **The gate's owner check, for a node whose loopback other users share (O61).** On an HPC batch node the gate has no network of its own: it listens on a loopback every user of the node shares. `peer_uid_check` in its JSON configuration (`true` or `false`, `false` when absent, anything else refuses the start) makes it attribute each connection it accepts, on its HTTPS listener and on every `tcp_forwards` one, to the uid of the peer's socket, from `/proc/net/tcp` and `tcp6` (which list every socket with its owner whatever `hidepid` hides), and serve only the gate's own uid. A peer of another uid, one the table shows no owner for (uid 0 or the overflow uid), one with no row (another host) and every peer when the tables cannot be read are closed with a reset before a byte is read or written, and the gate logs one `peer_refused` line (`reason`: `other_uid`, `no_owner_shown`, `not_found`, `table_unreadable`, `no_address` or `check_error`; `peer_uid`, `peer`, `listen`), which carries no `error_origin` and is counted as no error. The gate stops at its start if it could not run the check: it runs as uid 0 or the overflow uid, it cannot read `/proc/net/tcp`, or `gatepeer.py` is not beside `gate.py` (the sibling is loaded only with the flag on, so a gate directory of the three older files keeps working with it off). **Off, the gate is as it was:** `GateServer.verify_request` is the base server's, nothing is read, and Docker's gate, on a network of its own, never sets it. A backend that runs the gate on a shared node turns it on by writing `peer_uid_check` into the configuration, as a default its operator's gate options may still override. A row is looked up twice before it is called missing, since the kernel prints a table a page at a time.
- **The cell's connect-side 443 mapper (O61).** The published REST client dials port 443 only, and the gate cannot bind below 1024 on these nodes, so the cell loads `deploy/hpc-federation/bundle/gateport.c` (an `LD_PRELOAD` shim, built in the cell's image as the egress shim is, `-ldl` added): a TCP `connect()` (protocol TCP: SCTP and MPTCP stream sockets are left alone) to `127.0.0.1:443` goes to the gate's port, the same address kept, and no other connection is touched (every IPv6 destination, `[::1]:443` and `[::ffff:127.0.0.1]:443` included, another loopback address, another port, UDP and Unix sockets). The port is read once, when the shim loads, from the cell's `/etc/expcore/gate.json` (`{"address": "127.0.0.1", "port": P, ...}`: the address must be exactly `127.0.0.1`, the port an integer from 1 to 65535, the file at most 4096 bytes), which decides whenever it exists, a file that is unusable making the process inert, and otherwise from `EXPCORE_GATE_PORT` (digits only, 1 to 65535). A process that cannot use either redirects nothing and says so once on stderr; one with neither is inert and silent. The shim chains to the next `connect()` by `dlsym(RTLD_NEXT)`, so it must come before `egresslog.so` in the preload list: the egress log then records the gate's port, the connect's real destination, and after it the shim is never reached. No IPv6 destination is rewritten: the core's gate listens on 127.0.0.1 only, so `[::1]:443` rewritten to `[::1]:<gate port>` would reach whatever other socket holds that address, which on a shared node can be another user's listener. The federation's names map to 127.0.0.1.
- Every bind of a secret is `--mount type=bind,src=...,dst=...,readonly`, never `-v SOURCE:PATH:ro`: the sidecar's token file, the gate's token file and the directory of the gate's TLS keys (`containers.bind`; the gate's `gate.json`, the CA copies, the gate's log and the agent's binds, which hold no secret, stay `-v`). `--mount` refuses a source that is not there, where `-v` makes it a directory owned by root, and `docker cp` from a running container re-mounts its binds, so a copy of a running sidecar after its token file was removed left an empty directory at that path (O05). `sandbox.py`'s `_remove_token_files` examines whatever `rm -f` leaves, never reading it: `metadata()["teardown"]["token_files"]` lists each by `path` in the run's directory, `type` (`file`, `directory`, `symlink`, `other`, or `unknown` when it cannot be examined), `size`, owner `uid` and `action`, and `metadata()["teardown"]["run_directory"]` names the run's directory by its `id` and `sha256` (O12). An empty directory (one non-recursive listing) is removed with `rmdir`, as `empty directory left by a bind; removed`, and does not fail the teardown; a file, a link, a directory with an entry in it or an empty one `rmdir` cannot remove (`left in place`, `directory with entries; left in place`, `empty directory left by a bind; could not be removed`) fails it, and the error names the path and its type, never a content. A `docker cp` from a running sidecar or gate is never made (study guide, "Collecting a run's evidence").
- The mount sidecar's oneclient is always started with `--log-dir /var/log/oneclient`, and the run's `oneclient-log` directory (owner-only, 0700, in the run's directory) is bound read-write at that path: the sandbox owns oneclient's log directory. The log is read from the host as part of the run's evidence, kept with a kept run, and survives the container. oneclient takes the option once, so `[sandbox.mount] extra_args` may not give it, in any form oneclient reads: `-l`, `-l<value>`, a short switch cluster ending in it (`-fl`), `--log-dir`, `--log-dir=<value>`, and an abbreviation of the long name (oneclient parses its command line with boost::program_options in its default style, which allows abbreviations: `--log-d` and `--log-di` name the option; `--l`, `--lo`, `--log` and `--log-` also start `--log-read-write-perf`, which oneclient refuses as ambiguous, and are refused here as well). The refusal names the option, never its value, and is made by the one check of `extra_args` (`oneclient_args.extra_arg_problem`) where the settings are built (`MountSettings`), when `platform.toml` is loaded and again when the sidecar's command is built (`containers.sidecar_options`), so settings built in code cannot bypass it. `metadata()["mount"]["log_dir_from"]` is always `sandbox`. **The log is unbuffered in every mode (O54).** oneclient's glog holds INFO lines in a buffer for up to 30 s unless `--disable-log-buffering` is passed (`logging.cc`: `FLAGS_logbuflevel` -1), so the sidecar passes it with every mount, proxy as well as direct: the start lines `MountIoMode` and a direct mount's start check read are then in `oneclient.INFO` when the log is read, and a run is not set aside as `unknown` because they were still in the buffer. A proxy mount carried it from this change on; a direct mount always had. The switch sets when INFO lines are written and nothing else: the data path, the sidecar's capabilities, device and AppArmor scope are unchanged, and none of the start lines the core compares (`oneclient_start.expected_settings`) names it. It is one of the sandbox's options in `mount.options`, so a proxy run's `mount.options` now ends `..., "--config", "/dev/null", "--disable-log-buffering"` (and `"--insecure"` after it with `[tls] insecure`): that is one more item in a list of the sandbox's metadata (which a study may keep in its run record), with no new field and no change to the record's schema, and the campaign fingerprint holds the I/O mode word only, so it does not change. The cost is a flush per INFO line; the proxy log read on 11 Nov held 17. `extra_args` may not carry the switch, in any spelling, in either mode: oneclient refuses a switch given twice. oneclient's INFO log read on 11 Nov held start-up settings and no token, which oneclient takes from its environment; the directory is not redacted, so it stays owner-only.
- **The oneclient that served a Compose mount run (O65, `sandbox.oneclient_build`).** `metadata()["mount"]["oneclient_build"]` is `{"version", "fuse", "build_id", "image"}`: oneclient's own `--version` words (`fuse` only when it prints a FUSE version), the GNU build-id note of its binary (oneclient prints no build id; lowercase hex) and the sidecar image's id (`sha256:...`, the one the start showed to be the pinned one). It is the key set of the Apptainer host mount's record with `image` where that has `bundle_sha256`, read by the same code (`version_of`, `build_id_from`), and holds no path and no name. The sandbox reads it at the end of the mount's start by `docker exec` in the *running* sidecar: `oneclient --version`, and windows of `/opt/oneclient/bin/oneclient` (the header, the program-header table and each note segment, each up to a bound, `tail -c +N | head -c M | od -An -v -tx1`, which seeks, so the binary is never read whole). Never `docker cp` from a running container (it re-mounts its binds, O05), and no container is created for it. A reading that fails is `null` (`version`, `build_id`), logged without the tool's words, and never refuses the start; a run on the REST path or with no data path has no `mount` record and so none of this. The key is additive: it is one more optional key under `mount`, and a record made before it simply lacks it.
- The data gate's upstream connection and oneclient check the federation's certificate as the platform's one `[tls]` says, as the REST client and the tool server do (`expcore_onedata.tls`): against their default CAs, against the run's own read-only copy of the `ca_file` bundle's verified bytes (written once, after their hash is checked against the one loaded, and mounted read-only; a bundle that changed since the load refuses the start), or not at all with `insecure_tls`. The agent trusts the run's own CA alone. `metadata()["tls"]` records the mode, with the bundle's SHA-256 under `ca_file`, never its path or contents (O05, O12).
- A read-only input (`launch(..., inputs=[ReadOnlyInput(...)])`, which the executor passes from the run's condition or its own; `inputs.py`, O20) is copied into the run's directory, hashed while it is copied, and mounted read-only (`readonly`) in the agent's container from that copy, in every data path. `start` proves from inside the agent that writing, renaming, chmod-ing and deleting a file of it and creating a file or a directory beside it each fail with EROFS, which no permission the agent could change gives, that the agent can read it and run its executable files, and that `/work` stays writable; it then hashes the copy and the source again, and raises `SandboxError` for an input that changed between its hash and its mount. `metadata()["read_only_inputs"]` records each input, in order, by its path in the agent, the SHA-256 of its content (each entry's relative path and kind, and a file's executable bit, size and SHA-256, in path order), its files and its bytes, beside the other run inputs the metadata names by hash (`images`, `tls`, `platform_config_sha256`), and never by the host's path; `InputsRecord`, an instrument `launch` fills (`inputs_record`, required with `inputs`), puts the same in the run's record itself, `RunRecord.instruments["read_only_inputs"]`. The plugin declares its inputs once, `declared = inputs.declare(config.sandbox, {name: [ReadOnlyInput(...)]})` by name (a condition, or one name for every run), which checks each against every path the sandbox reserves (`inputs.taken_paths`) and gives it its content's hash; it puts them in the campaign fingerprint, `StudyPlugin(platform={**config.fingerprint(), **inputs.fingerprint(declared)})`, so a resume whose inputs changed is refused (C06), builds the platform with them, `SandboxPlatform(..., read_only_inputs=declared)`, and passes them to `launch` as declared. `launch` refuses an input it was not declared with, and every start hashes each input again and raises `StartRefused`, before anything exists, for one whose content is no longer the declared one. The run's copy is given to root and made read-only on the host too (0444 files, 0555 directories and executable files), and `collect` hashes it again: a copy that lost its hash raises `InfraError("output_collection")`. A path that is not plain and absolute, or that is, holds or lies under `/work`, `/tmp`, `/dev`, `/proc`, `/sys`, a file docker writes in `/etc`, a path the sandbox binds into a cell itself on either backend (the name-service files, `/etc/ld.so.preload`, `/etc/expcore`, the egress shim, its preloads and its socket directory; `inputs.TAKEN`, from the Apptainer settings' `OWN_TARGETS`), the run's CA, a trust path, the data mount or another input, and a source that holds a link, a special file or no file, raise `StartRefused` before anything exists.
- **A cell's `/etc/resolv.conf` is the sandbox's own, the same file on both backends** (O56; `sandbox.cellresolv`). The agent's container binds the run's copy of `cellresolv.RESOLV_TEXT` read-only (`--mount type=bind,...,readonly`, `containers.agent_run_args`, in every data path) over the engine's own file, which on a user-defined network names the embedded resolver (127.0.0.11) and copies the host's `search` and `options` lines: a host's search domain could reach the agent. A cell reads no `nameserver`, `search` or `domain` line, exactly as an Apptainer cell. Nothing of the agent resolves through the engine's resolver: the zone and the provider are `--add-host` entries (REST), a mount needs no name, and a cell's code arrives through `docker exec`. `/etc/resolv.conf` is in `inputs.TAKEN`, so a study cannot bind another file onto it. `start` has a cell read the file back (`cellresolv.PROBE`) and refuses the run, naming no text of the file, when the hash it read is not the neutral file's; the run records that hash as `infrastructure_facts.resolv_conf_sha256`, the same key and value on Docker and on the Apptainer backend. The file's text is never recorded. A program that sends a query to 127.0.0.11 by address still reaches the engine's resolver. The agent image's `/etc/nsswitch.conf` is left as it is: the Docker agent's loopback is its own, where nothing listens.
- `collect` copies the named outputs of `/work` and the gate log out as root without following or keeping a link (`copyout.py`, O19). The agent's container is frozen through its cgroup (`docker pause`, confirmed by `frozen 1` in the cgroup's `cgroup.events`, never by a list of processes) while its outputs are listed and copied, and thawed after; a freeze that cannot be confirmed raises `InfraError("output_collection")` before anything is listed. The listing and the copy are made by a root helper (`copyhelper.py`, run as `sudo PYTHON -I -B -c`, its request on stdin) that opens every name from its parent directory's descriptor with `O_NOFOLLOW` and checks every directory on the way and every object copied against the device, inode, kind and size the listing showed, so a name swapped for a link or another object after the listing stops the copy rather than being read through. A root listing that follows nothing must show each output, and every directory on the way to it, holding only regular files and directories: no symbolic link, no hard link to a file outside the output, no FIFO, socket or device, no other file system (on the way to an output too: only a regular file there makes the output absent), no path longer than 2048 bytes, and no more bytes in all than `work_disk_bytes`. Otherwise the agent made the outputs so, which is its own doing: nothing of the outputs is copied, and `collect` returns normally with `refused_output` in its result and in the metadata (and so in `metadata.json`), naming each object by its path under `/work` and what it is, never a link's target, with their count in all and by what they are. The study's scorers read it; the attempt is never set aside for it. The copy is the driver's (`fchown`, modes 0644 and 0755), and is then verified by lstat to hold only the driver's directories and files of one link, the files and bytes the listing showed; one that does not, or one the helper stopped, is removed. A collection that fails for the harness's own reasons (a container that cannot be frozen, the helper or the disk failing, a copy that is not what was listed, a gate log that is missing or not a plain file, a read-only input's copy that lost its hash) raises `InfraError("output_collection")`, a platform kind registered with `treatable=False` in `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds`, and keeps the sandbox's disks. The driver reads the gate log, for its refusals and its summary, without following a link: one that is a link is unreadable. The reaper (`reap.py`) copies a dead run's outputs under the same rule and keeps its disks when it refuses.
- **A cell's processes end with it** (O72), on both backends, so the code tool's default description, "State does not carry between calls", holds. Once a cell's `docker exec` has returned, the sandbox kills every process of the agent's container except its init and the init's `sleep` (the quiesce's set, from the container's cgroup, so a worker in a session of its own is one) and waits for them to be shown gone (`cells.CellRunner._sweep`); each cell's record has `left_after_exec`, how many were left (None when the run ended or the timer fired, since the deadline killed them then), and a sweep that killed something or could not list the processes is in `metadata()["quiesced"]` with its `cell` and `reason: "end of cell"`. A process left that is not shown gone (a zombie, or a process in D that the kill reached, which takes it on its next return, excepted), a kill command that failed with a process left, or a listing that fails, ends the run as `survived_kill`, judged on every process left (the record keeps 16, with `left_running` and `left_alive` counting all of them), as at the cap: every later `exec` raises `InfraError("cell_unkillable")`. The cell's own result is not touched by that: its record has the exit status, the streams and the exec's check the cell had (no kill, no timeout), and the run's ending is said apart, as `metadata()["run_ended"]` (`by: "end_of_cell_sweep"`, the `cell` it followed, `outcome: "survived_kill"`, `why`) and in the error of every later `exec` and of the collection, which names the sweep and the cell, never a deadline. A harness that needs a process of the agent's to outlive a cell (a live test's background reader) starts it with its own `docker exec`, beside the cells, and runs no cell while it is needed: it is in the agent's container, so the next cell's sweep kills it. A harness step's (`record=False`) leftovers end with it too. The Apptainer backend's cells end so already: each is an exec of a PID namespace of its own.
- **A signal reaches only the process that was listed** (O72). On Docker the sweep after a cell, the quiesce before the collection, the kill at a cell's cap and the teardown's readers list the agent container's processes from its cgroup and signal them later through the host's privilege, since the agent runs as another user. A pid that ended and was given to another process in between could be the data gate or the dispatcher on the agent's node, so none of them signals by bare pid (`procsignal.signal_processes`). Each listed process carries its start tick (field 22 of `/proc/PID/stat`) and the SHA-256 of its command line (`/proc/PID/cmdline` as `HostOps.read_text` reads it), and the root half (`killhelper.py`, run as `sudo PYTHON -I -B -c SOURCE TERM|KILL`, standard library only, the request on stdin as JSON and one outcome per target on stdout) signals a pid only while both still match. It opens a pidfd first (`pidfd_open`, the raw call where this Python lacks `os.pidfd_open`), holds it across the checks and signals through it (`pidfd_send_signal`), so a pid reused after the descriptor was opened is never signalled; on a kernel that answers ENOSYS (before Linux 5.3) it reads the identity and sends `os.kill` at once, which leaves only the instant between the last read and the kill, and any other failure of `pidfd_open` signals nothing. It never signals pid 1 or below, nor a process whose identity cannot be read (a listing that could not read a command line records none). The outcomes are `signalled`, `gone`, `replaced` (another start tick: the listed process ended and its pid was reused), `changed` (the same process with another command line, after an exec), `unreadable`, `refused` and `failed`. A process it did not signal is not marked done and is never read as gone: the sweep and the quiesce list it again in the next round (one that execed is then signalled as what it is now), the cap's deadline asks again at each poll of its grace period and its record counts only what a signal reached (`signalled`), saying how many it did not (`unreached`), and the teardown logs it among its survivors (`declined`). An answer is read only when it is the answer to the request (one entry for each pid asked, in order, each with that integer pid; nothing is coerced), and a command that fails as a whole reaches nobody, with what is left then listed anew. A leftover still alive that no signal reached is not quiet: the sweep and the quiesce end the run as `survived_kill`, and the cap's deadline escalates as for a process that outlives its kill. So a process whose command line cannot be read at the listing is never killed and ends its run that way. The Apptainer backend signals through pidfds of its own, as the driver's user, and checks each process's start tick (`NodeOps.signal`).
- A cell's `timeout_s` is a hard deadline (`deadline.py`, O34). At the cap the harness's timer SIGKILLs every process of the cell: every process of the agent's container except its init and the init's `sleep`, read from the container's cgroup, so a worker in a process group or session of its own is killed with the rest. The init's `sleep` is the one process of that pid and start time when the container started (`start` refuses a container whose `sleep` it cannot identify): a process of the agent's that names itself `sleep` is killed like any other. A process waiting in the kernel takes its kill only when its wait returns, as a read through the mount of a file only a stopped provider holds does not for a long time, so a process still alive after `[sandbox] kill_grace_s` (1 to 60 seconds, 5 by default, recorded as `metadata()["cell_deadline"]`) escalates:
  - the sandbox aborts the mount's FUSE connection from the oneclient sidecar: `docker exec -u 0` into the sidecar, which holds the mount and SYS_ADMIN, mounts fusectl in its own namespace when it is not there and writes the connection's `abort`, the connection being the device of the run's mount in the host's table. Every request waiting on the connection then fails with an errno, and the pending kill takes. Nothing runs in the agent's container. A lazy unmount (`fusermount -uz`) is not used: it leaves the agent's copy of the mount and the reads waiting on it;
  - when the abort fails, cannot be made (a REST or no data path has no mount) or frees nothing within the grace again, the sandbox's stop is ordered: it runs, by its ordered teardown, whose own last resort is the host's abort of the connection, once the outcome is recorded, so its teardown neither holds up the run's classification nor counts in the cell's time.

  Each grace is counted by the clock, and every command of the escalation (a listing, a kill, the abort) is given only the time left, so a slow docker daemon cannot stretch it. The whole escalation, from the cap to the order of the stop, lasts at most `deadline.margin_s(grace_s)`: two graces and `deadline.COMMANDS_S` (20 s), 30 s by default, recorded with the grace as `metadata()["cell_deadline"]["margin_s"]`; when the margin runs out first the escalation waits no longer and the stop is ordered all the same. A listing or kill of the escalation that raises (its `HostOps` call failed) is the step `raised`, with what was under way and the error's class, and the sandbox's quiesce kills what is left: `killed` only when it shows every process gone and the mount was not aborted before the raise; a process left, in whatever state, or none it could kill or list, is `survived_kill`, and the stop is ordered. An outcome never gets less severe than a step already taken (`deadline.SEVERITY`: `killed` < `mount_aborted` < `sandbox_stopped` < `survived_kill`): after a successful abort a raise ends at least as `mount_aborted`, whatever the quiesce finds, and the run is ended.

  Each cell whose timer fired has an entry in `metadata()["cell_deadlines"]`, published from the timer's thread with the run's `ended` before anything else happens: `cell`, `utc` (the cap), `grace_s`, `margin_s`, `outcome` and `steps` (`kill`, `raised`, `survived`, `abort`, `ended`, `margin`, `stop`, each with `t_s`, the seconds since the cap, and the processes signalled or left with their states, the abort's `ok`, connections, exit status and stderr digest; after the stop, `stopped`, with its `ok` and `teardown_s`). The outcome is `killed` (every process ended within the grace), `mount_aborted`, `sandbox_stopped` (the stop was ordered within the margin), or `survived_kill` (the margin ran out first, the escalation raised and its quiesce did not show the cell gone, or the outcome was not decided in time, recorded with its `why`). `killed` is the agent's own time limit, as before, and nothing else is. Every wait for the outcome is bounded by the margin and 30 s (`cells.SETTLE_SLACK_S`), the cell's own included: a cell whose outcome is not decided by then returns from `exec` as `kill_survived`, with that outcome published under `cell_deadlines` and as the run's end, so neither the cell nor the worker thread that runs it waits for ever. Any other outcome makes the cell's kill `kill_survived`, category `infrastructure`, whatever the counters say, and its attempt `InfraError("cell_unkillable")`, a platform kind registered with `treatable=False`, so no condition may declare it, an outage's included. The cell's own call is not where it is said: the cell ran, and hit its deadline, so `CodeExecutionProvider` returns what any cell that hit its deadline returns (error class `timeout`, the output it kept, the exec's check of a killed exec) and reads neither its gate refusals nor its kill, and the loop records a call that ran (O72); the run's ending is raised by the next `exec`, by `launch` before the gate's refusals, and by the collection (on Docker not after `mount_aborted` alone, as below), and its detail names the cell's deadline and its outcome and says the cell's own result stands. The agent loop gives a cell the agent's remaining time, so its watch cuts the call at the cell's own cap and would score the agent's `time_cap` while the escalation still runs: `launch`, on a body that returns or raises, and `collect` first settle the deadline of a cell still running (enforced at once if its timer has not fired, then waited for, at most its margin and 30 s; one not decided by then counts as `survived_kill`), and only then decide; a stop waits for a deadline under way first. The run is then over, and set aside, so its disks are kept when the sandbox stops, whichever stop comes first (`metadata()["evidence_kept"]`), as a failed collection's are: every later `exec` raises the same kind, and so does `collect`, except after `mount_aborted`, when the container is up and the outputs are collected as evidence (`metadata()["mount"]["aborted"]` is True and the mount is dead). A harness step (`record=False`) starts no timer and has no deadline beyond the container's `timeout` backstop.
  oneclient 25.0's `--provider-timeout` and `--storage-timeout` (120 s each by default, `deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-11-federation-facts-mount.md`) are a possible bound of a blocked read with an errno, through `[sandbox.mount] extra_args`; whether either ends a read while a provider is down is unmeasured, and neither is set: a change to the mount's behaviour is the study's to make.
- Teardown runs on every path that ends a sandbox, in the order readers, unmount, sidecar, agent, and verifies that each step took effect; a sandbox that is not verifiably gone makes `stop` raise `SandboxError` and `launch` raise `InfraError("sandbox")`, and the probe keeps the lease key quarantined (O15, C19).
- `SandboxProbe.resource_gone(key, note)` answers exactly `True` only when docker lists no container and no network labelled with the key and the mount table shows no mount in a directory of the key; any failed read is `False`.
- The gate passes only reads, answers a refusal as 4xx and never as an error, and marks a transfer it cut (`cut`, `unread_bytes`) so it is told from an error of the gate (`error_origin: gate`) or of the platform (`upstream`) (O16).
- Before a campaign admits its first run, the platform's standard health check (`health.PlatformHealth`, which composes `precampaign.PreCampaignHealth` itself, with no opt-out) measures the content type the gate streams on when a data path is REST: it reads `[sandbox] content_check_file` (required with a REST level) through the local provider, or through `[sandbox] content_check_provider` when the study names one (O87, below), whole and by its first byte, and a type other than `application/octet-stream` with 200 and 206, or one it cannot measure, refuses the campaign: the verdict is kept for the campaign, so the health gate stays closed with the refusal as its detail and no run is admitted; with no REST data path the file is never read and the campaign is admitted. The day-0 check `content_type` is the same check, for the operator (`precampaign.measure_content_type`, which also says whether a type was measured): it passes only on a measured right type, fails on a measured wrong one, and is unknown whenever it measured nothing, with no REST data path included (O05, O08, O11).
- **The provider the content check reads through (O87; `[sandbox] content_check_provider`).** A read through a provider that does not hold a file's blocks makes it fetch and keep them, a replica, so a study whose states want every file empty at the local provider before a run cannot name a file that is fully local. The optional key `content_check_provider = "<a provider's layout name>"` (both backends, only with `content_check_file`) has the check read through that provider, the file's owner, which holds it whole; every request of the check (the space's root, each listing page, the walk from the file up to its space, both reads) goes to it, and the detail and the day-0 check `content_type` name it by its position, `providers[i]`, as they named the local one. Unset, or naming the local provider, it reads through the local provider as before. Loading refuses a name that is not one of `[providers]`, or one that does not support the first space the data path's token reaches, the space the check file lies in, naming the key and never the provider; `PlatformConfig.content_check_reader` is the provider read through. The key is in the platform's fingerprint (`sandbox.content_check_provider`, as `providers[i]`) when it names a provider other than the local one, so a campaign never resumes onto another reader and a store older than the key resumes as before.
- After the content type, the same first call runs the **link check** (`expcore_onedata.linkcheck.check_links`, O35), with no opt-out for a campaign. Its pairs are every ordered (reading provider, source) pair of two providers that support a declared space in common (`linksettings.link_pairs`): n·(n-1) for n providers sharing a space, 6 for three and 20 for five, the local provider's own among them, since replication moves data between any two supporters and a link that does not involve the local provider carries a study's reads too. Its probe space is `[link_check]` of `platform.toml`: `name`, `id`, `prefixes` and `providers` (the providers that support it), with the optional bounds `probe_bytes` (65536, under 1 MiB), `timeout_s` (30), `visible_timeout_s` (60), `evict_timeout_s` (60) and `every_s` (600, at least 60). Loading refuses a probe space whose id or name is a declared space's, whose name a study prefix admits, or that a provider of a pair does not support, naming the key and the untested pair by position; the client's gate admits a request in it only when the request is bound to it (`space=config.probe_space`). Per pair the check writes one probe file of its own through the source and keeps it (`expcore-link-probe-` and a hash of the pair's key, which is the source's id for a pair the local provider reads and the source's and the reader's ids for any other, so that no two readers share a file: a copy the first reader holds would serve the second's fetch while its own link is stuck; written again when missing or not its bytes, read back through the source), waits until the reading provider's block map shows the source holding it whole and no other provider holding any (the whole map is read, every provider the answer lists: it evicts a holder that `[link_check].providers` names, and fails the pair, naming the holder, for any other holder, declared or not, and for a holding it cannot read, which is unknown and never empty), then reads it once, whole, through the reading provider within `timeout_s`, and checks its bytes. A read that times out or fails is the pair's failure, "no transfer from providers[j] to providers[i] within N s", with the remedy (`rtransfer_config:restart_link()` on the reading provider, or a restart of its container alone, never a Compose `down`); it is never read again in that check, nor in a later one, since a provider waits on a stuck synchronizer job with no timeout and a later read of the same file joins it until its restart after 1,800 s: the failed probe is retired, and **a file id whose fetch ever failed is never read again**, whatever its name or generation and whether it still exists. The retirement is recorded in the probe space first, as an empty marker file (`expcore-link-retired-<hash of the pair's key>-<hash of the file id>`) written through the reading provider; probe selection leaves out every file a marker names. Then the next generation is written through the source under a new name (`expcore-link-probe-<hash>-g1`, then `-g2`, above every generation the source lists) with a new file id, and the failed file and the source's other probe files are deleted, best-effort, so the next check reads another file and a recovered link passes at once. With no probe left that a marker does not retire and no fresh one writable, the pair fails closed, "no fresh probe for providers[j] to providers[i]: creation failed", and no retired file is read in its place; only when the marker cannot be written and the failed file is not deleted does the failure say a later check may read the file again, a new file written or not. A retired file whose deletion failed is deleted by the next check of the pair that passes, and a marker is deleted once the source no longer lists its file, so the record is bounded by the retired files that remain. **The check's own files have exactly two name shapes**, matched whole and in lower-case hex: a probe, `expcore-link-probe-<16 hex>` for generation 0 and `expcore-link-probe-<16 hex>-g<N>` for any generation N from 1 (no `-g0`, no leading zero, no upper bound, as `probe_name` has none), and a retirement marker, `expcore-link-retired-<16 hex>-<16 hex>`. The first 16 hex digits are the pair's **stem**, the SHA-256 of its key, which its probes and markers share; the second group of a marker is the SHA-256 of the retired file id.
