Any other name is not the check's, near misses (`-g0`, `-g01`, 15 or 17 digits, upper case, a suffix) included. `expcore_onedata.linkentries.link_check_entries(listing)` sorts a listing of the probe space into a frozen `LinkCheckEntries`, with no provider id and no call to the platform. `listing` is what the core's listing calls return: `provision.Entry` objects (whose `name` and `file_id` are read), as a list or as the dict by name that `Provisioner.children` returns, or `(name, file_id)` pairs; an item of any other shape (`TypeError`), an entry with no file id and a name listed with two file ids (`ValueError`) are refused. Each field is a tuple in the listing's order: `own` (every entry of the two shapes), `current` (one `CurrentProbe(stem, name, file_id, generation)` per pair that has one, ordered by stem: the highest generation among the pair's probes that no marker of its stem retires by the digest of the file id, which is the file `ProbeFiles.provision` selects, with generations read as `linkprobe.generation_of` reads them, up to nine digits), `superseded` (every other probe: the lower generations and the retired files, which a later passing check purges, and a name with ten or more digits of generation, which `provision` never reads and `purge` never deletes, and which is `own` all the same, as the check would write it after a billion failed fetches of one pair), `markers` (a marker whose file is gone included) and `other` (everything else, for the study to judge). `own` is `current`'s files, `superseded` and `markers` together. A pair whose every probe is retired has no `current`. `is_link_check_entry(name)` says whether a name is of either shape. A Day-0 or reset check of the probe space leaves `own` out and judges `other`; it deletes none of `own`, since a marker is what keeps a retired file from being read again. When every pair passed, each probe is evicted from its reading provider once and the outcome recorded; the next check confirms that provider's block map empty before it reads. Unlike the content type's, its verdict is not kept: `PlatformHealth` runs it after the space at the first call, and again at a call whenever the last run is older than `every_s` or failed, so a failure at the first call holds every run until a re-check of the closed gate passes (a remedied link reopens it without restarting the campaign), and one later fails that call, so the gate closes, the run whose check found it is set aside as infrastructure and the whole campaign is held until a re-check passes. A passing detail names each pair's seconds, so the gate's own record holds them. `PlatformHealth.links` is the last report (`LinkReport.to_json()`: each pair by position, as `reader` (the provider that reads the pair, the platform's local provider or another) and `source`, with `local` holding the same value as `reader` as its deprecated alias, kept for records written before every ordered pair was checked (it never means the platform's local provider; `PairResult.local` is the same alias on the object), `ok`, `seconds`, `bytes`, `provisioned`, the evictions before and after the fetch by transfer id, `evicted`, `reason`, `remedy`, `retired` (whether a failed probe was retired), `purged` and `unpurged` (the retired files a passing check deleted and could not delete); the window on the host's epoch clock; every transfer id). The probe space keeps the check's transfers out of every study space's listing. A federation with pairs and no `[link_check]` always fails the call; one with no pair passes. Day-0's reading `health` check builds `PlatformHealth(..., links=False)`, since the link check writes. A fault injector's restore runs the check for every ordered pair once the restored provider serves (`fault_serves.ServingProbe.links`, through `ComposeDriver.restored_links`; it and every read of the pairs a heal makes write `link-check.json`, as `up` does, see "The link-check record"), not the restored provider's pairs alone: an outage can leave a link between two other providers stuck, as on the live slot of 14 Nov 2026, where a mirror's link to the local provider stuck after a third provider's outage. A pair that does not pass leaves the restore unverified (`links_stuck`), the injection open and the fault gate failing; the failure that recovery raises says to restart the rtransfer link of the reading provider each failed pair names (or that provider's container alone, one at a time, re-checking every pair after each), and carries the check's detail, which names each failed pair by position, so the provider to restart is the pair's reader and not necessarily the restored provider or the platform's local provider. The pairs are prepared, fetched and evicted at most 8 at once. The check's time is a few seconds when the probes exist, about 6 s more when they are written, and grows by a round of that for every 8 pairs; keep `health_timeout_s` above `visible_timeout_s + timeout_s + evict_timeout_s`.
- The reads of the content check, like every mutation and range read by file id, pass the client's mutation gate (`client.OnezoneClient`, O09): a request under `/spaces/{id}` goes only to a space whose live name starts with one of `[space] prefixes`, and a file addressed by id must lie in the configured space, whose live name must start with one too. A file's space is found by walking its `parent_id` links through the provider up to the configured space's root (the `fileId` of the provider's `GET /spaces/{id}`), at most `client.MAX_SPACE_DEPTH` (64) links; a walk that ends above every known root or past the bound resolves to no space and nothing is sent (`OutOfScope`). Roots and parent links are kept as the provider's that gave them, since a file's space does not change: at most `client.MAX_KEPT_LINKS` links (least recently used dropped first, all dropped by `close()`), and a walk that passes the bound over kept links walks once more, every link read anew, before it refuses. The space's name is read from Onezone again before every request. Every read of the gate is the client's own, with the admin credential and the platform's `[tls]`, never through the data gate, so none counts as the agent's. A gate read that fails refuses the request unsent (`ScopeUnread`, keeping the read's status, error id and transport flag); `OutOfScope`, `ScopeUnread` and `MintNotSent` are `NotSent`, which a caller tells from an answer of the platform.
- The day-0 checks report unknown, never passed, for what they did not measure: `read_only` passes only when every supporting provider refused a write it was sent (401, 403, or exactly 400 `posix` `eacces`, as Onedata 25.0 refuses it), and a write that was never sent (the gate refused it or could not check it, or the space root could not be read) makes it unknown with the reason; a write answered otherwise is unknown with its status, error id and errno.
- The day-0 check `provider_outage` (O28, `day0/outage.py`) takes a provider down and records what reads and the knowledge plane do meanwhile. It runs per space (`per_space`, positions `spaces[i]`) with three different providers of the space's support set: the *target*, its data provider (the entry's `holder`, else `[placement] holder`), a *mirror* that holds a replica, and the agent's *local* provider, through which every read is made; a data provider that is the local one, or a support set of two, makes it unknown with that reason. It needs `EXPCORE_LIVE_MUTATE=1`, `EXPCORE_LIVE_FAULTS=1` (`runner.faults_allowed`, exactly `1`) and `sanctioned = true` in the `[faults]` table of platform.toml (`fault_settings.load_faults`), and without any of them sends nothing. The provider is taken down by the fault injector (`day0/outage_fault.py` over `faults.FaultInjector`, the outage being the body of its `running`, so a cancelled run waits for a start still stopping the provider and clears in `finally`: journalled before the stop, recovered when left open and before anything is prepared (#178), restored only with a changed identity and once the provider serves, a stop of unknown outcome waited out), with the federation's lock held exclusively for the space's whole scenario (`admission.FederationLock`, purpose `outage_run`). For the same scenario `day0/outage_agent.py` leases a slot of `[slots]` and starts, through `SandboxPlatform`, a sandbox at the first `[sandbox.data_paths]` level that maps to the mount (its oneclient sidecar owning the mount) and the tool server of `Context.tool_condition` (`--tool-condition`; by default the first condition allowing `get_file_distribution`, `list_space_providers`, `schedule_file_replication` and `get_transfer`), with the slot's tool-server user and token. The mount is read in the agent's container (`day0/outage_mount.py`: one cell per batch, each read in a child process with the bound as its deadline, recording the bytes and hash, the seconds, the errno by name and number and its exception class, and whether it blocked and was killed); the tools are called through MCP (`day0/outage_tools.py`: arguments filled from each tool's input schema, each call's outcome, seconds, timeout and attempts, a read-only tool called up to three times in all while it answers an error, an answer or a wording kept only as a SHA-256 prefix of it with every known name and id-like token replaced, its length and its error terms, never as text, since it can name what the check does not know). `Context` carries `allow_faults`, `fault_settings`, `fault_driver` (`build_context(driver=...)` hands in another than `compose_driver`), `outage_bound_s` (`--outage-bound-s`, 60), `agent_host` (where the sandbox may start: a `DockerHost` when `EXPCORE_LIVE_DOCKER=1` and the file has `[sandbox]`, else none), `agent_options` (keywords of the `SandboxPlatform`), `tool_condition` and `outage_ceiling_s` (the ceiling the sandbox's and the tool server's tokens are minted for; by default the longest the scenario can need them, `day0/outage_budget.py`: the injector's start and clear, every cell and tool call at its bound, the wait for the target and each re-read at `settle_timeout_s`, and a 600 s margin; a step that would use them after they surely ended is not made, and is the check's own failure, never the provider's). The scenario is a directory of scratch files in pairs, each pair read one way so that no read warms another's: `baseline`, `rest`, `knowledge`, and with a declared mount `baseline_mount` and `mount`; in each pair a *replicated* file (held by the target and the mirror) and an *owner_only* one (held by the target alone). The facts are `roles`, `agent` (the sandbox and the tool server started, or why not), `mount` (`{"declared": false}`, or measured or not with the reason), `knowledge` (measured, the missing tools), `held_before`, `baseline` (`rest`, `mount`, `knowledge`; each must serve or nothing is stopped), `outage` (the injector's start: `started`, `stop_sent`, the identity digest before, `seen_down`, and `rest_answers_while_down`, which must be false), `admission`, `during` (`rest`, `mount`, `knowledge` with `get_file_distribution`, `list_space_providers`, `schedule_file_replication`, which holds each `get_transfer`, and `from_where`, the `get_file_distribution` of each file read over REST and through the mount, by its pair and kind), `restore` (`cleared`, `verified`, `how` in the injector's words, `identity_changed`, `ready_after_s`, the injector's wait from the identity change to serving, `rest_answers_again`), `after` (the reads again, the transfers: through the mount, both files, `mount_owner_only` and `mount_replicated`, each of which must return what was written), `earlier_injection` (only when the injector's recovery settled an injection an earlier run left open before the space was prepared: `recovered`, each with its provider's position, the injector's word and `ready_after_s`) and `cleanup_verified`. Every cell has `seconds`, `bound_s` and an `outcome` of `served`, `partial`, `differs`, `refused`, `no answer` or `blocked`; the check is failed when the target answers while down, is not brought back or restored, does not answer REST again, leaves scratch, or the sandbox's teardown or the tool plane's check at its stop fails; unknown when anything could not be set up or measured (a declared mount not read, a knowledge tool not called), and passed only when every cell was measured, whatever the cells answered. A space whose provider is not shown back halts the later spaces, and so does one whose injector could not settle an injection an earlier run left open, or left one open (`restore.how` is `earlier_injection_open`, with `left_open` naming each such provider by its position, and the check failed): nothing of that space was prepared or stopped, but a provider may still be down. A start that failed with nothing journalled or open (a service that cannot be read before the stop) leaves the space unknown, `restore.how` `not_injected`, and halts nothing.
- An entry is absent only when a provider answers it with a 404 or exactly 400 `posix` `enoent` (`client.answered_absent`), as 25.0 answers a deleted entry; teardown and the day-0 clean-up stop as soon as their consecutive reads show it, and the waits for a fresh space root and for a transfer not shown yet wait on it. Any other answer, another 400 or an unreadable body included, is never absence: teardown and the clean-up read on to their bound and report the entry left, and the waits raise at once. The absence is the provider's that was read; a directory's delete may still be under way on another provider or on storage (#114). Every raw body a mutation sends is marked `application/octet-stream`, which 25.0 requires of a content upload. A provider sees a file written through another only some seconds later on 25.0, so provisioning waits, bounded by `consistency.VISIBLE_TIMEOUT_S`, until it reports the file at its size before reading or scheduling a transfer through it (`Provisioner.await_visible`); past the bound `NotVisible` names the provider by its position, and any answer other than absence fails closed. An eviction counts only when the block map shows the provider empty; one that ended while bytes remain is scheduled again, bounded (`provision.EVICTION_ATTEMPTS`). Placement records per file what the block map holds beside what was asked for, since a provider may place more than a range read asks.
- Every answer that is no file's content is buffered whole up to 4 MiB, so each is bounded by the request: in the paged mode, a listing whose explicit `limit` (in the query or a JSON body) is above `gatepolicy.MAX_LIST_LIMIT`, 1000, the server's own default page, is refused before anything is forwarded, as a 400 the agent reads; a listing without a limit passes unchanged. In the complete mode the gate reads pages of 1000 itself, each buffered so, and merges them into one answer of at most 64 MiB, one listing at a time. The worst case of a page of 1000, the bound on the other non-content answers and the gate's memory for both are worked out in `gatepolicy.py` and `gate.py` (O05, O16, O21).
- Every line of the gate's log for an answer says what its body was (`body`: `content`, `listing`, `other` or the gate's own, `gate`) and how many of its bytes the gate's socket accepted (`body_bytes`), and every answer of a file's content carries the platform's `Content-Range` as it sent it (`content_range`: a 206's range, which says where in the file its bytes begin, and null on a 200, which sends none), beside the platform's `status` and the bytes the gate delivered (`bytes`), so a line tells a whole answer from a range and a range from one clipped at the file's end (O37); the count of the bytes served, per run, per file and per directory, is taken from those (O22).
- **How long a relayed request took (O73):** the line of a request the gate relayed to an upstream carries `duration_ms` (whole milliseconds, rounded up, from the gate's reading of the request, its head already parsed, to the last byte of the answer relayed or to the failure; a complete listing's pages are one request) and, of the upstream attempt that waited longest for an answer, `upstream_wait_ms` (from just before its request went, so a new connection's connect and handshake are not in it, or from the start of its connect when that fails, to its first answer byte, or to its failure when none came), `upstream_wait_conn` (`new` or `reused`) and `upstream_wait_end` (`first_byte` or `failure`), beside `upstream_conn`, which stays the connection of the last attempt. A kept connection that hangs and is dropped with no byte is retried once on a new one (`upstream_retries`), so that request ends on `new` and its long wait is on `reused`: `upstream_wait_*` is where the time went. A request the gate refused itself and a request the parser refused reached no upstream and carry none of it. A stall of the body after its head shows as a `duration_ms` far beyond `upstream_wait_ms`. The fields are whole numbers and fixed words, never a body, a header's value or a name, and the log is where it was and as protected (the Apptainer gate's directory is mode 0700 and its file 0600, the Docker gate's log is bound into the gate's own container alone, and no cell binds it on either), so nothing is new to an agent. A request still being answered when the gate stops writes no line: the teardown line of its connection (`conn_close`, `ended` "teardown", `in_request` true) carries `request_age_ms`, how long it had waited, and the summary below is then unknown.
- **The gate's upstream connections (O85):** the gate keeps one upstream connection per client connection and reuses it for the next request, except after a request that carried a body. When the body the gate handed to the upstream was not empty (any method; the scope inference's rewritten body and each page of a complete listing that has a body count; a request with `Content-Length: 0` or none does not), the gate closes that upstream connection once the answer is read to its end, whatever the status, and the next request opens a new one (`upstream_conn` `new`). The request's line carries `upstream_closed` `"after_body"` when this rule closed it; a connection the upstream closed itself, or the gate dropped because the answer failed, is not marked. The rule decides only whether a connection is reused: the request is sent once, on the connection it was on, and nothing about retries (`upstream_retries` is unchanged), statuses, bodies, headers or timeouts changes. It is the same gate code on the Docker and the Apptainer backend, so an agent sees the same either way. The reason is a stall measured on 17 Nov 2026 directly against two Onedata providers with Python's `http.client` and no `Expect` header: after a request with a body of about 1.5 MiB (a PUT), the next request on the same kept connection (a GET or a POST) waited until the client's timeout in about half the tries, while a new connection answered in milliseconds; bodies of 400 KiB and of 4 MiB never stalled the next request. It is the stall the client already avoids for large content writes (O25), and the gate would have waited on it for its upstream read timeout (14,400 s by default; `[sandbox.gate]`, below). The gate itself relays no write: its policy refuses everything but the reads and the two lookup POSTs, and a body over `MAX_BODY` (1 MiB) is refused unread, so no body of 1.5 MiB reaches an upstream through it. What does reach one (a lookup's or a scope inference's body, and any allowed call that declares a body, at most 1 MiB) is closed after, in the size of the band or not: the gate does not guess a band. The cost is a connect and a handshake for the request after each such request; a body-less request keeps the reuse.
- **The gate's upstream read timeout (O85):** `[sandbox.gate] upstream_read_timeout_s` (optional, on both backends, a number of seconds from 1 to 86,400, refused with no rest data path, where no gate runs) is how long the gate waits for the upstream's next byte of an answer before it gives the read up. A platform that does not declare it, or declares `14400`, has the gate's constant, 14,400 s (4 h, the official client's own read timeout), so the gate never gives up before the client, and a hung read can last 4 h unless the run's own limits end it first; a shorter value makes the gate the one that ends it, after that long, and a value above 14,400 s changes nothing an agent sees, since the client gives up first. A read the gate gives up is, as it always was, a 502 `badGateway` of origin `gate` (an answer already begun is reset instead), counted under `gate_5xx` or `gate_resets` in the run's gate errors and never retried. The sandbox writes the value into the gate's configuration on both backends (Docker's `gate.json`, Apptainer's `gaterun.config`, where `gate_options` may not set the key) and every REST run records what its gate was given as `infrastructure_facts.gate_upstream_read_timeout_s` (the effective number, the default's included, read back from the file; a run with no gate has none). The campaign fingerprint holds it as `sandbox.gate.upstream_read_timeout_s`, a float, only when it is not the default (as `content_check_provider` is), so a platform that declares nothing keeps the fingerprint it had before the key, a resume or a merge under another value is refused, and a value declared as `14400` or `14400.0` is no change (C06). The value is the timeout of the gate's upstream socket, so it bounds each wait of an upstream read, and a send to the upstream, for the REST path (the gate's plain TCP forwards are not given one, and neither backend configures any). A gate that is handed a value that is no positive finite number refuses to start; the bounds of 1 and 86,400 s are the platform loader's. The connect timeout (`upstream_connect_timeout_s`, 60 s) is a separate gate constant that this table does not declare.
- `exec(code, timeout_s, record=False)` is a step of the harness's own, a check before the agent's loop, and it runs before the agent's run only: it raises `SandboxError` once `mark_run_start` was called or one of the agent's cells has begun (its `exec` entered, not ended), since after the mark its requests would count as the agent's, and between the agent's cells it would take a cell's refusals and kills. It runs as the agent's user in the agent's container, as a cell does, and is recorded under `metadata()["harness_steps"]` (`step`, `status`, `exit_code`, `duration_s`, `timed_out`, `bound`, `gate_refusals`, and `earlyoom_kills`, the host killer's kills of the run read when the step ended, `None` when its journal could not be read), never as a cell of the agent's (`metadata()["cells"]` counts the agent's alone). The gate's refusals while it ran and the host killer's kills are its own and never put on the agent's first cell, and that cell reads its memory counters afresh, so a kill under the step is never the cell's. Its only time limit is the backstop `timeout` in the container, a second after `timeout_s`: the harness's timer, which would stop every process of the agent's, is not started, and no kill cause is read (O24). A step that backstop killed is `timed_out` all the same, and `bound` says so (O39): the result's `bound` and the step's are `"container_timeout"` (the backstop; `"cell_timer"` is the harness's timer, which only a cell of the agent's has, and `"host_call"` is `docker exec` not returning, which is `timed_out` as well), and `None` exactly when `timed_out` is false. The backstop leaves nothing but its exit code, 137 (GNU `timeout -s KILL`; 124 from a `timeout` that reports its kill so), which a step that kills itself (`os.kill(os.getpid(), 9)`) exits with too, so the time decides: a step that ran the whole of its limit (`timeout_s` plus the second) and ended with one of those codes was ended by the bound, and one that ended sooner, or with any other code, 0 included, was not, so it reads as its own exit or crash. A step that kills itself within the start-up time of `docker exec` (a fraction of a second) before the backstop would have is the one case misread, as the backstop's. A cell of the agent's (`record=True`) is unchanged: its `timed_out` is its timer's record (O17, O34), and its `bound` is `"cell_timer"`, or `"host_call"` when the host gave up.
- A killed cell's cause comes from the cgroup counters, the kernel log, the host killer's journal and the container's state, never from the exit code. The agent's own limit is its outcome; a failure of the host or a component raises `InfraError("sandbox")`; a kill nothing attributes raises `InfraError("unattributed_kill")`, which the core retries within the attempt limit (O17). Errors the agent causes through the mount (ENOENT, EROFS, EISDIR) are not counted against the platform (O18).
- A message of any exception names roles, keys and positions, never a configured value (image, pool, path, login, level) or the tail of a tool's stderr (which goes to the log).

Not carried over from the earlier study: the gate's whole-file mode, the tracer and its checks, the capacity probes tied to two data layouts, and the replication branch of its fourth condition. Its merged-listing mode is the complete listing above, written anew against this gate with bounds and checks the earlier one lacked.

**The host's readings on Docker (O87; `sandbox.hostfacts`).** A Docker run's `metadata()["infrastructure_facts"]` holds the key `node`, with the readings of the host that runs the agent's container, in the shape and under the name an Apptainer run keeps its node's (`{"os": {"id", "version_id"}, "glibc", "kernel"}`, with `unknown` beside them for a reading that cannot be made), so a comparison of two shards on the two backends reads one place. They are the driver's own machine's, as `host.kernel` is (`HostOps.host_readings`; `DockerHost` reads this machine with the same reader as the node's): an engine reached over the network is not read. Recorded only: a host whose reading raises, answers in another shape, or has no such method is `unknown` and the run starts all the same. `metadata()["host"]` (`kernel`, `docker_server`) is as it was, and the block has no `facts` list and no `tool_server_host`: the tool server runs on that host. It holds two keys beside `node`: `cell_hostname` (O73, below), and `resolv_conf_sha256` (O56), the hash of the `/etc/resolv.conf` a cell read at the start, on both backends alike.

### The Apptainer backend on an HPC batch node

`expcore_onedata.sandbox.apptainer` (O56). `[sandbox] backend = "apptainer"` makes `SandboxPlatform` build `ApptainerSandbox` and prove a lease's sandbox gone with `apptainer.runs.ApptainerProbe`. The platform's `host` is then the node (`apptainer.node.NodeOps`; `LocalNode` on a real node). The class answers everything the platform and the code tool ask of a sandbox, as `DockerSandbox` does, and the egress instrument's source besides:

```toml
[sandbox]
backend = "apptainer"
work_root = "/node/tmpfs/dir"       # an existing 0700 directory of the driver's, not a link
content_check_file = "dir/f"
content_check_provider = "owner"    # optional (O87): the provider the content check reads through, as on Docker
[sandbox.limits]                    # all kept (limits.py): the watch's bounds, the cell's prlimit and taskset
cpus = 2.0
memory_bytes = 8589934592
pids = 512
nofile = 4096
tmp_bytes = 1073741824
shm_bytes = 1073741824
work_disk_bytes = 4294967296
[sandbox.gate]                      # optional (O85), as on Docker: with a rest level only
upstream_read_timeout_s = 14400     # 1 to 86400: how long the gate waits for the upstream's next byte; the default
[sandbox.data_paths]
via-gate = "rest"
[sandbox.apptainer]
image = "agent.sif"
image_sha256 = "<64 hex>"
egress_shim = "egresslog.so"        # deploy/hpc-federation/bundle/egresslog.c, built for the image
egress_shim_sha256 = "<64 hex>"
egress_collector = "egress-collector.py"
egress_collector_sha256 = "<64 hex>"
readlog_collector_sha256 = "<64 hex>"   # optional (O91): the SHA-256 of the core's own readlog/collector.py; declared, the read-log channel is on
hidden_memory_margin_bytes = 134217728   # optional, 1 MiB to 64 GiB: the default
provider_storage = "tmpfs"          # optional (O57): where the federation's providers keep their data, "tmpfs" or "shared_filesystem"
work_disk = "directory"             # optional (O71): the default, or "image" for /work as an ext3 image
work_disk_mkfs = ["mkfs.ext3"]      # optional: the node's mkfs.ext3 (e2fsprogs 1.43 or later), for "image": ONE program
                                    # word, a bare name or an absolute path, never an option, a second word or a relative
                                    # path such as ./x, ../x or a/b (refused at load); the image is ext3 whatever it is called
```

- **Its egress:** `EgressLog(sandbox, sandbox.egress_declaration(), sandbox.egress_nonce)`. The declaration names the gate at `127.0.0.1:<its port>`, the canary's listener, and the gate's names. `egress_log()` returns the collector's log once `collect` has stopped the collector, and None before that.
- **Its read log** (O91, `apptainer.readlogrun`): with `readlog_collector_sha256` declared, the run has the read-log channel ("The read-log channel"). The pin is the SHA-256 of the core's own collector file, so a core whose collector has another hash refuses the start (`StartRefused`, before anything exists); it is in the backend's record only when declared (another collector is another experiment, and a platform that declares none keeps its fingerprint and has no channel: no helper, no directory, no bind, `read_log()` None).
  - **Started first:** the collector runs as a second helper (role `readlog`, beside the egress collector) before any cell, as `python3 -I`, told the run's nonce and the uid of the cells: the driver's own, which every cell runs as. Its copy, its log and its streams are in `readlog/` of the run's directory, its socket in an empty `readlog-sock/` and the client module's copy in `readlog-client/`, each 0700, the copies read-only and hashed against their sources.
  - **In every cell:** `readlog-sock/` is bound read-only at `/expcore-readlog` and `readlog-client/` at `/expcore-readlog-client` (`readlog.paths.TARGETS`), in every `apptainer exec` of `cell_argv`, the start's canaries and probes included; `cell_argv` is the only builder of the execs that run the agent's code. The harness's one-shot execs of the image (the certifi probe, the trust-bundle reads, the collection's copy helper) run programs of the harness alone and carry no channel binds, as they carry no egress shim. Nothing else of the channel is bound, so the log and the collector are out of every cell's reach. Both targets are the sandbox's own: a `binds` entry (refused at load) and a read-only input of a study (refused at the declaration and at the start, on both backends: `inputs.taken_paths`) may not be, hold or lie under either.
  - **The canary:** a cell sends the canary through the real client, so it is the log's first record; a start whose cell cannot send it fails, and its collector is stopped.
  - **The collection:** `collect` stops the collector on SIGTERM (it drains what is queued and writes its end line), reads its log once, copies it into the evidence directory as `read-log.jsonl` (`result["read_log"]`, None when it could not be read) and scans the copy for the data-path token as every file of evidence: a hit removes the copy and fails the collection. The copy is the file the executor stages as the study's declared side file (`stage_harness_side_file(request, side_files, name, Path(dest) / result["read_log"])`), and holds the very bytes `read_log()` returns, so the instrument's `side_sha256` is the staged file's hash. `read_log()` then returns the bytes (None before the collection, when the log could not be read, and without the channel), and the instrument is `ReadLog(sandbox, sandbox.readlog_nonce, sandbox.readlog_expected_peer_uid)`. `metadata()["readlog_stop"]` says how the stop went.
  - **A collector that died:** `infra_check()` names the role `readlog` and a death is infrastructure, as the egress collector's. A collector that was killed wrote no end line, so its log reads UNKNOWN (`not_closed`), never as a run that read nothing, and is kept as evidence. A collector that ended BEFORE the collection stopped it (told to end by something else, it writes a clean end line), or whose stop was not clean (it needed the kill, or could not be shown gone: `metadata()["readlog_stop"]` holds `ran_until_stop`, `ended_on_term` and any `error`), has a log that would read intact with the reads it took and none after, since the client raises once the socket is gone; that log is withheld (`read_log()` None, `collect()`'s `read_log` None, no copy), so it reads `log_missing`: unknown, never a clean log.
  - **Facts:** `infrastructure_facts.read_log` holds the collector's and the client's hashes, `placement` `apptainer_helper` and `expected_peer_uid`, written once through the one writer (owner `readlog`, O89).
  - **Not in the egress log:** the egress shim, preloaded in every cell, logs and gates IPv4 and IPv6 destinations only and passes a Unix socket through, so the client's send neither appears in the egress log nor can be refused by it; the egress log's first record stays its TCP canary.
  - **Limits:** a script that calls the client directly, or sends to the socket, forges a read: it adds a line and removes none, and the read-log scan (and on REST the gate's per-file table) is what detects it. Whether an unprivileged user namespace in a cell can over-mount the bound socket directory on a node is not excluded here: the live slot proves it.
- **Hook (a):** `[sandbox.apptainer.gate_options]` adds keys to the gate's configuration. A key the sandbox writes itself is refused. The gate's peer-uid check (`peer_uid_check`, #317) is on by default whenever the gate's directory holds `gatepeer.py`, and `metadata()["gate"]["peer_uid_check"]` says whether it was asked for.
- **Hook (b):** `preload = [{ path, sha256 }]` lists libraries each cell preloads before the egress shim. A connect hook rewrites first and calls the next `connect`, so the shim logs the destination actually dialled. `binds = [{ path, sha256, target }]` adds read-only files. A REST cell also gets `/etc/expcore/gate.json` (`{"address", "port", "names"}`) and `EXPCORE_GATE_PORT`.
- **Hook (c):** `ApptainerSandbox(..., mount_helper=factory)` with `apptainer.mounthook.MountHelper` (`start() -> Path` bound read-only at `[sandbox.mount] path`, then `stop()`, then `metadata()`). `hostmount.host_mount_factory()` makes the shipped one. Without a factory, or with no `[sandbox.mount]` in the platform's file, a mount run raises `StartRefused(MOUNT_REFUSED)` before anything exists; the shipped helper then refuses `io_mode = "direct"` (`StartRefused` at its start), so HPC mounts are proxy-only until the direct-I/O comparison passes.
- **What `/proc` hides** (`apptainer.hidden`, O56). Under `hidepid=invisible` a cell process that makes itself non-dumpable has no `/proc` entry, so the watch cannot count or kill it. At every poll the watch reads `anon` of the `memory.stat` of the driver's own cgroup (`NodeOps.own_cgroup`, `cgroup_anon`) before and after its listing, and subtracts the RssAnon of every process of the uid the listing shows in that cgroup or below (`Proc.anon`, `Proc.cgroup`). The rest is memory nothing visible explains. Its growth during a cell, from the last reading with no cell's PID namespace in the cgroup (never below 0), past `hidden_memory_margin_bytes` for the running cell and once more for each other run's cell in the cgroup (a namespace of the run's own adds none), for `HIDDEN_CONFIRM` (2) polls in a row, each confirmed by a second listing taken after the cgroup's second reading, the visible memory being the larger of the two listings' totals (so a worker that frees memory and takes it again around a read is never taken for hidden memory, and two processes that hold memory in turn are never counted twice):
  - with no other run's cell in the cgroup, it kills the run's processes once per cell as `memory_limit` with `what: "memory_hidden"`, `amount` the growth, `limit` the allowance and `figures` (`cgroup_anon_bytes`, `visible_anon_bytes`): memory the watch cannot see is taken as past the limit;
  - with another run's cell there, it cannot be told this cell's, so the cell cannot be isolated (O72): the watch kills the cell's processes and ends the run, once, recording the kill with `scope: "run"`, `attributed: false` and its `why`; the cell's `kill` is `memory_limit` of category `infrastructure`, so the code tool raises `InfraError("cell_not_isolated")` (`kinds.CELL_NOT_ISOLATED`, untreatable and charged as `sandbox` is, counted apart in the export and the report), every later `exec` and `collect` raise it too (`kinds.run_ended_error`), and the reading is `unknown` (`unattributed`), never `over`, the run not counted as hiding, since the memory may be the other run's. Every run whose watch sees such a growth ends so, and says so as a fact of its own: `metadata()["infrastructure_facts"]["run_ended"]` holds `scope: "run"`, `attributed: false`, `reason`, `what` and `why`, and is absent from a run the watch did not end;
  - after a start whose own cells (the canary and the read-only probe, `begin_cell(..., calibration=True)`) grew past the margin, it is `margin_exceeded_at_start`, which is no kill.

  A pid that a visible process of the running cell's `children` files list and `/proc` does not show as the uid's while a process holds it, confirmed by a second read (`NodeOps.hidden_children`), is a `hidden_process`: recorded, never a kill. A double fork under the cell's hidden init is no visible process's child, so this supplements the cgroup's check and never replaces it.
- **The memory reading** (`Watch.reading()`, in `metadata()["watch"]["memory"]` and `metadata()["infrastructure_facts"]["memory"]`):
  - `reading`: `over` once a kill of the watch's for memory reached a process; `unknown` with every reason that held (`hidden_process`, `unattributed`, `margin_exceeded_at_start`, `cgroup_unreadable` for a v1 host or a failed read, `proc_unreadable`, `children_unreadable`, `kill_reached_none`, for a kill of either kind that signalled no process); otherwise `within`;
  - `unknown`, `margin_bytes`, `start_unexplained_bytes`, `peak` (`unexplained_bytes`, `cgroup_anon_bytes`, `visible_anon_bytes`, `baseline_bytes`, `allowance_bytes`), `hidden_processes`, `hidden_memory_cells`, `children_unreadable_polls` and `cgroup_unreadable_polls`.

  Each cell's record has its own (`watched.memory`). `process_visibility()` gives the same reading to the `process_visibility` instrument. A hidden process's shared memory (a memfd, a shared anonymous mapping) is not compared, since the cgroup counts it with the run's tmpfs files.
- **The limits** (`apptainer.limits`, `limitspoll`, O70; `metadata()["infrastructure_facts"]["limits"]`, beside `["memory"]`, also in the collected `metadata.json`), every key always present:

  ```
  {"version": 1,
   "cpus": {"declared": float, "reading": "pinned" | "widened" | "unknown", "unknown": [reason...],
            "effective_set": [int...] | null, "effective_count": int | null, "step_set": [int...] | null,
            "widened": bool, "widened_processes": int},
   "pids": {"declared": int, "reading": "within" | "over" | "unknown", "unknown": [...], "max_seen": int | null, "kills": int},
   "nofile": {"declared": int, "reading": "set" | "unknown", "unknown": [...], "hard_limit": int | null},
   "gate_memory": {"declared": int, "reading": "within" | "over" | "unknown" | "not_applicable", "unknown": [...],
                   "peak_bytes": int | null}}
  ```

  - **nofile:** every cell command starts `prlimit --nofile=N:N -- ...` (`limits.prefix`), so the cell holds a hard and soft `RLIMIT_NOFILE` of `limits.nofile` it cannot raise. The start's canary prints `resource.getrlimit(RLIMIT_NOFILE)` and `os.sched_getaffinity(0)` from inside a cell (`limits.CANARY`); a hard limit that is not the declared one fails the start with `SandboxError`, and so does a `prlimit` or `taskset` that cannot run (named). `reading` is `set` only when the canary showed the declared hard limit; `hard_limit` is what it showed.
  - **cpus:** the prefix continues `taskset -c <cores>`: the first `ceil(cpus)` of the driver's own affinity (`NodeOps.own_affinity`, the step's cores, `step_set`), all of them when `cpus` is more, none when they could not be read. `effective_set` is the canary's reading inside a cell and `effective_count` its length. At every poll the watch reads the union of each task's `Cpus_allowed_list` for every process of the run (`NodeOps.task_affinity`; `sched_setaffinity` is per thread) and counts a process outside the effective set in `widened_processes`, never killing it (until the canary has answered, against the pin). `reading` is `widened` when one was seen or the canary's set is wider than the pin, `pinned` only when none was and nothing below made it unknown.
  - **pids:** at every poll the watch sums `Threads:` (`Proc.threads`) of the run's processes into `max_seen` and over `limits.pids` kills them as `pids_limit` (`scope: "cell"`, `what: "pids"`, `amount` the count; `kills` counts those that signalled a process), `over` once one did.
  - **gate_memory:** at every poll the watch reads the gate helper's `RssAnon` and `RssShmem` (`peak_bytes`) and over `limits.gate_memory_bytes` SIGKILLs it through its pidfd, recorded in `metadata()["watch"]["gate_kills"]` and not among the cells' `kills`; `over` once one signalled it. `Collection.deaths()` then gives the gate's death `cause: "component_defect"` (infrastructure, kind `sandbox`) and a basis naming the watch, only for a death that signal caused. `not_applicable`: a run with no gate (no REST data path).
  - **Reasons** (`unknown`): `affinity_unreadable` (a task's cores could not be read), `step_set_unreadable`, `proc_unreadable` (a listing of `/proc` failed during a cell, a process's status had no task count, or no process was ever counted), `hidden_process` (the run's memory reading holds `hidden_process`, or a cell was killed for hidden memory: `/proc` hides such a process's tasks and cores), `probe_unreadable` (the canary's line did not come back whole), `gate_unreadable` (the gate's memory could not be read), `kill_reached_none` (the watch's kill signalled no process). A shown `over` or `widened` outweighs them; an `unknown` reading never reads as within, pinned or set.
- **No name server:** every cell binds the neutral `/etc/resolv.conf` a Docker agent binds too (`cellresolv.RESOLV_TEXT`: no `nameserver`, `search` or `domain` line, only `options attempts:1 timeout:1`) and a `/etc/nsswitch.conf` whose databases are `files` (`argv.NSSWITCH_TEXT`), read-only; a hook (b) bind onto either is refused. So the C library resolves the names of `/etc/hosts` alone, and `infrastructure_facts.dns` is `"none"`. A program with a resolver of its own can still send a query, which is egress like any other. On Docker the agent's network is `--internal` with `--add-host` entries and no `--dns`, and since O56 the sandbox binds the same file there (the Docker bullet "A cell's `/etc/resolv.conf` is the sandbox's own"). Before that bind the agent's file was the engine's, which names the embedded resolver (127.0.0.11) and copies the host's `search` line; measured on engine 29.2.1 with it, the embedded resolver answered every outside name with SERVFAIL at once, the `--add-host` names resolved only through `/etc/hosts`, and a connect to an outside address failed at once with ENETUNREACH (no default route). Not measured with the bind: that the C library's lookup of an outside name asks the agent's own loopback, where nothing listens, and fails at once (a live slot's).
- **The cell's host name (O73; `sandbox/cellhost.py`):** every cell has one fixed, neutral host name, `sandbox`, the same string on both backends, so `hostname`, `uname -n`, `uname -a` and `/proc/sys/kernel/hostname` in a cell show it and never the batch node's own name (a cell without a UTS namespace of its own reads the node's). It is a constant of the core, not a setting: a study cannot change it and it is in no fingerprint. Every `apptainer exec` carries `--uts --hostname sandbox` (`argv.exec_argv`: the agent's cells, the canary and the harness's one-shot reads of the image alike). The sandbox binds no `/etc/hostname` of its own: by Apptainer's source (`main`, not the site's release) `--uts --hostname` makes Apptainer write one of the name and bind it, and it never binds the node's; a hook (b) bind onto it is refused, since it would replace the name. The Docker agent's container is created with `--hostname sandbox` (`containers.agent_run_args`), where `hostname` would otherwise read the engine's container id, and the engine's `/etc/hostname` follows `--hostname` (its usual behaviour; not measured here); the gate's and the sidecar's containers keep the engine's own. `infrastructure_facts.cell_hostname` is the name the cells were given, added when `metadata()` is read on both backends (a later record of the facts cannot drop it), and no reading from inside a cell. The name must not be a node's: a start whose `SandboxSpec.node_names` holds `sandbox`, or a fully qualified name whose first label is `sandbox`, in any case, is refused with a `StartRefused` that names no node. It is not added to the gate's scan for node names (`scan_names`): the word is common, so it would be counted and redacted in every answer that holds it. The scan holds instead, after the federation's nodes' names, the agent node's own host name in its fully qualified and one-label forms, read from the node at the start (`NodeOps.own_names`, `cellhost.scan_names`), so a leak of the name the cell no longer shows is still counted; a name the federation declares, `localhost` and the cell name are left out of it, and a run with no names to scan (a federation whose names are the real ones) scans none and does not ask the node. The fully qualified names are a resolver lookup (`gethostbyaddr` of the host name: its primary name and every alias, each scanned whole and by its first label), so it is bounded (`loopback.FQDN_TIMEOUT_S`, 5 s, in a daemon thread): on a timeout or a failure the host name is scanned alone, and `infrastructure_facts.own_node_scan` (`{version, forms, unread}`, only for a run that scans, added when `metadata()` is read) says how many forms were scanned and, by reason alone (`fqdn_timeout`, `fqdn_failed`, `host_name_failed`, and `fqdn_unqualified` when no name the scan keeps has a domain: a loopback alias or a declared name does not give one), what was not read; never a name. A start with node names to scan, on an agent node whose own host name is `sandbox`, or a fully qualified name with that first label, is refused with a `StartRefused` that names no node, before anything is created. **A known difference:** Docker's engine adds the container's own host name to the container's `/etc/hosts` (its usual behaviour; not measured here), where this backend's generated `/etc/hosts` holds `localhost` and the federation's names, as before, so a program that resolves its own host name (`socket.gethostbyname(socket.gethostname())`) finds no address on this backend, as it found none for the node's name; this change leaves that as it was. What was measured on the site, and what a live slot still owes, is in `deploy/hpc-federation/README.md` ("The cell's host name").
- **The names the agent sees (O69):** `[sandbox.federation]` takes `zone` and `providers.<local>` on this backend, and nothing else (`network` is refused, there being none to join), only with a rest level, and with two different names, kept in lower case as the gate matches `Host` and SNI. With it, the cell's `ONEDATA_ONEZONE_HOST`, its `/etc/hosts` (the names on 127.0.0.1, which the cell dials on 443), `/etc/expcore/gate.json`'s `names`, the gate's certificates and the run's egress declaration hold those names, and the gate dials the hosts and ports of the URLs of `[zone]` and the local provider behind them, checking their certificates there as `[tls]` says (`gaterun.hosts(zone, provider, names)`). So a cell on HPC sees the names its layout declares on 443, as a Docker agent on `[sandbox.federation]` does, and never a node's name or port 8443. Without the table the names are the URLs' hosts, as before. `metadata()["gate"]["names"]` is `declared` or `urls`, never a name. The gate's 502 for a failed upstream call names the host it dialled by the name it serves on 443 (`gate._as_served`; a name mismatch of the upstream's certificate quotes the dialled host), and its log line keeps the text as it was. What the platform's own answers carry is not changed: a provider's registered domain reaches the agent in the zone's scope answer (`providers.<id>.domain`) and in Onedata's no-connection errors. The published client dials the domain from the scope answer, so the table works only where the local provider registered its declared name. A provider still registered under its node's FQDN does not resolve in the cell, and the lookup's error names that FQDN.
- **Dialling a declared name (O73):** a federation whose layout's names resolve nowhere (the HPC federation's) gives `platform.toml` `[addresses] file = "<path>"`, the private map its `up` wrote (`expcore_onedata/addresses.py`: mode 0600, a regular file of yours, never a link, `{"format": "expcore-address-map/1", "hosts": {"<declared name>": {"address": "...", "nodes": ["<the node's own names>"]}}}`, a node's name or address under one declared name only). `PlatformConfig.addresses` holds it, out of the repr, the fingerprint and every record, and a refusal names the key. With it `Endpoint.from_url(url, addresses)` carries `address`, `Endpoint.dial` is where a connection goes, and the URL stays the declared name: `client.MappedTransport` (the platform layer's client and the bootstrap's) sends a copy of the request to the address with the declared name as its `Host` and `sni_hostname`, so the certificate is checked against the declared name; the gate's host entry gets `dial` beside `addr` (`gaterun.hosts`; `gate._upstream_conn` connects to `dial` and handshakes with `addr`), and `_as_served` names `dial` by the served name in a failure; the host mount's oneclient is started with `-H <address>`; the day-0 reachability check connects to it. None of the address reaches a cell, the gate's file or a record. A host with no entry is dialled as written. A Python tool server resolves the declared names through the map too: `[addresses] resolver` names the launcher `up` copied into the root (`resolve_launch.py`), and `toolserver.server_argv` starts the server as `<interpreter> -I -B <launcher> <map> -- <the declared args>`, so that `socket.getaddrinfo` maps exactly the map's names and every other host as before, before the server runs (`runpy`); a `[toolserver]` that is not a Python interpreter with `-m <module>` or a script is refused at load, a map the launcher may not use (readable by others, a link, garbled) stops the start (exit 78), and nothing of it is in the server's environment. No `ONEDATA_MCP_NODE_NAMES` is set on either platform. **What still gets through is counted:** a run given the map's real names (`SandboxSpec.node_names`) starts its gate with them as `scan_names` (its own configuration, never a cell's file), and the gate counts, in memory, the whole names and addresses in each non-content answer it relays and in its own, writing an integer per request (`node_name_hits`; no body, matched name or context; a file's bytes are data and are not scanned); the run's `infrastructure_facts.rest_node_name_hits` is `{version: 1, rest_answers, unknown}`, the sum once the gate is stopped and its log whole, `rest_answers` `None` with the reasons otherwise (`gatelog.node_name_hits`), and absent on a run given no names. The tool server's answers do not pass the gate and are not counted.
- **The work disk** (`apptainer.workdisk`, O71). With `work_disk = "directory"`, the default, `/work` is a directory of the run's on the node's tmpfs, and the watch kills the cell past `work_disk_bytes` (`disk_limit`). With `work_disk = "image"`, `/work` is a file system of its own, so a write past it is refused with ENOSPC and nothing is killed, as on Docker:
  - **The image:** `<run>/work/disk.img`, a sparse file of `work_disk_bytes` rounded down to 2 MiB (a start below 2 MiB is refused), laid out by `work_disk_mkfs -t ext3 -q -F -d <tree>` from a tree of one directory of the driver's, `work`. `work_disk_mkfs` is the tool alone, one program word that is a bare name (no `/`) or an absolute path with no empty, `.` or `..` part: the file system's type and options shape the image, so a value with an option, a second word or a relative path (`./x`, `../x`, `a/b`, `.`, `..`, `/`) is refused at load, naming the key, and the tool is out of the campaign fingerprint (C06). The type is passed as `-t ext3` whatever the program is called, since e2fsprogs takes it from the program's name otherwise (`mke2fs` makes ext2, `mkfs.ext4` ext4), so that the tool, which the fingerprint leaves out, cannot change the image the agent writes to. Every cell binds that directory at `/work` (`-B <image>:/work:image-src=/work`, `argv.Bind(image_src=...)`), read/write. Apptainer mounts it: with the kernel's driver where the site's `apptainer.conf` allows `setuid-mount extfs`, with fuse2fs otherwise. `/work` holds no `lost+found`, which Docker's disk does. The image's pages count against the step's memory as they are written, up to its size. The watch still walks `<run>/work`, which holds the image alone, against `work_disk_bytes`.
  - **The proof, before the agent's first cell:** a cell writes zeros into `/work` until a write fails, at most one chunk past the image's size (`workdisk.PROBE`). The start goes on only for ENOSPC with more than half the size and at most the size written (`workdisk.judge`). No bound, a bound only at `fsync`, another error, or no answer raises `SandboxError` and the start fails. The probe also leaves a small file, `.expcore-copy-probe`, which the collection's own path must copy out byte for byte (`Collection.prove_image_copy`), or the start fails, so a run whose outputs could not be collected is never paid for. The probe fills the whole image, so the image is made again by the same command. A last cell then shows the new image to be a file system of its own, not `/tmp`'s, of more than half the size and at most the size (`workdisk.CHECK`). Until then `infrastructure_facts.facts` holds `work_disk_unverified`; after it, `work_disk_image`. `metadata()["work_disk"]` holds `mode`, `size_bytes`, `mkfs` (the first line of `mkfs -V`), `enospc_verified`, `held_bytes`, `filesystem_bytes` and `remade`. The image mode is in the backend's `record()` as `work_disk`, so the campaign fingerprint differs; the directory mode adds nothing, so a fingerprint made before the key existed is unchanged.
  - **Once per node and driver:** the full fill runs at the first start of a driver (a shard step: its pid and start time) on a boot of the node (`NodeOps.boot_id`) with a configuration (the image's SHA-256, the size, the mkfs and Apptainer commands and their versions). It keeps its proof in `<work_root>/_proofs/<key>.json`, written 0600 in a 0700 directory of the driver's on the node's tmpfs, which no cell binds (`apptainer.workproof`). A later start with the same key loads it, taking it only when it is a regular file of the driver's, 0600, with one link, that parses and whose SHA-256 of its fields holds. It then checks the invariants instead of filling: a cell leaves the copy probe's file and it must come out through the collection's path; the image is made again; its ext superblock, read on the host (`workdisk.superblock`), and the size and mount type of `/work` as a cell sees it (`/proc/self/mountinfo`) must be the proof's. A proof that is missing, unreadable, tampered with or of another key, or invariants that differ, make the full fill run again; a key with an unknown part, or a mount type a cell cannot name, keeps no proof. `metadata()["work_disk"]["proof"]` says which the start relied on: `kind` `full_fill` (with `full_fill_because`: `missing`, `unreadable`, `invariants_differ` or `unknown_configuration`, and `kept`) or `invariants`, with the proof's `id`, `proved_utc`, `held_bytes`, `size_bytes`, `filesystem_bytes` and `fstype`. `enospc_verified` is true only when the start filled itself.
  - **The collection:** the host cannot read the image without privilege, so `copyout.copy_tree` runs with its copy helper in one-shot execs of the image (`workdisk.ImageCopyHost`, through `copyout`'s `copy_helper` hook). Each exec binds the run's disk at `/work` and a staging directory at `/expcore-export`, binds no preload, and runs after the image's identity is checked. It carries a tag of its own (`EXPCORE_WORK_COPY=<sid>`, `workdisk.COPY_TAG`), never the run's, so the watch never takes it for the agent's (a `/tmp` the agent's last cell left past its size would otherwise kill the copy and fail the collection). The listing is the image's own, so every refusal is the same as on Docker, and the outputs land where Docker's collection puts them. Each exec mounts the image anew, so the helper compares each object with the device the root has in its own exec (`workdisk.wrapper_source`); the inode, kind and size are unchanged.
  - **Teardown and reaper:** both kill, and show gone, every process of the uid whose command line names the image or that carries the run's copy tag (`workdisk.end_holders`), listing again until a fresh listing shows none, and fail rather than pass when one stays. A process once found is waited for until it is shown gone, whatever a later listing can read of its command line; a zombie holds nothing and is not waited for. A run kept as evidence drops the image when the run had a token, since a raw image cannot be scanned for one (`evidence_kept.work_disk_image_dropped`).
- **How long the gate's requests took (O73):** a run whose data path is the gate has `infrastructure_facts.rest_request_durations` (the Apptainer backend; the Docker sandbox's gate writes the same fields to its log and has no such summary): `{version: 1, requests, by_connection: {new: {requests, over_10s, over_30s, over_60s}, reused: {...}}, max_duration_ms, unknown}`, from the stopped gate's log (`gatelog.request_durations`). Only a request relayed to an upstream is counted (the line names `upstream_conn`), those before the run's start mark included (the harness's own check of the data path); a connection is the one a request waited longest on (`upstream_wait_conn`), which is the one a request retried after a stall began on; `over_10s`, `over_30s` and `over_60s` count the requests that took strictly more than that, so they nest (a request of 61 s is in all three); `max_duration_ms` is the longest, `None` when no request was relayed and `unknown` is empty. As for the bytes served and the node-name count the summary is shown or unknown, never a partial sum taken for the whole: when the gate was not shown stopped, its log could not be read or written whole, a line's timing is no figure, a request was in flight at the stop or the log holds no `teardown_complete`, every count and `max_duration_ms` is `None` and `unknown` names why; before the collection it is `gate_not_collected`. The key is on every run with a gate, and absent on a run with none. It changes nothing the gate does: the timeouts, the retry and the reuse of connections are as before.
- **Completion causes** name `apptainer exec` (`Completion.for_source(code, runner="apptainer exec")`).
- **The readings after the loop** (`infra_check`, `mount_log`, `mount_errors`, O17, O18) are Docker's names and shapes; see "Reading a run's infrastructure after its loop" above. The collection's `deaths`, and `container_deaths` in the metadata, come from `infra_check()`, so a mount run's oneclient, its mount and the guard's watcher are deaths there too when they end during the run, as Docker's sidecar is. No per-process limit or OOM counter is kept for a process of the driver's, so such a death is `undetermined` infrastructure, and `kinds.death_error` makes the run `unattributed_kill`; the one exception is a gate the watch killed for its memory over `gate_memory_bytes` (O70), a `component_defect` of kind `sandbox`. A watcher that died also leaves the guard's log without its end line, which the mount guard reads as unknown (O60).
- **The tool server** (O56). `SandboxPlatform.tool_server` starts the run's tool server through the same launcher as on Docker (`toolserver.ToolServerLauncher`): a process of the driver's, spawned by the MCP client over stdio, outside every cell and in the node's PID namespace, which no cell can address, as on Docker it is a process on the host outside every container. The sandbox names where it runs (`ApptainerSandbox.tool_server_process()`, an `api.ServerProcess`), and the launcher then names its whole environment: `env -u` unsets each variable the MCP client would pass on from the driver (`LOGNAME`, `SHELL`, `TERM`, `USER`) before the command, so the server gets the launcher's settings (`PATH`, the hosts, the token, the allow-list, the TLS settings), `HOME`, `TMPDIR` and the run's tag (`EXPCORE_SANDBOX=<sid>`), and no value is ever an argument. `HOME`, and its working directory, are `toolserver/` of the run's directory (0700, refused if it is there, removed once the server has stopped, and by a teardown that keeps the run as evidence before it keeps it, since what the server wrote there may hold its token), and `TMPDIR` its `tmp/`, never the node's shared `/tmp`; the tag lets the next start's reaper kill a server a dead driver left, and the lease's probe hold the key while one lives, as for the gate. `extra_env` may not set `HOME`, `TMPDIR` or the tag. `metadata()["tool_server_process"]` records it by name: `{"on": "node", "home": "run_directory", "tmpdir": "home", "run_tag": true, "inherited": ["PATH"]}`. What the node must hold, since the driver runs there: `[toolserver] command` (an absolute path: the step's `PATH` holds no venv) with its interpreter and venv; the checkout `[toolserver] clone` at `commit`, when declared, and `git` on the `PATH` for its check (`pin.verify_pin`: `HEAD` is the commit, and no tracked file changed); and each condition's frozen manifest, which `platform.toml` names relative to itself. Of these only the checkout is pinned, by its commit, and only when declared; the manifest pins the tools the server shows (L13), not its code; the command, the venv and the interpreter are not hashed. `deploy/hpc-federation`'s templates render no `[toolserver]`, so a federation's `platform.toml` has none until a study's deployment adds it.
- **Facts** are listed by key in every run's `metadata()["infrastructure_facts"]` (`apptainer.facts.FACTS`): the departures from the Docker sandbox, and one fact that is no departure (`dns_none`):
  - the network is shared;
  - the gate is on the node's loopback;
  - the cell runs as the driver's uid;
  - memory and disks are kept by a watch, whose kill ends the cell (`scope: cell` on each kill and on the cell's `kill`) and never the run, as a container's OOM kill on Docker, though it kills every process of the cell where Docker's kernel kills the largest alone; a disk past its size kills the cell, where Docker's write fails with ENOSPC (`disk_watch`). With `work_disk = "image"` that is `/tmp` and `/dev/shm` alone (`disk_watch_tmp`), and `/work` refuses the write with ENOSPC as on Docker (`work_disk_image`);
  - what `/proc` hides is checked from the cgroup (`hidden_memory`);
  - a cell has no name server (`dns_none`; a Docker cell resolves the same, so no departure);
  - cpus is a set of cores where Docker's is a quota (`cpu_affinity`), pids a poll that kills where Docker refuses the fork with EAGAIN (`pids_watch`), and the gate's memory limit a poll (`gate_memory_watch`); nofile is kept as Docker's is and is no departure;
  - no freezer;
  - a mount is refused, `mount_refused`, listed only for a run whose sandbox refuses one (no mount helper, or no `[sandbox.mount]`): a mount run never carries it.

- **The node's OS release, glibc and kernel** (O87; `sandbox.hostfacts`). Beside that list, each run's `metadata()["infrastructure_facts"]["node"]` holds `{"os": {"id", "version_id"}, "glibc", "kernel"}`: the `ID` and `VERSION_ID` of `/etc/os-release`, the C library's own version string (`os.confstr("CS_GNU_LIBC_VERSION")`, such as `glibc 2.34`) and the kernel's release (`os.uname().release`), read when the run starts. `tool_server_host` has the same three for the host the tool server runs on, and is there only when they differ from the node's (`NodeOps.tool_server_readings`; `LocalNode` answers the node's own, since the server is the driver's child, so a run on a real node never carries it). They are recorded and never refused: a reading that cannot be made is the string `unknown`, with `unknown` beside the three naming each one and why (`os.id`, `os.version_id`, `glibc`, `kernel`: a missing or empty line, `not a plain value`, `not text`, or the type of the exception, never its message or a path), and a node whose reading raises or answers in another shape is `unknown` for that reason. The os-release file is read as text, its first 64 KiB, and never executed: a line is split by hand, a value loses one pair of quotes and must be plain characters, and the last assignment of a key wins. The HPC federation driver's node facts step (`bundle/nodefacts.py`) makes the same three readings on each node and the state file keeps them as `services.<role>.readings`, in the same shape (`hpcboot.load_readings`; a step that prints none, or none of this shape, is `unknown` for that reason and `up` goes on). The metadata's `host` (`kernel`, `apptainer`) is as it was. A Docker run records the same `node` (below, "The host's readings on Docker").

- **The providers' storage kind** (O57; `provider_storage`). Where a provider stores its data shapes a run's I/O timing, so `[sandbox.apptainer] provider_storage` declares it, `"tmpfs"` or `"shared_filesystem"` (any other word is refused at load, naming the key). The HPC driver renders it from `site.env` (`HPC_STORAGE_ROOT` set: `shared_filesystem`), the word and never the root's path. Declared, it is in `PlatformConfig.fingerprint()` (`sandbox_backend`), so shards on the two kinds never merge and a campaign never resumes from one onto the other, in each run's `metadata()["backend"]` and as `metadata()["infrastructure_facts"]["provider_storage"]`. A platform that declares none (the Docker backend, so a Compose federation, or an Apptainer platform written before the key) has no such key anywhere and keeps its fingerprint.

- **The federation's window** (O86; `federation_window`), in every run of a platform: from the run's preparation on it is `unknown` ("no federation watch configured", or pending for a platform built with `SandboxPlatform(..., federation_watch=)`), and the launch closes it once the run's body has ended: the verdict, each service's status and restarts, the seconds an HPC read waited at each end for Slurm's accounting (`accounting_wait_s_used`), the findings and the unknowns. It sits beside the keys above, not among `facts`, and a Docker run holds it in an `infrastructure_facts` of its own. See "The platform layer's read of the federation's own services".

- **One writer for the facts** (O89; `sandbox.infrafacts`). Every write to a run's `infrastructure_facts`, on both backends, goes through one helper that merges under the sandbox's lock and raises `FactsConflict` (a bug of the writer) for a key another writer owns, so no feature drops another's fact; `record_infrastructure_fact(key, value, *, owner)` names the owner, and `record(**fields)` refuses a field named for the table.

## The platform layer's read of the federation's own services

`expcore_onedata.fedwatch` (O86). A study's post-check asks of a run's or a batch's window: did the federation's services, the zone and each provider, run and answer throughout, and was a restart the injector's doing? One watch, the same on Compose and on HPC; every call only reads (`docker compose ps` and `docker inspect`; `squeue -s` and `sacct`; `GET /configuration`; the journal's file, opened read-only and without its lock).

```python
from expcore_onedata.fedwatch import watch_for

watch = watch_for(config, settings, client, zone="<the zone's service>", hpc_command=None)
start = watch.read()  # at the window's start: plain JSON, keep it
...
result = watch.window(start)  # now: the window since start
```

`watch_for(config, settings, client, *, zone, hpc_command=None, runner=run_command, clock=time.time, accounting_wait_s=60.0)` builds the watch `[faults]` of platform.toml describes: the providers of `[faults.services]`, the journal `[faults] journal` names, Compose's containers or, for `backend = "hpc"`, `hpc_command`, the argv that runs `deploy/hpc-federation/hpcobserve.py --root <the federation's root>` (with `python -I -B`) where Slurm's client commands answer (the login node); the watch appends `--accounting-wait-s <accounting_wait_s>` to it (so the login node's `hpcobserve.py` must come from the same pin: an older one exits 2 on the option, and every service reads unknown, never silently without the wait; a wrapper passes trailing arguments on) and gives the command that long on top of `[faults] command_timeout_s` (the bound is the next paragraph's). The zone's service is not in `[faults.services]`, so the caller names it. `FederationWatch(members, source, client, *, journal, clock)` is the same with the parts given (`Member`, `ComposeSource`, `HpcSource`, `members_of`). `client` is the platform client; it goes where platform.toml says, so a deployment that reaches the federation by declared names hands it a client built for them.

**A reading** (`read()`): `{"format": "expcore-fedwatch/1", "backend", "at", "journal": {"declared", "lines", "read"}, "services": {label: side}}`, keyed by `zone` and `providers[i]` (the provider's position in platform.toml), never by a name. A `side` has the same keys on both backends, `None` where a backend has no such thing:

| Key | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `state` | `running`, `not_running` or `unknown` |
| `running`, `identity`, `health` | the fields of `compose.Observation`, which this extends; `identity` is `<container or step id>@<start>` |
| `container`, `restart_count` | Compose: the container id and Docker's `RestartCount` |
| `step`, `launch`, `stops` | HPC: the step id read, the state's launch counter, how many stops the state records for the service |
| `started_at`, `ended_at`, `exit_code` | the container's or the step's own start, end and exit (an ended one's) |
| `unrecorded` | HPC: squeue lists a step under the service's name that the state does not name |
| `accounting_wait_s_used` | HPC: the seconds the read waited for Slurm's accounting to show a step the state says was stopped (0 when it did not wait); none on Compose and for a document that predates it |
| `live` | `{"answered": true, "reason": null}`, or `{"answered": null, "reason": <why>}`: the service's own `GET /configuration`, never asked of a service that is not running, never `false` (a service that does not answer is unread) |
| `notes`, `unknown` | what was seen that is worth saying; the reasons the service could not be read |

**A window** (`window(start, *, owner=None)`): `{"format", "backend", "window": {"start", "end"}, "verdict", "ownership", "services": {label: {"status", "start", "end", "restarts", "held", "unknown"}}, "findings", "unknown", "journal": {"declared", "lines_start", "lines_end", "read"}}`. A service's `status` is `unknown`, `down`, `held`, `restarted` or `stable`. **The verdict follows the worst service, in this order: `finding`** (any `findings` entry stands) **over `unknown`** (any `unknown` entry does) **over `held`** (a service is held down by an open injection the run owns) **over `restarted`** (a service restarted, each restart explained) **over `stable`**. Nothing but `stable` is a pass, and `held` is not one until the run's restore is read with it (below). A message names a service by its label only.

**A stop that Slurm's accounting has not caught up with (`accounting_wait_s`).** The driver confirms a stop once `squeue -s` no longer lists the step, and `sacct` follows seconds later, so a read made at once finds the step neither listed nor recorded as ended: unknown. The first study's order reads the window's start right after it stops the target, so the pilot would read unknown on every run. `hpcobserve.py` therefore waits when, and only when, **the driver's state says the step was stopped** (the service's `state` is `stopped`, its last recorded stop is a confirmed one, and the step the state names is the one of its `launch`) and the read is of that very step but `squeue` and `sacct` do not yet agree that it ended (no row, a row that still runs, one that is not ended or has no end time, or `squeue` listing a step `sacct` has ended; a step `squeue` still lists as running while the state says it was stopped is waited for too, and reads `accounting_lag` at the bound). It polls every 2 s, every such service at once under one bound, until they agree, and each side records the seconds it waited (`accounting_wait_s_used`, in the side and, as `start` and `end`, in a run's facts). **The bound is a declared setting of the watch:** `watch_for(..., accounting_wait_s=)`, 60 s unless declared, from 0 to 300 (anything else is refused, naming it); 0 reads once and never waits. Past the bound the service is `unknown` with the reason `accounting_lag` (and a note that says so), never running and never stopped by assumption, so the window is `unknown` and not `held`. It never waits for a service the state does not say was stopped (`stops` outlive a restart, so a stop alone says nothing), for a step `squeue` lists under another id than the state's, or for a command that failed, which keeps its own reason. Everything stays read-only (`squeue` and `sacct`, nothing else). **The cross-check is unchanged:** the state's recorded stops and launches must still agree with what is read for a restart to be explained.

**Unknown is never healthy.** A command that fails, does not finish or answers something unreadable (an inspection without `RestartCount` or `StartedAt`, a `sacct` or `squeue` row that is not one, accounting with no row for the job); a container or step missing (a service with no container is unknown, not down); Slurm's queue and accounting disagreeing, or an id neither listed nor recorded; a `/configuration` read that fails; no reading at the window's start (a missing one, one of another backend or federation): the service is `unknown`, with the reason, and the window is `unknown` unless a finding outranks it.

**A restart** is an end reading whose container id or step id differs from the start's, whose start differs, or whose counter rose (`RestartCount`; the state's `launch`). On HPC the step is read by its exact id (`hpcslurm.Slurm.step_observed`), never the state file's word for it. It is explained only by the injector's journal (`fault_journal`), whose lines carry no time: the journal is append-only, so the window is the lines after the start reading's count. An injection of the service (its `service`, as `[faults.services]` names it) explains a restart when its stop was asked for (`stopping`, `injected` or `stop_unknown`), it did not end `never_down`, and either the stop was asked inside the window or the service was down at the start and the injection has a line in the window (its restore is the restart). The label is `heal` for an injection whose line carries `heal` (a heal's container restart), `outage` for any other. On HPC the count is checked as well: `launch` says how many starts there were, each restart of a running service owes a stop the state records (`stops`) after the start reading, and an injection explains one launch; a step squeue lists that the state does not name is a restart the driver did not make. A restart nothing explains is `finding`, and so are: a `RestartCount` that rose (Docker's restart policy, never the injector's stop and start), a journal that is not declared, cannot be read, or is shorter than at the start, a service not running at the end that no open injection of the run's own holds down, and a container whose health check does not report healthy.

**Whose an injection is (`owner`).** A window asked of one run, `window(start, owner=liveness.owner())` (the platform's launch passes the run's, taken in its own context), counts only the injections whose `owner` names that run's attempt (`fault_owner.attempt_of`: the process, the campaign and the attempt's id, as the injector writes them since #371). So an earlier attempt's leftover explains nothing, and nor does a line written before owners were recorded or an injection outside any attempt (a process alone, as a heal's gate runs): an owner is never guessed, and such an injection leaves a restart a `finding` and a down service a `finding` that says so ("held down only by N open injection(s) that are not this run's"). A run that names no attempt (a process alone) owns no injection (`ownership` `unattributed`). With no `owner` (a batch's window) any injection counts, and the result says `ownership` `not_checked`; with one, `run`. Only a service some owned injection names can read `held` or `restarted`: every other service in the window must read `stable`, and a non-target that restarted or is down is a finding even inside an injecting run.

**`held`.** A service not running at the window's end that an open injection holds down (its stop asked, not cleared, not `never_down`) and which the run owns, is `held`: explained, never a finding, and distinct from `restarted`, as nothing restarted. The injection must fit the start reading: the service was down then too (the injection came before the window and held it down across it), or its stop was asked inside the window; an injection stopped before the window that the service was running despite at the start (left open, its restore unverified) holds nothing down, and a crash after it is a finding. This is the shape of an executor that injects before the launch and restores after it (the pilot's): the target is down at both ends of the window. `held` is the service's `held` entry (`injections`, `opened`: before the window, inside it or both, and `reason`, which says the run owns the injection and that its restore comes after the window). **The window cannot vouch for that restore**: read `held` with the run's restore outcome as the injector records it (`instruments["fault_injector"]["restore_verified"]` in the run's record, #367's verified step), and a held service whose restore then failed is not a pass. The facts carry a pointer to it (`restore`).

On Compose only a change is seen, so a service restarted twice in a window reads as one restart (the reason says "at least"), and the window's injections must still account for it. The relay keeps no durable request log, so the HPC cross-check uses the state's `stops` and `launch`, which the verbs write, instead of the relay's files.

**Every run carries its own window.** A platform built with the watch, `SandboxPlatform(..., federation_watch=watch_for(...))` (built once for the platform: the platform's client, its configuration and the zone's service; optional), reads it for each run in `launch`, with nothing for the study's executor to call:
