**The scale.** A ratio contrast's estimate, interval and minimum detectable effect are natural-log ratios ([above](#a-ratio-contrast)), so its smallest ratio r is compared as |ln r|: r and 1 / r are one size, and `direction` says which side the claim is on. `Rule.threshold` is the one place a declared ratio is taken to the log scale; every comparison takes that threshold and the result's own numbers, and exp serves only to show a log ratio as a ratio. A contrast of one outcome compares its difference as declared.

**Refused, naming the key** (by the loader, and as a `ValueError` naming the contrast for a spec built past it): `smallest_ratio` on a contrast of one outcome and `smallest_effect` on a ratio contrast; a value that is not a finite number; a `smallest_effect` of 0 or less (it is a size; `direction = "lower"` claims a decrease); a `smallest_ratio` of 0 or less, or of 1, which is no effect; a `direction` other than `"higher"` or `"lower"`, or one with no threshold beside it; and a `smallest_ratio` below 1 beside `direction = "higher"`, which names a decrease as an increase. A contrast in the Holm family and an exploratory one may both declare one.

**The decision.** The study's rule: a difference counts when it is significant and its point estimate is at least the threshold. Significant is the contrast's own `significant`, Holm's decision for a member of the family and the raw p-value at alpha for any other, so `analyse` judges the rule after Holm has decided (`run_contrast` alone judges it on the raw p-value):
- `meets`: a direction is declared, the test is significant and the estimate is at or beyond the threshold on that side (estimate >= threshold for `"higher"`, <= -threshold for `"lower"`);
- `meets_either_direction`: no direction is declared, the test is significant and |estimate| >= threshold; the report and the terminal write the estimate's sign beside it, so it never reads as a bare "meets";
- `opposite_direction`: a direction is declared, the test is significant and the estimate is on the other side of zero, whatever its size: a significant drop never confirms a claimed rise;
- `falls_short`: significant, the estimate short of the threshold on its side (zero included);
- `not_significant`, and `untestable` for a contrast that used no pair (S11).

**Beside it.** `interval` sets the contrast's reported interval (at `ci_level`) against the threshold on the direction's side (either side for `"either"`): `beyond` when every value is at or beyond it, `short_of` when every value is short of it, so the interval excludes the smallest effect either way, and `includes` otherwise. `detectable` is true when the contrast's minimum detectable effect, a size on the same scale, is at most the threshold, false when it is larger, and None when it is unknown (no `[power]` section, or a NaN figure), never false on that account. A member's minimum detectable effect is at alpha over the family size ([the estimator](#estimator)), so a true `detectable` is conservative. It says the test reaches the target power at a true effect of exactly the threshold; the estimate reaches the threshold only about half the time there, so `meets` does not reach that power. Every comparison is on the kernel's snapping grid (`contrasts.snap`), so 0.6 - 0.5 meets a threshold of 0.1.

**An equivalence margin beside it.** A contrast may declare both, and neither is derived from the other or checked against it: the margin bounds the differences the study calls negligible and decides `equivalent` and `trivial_difference`, while the smallest effect is what a difference must reach to count. The conclusion word and the decision are reported side by side, each on its own rule.

**In the outputs.** The declaration is in `preregistration.toml`, so in the preregistration hash every output carries (P04). `analysis.json` gives a declaring contrast `smallest_effect`, the `SmallestEffect` above; a contrast that declares none has no such key, so a study that declares none keeps the bytes of its `analysis.json`. `report.md` adds a section "Smallest effects of interest", only when a contrast declares one, with the smallest effect as declared (a ratio with its natural log), the direction, the signed estimate and its interval (a ratio contrast's with their ratios), the decision (`meets (either direction)` for the two-sided word, labelled exploratory outside the family), the interval against it, the minimum detectable effect (with its ratio) and whether the design detects it; the terminal line of a declaring contrast adds its smallest effect, the decision and the signed estimate.

### One-arm estimates and each arm's runs

`expcore.stats.onearm` and `expcore.stats.armruns` (S14, #139).

```toml
[descriptives.waited-in-a]       # a rate or share of an outcome within one arm
conditions = ["cond-a"]          # one condition, or several pooled into one arm
outcome = "waited"
cluster = "group"

[descriptives.queries-per-placement]   # a ratio of two outcomes' totals within one arm
conditions = ["cond-a", "cond-b"]
numerator = "queries"
denominator = "placements"
cluster = "group"
```

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ArmRuns:  # ContrastResult.arm_runs (arm A, arm B) and DescriptiveResult.runs
    arm: str  # a condition, a factor's level, or a one-arm estimate's conditions joined by " + "
    planned: int  # = finished + exhausted + unfinished + excluded
    finished: int  # analysed: = measured + treatment_outcome_none + outcome_none
    measured: int  # finished, with the outcome known
    lost: Mapping[str, int]  # LOST_CAUSES in order, zeros included
    finish_rate: float  # a property: finished / planned, NaN for none planned


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class DescriptiveResult:  # AnalysisResult.descriptives, in declared order
    name: str
    kind: str  # "rate" or "ratio_of_totals"
    outcome: str
    conditions: tuple[str, ...]
    cluster: str
    estimate: float
    ci_low: float
    ci_high: float  # NaN where nothing can be computed, never 0
    n_clusters: int
    n_runs: int  # the clusters and runs that entered the estimate
    n_undefined_replicates: int  # a ratio's replicates whose drawn denominators sum to 0
    runs: ArmRuns
    per_model: tuple[ModelEstimate, ...]  # each model's estimate and interval, from the same draws
```

**A rate.** The mean of the outcome over the arm's runs that have it, in the stages a contrast's differences take: the runs of a (task, model), every seed and condition of the arm together; the tasks of a (cluster, model); the models of a cluster; and the clusters, each step with equal weight. For an outcome of 0 or 1 it is a share. The interval is the percentile interval of the kernel's cluster bootstrap (clusters drawn with replacement, one draw for every model, S04) at the preregistration's replicates and level and a seed from the estimate's name, so it draws the same resamples whichever others run; each model's comes from the same draws (S05). A percentile interval is never wider than the data: clusters all at one value give that point, so a share of 0 or 1 over few clusters has a zero-width interval.

**A ratio of totals.** theta = (sum of the numerator) / (sum of the denominator) over every run of the arm that has both, all clusters, tasks, models and seeds together, so a cluster weighs by its runs and a run whose denominator is 0 still adds its numerator. Each replicate is the ratio of the drawn clusters' totals; one whose drawn denominators sum to 0 has no ratio, is NaN, never 0 or an infinity, and is counted (`n_undefined_replicates`); the interval is NaN when fewer than half are defined. With every denominator 0 the estimate is NaN. The totals, and each replicate's sums of them, are exact until the final rounding, so nothing overflows or underflows on the way, and a ratio past the float range is refused rather than made infinite. A negative numerator or denominator is refused, naming the run and the outcome; so is a rate's value beyond 1e300 in magnitude (`onearm.RATE_BOUND`), whose float mean could overflow, and a run key the arm holds twice, analysed or absent.

**Descriptive.** A one-arm estimate has no p-value, no conclusion word and no place in a Holm family; its name may be no contrast's, and `holm_family` cannot name it. A run whose outcome is None is left out of it and counted in its `runs`.

**Each arm's runs.** For a contrast, `arm_runs` counts each arm's planned runs of the contrast's planned pairs, those whose pairing key the other arm plans too, analysed or absent; a run with no planned partner is outside every pair and is not counted. A pair lost to one run's None is one run lost, in the arm that lost it, whatever became of its partner: the pairs table counts pairs, this counts each arm's runs.

**In the outputs.** `analysis.json` gives every contrast `arm_runs` and the analysis `descriptives`. `report.md` has a table "Runs of each arm" after the missing pairs, one row per contrast and arm, with the finish rate (`n/a` for an arm with none planned), and, only when one is declared, a section "One-arm estimates (descriptive)"; the terminal summary prints each one-arm estimate as descriptive, with its interval.

### Several Holm families

`expcore.stats.prereg` and `expcore.stats.analysis` (S17, #76). A preregistration whose confirmatory hypotheses form distinct families, such as one per outcome or one per phase, declares them by name in place of `holm_family`:

```toml
[holm_families]                # each key a family's name, each value its contrasts; a family of one is a stand-alone test
phase1 = ["h1a", "h1b", "h1c"]
phase2 = ["h2a", "h2b"]
```

```python
# expcore.stats.prereg
Preregistration.holm_families: Mapping[str, tuple[str, ...]]  # the named families in declared order; {} for one flat family
Preregistration.holm_family: tuple[str, ...]  # every family's members, the families in order: the confirmatory set
Preregistration.families() -> tuple[tuple[str | None, tuple[str, ...]], ...]  # (name, members); one flat family's name is None
Preregistration.family_of(name) -> tuple[str | None, tuple[str, ...]] | None  # the family a contrast is in, None outside all

# expcore.stats.analysis
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class HolmStep:      # step (1 for the smallest p-value), name, p_value, p_holm, level, reject
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class HolmFamily:    # name, members (declared order), steps (Holm's order), equivalence (its TOST decisions, S15)
AnalysisResult.holm_families: tuple[HolmFamily, ...]  # the named families; () for one flat family
def equivalence_groups(prereg) -> tuple[tuple[str | None, tuple[str, ...]], ...]  # the claims' Holm groups (see S18 below)
```

**Each family on its own.** Holm runs over each family apart, at the preregistration's one `alpha`: a family of m compares its k-th smallest p-value with alpha / (m - k + 1) and stops at the first that misses, so a member's level, adjusted p-value and decision are its own family's and no family's size or p-values move another's. Families of three and two start at alpha / 3 and alpha / 2; one flat family of the same five would start at alpha / 5 and can reject other contrasts. An untestable member counts as p = 1 in its own family (S02). `AnalysisResult.holm` still holds every member's decision, the families one after the other, so whatever reads a decision by name finds its own family's; `holm_families` adds each family's members and its steps. Everything a member's family size enters follows the member's own family: its minimum detectable effect is judged at alpha over its family's size, the refusals of S10 and S13 apply to every family's members, and `power_study`'s corrected row is at alpha over the largest family's size, the hardest first step any family has, so the figure is conservative for the smaller ones.

**Equivalence under several families (S15).** A flat `equivalence_family` is one list, and each member must be in a Holm family (refused naming `[holm_families]` otherwise). Holm over the TOST p-values runs within each Holm family, over the members of the equivalence family it holds (`equivalence_groups`), so an equivalence claim is adjusted with the other claims of its own Holm family only; `equivalence_holm` keeps the equivalence family's declared order, and each `HolmFamily.equivalence` holds its own claims' decisions. `equivalence_feasibility` judges each claim at alpha over the claims of its own Holm family. With one flat family there is one group, the whole equivalence family, as before. A preregistration whose equivalence claims form families of their own, across the Holm families, declares them in `[equivalence_families]` instead ([below](#declared-equivalence-families)).

**What is refused.** `holm_family` and `[holm_families]` together; `[holm_families]` beside `exploratory_only = true` (S12); an empty table, an empty family or a blank family name; a family naming an undeclared contrast, or one contrast twice; a contrast in two families; and a value that is not an array of contrast names (a list of lists among them). Each refusal names the file, `[holm_families]` and the family. A `Preregistration` built in code is held to the same rules, and its `holm_family`, when given beside `holm_families`, must be exactly their union (left empty, it is filled in). `max_unscored_treatment` on a contrast in no family is refused naming `holm_families`.

**The hash.** The families are in `preregistration.toml`, so in its SHA-256, which every output manifest carries (P04) and the campaign fingerprint takes with the other configuration files: the same contrasts grouped otherwise are another preregistration, and a copy made with `with_override(holm_families=...)` is marked `+override`.

**In the outputs.** `analysis.json` keeps `holm` and, only for named families, adds `holm_families`: per family its `name`, its `members`, its `steps` (`step`, `name`, `p_value`, `p_holm`, `level`, `reject`) and its `equivalence` decisions. `report.md` heads the confirmatory contrasts "Preregistered contrasts (n Holm families, each adjusted on its own at alpha = ...)" and gives each family a table of its own, "Holm family <name> (k contrasts)", followed by "Holm steps of <name>" (each step's contrast, raw p, level as alpha / k, Holm p and verdict) and notes on what each family's adjustment means; the exploratory contrasts are "outside every Holm family", the equivalence table has a "Holm family" column, and the terminal summary writes a member's Holm p as `Holm p 0.0240 (family phase1)`. The report refuses a result whose families and Holm decisions disagree (a member in no family or in two, or a step that is not its member's decision). One flat `holm_family` has no named family: its `analysis.json` and `report.md` are byte for byte what they were before families were named (`tests/data/tiny_study_analysis.json` and `tests/data/tiny_study_report.md` pin them).

### Declared equivalence families

`expcore.stats.prereg` and `expcore.stats.analysis` (S18). A preregistration that registers its equivalence claims as families of their own, such as one hypothesis's twelve claims adjusted together, declares them by name in place of the flat `equivalence_family`:

```toml
[equivalence_families]         # each key a family's name, each value its claims; each claim in one family at most
h1 = ["h1a", "h1b", "h1c", "h2a", "h2b", "h2c", "h3a", "h3b", "h3c", "h4a", "h4b", "h4c"]
```

```python
# expcore.stats.prereg
Preregistration.equivalence_families: Mapping[str, tuple[str, ...]]  # the declared families in order; {} without the table
Preregistration.equivalence_family: tuple[str, ...]  # every family's members, the families in order: every TOST claim

# expcore.stats.analysis
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class EquivalenceFamily:  # name, members (declared order), steps (HolmStep over the TOST p-values, Holm's order)
AnalysisResult.equivalence_families: tuple[EquivalenceFamily, ...]  # the declared families; () without the table
def equivalence_groups(prereg)  # the declared families when there are any, else the flat list split by Holm family
```

**Each family over its own claims.** Holm runs over each declared family's TOST p-values apart, at the preregistration's one `alpha`, whatever Holm families the claims' significance tests are in: a family of m compares its k-th smallest TOST p with alpha / (m - k + 1) and stops at the first that misses, so twelve claims in one family across four Holm families of three face alpha / 12 first, where the flat list would face alpha / 3 within each Holm family. A claim is `equivalent` only when its family's Holm rejects, with `tost_p_holm`, `equivalence_level` and `equivalence_interval_adjusted` from its own family's step; an untestable claim counts as p = 1 and keeps its family's size (S02). `equivalence_holm` holds every claim's decision in the families' declared order, `equivalence_families` each family's members and steps, and no `HolmFamily.equivalence` holds a decision, since no Holm family adjusted one. `equivalence_feasibility` judges each claim at alpha over its own equivalence family's size. The significance tests are untouched: each claim's contrast is still decided by its own Holm family.

**What is refused.** `[equivalence_families]` beside `equivalence_family` (naming both: one says the claims are adjusted within each Holm family, the other in families of their own) and beside `exploratory_only = true`; an empty table, an empty family or a blank family name; a member that is not a declared contrast, has no `equivalence_margin`, is in no Holm family (`holm_family` or `[holm_families]`: its significance test must be confirmatory), or has an estimator with no TOST p-value; a contrast twice in one family or in two families; and a value that is not an array of contrast names. Each refusal names the file, `[equivalence_families]` and the family. A `Preregistration` built in code is held to the same rules, and its `equivalence_family`, when given beside `equivalence_families`, must be exactly their union (left empty, it is filled in).

**The hash.** The table is in `preregistration.toml`, so in its SHA-256, which every output manifest carries (P04): the same claims declared as one family or as a flat list are two preregistrations, and a copy made with `with_override(equivalence_families=...)` is marked `+override`.

**In the outputs.** `analysis.json` adds, only when the table is declared, `equivalence_families`: per family its `name`, its `members` and its `steps` (`step`, `name`, `p_value` the raw TOST p, `p_holm` the adjusted one, `level`, `reject` whether it is equivalent); each named Holm family's `equivalence` is then `[]`. `report.md` heads the equivalence table "Equivalence families (Holm over two one-sided tests, within each declared equivalence family)", gives each claim's equivalence family (and, with named Holm families, the Holm family of its significance test) beside its margin, raw and adjusted TOST p, interval level, adjusted interval and verdict, then "TOST steps of equivalence family <name>" (each step's claim, TOST p, level as alpha / k, Holm TOST p and verdict) and notes on what each family's adjustment means; the terminal summary writes `Holm TOST p 0.0120 (equivalence family h1)`. The report refuses a result whose equivalence families and decisions disagree (a claim in no family or in two, a step that is not its claim's decision, a member that is no claim, or a Holm family holding a decision), and one whose steps are not Holm's over the family's own claims: it recomputes each family's levels, adjusted TOST p-values and verdicts from its claims' raw TOST p-values at alpha with the analysis's own kernel, and requires each claim's TOST p, Holm TOST p, verdict and interval level to be its step's, so decisions made at another family's size cannot be shown. Without the table, `analysis.json` and `report.md` are byte for byte what they were (`tests/data/analysis-equivalence-within-families.json`, `tests/data/analysis-equivalence-flat.json` and their reports pin both existing layouts).

### Declared sensitivity reruns

`expcore.stats.prereg` and `expcore.stats.reruns` (S19). A preregistration declares which sensitivity reruns it expects, and `analyze` runs exactly those:

```toml
[sensitivity]
reruns = ["egress", "mount_guard", "process_visibility", "any_guard"]   # each name at most once, in any order
```

```python
# expcore.stats.prereg
Preregistration.sensitivity_reruns: tuple[str, ...] | None  # the declared names in order; None without the table

# expcore.stats.reruns
RERUNS = ("egress", "mount_guard", "process_visibility", "any_guard")
def ran_reruns(egress, mount_guard, visibility, guards) -> tuple[str, ...]  # the reruns whose analysis exists
def difference(declared, ran) -> str | None  # None when they match, whatever the order; else the message
```

**What each name is.** `egress`, `mount_guard` and `process_visibility` are the reruns of one guard (its analysis exists once one analysed record carries its instrument), and `any_guard` is the rerun without every run any guard leaves out (`expcore.guards`, O76), which exists once two or more guards checked the batch. **A rerun counts as run when its guard checked the batch, whether or not it left a run out:** with every run clean there is no second analysis, and it is still the declared rerun.

**What is refused.** At load, a `[sensitivity]` that is not a table, has a key other than `reruns`, lacks `reruns`, or whose `reruns` is not an array of the four names, or names one twice; each message names the file, `[sensitivity]` and the key, an unknown name with the closest of the four. At `analyse_study` (so `analyze`, `report` and what builds on them), `Refused` when the reruns the records would produce are not exactly the declared ones, in either direction: it names each declared rerun that cannot run, with why (`no analysed record carries the <guard> instrument`; for `any_guard`, `it needs two or more guards to have checked the batch; only egress did`), each rerun that would run and is not declared, and both lists. A partial analysis is held to the same.

**A preregistration with no `[sensitivity]` table reads as `reruns = []`**: `Preregistration.sensitivity_reruns` is None for it (and `()` for `reruns = []`, so that `analysis.json` says only what was written), and `analyse_study` checks it as an empty declaration. A batch no guard checked runs no rerun and passes with no table, its `analysis.json` unchanged; a batch a guard checked is refused, naming each rerun it would run and the missing table (`expcore.stats.reruns.missing_table`: `<file> has no [sensitivity] table, and an absent table declares none; add [sensitivity] reruns = [...]`), so no rerun goes unpreregistered. `reruns = []` declares that none is expected, so a batch that would run one is refused as well. The table is in `preregistration.toml`, so in its SHA-256 (P04), and a copy made with `with_override(sensitivity_reruns=...)` is checked as the file is and marked `+override`. `analysis.json` carries `sensitivity_reruns` only for a preregistration that has the table, so the output of a study with none (a batch no guard checked) is as it was.

## The platform layer's spaces and reset

`expcore_onedata.spaces`, `provision`, `eviction` and `reset`. A federation study has one space per data owner, each supported by its owner's provider, by the agent's local provider and sometimes by a mirror. `platform.toml` declares them as an array of tables, in place of `[space]` and `[support]` (one form or the other, never both):

```toml
[[spaces]]
label = "<label>"               # how code names the space; a message names it by its position, spaces[i]
name = "..."                    # name, id and prefixes as in [space]
id = "..."
prefixes = ["..."]
providers = ["<name>", ...]     # the providers declared to support this space (O04)
local = "<name>"                # the agent's own provider, one of these; the same in every entry (O03)
holder = "<name>"               # this space's data provider, one of its providers: required (O09)
tokens = ["data_path", "tool_server"]  # optional: the token planes that reach this space; both by default
data_manifest = "<manifest>"    # optional: the manifest of the data it holds (importmanifest), relative or absolute
                                # (on an HPC federation the layout's own `data_manifest` renders it: an ingested space)
```

A `[[placement.map]]` row (or `[[map]]` in a study's file) names its space with `space = "<label>"`, required with several spaces. `[placement] concurrency` (16 by default) bounds the requests a bulk operation has in flight. `[placement] qos_scan` (`"listing"` by default, or `"summaries"`) chooses how the reset finds QoS requirements (O33, below); it is in no fingerprint, so changing it never refuses a resume, and `[placement] qos_fulfil_timeout_s` (120 s by default, never negative) bounds the reset's wait for a run's requirement to be fulfilled before it is deleted. `[placement] reset_concurrency` (4 by default, at least 1) bounds the spaces a reset of several works on at once (`resetspaces.SpacesReset`, O51). `[placement] reset_shortcut` (false by default; true only with `[faults]` in the same platform.toml, else `ConfigError`) lets a reset given a planner's reading made inside the reset lock's exclusive section re-read only what may have changed since (below); `[placement] reset_exclusive_wait_s` (60 s by default, never negative) is still accepted and checked, and no longer bounds anything: every reset waits for the reset lock within `[faults] admission_timeout_s` (O66). `[placement] provider_concurrency` (16 by default, at least 1) bounds the reads of a reset's readings in flight at one provider, every space together (`readslots.py`, O51): without it, `reset_concurrency` spaces of `concurrency` reads each put 64 at one shared provider.

**Upload chunks are 16 MiB and each full chunk goes on a new connection (#186, O25).** On the 25.0 development build the federation runs (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-12-federation-facts-upload-and-stalls.md`), a 200 MiB upload through `Provisioner.upload` stalled at chunks of 64 and 32 MiB on a reused keep-alive connection: 3 of 289 PUTs on reused connections stalled and none of 336 on new ones. The body started and never finished, the provider left data unread and closed the connection at about 300 s, and the client's send failed some 45 s later. 16 MiB, and every smaller size, passed every run, reused connections or not, and with keep-alive off every size passed. So `[placement] chunk_bytes` is 16 MiB by default (it was 64 MiB), and `[placement] fresh_connection_bytes` (16 MiB by default) is the size from which a content write goes on a connection of its own: `Provisioner` passes `fresh=True` to `OnezoneClient.mutate` for a body of at least that many bytes, and a client that built its own HTTP client sends that request through a one-shot client built as the pool's is (the same `[tls]`), closed when the answer is in, never on an idle connection of the pool. With the defaults every full chunk goes on a new connection and the remainder, a smaller write, keeps the pool; a threshold above `chunk_bytes` leaves every write on the pool. A client given an HTTP client by its caller sends through that, whose connections are the caller's, so `fresh` does nothing there. A write of content, `fresh` or not, is logged (logger `expcore_onedata.client`) with its size, the seconds it took, and how its connection began, as a retried read's attempt is: `new`, or `reused` with the requests that connection had served; a line names the provider by its position and holds no path, id or token. Reads keep the connection pool, but `chunk_bytes` also sizes the ranges of a whole-file read (`read_file`, `read_content`, range placement), so a read now takes 16 MiB ranges: four times as many ranges, and gate reads, per file as at 64 MiB.

```python
class PlatformConfig:               # config.py
    spaces: tuple[StudySpace, ...]  # the one-space form's single space (label "space"), or the [[spaces]] entries
    def space_named(self, label: str | None = None) -> StudySpace   # None: the only space
    space: Space; support: Support  # the only space's; refused when several are declared
    prefixes: tuple[str, ...]       # every space's, each once: what the mutation gate accepts
    def space_ids(self, plane: str) -> tuple[str, ...]              # the spaces a token plane is scoped to

class Provisioner:                  # provision.py: bound to one space
    def __init__(self, client, config, settings, *, provider_ids, log, space: str | None = None, ...)
    def resolve_many(self, paths, *, retry=None) -> dict[str, Entry]  # one listing of each directory
    def walk(self, directory_id=None, *, retry=None) -> Iterator[tuple[Entry, bool]]  # every entry, and its leafness
    # `retry` (and `gate_retry` of `mutate` and `schedule_transfer`): a reset's policy, the provisioner's when None

def evict_tree(provisioner, path: str | Entry, from_provider) -> dict   # eviction.py: one transfer of a directory
def evict_ids(provisioner, file_ids, from_provider) -> dict             # files in parallel under the bound

class SpaceReset:                   # reset.py
    def __init__(self, provisioner, *, providers=None, listed_max=LISTED_MAX, now=time.time)
    def qos_requirements(self) -> frozenset[str]                       # the run-start baseline of QoS requirements
    def run(self, evict: Mapping[str, Iterable[str]] | None = None,   # provider -> paths that must leave it
            allowed: Mapping[str, int] | None = None,                  # provider -> physical bytes it may keep
            *, since: float | None = None,                             # the history's window start, epoch s on `now`
            qos_baseline: Collection[str] = (),                        # requirement ids to keep
            qos_rights: bool | None = None,                            # could any run since the last reset set QoS? None: not said
            never_holds: Collection[str] = (),                         # providers that never hold the space's bytes (O46)
            planned: Holdings | None = None,                           # a planner's reading: its scan is the first (O51)
            full_reason: str | None = None) -> dict                    # why this reset reads in full, inside its section (O51)
    def plan_scan(self, *, never_holds=()) -> Holdings                 # one listing, each file's holdings (O51)
    def exclusive(self) -> ContextManager[ResetGrant]                  # the reset lock's exclusive hold (O11, O51, O66)

class ResetPlan:                    # resetspaces.py: one space's `run` arguments, the same names and defaults
    evict; allowed; since; qos_baseline; qos_rights; never_holds; planned; full_reason
class SpacesReset:                  # resetspaces.py: several spaces reset as one (O51)
    def __init__(self, resets: Sequence[SpaceReset], *, concurrency: int | None = None)  # None: [placement] reset_concurrency
    def run(self, plans: Sequence[ResetPlan] | None = None) -> dict    # one plan per reset, in order
    def exclusive(self) -> ContextManager[ResetGrant]                  # one hold for every space, of one federation

class Holdings:                     # resetplan.py: a planner's reading of one space
    scan: Found; held: dict[str, dict[str, int]]; read_at: float; seconds: float  # provider -> file id -> bytes
    read_epoch: float | None; grant: ResetGrant | None                 # this host's epoch at the listing; its section
    read_retry: dict                                                   # its reads' tally (ReadRetry.facts, O46)
    def plan(self) -> dict[str, list[str]]                             # provider -> the paths of the files it holds
    def held_paths(self, provider) -> list[str]

class ResetGrant:                   # resetgrant.py: what a reset's exclusive hold holds
    mode: str | None                # "exclusive", "federation_exclusive", or None (nothing held: no [faults])
    why: str | None                 # None: the shortcut is given; else "off", "no_admission" (no [faults]),
                                    # "holder_gone", "holders_unread" or "shares_retained", each with the lock held
    wait_s: float; granted: bool; holders: list[dict]                  # holders: pid, boot_id, start_time, since_utc,
                                                                       # in_campaign, status ("live", "holder_gone", "unknown")
    held: bool; lapsed: bool; shortcut: bool                           # properties
    def facts(self) -> dict
def exclusive(config, settings) -> ContextManager[ResetGrant]          # resetgrant.py; SpaceReset.exclusive calls it
class ResetLockBusy(FederationBusy):  # resetgrant.py: the wait ran out at admission_timeout_s, or was cancelled
    wait_s: float; holders: list[dict] | None                          # None: the record could not be read
```

- **The shortcut (O51, `resetshortcut.py`, `resetgrant.py`).** With `[placement] reset_shortcut` on, `with reset.exclusive() as grant:` around `plan_scan()` and `run(planned=...)` (or `SpacesReset.exclusive()` around every space's) lets the occupancy and quiet stages re-read only, at each provider the reading read: the files it showed holding bytes there and those it has no figure for; every file of a job the ended listings show finished since the reading's start (`read_epoch` less `clock_skew_allowance_s` and `transferorder.STOP_MARGIN_S`, the global clock placed by Onezone's readings, `transferorder.start_band`) or that the transfers stage cancelled, by its record's `fileId`, the reset's own evictions excepted; the files the reset evicted; every file a QoS requirement of the planner's scan applies to, kept or removed; and every hard link. The rest is carried as holding nothing (`occupancy.Reading.carried`; the stage's record gives `files_read`, the reads made, and `files_carried`). The walk comes no earlier than `transfer_settle_s` after the transfers stage's last observation (a later occupancy wait when it would come sooner). **The premise** is that no reader placed bytes since the reading: an on-demand read leaves no transfer record, so the section holds `federation.reset` exclusively, which every shared admission holds shared (below). It falls back to every file, never failing the reset, when the setting is off (`off`), no reading was given (`not_planned`), the reading was made outside a section that still holds (`outside_grant`), the section gave no shortcut (the grant's `why`), its hold lapsed before the occupancy stage (`grant_lapsed`), a job since the reading names no listed file (`transfer_not_on_a_file`: a directory, the root), or the listings, a record or the clock cannot be read (`transfers_unread`, with `error`, the type). The fallback is of the reads only: the reset holds the lock all the same, and one that cannot get it refuses (below, O66). A hold that lapses after narrowed reads fails the stage that finds it. Each space's record has `occupancy_reads`: `mode` (`shortcut` or `full`), `why`, `grant` (`ResetGrant.facts()`), `reading` (`read_epoch`, `files`, `pairs_read`, `held_pairs`) and, under the shortcut, `reread` (`transferred`, `required`, `evicted`, `links`, `pairs`).
- **One full reset on request (O51).** `run(..., full_reason="<why>")` (`ResetPlan.full_reason` for `SpacesReset`) gives the occupancy and quiet stages the full reads inside the exclusive section, whose grant is held as without it, so readers stay out for the whole reset; leaving the section out gives the full reads too, and the reset still takes the hold itself (O66). `occupancy_reads` then says `mode` `"full"`, keeps the core's own `why` where the core gives the full reads anyway (`off`, `outside_grant`, ...) and says `"requested"` where it would have given the shortcut, and holds the caller's reason as `full_reason`. The record is published, so the reason is a short code (`resetshortcut.checked_reason`, which is `shortcode.checked_code`, the rule `shared_admission`'s reason follows too): a lower-case letter, then up to 63 lower-case letters, digits or underscores (no path, host, address or sentence), holding none of the names the configuration gives (the zone's name and host, each provider's name, id and host, each space's label, name, id and prefixes), compared with case and separators dropped (O12); a name the study chose and the configuration does not hold is the study's to keep out, as in any field it writes. Any other reason raises `ValueError` before anything is sent. With no `full_reason` the record is what it always was.
- **Every reset holds the reset lock exclusively, or refuses (O11, O66, `resetgrant.exclusive`).** `SpaceReset.run` and `SpacesReset.run` take the hold themselves, after checking every plan and before anything is sent, or use the live section of the same federation they run in; the record of each space gives it as `reset_lock` (`ResetGrant.facts()`; its `seconds` count from the hold). Under a shared admission the hold gives up the run's share of `federation.reset` (never of `federation.lock`), polls for `federation.reset` exclusively within `[faults] admission_timeout_s` and until the admission's `cancel` (the campaign's, `Admission.cancel`) is set, and on leaving lets it go and takes the share back within the same bound, each on a new descriptor; past that bound on the way back it raises `FederationBusy` and the run's infrastructure stays refused (`require_admission`). **At the bound, or cancelled, it refuses** as `ResetLockBusy` (a `FederationBusy`, kind `federation_busy`, which costs the run no attempt), its `detail` naming the open lines of `reset-holds.jsonl` by pid and status, never a reason (O12), with `wait_s` and `holders`, the share taken back and nothing sent; it never resets under another holder. **It is refused (`AdmissionRefused`)** on a platform with `[faults]` in a context that holds no admission, its message naming the remedy (outside a campaign, wrap the reset in `shared_admission(config, reason=<code>)`, below; inside one, reset in the run's own context, which `asyncio.to_thread` carries and a thread pool's `run_in_executor` does not), and under an admission whose owner has left. **It is refused too while the run's sandbox or tool server is up** (`Admission.open_steps`: a step that uses the run's tokens, `token_step`, from the launch to the end of its stop), since the agent may be running a cell, calling a tool or reading: checked before the hold is taken and again once it is, both before anything is sent, and a step that begins during the reset fails the stage it began in (`ResetGrant.agent_steps`). With the launches refused while the hold stands (`require_admission`), a share the run keeps during its reset is a harness step's, never its agent's. Granted, it ends every open line (`holder_gone` when its process is gone or cannot be told, `lock_free` when it runs) and gives no shortcut (`holder_gone`), keeping the hold, when one names a process that is gone or cannot be told, or when a line was ended `holder_gone` less than `resetgrant.DEAD_HOLDER_QUIET_S` (3,600 s) before: a read it sent may still be placing bytes, and a tool server it started may still run. It first seals the run's admission (`Admission.seal`, `Admission.unseal`): only while the owner's share is its one share (else `shares_retained`, no shortcut, the hold taken all the same, since a worker holding a share may be reading), and from then no worker takes a share (`try_retain` answers None) and `Admission.may_act` is false until it ends, so the run's sandbox, tool server, a reusing tool and a health check in the run's context are refused or deferred. With `reset_shortcut` off it holds the lock likewise and gives no shortcut (`off`). Under an exclusive admission of `federation.lock` it is granted at once (`federation_exclusive`), sealed likewise; with no `[faults]` there is no lock, and it is `no_admission` (or `off`). The grant keeps a share of the run's admission (`Admission.retain_for_reset`) and counts the sections using it (`ResetGrant.join`, `leave`): an owner that leaves while its reset runs in a thread lets neither the admission nor its locks go until the reset ends, and a nested hold in a thread that inherited the caller's section keeps the lock after the outer section ends. A hold that lapses all the same (`ResetGrant.lapsed`: its lock let go under it, or its grant ended while the reset uses it) fails the stage it lapsed in, the last included, and no later stage begins. A worker of the run's own that already holds a share (`shares_retained`) and is at work goes on: only other holders are excluded. A grant names its federation (`ResetGrant.federation`): a hold inside a live one of the same federation uses the outer grant, one for another federation is refused (`AdmissionRefused`), a reset of several spaces of two federations is refused (`ValueError`), and a reading made in another federation's section gives the full reads (`other_federation`). `shortcut_unavailable` (`resetgrant.UNAVAILABLE`) is no longer given.

A scripted reset outside a campaign (a script between batches, a smoke driver) takes the tools' shared admission around it, its reason a short code (`shortcode.checked_code`: a lower-case letter, then up to 63 lower-case letters, digits or underscores, holding no configured name):

```python
from expcore_onedata.reset import SpaceReset
from expcore_onedata.tool_admission import shared_admission

with shared_admission(config, reason="reset_between_batches"):
    record = SpaceReset(provisioner).run(evict)
```

The platform layer guarantees:
- **The platform's fingerprint holds every declared setting that shapes a run, and nothing that is one federation's own (C06).** `PlatformConfig.fingerprint()` is the TLS mode and CA bundle's SHA-256 (`tls`); the lifetime rule of a run's tokens (`tokens`, every `[tokens]` key after its default); the spaces (below); with a `[sandbox]`, each level's data path (`data_paths`), the declared served caps (`served_bytes`), the Apptainer backend's pins as before (`sandbox_backend`, with the providers' storage kind, `provider_storage`, when declared: O57) and `sandbox`: the backend, the Docker images by their pinned ids, every limit, the mount's path, I/O mode and oneclient's extra arguments, the trust paths, the agent's user and interpreter, the kill's grace and the placeholder token's SHA-256, and `content_check_provider` (O87) as the provider's position `providers[i]`, only when it is not the local provider, so a setting at its default and a store started before the key are as they were; with a `[toolserver]`, its pinned commit, its command line, its start's bound and each condition, keyed by its name's SHA-256, with its allow-list, rights and manifest's SHA-256 (`toolserver`); with a `[placement]`, the data provider by label, the range map's digest and the bounds a run's probes and progressive ingest read during the run (`placement`, `runsettings.PLACEMENT_IN_RUN`); with `[faults]`, the sanction, each target by label and the SHA-256 of its backend and service, and every bound of the restore and heal after its default (`faults`). Each value is the effective one in one canonical form: a key at its default and one left out, `2` and `2.0`, and a set in another order give one fingerprint (`served_bytes` keeps its earlier rule, in only when declared). A command line (the tool server's command and arguments, oneclient's extra arguments) is in by the SHA-256 of its federation-neutral form (`runsettings.neutral_words`): each of the federation's URLs, hosts and ids (providers, spaces, fixed users), the clone's path and the platform file's directory is replaced by its role, and every option is kept as written; a federation's value no role names stays, so such a line keeps two federations apart. Out of it: every address, id, port, host, network and Compose project name, path, secret and credential source, the host's own commands and names (`sudo`, `name_prefix`, `label_namespace`, `earlyoom_comm`, `[sandbox.network]`, `[sandbox.apptainer] apptainer` and `host_python`, `[faults] command` and `docker`), the tool server's clone path, what is checked before a campaign (`[link_check]`, `content_check_file`, `direct_check_file`) and `[placement]`'s concurrency, QoS scan and reset settings, which `reset_settings` records (C35). Two renders of one template on two federations so give one fingerprint, and `tests/support/runsettings_support.py` classifies every key the loaders accept, in or out, with a guard that fails on a key in neither. A store recorded before this form is refused at its resume, naming each setting as absent when the campaign started; there is no legacy form for it. A condition added to `[toolserver.conditions]` is a refused resume, never growth: declare every condition before the campaign starts.
- The one-space form holds its support set and local provider by label and its name and prefixes by their SHA-256 (`support`). Whenever `[[spaces]]` is used, with one entry or several, the fingerprint holds each entry's SHA-256 in order, never a label, name or id, so a resume under another set, order, support, data provider or token planes is refused (C06). A space is the same experimental condition across federations when its declared identity and its data identity match: an entry that declares `data_manifest` (the loader reads and checks it, `importmanifest.load_manifest`, and keeps its content hash, never the path; one that cannot be read or checked is refused naming the key) is hashed with that content hash and without its Onezone id, so two federations holding the same data agree and one file different does not; an entry without it is hashed as it always was, its id included. `PlatformConfig.legacy_fingerprint()` is the platform's half with every entry by that id-bearing digest (`StudySpace.legacy_digest()`), None when no entry declares its data; a plugin gives it as `StudyPlugin.platform_legacy`, and a store that recorded exactly the campaign's fingerprint with it resumes under its own record, unchanged (`expcore.study.platform_legacy`), while the merge compares recorded fingerprints as they are. The one-space form takes `data_manifest` in `[space]` too: with it the fingerprint gains `space_data`, the manifest's content hash, and without it the fingerprint holds no data identity of the space.
- A provisioner acts in its own space alone: its live name is checked against that space's name and prefixes before the first change, every mutation it sends is bound to that space (the gate refuses a target in any other space, even under a shared prefix), its data provider, which must support the space, is never evicted, by name or by id (O09), and with several spaces every message starts with the space's position. Placement, eviction and the probes take the space from the provisioner, and each space's probes have their own namespace, `platform_probes_space<i>`, and name the space in their snapshot.
- A read-only token's `data.path` caveat lists exactly the spaces its plane is scoped to, its principal's rights are read in each, and the examination refuses a token that misses one or adds another (O05). The data path's token is read-only, and so is the tool server's when its condition declares no write right. A writing condition's tool-server token carries no data-access caveat (`data.path`, `data.objectid`, `data.readonly`), only its expiry and the `rest` interface, because on Onedata 25.0 any of them limits a token to data access and its transfers and QoS requirements are refused (`tokenCaveatUnverified`); its examination refuses one that carries any. Its reach is its principal, held to the plane before the mint, at the tool server's launch and at its teardown: the user's rights are read in each space of the plane and must be the condition's, Onezone's scope of the token (`infer_access_token_scope`, every space the user is an effective member of) must be exactly the plane's spaces, the user must hold no Onezone admin privilege, and it must be an effective member of no cluster, since a member of a provider's cluster can mint that provider's tokens and change or delete the provider (`tokenscope`, O09). Onezone has no read of another user's clusters, so the clusters are read as the token itself (`GET /user/effective_clusters`, the current user's), and the admin privileges with the admin credential. A refusal names a space by its position, or counts the undeclared ones, and counts clusters, never naming one. The tool server's launch checks and the data gate's token follow the plane's set. The health check's token is the data path's, scoped to the spaces the data path reaches, and it compares each of them with its own support set (O04); its content-type check reads `[sandbox] content_check_file` under the root of the first space the data path reaches.
- `run` first refuses a plan that names the data provider (by name or id) or a provider that does not support the space, and verifies the space's live name; then it takes seven stages in order (`STAGES`), each recorded with its seconds: `qos` (every requirement found, by `[placement] qos_scan`, and each one not in `qos_baseline` removed, in this order (`resetremove.py`): its record is read on every provider until its `status` is `fulfilled` or `impossible` there on consecutive reads, a provider that answers that it is not there being one dbsync has not reached yet, within `[placement] qos_fulfil_timeout_s` (120 s), or the reset fails with nothing deleted; it is deleted through the gate; then the deletion barrier holds until, on every provider, the summary of its entry no longer lists it and its record answers that it is not there (`answered_absent`) on consecutive reads, within `resetremove.BARRIER_BOUND_S` (60 s), or the reset fails; and a second scan finds none but the baseline's. A deletion cancels the replication only on the provider that processes it, and best-effort; the replicating provider goes on until dbsync brings it the deletion, and an eviction a provider processes before then is cancelled for every file the requirement still holds there, so no file is settled or evicted before every provider has processed every deletion. `pending` does not prove that bytes are moving: no REST answer shows bytes in flight. The record says `scan`, `found`, `kept`, `removed`, `affected_files`, `fulfil_s` and `fulfil_polls` (the wait for fulfilment), `impossible` (the removed requirements a provider reported impossible), `barrier_s` and `barrier_polls` (the barrier's wait), and the `reads` of each kind), `qos_settle` (the block maps of the regular files a removed requirement applied to, on every provider, unchanged for `resetqos.SETTLE_STABLE_S` (3 s) within `resetqos.SETTLE_BOUND_S` (60 s), or the reset fails: a QoS replication has no transfer to wait for, and a cancel can still place the blocks it had in flight), `transfers` (every waiting or ongoing job cancelled on every provider that lists it until `transfer_settle_polls` observations in a row, `transfer_settle_s` apart, show none; a space whose every observation showed nothing, so that nothing was cancelled, settles on two, one `transfer_settle_s` apart, and the record says `fast`, O51), `eviction` (each provider's paths, a directory in one transfer and files together, verified on the block maps only once no transfer runs on any provider, and none running after, O01; what still holds bytes is scheduled again, as `Provisioner.evict` does, since an eviction on 25.0 can complete having evicted nothing, up to `EVICTION_ATTEMPTS`; the block maps judge, and the transfers' counters are recorded and name the cause: each provider's record carries the last attempt's `files_processed`, `files_evicted` and `files_failed` (None when a record lacks one), and when files still hold bytes at the last attempt the failure (`eviction.EvictionIncomplete`, a `NotPlaced`, its counts also in the failed stage's record) names the provider's position, those counts and, when files were processed and neither evicted nor failed, the likely cause: an eviction cancelled per file, e.g. a QoS requirement still covering the file there, which op-worker counts in `filesProcessed` alone; a gap with every map empty is no failure, since a file the provider holds nothing of is counted the same way), `occupancy` (first a wait of `resetqos.occupancy_wait_s` from the eviction stage's end, the occupancy's longest lag for the deepest regular file of the space plus `resetqos.QUIET_MARGIN_S`, on every reset (O46): an eviction's change reaches the space root's physical size as late as a placement's, measured live on 14 Nov as a drop 20.03 s after an eviction at depth 3 was scheduled, the job's run included, so the lag, 10 + 5 x (L - 1) s, is counted from the stage's end, at or after every eviction's and every transfer's finish, the reset's and an earlier run's; then, with no transfer running, the listings read again after the wait, the occupancy (`occupancy.holdings`): **every regular file's block map at each provider that can hold the space's bytes, read there and summed, is the verdict, whatever the space root's counter says**, and the space root's per-provider physical size (the directory-statistics counter, never the lagging logical figures) is read beside it; the reading is kept only when no transfer ran or ended since the wait began: the first page of each provider's ended listing (newest first by finish time), read at the eviction stage's end and again after the reading, holds no job the earlier one lacked, and no transfer is waiting or ongoing. Otherwise a job no stage sent changed the space after the instant the wait counts from, so the stage lets it end and waits again from then, up to `reset.OCCUPANCY_ROUNDS` (3) waits, and then fails. A listing shows a job up to `transfer_settle_s` late (O07): with the quiet check skipped the listings are looked at `transfer_settle_s` after the reading; with it they are looked at at once, and the quiet stage's look, `transfer_settle_s` after its own reading and against the same first pages, fails the reset on a job either look missed. The reset's own jobs, the evictions it sent and the jobs it cancelled, all seen to end before the wait, never count as new, however late they are listed. A provider other than the data provider whose files hold more than `allowed` fails the reset, and the message states, per provider, the bytes its files hold, the figure allowed, the counter, and the files that hold bytes by position (`files[i]`, among the files read there, sorted by id). **The counter is never the sole verdict** (O46): on op-worker f118ed9 it credited a mirror 1,048,576 B for more than 23 minutes after the eviction emptied the space's only regular file there (depth 3, 43,096,327 B; the file's distribution showed the mirror physical 0 and blocks `[]`, the mirror's storage 0 B allocated, nothing else held, no transfer ran), an earlier 64 KiB read at offset 8 MiB having placed one 1 MiB block in the file's middle; and a placement of 1,572,864 B showed in it only 9.534 s later (34 polls, the Day-0 facts of 13 Nov), the counter reading 0 meanwhile, as the source's flush path explains (a placement's change waits `blocks_flush_delay`, 10 s, then climbs one directory per 5 s). So every provider of the space other than the data provider has its files read, unless the caller declares it never holds the space's bytes (`never_holds=`, none by default), and such a provider is skipped only while its counter reads an explicit 0 (one the answer leaves out is read). The files are the regular files (`REG`) and hard links (`LNK`) any provider's listing showed the QoS scan, either scan when it ran two, each read at every such provider, at no listing of their own; a provider that answers it has no such file holds none of it, and a file created after the scan is not looked for (nothing but the reset acts between its stages). A hard link is counted on its own, so a refusal with bytes in a link says that a link and its target may count the same bytes twice. The trash and the space's tmp directory (a deleted file still open) are in no listing and are not read. A provider no declaration fits that supports the space fails the stage, closed, whatever its counter says. The reads go `[placement] concurrency` at a time, at most `occupancy.READS_MAX` (200,000) in one reading, its calls together, within `occupancy.READ_BOUND_S` (1,800 s), looked at after every batch, and past either the stage fails, closed, with the figures. A block map or a counter that is unknown (a failed entry, a null size, a provider left out) fails the stage, never counted as 0. Each provider whose counter differs from its files' sum, either way, gives an `occupancy_counter_disagrees` event with `counter_bytes` and `files_bytes`, and passes or fails on its files. Read before the evictions could show, the figure held evicted bytes, so a reset that allows nothing failed after a good eviction and the quiet stage, held to it, passed a growth up to their size. The record gives `physical_bytes` (the counter, every provider), `files_bytes` (each provider whose files were read, their sum), `files_read`, `events` and, on a refusal, `over` (per provider: `files_bytes`, `allowed_bytes`, `counter_bytes`, and `files` by position), and adds `waited_s` (every wait together), `depth`, `rounds` and `listings_after_s`, the delay of the look at the listings, 0 when the quiet stage makes it), `quiet` (the block maps of the eviction plan's own files, the ones a file path of the plan names and every file the eviction lists under a directory path (`resetheld.SOURCE`, `"eviction plan"`), each on the providers it was evicted from, and of the files a removed requirement applied to, on every provider, are read **once, at one instant T**: a pair the eviction verified empty must still show nothing, and a file a removed requirement applied to must show no more than the settle saw (`resetheld.expected_maps`); the stage then waits `resetqos.quiet_wait_s` from T, the space root's longest occupancy lag for the deepest file a late replication could reach, from the source (10 s for the file location's flush and 5 s per directory level above the file's parent), plus `resetqos.QUIET_MARGIN_S` (5 s), and reads the occupancy as the occupancy stage does: no provider other than the data provider may hold more by its files' block maps, summed, than at the occupancy stage, the counter being no verdict here either (O46; the record has the same `physical_bytes`, `files_bytes`, `files_read` and `events`). **What it proves, exactly: no replica of the plan's files, and no occupancy growth, had landed by T.** The occupancy read at T + the lag + the margin shows every placement that landed by T, whichever file it is on, so the occupancy alone catches a replication that lands before T on a file the plan does not cover, since no block map of the rest of the space is read; a zero occupancy is never taken alone as nothing having landed, since it lags. **It does not prove that nothing lands after T:** a replication still running at T can place its bytes later, which may not show in the occupancy read, and the stage then passes. **A transfer that ran or ended since the occupancy stage's wait began fails the stage** (O46): the same first pages and listings are looked at `transfer_settle_s` after its occupancy read (`listings_after_s` in the record), by when a listing shows every job that ended before it, since an eviction in that window can hide a growth in the reading, which then certifies nothing, and a job listed too late for the occupancy stage's own look made that stage's figure wrong. **The occupancy is read after the wait whenever the stage runs**, an empty plan included. **It runs unless the caller knows that no run since the last verified reset could have set a QoS requirement, whether or not a requirement is left at the reset.** **The residual:** a fulfilment whose cancel missed may still be replicating one file, with no requirement left and no transfer document to show it: one of a requirement the agent added and removed during the run, or of one the reset removed after a reconciliation restarted between its fulfilled status and its deletion reaching the replicating provider. It is at most one file's size per missed cancel, and rare, since it needs a missed cancel; it may land after T, and is then caught by the next start's reset. `qos_rights=` answers *could any run since the last verified reset have set a QoS requirement*: a study's reset runs at the start of each run and clears what earlier runs left, so it is never the rights of the run about to start, and a read-only run that follows a writing run would skip the check exactly when it is needed. `True` or `None` runs the check and is the safe choice (`None`, the default, is recorded as `default`); pass `False` only when you know that no run since the last verified reset had the right to manage QoS (`space_manage_qos`), and then only a reset that removed no requirement skips it. `Condition.manages_qos` describes one condition, so a caller combines it over every run since the last verified reset and never passes the next run's alone. The record gives `ran`, `why` (`requirements` when one was removed at the reset, else `rights`, else `default`), `qos_rights` as passed, `files`, `block_maps`, `held_files` (the plan's files), `held_source` (`"eviction plan"`), `waited_s`, `listings_after_s` (the delay of its late look at the transfer listings, O46) and the stage's seconds. The wait is for the deepest regular file of the space, which the QoS scan already saw, since a replication nothing names could reach any file (for `qos_rights=False`, the deepest file a removed requirement applied to). **Cost:** the wait, 20 s for files one directory below the root (15 s directly under it, 5 s more per further level), on top of the occupancy stage's own wait (O46), one block-map read at T per evicted (file, provider) pair of the plan and per (file, provider) of a removed requirement's files, once, bounded by the plan, the files being the ones the eviction lists already, so there is no second walk of the tree; and the occupancy's reading after the wait, one block-map read per regular file of the space at each provider that can hold its bytes, as the occupancy stage's (O46), which follows the size of the space) and `history` (what the ended listing shows since `since`, the run's start when the caller gives it and else the reset's own start, both epoch seconds on `now`: per provider and together, `ended`, the count of the jobs that ended with a finish time since then, `uncertain`, the count of those that may have ended on either side of it, `newest_finish`, the listing's newest finish time, and `earlier`, whether older ended jobs are listed too; ended transfers cannot be removed, so they are recorded, `removable` false). **Changed meaning (O32):** `ended` once counted every ended job the listing held; the history grows with every run and reset, and the listing is now read newest first down to `since` less the clock-skew allowance and `transferorder.STOP_MARGIN_S`, never whole, and `listed_max` (`LISTED_MAX`, 100,000) bounds the jobs read down to there, never the space's history. A finish time is stamped on the platform's global clock, which neither a provider's Date header (its system clock) nor Onezone's (the answering node's, which a multi-node Onezone's global clock can differ from by a bias) gives, so the band at the window's start (`transferorder.start_band`), the band in which the probes flag a job near the window's edge, is placed on the global clock itself: Onezone's public current-time endpoint (`GET /provider/public/get_current_time`, no credential, answered `timeMillis`; oz-worker `14b5bda`, `provider_routes.erl:192-198`; in `client.UNVERIFIED_ENDPOINTS` until a live run makes it) is read before and after the listings under the reset's retry policy (`OnezoneClient.get_dated(..., retry=, anonymous=True)`), each reading's offset from this host being the global time less the midpoint of this host's clock around the read, with half the round trip as its uncertainty; the band runs from `since` moved by the least offset the readings allow (each one's less its half round trip) less `clock_skew_allowance_s`, rounded down, to `since` moved by the greatest (each one's plus it) plus it, rounded up, both ends included. The allowance covers a provider's synchronisation error to Onezone's clock; a provider that is not synchronised stamps on its own clock, which the band does not bound. The new `clock` records `zone_least_offset_s`, `zone_greatest_offset_s`, `zone_half_trip_s`, `zone_date_offsets_s` (the Date headers of the same answers, a cross-check), `allowance_s`, `band` and `provider_offsets_s` (each provider's least and greatest Date offset, None when none could be read, a diagnostic). `ended` counts the jobs whose every provider's record puts the finish after the band; the new `uncertain`, per provider and together, counts those any record puts in it, or that records put on both sides, and none of them is in `ended`. **`ended` is exact when `uncertain` is 0**, and a lower bound otherwise (`ended + uncertain` the upper): a consumer that needs an exact count must treat `uncertain > 0` as not exact. `earlier` is true when the walk stopped at an older job or kept one that finished before the band, never for one in it. **`ended` counts the jobs that ended with a finish time in the window:** a job cancelled before it started carries no finish time (`finishTime` 0), sorts after every job that has one, and the walk stops at it: it and what follows are neither read as part of the window nor counted against `listed_max`, however many such cancellations the history holds, so `ended` and `uncertain` never hold it, nor does `earlier`; it moved no bytes. A job with a finish time listed after one with none is an order the listing cannot have, and fails the stage. The reset's own cancels are counted in `transfers.cancelled`, the distinct jobs its transfers stage sent a cancel to, so the ones it cancelled while still scheduled are never invisible. The margin decides where reading stops, never what counts: a job read in it, below the band, makes `earlier` true and is not counted. A reading of the global clock that fails or cannot be read, or a global clock more than the margin behind this host's, fails the stage. The waiting and ongoing listings are still read to their end under that bound. **The QoS scan (O33):** the build has no listing of a space's requirements, so `[placement] qos_scan = "listing"`, the default, reads one recursive listing of the space root on **every provider that supports the space** (`GET /data/{root}/files` with `attributes` and `include_directories`, a page per 1000 entries, ended by a null `nextPageToken`, failed closed on `inaccessiblePaths`), the entries united by id, then the summaries of the entries whose `effQosInheritancePath` is `direct` or `directAndAncestor`, each read on the provider whose listing marked the entry, and each requirement's record, read on the provider whose summary showed it. Every provider is listed because a requirement is visible at once only on the provider it was added through: an agent that adds one through one provider just before the reset leaves the others without it until dbsync delivers it, and a listing of the data provider alone would find none and skip the removal. For N supporting providers the scan reads each provider's space root, N x ceil(E / 1000) pages for E entries, one summary per marked entry per provider that marks it, and one record per requirement; the stage's `reads` gives `listings`, `listing_pages`, `summaries` and `records`. `"summaries"` reads the root's and every leaf's summary on every provider instead (about 52,000 reads for a large tree) and is still accepted. The listing is the default since the 14 Nov 2026 live slot (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-14-federation-facts-slot.md`, the 25.0 development build, op-worker `f118ed9`): the recursive listing took `include_directories` (1,210 entries in 2 pages of 1,000), and a requirement added through the mirror was found by the data provider's listing after 3.89 s, two polls, measured by hand since the gated test's own POST reached the mirror before it knew the file. Not measured separately: that the provider a requirement is added through shows it in its own listing at once, the premise for listing every provider; the quiet stage catches a replication's effect regardless, apart from its documented residual. `qos_scan` is in no fingerprint (`PlatformConfig.fingerprint` reads nothing of `[placement]`), so a store started under `"summaries"` resumes under the new default; the reset's records say which scan ran (`scan`). `qos_requirements()` reads the baseline at the run's start, and `run(qos_baseline=)` keeps it; by default none is kept, as before. `qos_settle` stays keyed on the affected files and is skipped with none. A stage that cannot verify what it did raises `ResetFailed` with the stages so far and runs no later stage; the caller must not start the next run. The record, the failed one's included, carries `reads_retried`: how many of the reset's reads timed out and were tried again during it (O29), 0 when none did.
- **Several spaces reset as one (O51).** `SpacesReset.run` takes every space's stages in `STAGES` order, stage by stage: no space begins a stage before its previous one ended, nor while another is still in an earlier one. The transfer settle's waits, the occupancy stage's wait (to the latest instant any space may read its occupancy at, each space's eviction end plus the lag for its deepest file), the quiet stage's wait (to the latest T plus that space's wait) and the listings' late look (`transfer_settle_s` after every space's reading) are each taken once for every space, on the calling thread. Each is a lower bound, so one wait to the latest instant keeps every space's, and a job that ran or ended in the longer window still makes a stage wait again or fail: a longer wait can fail a reset that would have passed, never pass one that would have failed. The rest of each stage's work runs for at most `concurrency` spaces at once, each under its own `[placement] concurrency`, so at most their product of requests is in flight (64 with the defaults, under the 100 connections of the HTTP client's pool, which makes a request beyond them wait). A stage that fails in one space fails the reset once every space has ended that stage: `ResetFailed` (`stage`, the first failed space's) holds every space's record (`spaces`), each other space's up to where it stopped with `halted_before`, and `failed`, the failed spaces' positions. The record: `ok`, `spaces` (each as `SpaceReset.run` returns it), `seconds`, `stages` (each stage's seconds across the spaces), `shared_waits` (per stage, the `count` and `seconds` of the waits taken once) and `concurrency`. `SpaceReset.run` is the driver with its one space. **The eviction's waits:** a job of the reset's own whose record has reported it ended on the provider it ran on (an eviction it sent, a job it cancelled) holds no read of the block maps or the occupancy up, whatever a listing still shows; any other job a listing shows waiting or ongoing still does; and the eviction stage's record gives `waits`, each reason's `count` and `seconds` (`transfers_ending`, `block_maps`, `listed_running`, `retry_pause`; `evict_ids` and `evict_tree` take `ended=` and `waits=`, a `consistency.WaitLog`). **The planner's reading:** `plan_scan` makes the recursive listing of the space root on every provider the reset reads, at once (`resetqos.by_listing`, the QoS scan; its entries are `resetqos.Listed`, its `Found` now names the space, the providers and each regular file's path, and its `listing` holds the regular files and hard links of every provider's listing), then reads the block map of every regular file any provider's listing showed at every provider of the space but the data provider and the ones in `never_holds`, so a file one provider's listing missed is still read there (a provider that answers it has no such file holds none of it), never skipping one on the counter's word, under `[placement] concurrency` and the occupancy's bounds (`occupancy.read_files`, under a budget of its own). `Holdings.plan()` is the eviction plan of the held files; a hard link is named in no plan, and the occupancy stage reads it as it reads every listed file. Given to `run` (`planned=`), its scan is the qos stage's first: a reading of another space or of other providers is refused with `ValueError` before any stage, the second scan is the reset's own, and the stage records `scan: "given"` and `scan_age_s`. A requirement added between the planner's scan and the reset is missed, as one added just after the reset's own first scan would be. **What the occupancy wait guards now:** the verdict comes from the files' block maps, which show at once; the wait still guards the counter's two uses, the `never_holds` skip and the `occupancy_counter_disagrees` events. Polling instead of the fixed wait is not done: each poll would be a whole per-file reading.
- The mutation gate resolves a transfer's cancel by its record's `spaceId` and a QoS requirement's removal by its record's `fileId`, read right before the mutation and never kept. A directory's eviction and a transfer record's `spaceId` were read on the live federation (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-11-federation-facts.md`), and so was the shape of a directory's distribution (`day0/2026-11-11-federation-facts-posix-and-fuse.md`); the cancel, the QoS summary, a requirement's record and its removal are named only by Oneprovider 25.0's REST description, so they are in `client.UNVERIFIED_ENDPOINTS`. Each has a live test (`EXPCORE_LIVE_MUTATE`; the whole reset also `EXPCORE_LIVE_RESET`).

## The platform layer's storage import

`expcore_onedata.storageimport` and `importmanifest` (O41, #184; the sources and what is unsupported are in [`storage-import.md`](storage-import.md)). A space whose data provider holds it on an imported storage is declared in `platform.toml`, in `[space]` or a `[[spaces]]` entry:

```toml
import = { readonly = true }    # the data provider holds the space on an imported storage; read-only or not (required)
import = { readonly = true, shared = true }   # a directory other federations import too (O59): read-only is required
```

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True)
class ImportedStorage:                          # spaces.py: StudySpace.imported, None for a regular space
    readonly: bool
    shared: bool = False                        # O59: refused at load beside readonly = false; in the fingerprint when true
    def record(self) -> dict[str, bool]         # {"readonly"} and {"shared": True} only when shared

class Manifest:                                 # importmanifest.py: what the space must hold
    files: tuple[ManifestFile, ...]             # path, size, sha256, sorted by path
    directories: tuple[str, ...]                # every directory, an empty one included
    def digest(self) -> str; def label(self, index: int) -> str     # "manifest.files[i]", never a path
def manifest_of_tree(root) -> Manifest          # refuses a tree holding anything but regular files and directories
def load_manifest(path) -> Manifest; def write_manifest(manifest, path) -> None

class SpaceCheck:                               # any space, an uploaded one included
    def __init__(self, provisioner, manifest)
    def verify(self, *, timeout_s, providers=None, hashes=False) -> dict
    def listing(self, provider) -> dict[str, Entry]
class ImportedSpace(SpaceCheck):                # a space declared imported; refused otherwise
    def check_storage(self) -> dict             # the owner's panel: imported, read-only as declared, auto, no continuous scan
    def scan_info(self) -> dict
    def await_scan(self, *, timeout_s, poll_s=SCAN_POLL_S, after=0) -> dict
    def provision(self, *, timeout_s, providers=None, hashes=False) -> dict    # the three, in order
    def rescan(self, *, timeout_s, providers=None) -> dict                    # adds ids_stable and new_ids
class ImportFailed(ProvisionError): record: dict

class OnezoneClient:
    def panel_get(self, provider, path, *, retry=ONE_ATTEMPT)    # PANEL_READS alone, on the provider's Onepanel
    def force_scan(self, provider, space, *, gate_retry=ONE_ATTEMPT)  # gated on the space's live name
class Provisioner:
    read_only: bool                             # the space is held on a read-only imported storage
```

The platform layer guarantees:
- **The declaration is checked against the federation before anything is waited for.** `check_storage` reads the data provider's Onepanel (`GET /provider/spaces/{space}` and `GET /provider/storages/{id}`, with the admin credential, which must be a member of that provider's cluster): the support's storage must be imported, read-only as declared, in the `auto` mode, with `continuousScan` false; anything else is `ImportFailed`, a manual import included.
- **The scan's state is a report, the listing and the block maps the evidence (O01).** `await_scan` reads `GET .../storage-import/auto/info` every `poll_s` until a scan later than the `after`-th has `completed` with no failed file, within `timeout_s`; `failed` or `aborted` fails at once. `verify` then requires, on each provider named (the data provider always, first), that the listing holds exactly the manifest's paths, kinds and sizes and nothing more on `consecutive_polls` reads in a row, all providers within one `timeout_s`; then that every file is whole on the data provider by its block map, read under `[placement] concurrency`; and with `hashes`, every file read back through the data provider with the manifest's SHA-256.
- **The record** (version 1): `space` (its position), `ok`, `readonly`, `manifest` (counts, bytes, its SHA-256), `storage` (the flags `check_storage` read), `scan` (the last state read, with `polls` and `waited_s`), `listing` per provider position (`files`, `directories`, `bytes`, `missing`, `wrong_size` and `wrong_kind` as manifest positions, `missing_directories` and `extra` as counts, `converged`, `seconds` from the verification's start to the first of the matching reads, `polls`), `blocks` (`files`, `whole`, `not_whole`), `hashes` (`files`, `equal`, `differ`), `seconds` per phase and `reads_retried` (O29). `ImportFailed.record` is the record so far. Nothing in it or in a message is a path, a name or a label (O12).
- **A shared tree is read-only in every layer (O59).** `shared = true` beside `readonly = false` is a `ConfigError` at load naming the key; a shared import's `check_storage` refuses a storage whose `readonly` is not the boolean `true` (`1`, `"true"`, null and a missing key are none of them), before the scan is read; and the record carries `shared: true`, only for such a space. The deployment's side (the layout's `shared`, `manifest_env` and `direct_io`, `fedimport.verify_imports`, the HPC provider's read-only bind and its mount check) is in [`storage-import.md`](storage-import.md), "A tree on the cluster's shared filesystem".
- **A read-only imported space is never written by the core.** `Provisioner.mutate` refuses, before anything is sent, every request under `/data/` (a creation, a write, a delete, through any provider), and `schedule_transfer` refuses a replication onto the data provider: `Refused`. `ProgressiveIngest` refuses such a space when built (`Refused`), and the day-0 round trip is unknown there. Reads, replications onto the other providers, their evictions, the reset and QoS requirements are unchanged.
