## The platform layer's testing module

`expcore_onedata.testing`, installed with the platform layer (the `[onedata]` extra). It holds the fakes of the Onedata platform that the core's own tests use, so a study tests its instruments, probes and scorers against the same stand-ins without a live federation. [`study-guide.md`](study-guide.md#testing-a-studys-instruments-and-scorers) shows them in use.

```python
class FakeData(FakeFederation):     # fake_data.py: Onezone plus every provider's data API
    def __init__(self, *, error_shape: str = ERRORS_25_0, visibility_delay_s: float = 0.0, **kwargs)
    def add_file(self, path: str, content: bytes, *, holder: str | None = None) -> str
    def add_tree(self, root_id: str, path: str) -> str                     # a tree of a space no configuration knows
    def answer_missing_as_25(self, answer=(400, "posix", "enoent")) -> None # what is gone, as 25.0 answers it
    def add_transfer(self, kind, path, provider_id, *, scheduled, source_id=None, ended=True, waiting=False,
                     finished=None, bytes_replicated=None) -> str          # a job no request to /transfers made
    def end_transfer(self, tid: str, *, at: float | None = None) -> None    # places or removes the blocks, as of then
    def id_of(self, path: str) -> str
    def held(self, path: str, provider_id: str) -> list[list[int]]
    def client_http(self) -> httpx.Client                                   # the client the platform layer takes
    def respond(self, request: httpx.Request) -> httpx.Response

def make_federation(*, clock: Callable[[], float] | None = None, error_shape: str = ERRORS_25_0,
                    visibility_delay_s: float = 0.0) -> FakeData
def write_platform(directory: Path, edit: Callable[[dict], None] | None = None) -> Path
def write_with_placement(directory: Path, rows: str = "", **override) -> Path
def tool_plane_replicates(fake: FakeData, client: OnezoneClient, path: str, target_id: str) -> str
```

**What it may import, and who may import it.** It imports the core, the platform layer and the standard library, never pytest and nothing of a test tree, so a helper that needs pytest, a fixture, is the study's own thin wrapper around a name here. No runtime path imports it: `tests/test_import_boundary.py` fails if any module of `expcore`, any other module of `expcore_onedata`, the CLI or the shipped example imports it, by an import statement, a relative import or a literal dynamic import, and it imports every runtime module in a fresh interpreter and requires that the testing package is not among the loaded modules. The core's own tests take their fakes from here, so a fake cannot drift from the code it stands in for. Not in it: the sandbox's host fake (`FakeHost`), which only the sandbox's tests need, and every pytest fixture.

**The names are the interface.** A name below changes only with an entry in the changelog. Each fake's docstring says what it models and what it does not, in two paragraphs that begin `Models:` and `Does not model:`; a test fails if one lacks either. The table is the short form.

| Name | What it is | Models | Does not model |
|---|---|---|---|
| `MockOnedata`, `Request`, `BODY_KEPT_BYTES` | the provider REST surface the fsspec client uses, served over a real local HTTP socket from a fixed file set; `Request` is what it records of each call, and what `FakeFederation` and `FakeData` record of each too: method, path, query, `Range`, token and `body`, which is kept whole up to `BODY_KEPT_BYTES` (64 KiB) and is empty beyond it, where `size` and `sha256` (always the body's) say what it was, so a log of a batch of large uploads is no copy of the batch (#259, O47) | lookup, attributes, paged children (a `limit` over the maximum is refused, not clamped), ranged content, the one token it accepts | writes, transfers, block maps, file times and permissions, more than one space |
| `FakeFederation` | an in-process Onezone: `client_http()` answers through an `httpx.MockTransport`, no socket | token examination and scope, temporary tokens with a time caveat, privileges (direct and effective), the study space and others added by `add_space`, with `grant(..., space=)` for their privileges | other Onezone calls, a token's expiry or revocation, group-derived privileges, files in an added space |
| `FakeData`, `Node`, `Transfer`, `PROVIDER`, `ROOT_ID`, `ERRORS_25_0`, `ERRORS_LEGACY` | Onezone plus the data API of every provider it reports, with files, block maps, range reads and transfer jobs; time is counted in calls, or in seconds of the fake's own `clock` | files, directories, per-provider block maps that a range read, a replication and an eviction change, per-reader views with a visibility delay, deletes that land late (`delete_delay_s`), replication, eviction and migration jobs, the transfer listing and a job's record, provider clock skew and listing lag, Onedata 25.0's error shapes (or the older one), injected faults, QoS requirements (records, summaries and the recursive listing's marks) whose addition and removal reach the other providers after a dbsync delay (`qos_sync_s`), `pending` until their replication has landed, a replication that goes on landing on a provider until the removal reaches it, and a cancel that misses one file (`qos_missed_cancel`; `fake_qos.FakeQos`), and a replication that copies only what its target's lagging copy of the other providers' maps showed (`replication_view_lag_s`), the block map through any provider showing their current blocks | a transfer's duration, partial replication other than by that lag, partial eviction, a QoS expression's evaluation, a status aggregated over the providers, an eviction a requirement protects, storage backends beyond one, quotas, a token's space or interface caveats, a delete that other providers show late (unless `delete_delay_s` is set) |
| `fake_import.FakeImportData`, `fake_import.make_import_federation` (from their module) | `FakeData` whose data provider holds the study space on an imported storage (`storage`, `put_on_storage`, `start_scan`), with that provider's Onepanel (O41) | a scan that makes what the space lacks, each file whole on the importing provider; the panel's support, storage and scan reads and its force-start (`panel_calls`, `panel_status`); a read-only storage's EROFS refusals, and its deletes that leave the file for the next scan | the scan's speed, owners, modes and times, change detection, manual registration, a write through another provider |
| `Ticker` | a clock that moves only when something sleeps: `clock`, `sleep`, `now` | a bounded wait that runs instantly and exactly | real time, threads |
| `make_federation`, `add_users` | the federation the default `platform.toml` describes: the study space, `alpha` supporting it, `beta` known and not supporting it, the three users with the baseline privileges | what `write_platform` names | a federation of another shape: build `FakeData` and bend it |
| `write_platform`, `platform_document`, `write_with_placement`, `with_placement` | the default `platform.toml` as a dictionary and as a file, with the credential files and empty manifests it names, a declared `[tokens] margin_s = 900` (a `[faults]` table whose restore needs more must raise it), and a `[placement]` table with test settings | a document `load_platform` accepts | a real federation's names: every one is invented (`.test` hosts, `login-*` users) |
| `tool_plane_replicates` | the agent's replication through the tool plane: a job the harness did not schedule, so a probe finds it in the space's listing | the tool server posting the job with its own user's token | the tool server |

The names the default document and the federation share are `ADMIN_ENV` (the variable that holds the admin credential, which a test sets to `fake.admin_token`), `ALPHA_ID` and `BETA_ID` (the providers' ids), `SPACE_NAME` and `SPACE_PREFIX`, `USERS` (login to password and user id), `DATA_PATH_USER`, `TOOL_SERVER_USER` and `SERVICE_USER` (the three principals), `CREDENTIALS_DIR`, `BASELINE` and `MANAGE` (privilege sets), `NOW` and `CEILING_S` (the issuer's clock and a run's ceiling), `SETTINGS` (the `[placement]` table), `DATA` (4096 bytes whose every offset has its own value) and `TOOL_SERVER_ARGS` (the stand-in tool server's arguments after the interpreter).

Two stand-in MCP servers are run, not imported, so importing the package does not load the MCP SDK: `python -m expcore_onedata.testing.fake_mcp_server --tools echo,add` is a real MCP stdio server with a small catalogue and a tool allow-list, and `python -m expcore_onedata.testing.fake_onedata_mcp` is the Onedata tool server's stand-in, configured by environment as the real one is (`TOOL_SERVER_ARGS` is its command line). Neither models the real server's tools.

**Listings.** `FakeData` and `MockOnedata` end a children listing as Onedata 25.0 was measured to (`fake_data.LISTING_END_FACTS`, `deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-12-federation-facts-listing-end.md`): every page has `nextPageToken`, `isLast` and `children`, in that order, with a token on the last page too; `isLast` is true only on a page shorter than its limit, so a page the last entries fill is followed by an empty last page; and the page after a last page answers 400 `posix` `eagain`. `FakeData`'s transfer listing gives a full page a token and the last page a null one, with no `isLast`, so a full page is followed by an empty one. A reader that stops on a missing or null token, or treats an empty last page as an error, fails against them as it would against 25.0.

**Per-reader views.** A file or directory is made on one provider (`add_file`'s holder, or the provider a `POST` of children was sent to), and every other provider shows it only after `visibility_delay_s` seconds of the fake's `clock` (`visibility_delay_by_provider` gives one reader its own delay; the default is none). Until then that reader's reads of it answer as a deleted entry does, and its parent's listing leaves it out. The delay is counted on the fake's clock, which a test binds to a `Ticker`, so a test of a six-second delay takes no time. About 6 s was seen live on Onedata 25.0.

**Pending deletes.** `FakeData.delete_delay_s` (also an argument of `make_federation`) keeps a delete under way for that many seconds of the fake's `clock` after it is answered, so a study can test that its code waits before it uses a name again. The default, 0, finishes every delete at once and everywhere, and a test that does not set it sees one change: a transfer scheduled for a file id that was deleted is now answered 201 (accepted, ending at once with 0 files processed), where it was answered 400. With a delay, a DELETE of a file or a directory answers 204 and lands when the clock has moved by the delay (a test binds the clock to a `Ticker`, so none waits); `land_deletes()` lands every delete under way now. A delay needs a clock that moves: the fake's default never does, so the first DELETE that would pend with it raises `ValueError` naming `delete_delay_s` and how to bind a clock (`make_federation(clock=ticker.clock)` or `fake.clock = ticker.clock`), rather than let a delete never land and a test of a reused name pass for no reason. The fake answers one provider call at a time, so threads may read it at once, as the core's bulk reads do. `fake.nodes` still holds an entry whose delete is under way until it lands, though `id_of` and `exists` find it gone. `lose_recreated` (also an argument of `make_federation`, off by default) is the opt-in for the loss of a directory made again's files when the old delete lands, which the 12 Nov live check did not reproduce (below). `fake_data.DELETE_FACTS` lists where what is recorded was recorded; that check of this model on the federation is `deploy/compose-federation/day0/2026-11-12-federation-facts-pending-deletes.md`.

| While a delete is under way | |
|---|---|
| Recorded on 25.0 | the provider the DELETE went to answers a deleted file, and what is under a deleted directory, as a deleted entry at once, 400 `posix` `enoent`, except a read of its `parent_id` alone, which kept answering live (12 Nov); by the source, not live, the same holds for `file_id` alone, and for `size` alone of a directory (below); the providers it did not go to keep showing the entry, its files and its listing entry until the delete lands, and all of them stop in the same second; a listing through the deleting provider already leaves out a directory still going; a file's delete is late in the same way as a directory's; a name made again meanwhile is accepted and loses nothing, a directory's or a file's (0 of 21 files lost in four trials watched for 120 s, by hash on every provider, with a 30 s control); a transfer scheduled through the deleting provider for a file it answers as deleted is accepted and completes at once with 0 files processed, replicated and failed (a replication was tried; the fake does the same for an eviction and a migration, an assumption), and places and removes no block |
| Corrected by the 12 Nov check | the loss: a directory made again under the same name does not lose its files when the old delete lands. `lose_recreated=True` keeps it, as not reproduced live (12 Nov facts) and kept for testing defensive waits; the check did not cover a file over 64 KiB, or a delete still working through many files when the name is made again. (h): a transfer of a deleted file is accepted, not refused 400. And (f): a provider that still shows the old entry shows only it until the delete lands, then only the new one in the same step, where the fake listed both; the provider the DELETE went to shows the new one at once (12 Nov facts) |
| Assumed, not seen live | (a) the providers the DELETE did not go to refuse the entry's name (409, as for any taken name), and a second DELETE through them changes nothing, since no mutation was sent through them; (d) with `lose_recreated`, the loss takes the files of the directory made again at any depth, and no directory; (i) a provider that holds the new entry back answers its id, and what is under it, as an entry it does not show yet, as it does not list it (no read of a new entry's id through a mirror was made before the old one went) |
| Simplified or not modelled | one `delete_delay_s` serves every provider, where the mirrors took 3 s in four trials and 8 s in two; the deleting provider answers a deleted directory as gone at once, where live its attributes read for up to a second, under another parent and partly emptied; a new file shown on a mirror late, at size 0 or with only some of its directory's files, before about 8 s (lag, not loss: `visibility_delay_s` hides a new entry whole); directory sizes, which followed a change 5 s to 13 s late |

**Tombstones.** A deleted entry is gone for a read of it by id only when that read asks for something the platform reads strictly. On 25.0 (the development build, op-worker f118ed9) `GET /data/{id}` resolves only the attributes it is asked for: no `attribute` parameter, or `name` or `type` among them, answers 400 `posix` `enoent`, and a read of `parent_id` or `file_id` and nothing else keeps answering from the entry's last state, only those attributes, on every provider, until the tombstone expires (`document_expiry`, 14 days by default, from the source and not measured). So does a read of a deleted directory's `size` alone (by the source), and of a deleted file's: another study's live probe on 13 Nov saw a file's `size` alone answer for its whole 300 s at all three providers (the source branch, `file_ctx:get_file_size`, was not traced). `FakeData` models a deleted file's `size` as it does a directory's, from the tombstone, which is an assumption. The id attribute is `file_id`: `?attribute=guid` is no name the parameter takes, and the fake answers it 400 `badValueNotAllowed`, with no `errno`, for every entry, live or deleted, before any lookup. `FakeData` models it (`fake_data.TOMBSTONE_FACTS`, which cite the source and the live observation): `tombstone_s`, also an argument of `FakeData`, is how long it answers, from the delete landing, and its default, `fake_data.TOMBSTONE_S`, is the 14 days, which no test that advances the fake's clock reaches, so a presence check by `size`, `parent_id` or `file_id` alone never sees a deleted entry go and fails in a test, as it does live for `parent_id`, `file_id` and a directory's `size`. A read of the entry's children, content or block map is not answered from it. `ERRORS_LEGACY` keeps no tombstone. The core's own visibility waits read `name`, `size` and `parent_id` together (`consistency.VISIBLE_ATTRIBUTES`), so a file deleted during a wait is never reported visible; a study that waits for an entry to be gone reads it with no attribute selection, or with `name`. Not recorded, and assumed here: that a deleted file's `size` alone answers from its tombstone as a directory's does (the source read was of a directory's), and that a deleted entry's `size` is the one it had when its delete landed.

The provider the DELETE went to cannot tell a delete under way from one that landed, since it answers both as a deleted entry, so a wait on its own reads is over before the other providers stop showing the entry (3 s or 8 s later in the 12 Nov check); a wait that reads every provider of the space covers that lag. A name made again at once lost nothing live, so a wait written for the loss is conservative, not wrong. A read of the name through a provider that did not delete it, with no wait, gets the old entry and its files until the delete lands there (live, a mirror showed only the old entry for 3 s to 8 s), and the new one after; the fake does the same. A test of a wait sets the delay, deletes, and reuses the name after that much time and at once, through the provider that deleted; the files are kept in both unless it sets `lose_recreated=True`, which loses them in the at-once case.

**Error shapes, by version.** `FakeData.error_shape` selects what the data API answers with when an entry is gone, a write is refused or an upload is untyped:

| | `ERRORS_25_0` (the default) | `ERRORS_LEGACY` |
|---|---|---|
| Belongs to | Onedata 25.0, as measured live (`deploy/compose-federation/day0/`) | none known: the fake's first shape, kept for a study that targets an older platform |
| An entry that is deleted or not visible yet (`missing_answer`, `unseen_answer`) | 400, error `posix`, `errno` `enoent` | 404, error `notFound` |
| A deleted entry's tombstone (`keep_deleted_parents`, `tombstone_s`) | a read of `parent_id`, `file_id` or `size` alone still answers, from the entry's last state, for 14 days (`size` of a file: assumed) | none: the entry is gone for every read |
| The space root before it is there (`root_enoent_answer`) | 400, error `posix`, `errno` `enoent` | 404, error `posix`, `errno` `enoent` |
| A write with a read-only token (`readonly_answer`) | 400, error `posix`, `errno` `eacces` | 403, error `forbidden` |
| An upload (`PUT` of content) not marked `application/octet-stream` (`require_octet_stream`) | 415, error `unsupportedMediaType` | accepted |

The statuses and the `posix` errors of the first column are what was seen. 25.0 was seen to answer 415 to an upload with no Content-Type at all; that the fake refuses every other type too, and the error id of its 415, are its own. Setting `error_shape` sets each attribute named in the table to that column's; a test sets one after it to bend one answer, as the core's tests do to see it fail closed on another 400. A job a provider does not show yet answers `transfer_unseen_answer`, 404 `notFound` in both shapes. The platform layer reads both columns: a 404 or exactly 400 `posix`/`enoent` is absence, 403 or 400 `posix`/`eacces` a refusal, and every upload is marked `application/octet-stream`.

**Transfers carried over.** A job carries the times the platform reports. The fake does not decide which jobs belong to a run's window: `PlatformProbes` does, from those times and Onezone's `Date` headers (`zone_skew` moves them, `zone_date` replaces them; a provider's `provider_skew` moves its own alone, which places nothing). A job that started before the window and had not ended when it opened is carried over (`space_transfers.carried_over`, and `transfer_carried_over` among the reasons an `on_demand` bound is unknown): it is not one of the run's records and its bytes are not in `transfer_bytes_in`, even if it places them during the run. A job that ended before the window is neither. A stand-in that sums every job it holds counts the first kind and differs from the core there; the tests that pin this are in `tests/onedata/test_testing_module.py`.

## The run record's schema

`expcore.records.schema`. A record names its schema version (`expcore-record/N`), and every section of it is a table of fields in `SECTIONS`. `SCHEMAS` lists the versions; a version names the fields it adds (`added`), and may mark some of those optional (`optional`). A record is validated against its own version: a field of a later version is refused in it, a required field of its version may not be left out, and an optional one may be absent and is checked when it is present.

- **A rule attaches to the version that wrote the record.** What a version's records mean is read from the record's own version, never from the newest code: validation (which fields exist, which are required) and each rule the format does not show, as the first-turn rule of version 2 (`SchemaVersion.first_turn_rule`) is read by `first_turn_rule(record.schema_version)` and the target rule of version 3 (`SchemaVersion.target_rule`) by `target_rule(record.schema_version)`. A later rule never applies retroactively to an older record: a record of version 1 or 2 still validates, writes back to its own bytes and replays under the rule of its time, and a replay of it never writes a field its version lacks.
- **An absent field is absent.** Writing and replaying a record keep an absent optional field absent. It is never filled in as a null or a zero, so "not recorded" and "recorded as empty" stay different (R10).
- **Extending version 3 before v0.1.0.** Live runs write version-3 records while several changes still extend it, so until v0.1.0 is tagged every extension of `expcore-record/3` is strictly additive: a new field is optional and its absence means "not recorded"; no existing field changes its meaning or its validation; and a record written at any earlier merge point replays byte for byte under the newest code. To extend it, add the field to `SECTIONS`, list it in the `added` and the `optional` of the version-3 entry of `SCHEMAS`, write it into the record only when the run supplies it (the builder leaves a `None` out, and `RunRecord.to_json` leaves out a field of the record itself that holds its default), and serve it back from the record on replay, absent when it was absent. Then add the next fixture to `tests/data/record-v3/`, named `NN-topic.json` and written by your code, and leave every earlier fixture as it is: `tests/records/test_v3_fixtures.py` holds the rule in two ways. Structural tests check the schema itself. A `failed_request`, an outcome's `defect` and the malformed events of the totals are objects a fixture may hold none of, and each is a section of `SECTIONS` (`failed_request`, `defect`, `malformed_event`), so its keys are versioned like any other; every field version 3 adds, in every section, is listed in that version's `optional`; and the fields version 3 had at its first merge point, frozen in `tests/data/record-v3-fields.json`, keep their types, their order and whether they are required, while every field not in that file is optional. So a required key added to any section is caught, whether or not the version lists it in `added`, and that file is never edited. The objects outside `SECTIONS` are held otherwise: every record holds a `code` identity and an `agent_config` with its `limits`, so the fixtures catch a required key added to them, and every key of a retry entry may be left out (`RETRY_FIELDS`), so a new one is additive as it stands. And the test validates every fixture and replays it byte for byte under the current code, which catches a change to a field or a check that a fixture holds. A non-additive extension is caught by the two together; the fixtures alone would miss a required field in a section they leave empty. A change that cannot be additive waits for the next version, `expcore-record/4`, which is appended to the table as version 2 and 3 were.
- **The merge points of version 3 so far.** `01-usage-extra.json`: `usage.extra` and `usage.extra_cut` of each turn. `02-billed-unread-reply.json`: the reply-count rule, under which a billed reply that became no turn leaves the totals' usage incomplete. `03-fault-target.json`: the target of a fault, `outcome.infra_target` (the position label an infrastructure failure names, such as `providers[1]`) and `treatment_targets` (the target the condition declares per treatment kind, written sorted by kind), each absent when there is none and refused when present empty or as a null; with them the target rule (`target_rule`), which scores a failure of a declared kind as treatment only when it names the declared target and the fault injector's reading in `instruments.fault_injector` shows that target injected in this attempt (`expcore.records.injections`). A record that declares no target is decided by the kind and the first-turn rule alone, as before. `04-instrument-refusal.json`: `instrument_refusal`, at the top of the record after `treatment_targets`: the instrument whose post-run check refused the attempt, `{"namespace", "infra_kind", "detail"}`, where the kind is in the registry and no condition can declare it as its treatment. It is validated with its keys listed in `REFUSAL_FIELDS`, not as a section; a record of version 1 or 2 that holds it is refused, and one that does not is never given it. `05-reserved-capped.json`: `totals.reserved_capped` and `totals.reply_cap_unheld`, after `final_text`: whether the attempt was held to the reservation R (C27). `reserved_capped` is the bound the budget gate reserved the run by, the value the campaign store keeps in the attempt's row (True: the reply maximum, False: the window), and `reply_cap_unheld` whether the store had taken the attempt's model off the reply maximum when the loop ended. Both are booleans, left out when no gate decided them (a campaign without a budget) and refused as a null or a number; a record of version 1 or 2 that holds either is refused, and one that does not is never given it. A replay serves both back as recorded. `06-tool-call-bound.json`: `agent_config.tool_server_call_bound_s`, the study's declared per-call bound on a call to a tool server (L17), written between `cleanup_deadline_s` and `terminal_tools` and left out when the study declares none (never a null), with `agent_config.tool_server_tools` after `terminal_tools`: the tools the bound reached, written with it and only with it (the bound was first merged as `tool_call_bound_s` and capped every call; it was renamed before any release); and `elapsed_s`, the last field of a tool call that started, the seconds from the reading before the call to the one that ended it (`ended_s - started_s`, waits on infrastructure inside it), left out of a call that never started (never a null). Both are optional in version 3 and unknown to a record of version 1 or 2 that holds either; a replay derives `elapsed_s` again from the recorded readings and writes it only when the stored record holds it, so a record written before it replays to its own bytes. The configuration's value must be a number above nothing and finite, and `tool_server_tools` a list of tool names. `07-exec-check.json`: `exec_check`, the last field of a tool call, after `elapsed_s`: what a code tool verified of the exec that ran the call's program (O55), as the tool gave it in `ToolOutput.exec_check`: `source_whole` (whether the program arrived whole in the container; a null when no completion record came), `exit_status` (the status the completion record saw; a null when none came or the program did not run), `nonce_matched` (whether a record of that exec came back). It is left out of a call whose tool gives none (never a null), each of its three keys is optional and checked by type when present (the section `exec_check`), a record of version 1 or 2 that holds it is refused, and a replay serves it as recorded, so it is written exactly where the stored record holds it. `10-sweep-survivor.json`: no field is added. It is the record of a Docker cell whose exec returned normally and whose end-of-cell sweep then could not end a worker it left (O72): the cell's call keeps its real status, output and `exec_check`, and the outcome is infrastructure of kind `cell_unkillable` whose detail says the run was ended by the end-of-cell sweep after that cell, apart from the cell's own result. `11-deadline-unkillable.json`: no field is added. It is the record of a Docker cell that hit its own deadline and whose kill could not end a process (O72, O34): the cell's call is the timeout of a call that ran (status `error`, class `timeout`, its kept output, the exec's check of a killed exec), never `skipped`; the agent's next call is the one the run refused; and the outcome is infrastructure of kind `cell_unkillable` whose detail names that cell's deadline and its outcome and says the cell's own result stands.
- **After v0.1.0.** A change to the format is a new version in `SCHEMAS`, as before. The fixtures of version 3 stay, and records of every earlier version keep loading and replaying.

## Other protocols the core defines

| Protocol | Where | Signature |
|---|---|---|
| `Estimator` | `expcore.stats.estimators` | `__call__(self, paired: PairedDifferences, settings: EstimatorSettings) -> EstimatorResult`, [above](#estimator) |
| `Recompute` | `expcore.replay.check` | `name: str`, `version: str`, `__call__(self, record: RunRecord) -> int`; recomputes a recorded decision offline and raises on a contradiction (R05) |

## Closed vocabularies

An unknown value raises where it is built, so a typo never reaches a record.

| Vocabulary | Values | Defined in |
|---|---|---|
| Outcomes | `finished`, `turn_cap`, `time_cap`, `token_cap`, `output_cap`, `malformed_end`, `leaked_tool_call`, `harness_defect`, `error`, `infrastructure` | `expcore.loop.outcomes.OUTCOMES` |
| Infrastructure kinds | `endpoint`, `key_rejected`, `sandbox`, `knowledge`, `unattributed_kill`, `ceiling`, `tool_plane_integrity`, `budget_hold`, `post_check_unverified`, plus kinds a platform registers ([below](#infrastructure-kinds-of-a-platform-or-a-study)) and kinds a study declares in `[study.kinds]` | `expcore.errors.CORE_KINDS`, `infra_kinds()`, `untreatable_kinds()` |
| Wait kinds | `model_retry`, `lease_queue`, `sandbox_lifecycle`, `toolserver_lifecycle` | `expcore.plugin.WAIT_KINDS` |
| Conclusion words | `difference`, `trivial_difference`, `equivalent`, `not_detected`, `untestable` | `expcore.stats.analysis.CONCLUSIONS` |
| Tool call statuses | `ok`, `error`, `malformed`, `skipped`, `cut`, `failed` | `expcore.loop.records.TOOL_STATUSES` |
| Attempt states | `running`, `done`, `infra_fail`, `outage_discarded`, `interrupted`, `harness_error` | `expcore.campaign.attempts.STATES` |
| Run statuses | `pending`, `done`, `infra_exhausted`, `harness_error` | `expcore.campaign.attempts.RUN_STATUSES` |
| Lease outcomes of an attempt | `granted`, `none` (a refused lease starts no attempt, so it is in the lease wait log and the event log) | `expcore.campaign.attempts.LEASE_OUTCOMES` |
| Malformed-call policies | `report`, `end` | `expcore.loop.config.MALFORMED_POLICIES` |

The conclusion words encode significance: `difference` and `trivial_difference` are significant, the other three are not; a contrast outside the Holm family is judged by its raw p-value and labelled exploratory wherever it is shown, and under a preregistration with `exploratory_only = true`, which has no family, every contrast is. Failing to detect a difference is not evidence of none. `untestable` is the word of a contrast that used no pair, and of no other: nothing was tested, and in the Holm family it counts as p = 1.

### Infrastructure kinds of a platform or a study

```python
def register_infra_kind(
    kind: str, description: str, *, treatable: bool = True, charged: bool = True, requires_target: bool = False
) -> None
def uncharged_kinds() -> frozenset[str]
def targeted_kinds() -> frozenset[str]
```

A platform adds a kind with `expcore.errors.register_infra_kind`, once, at the top level of a module of its own, so the kind exists as soon as that module is imported. `kind` is lower-case letters, digits and underscores and not a core kind; `description` is not blank and is the reason given when the kind is refused as a treatment; `treatable=False` marks a kind that can never be a condition's treatment, such as a failed integrity check of the harness. `charged=False` (C33) marks a **hold**: a start the platform held back, with nothing sent, because what the run needs is held by another live attempt or was left unfit by an earlier one. An attempt that ends in a hold is set aside as `infra_fail` with `charged` 0, as a `budget_hold` is, so its run is tried again under the same attempt number and is never lost to `max_attempts` for it (`expcore.campaign.attempts.classify`, which reads `uncharged_kinds()`). A hold must be registered `treatable=False`, or the registration is refused. `requires_target=True` (C34) marks a kind that is a **fault on one target**, such as a provider taken down (the Onedata layer's `provider_outage`), listed by `targeted_kinds()`: a failure of it names a target (a position label such as `providers[1]`), and a condition can be its treatment only by naming the target it manipulates, `infrastructure_is_treatment = [{ kind = "provider_outage", target = "providers[1]" }]`. The plain name `["provider_outage"]` is refused, in `study.toml` and in a schema built in code (`FactorSchema`), naming the file, the condition and the kind, because it would score any provider's outage as the manipulation; no targetless form is offered, since a condition that manipulates no particular target is not a fault on one. A targeted kind must be treatable. The core holds the name `provider_outage` (`expcore.errors.TARGETED_NAMES`) to a target whoever registers or declares it: a platform cannot register it without `requires_target=True`, and a kind of that name a study declares of its own in `[study.kinds]`, in a process that never imported the platform, is held to the same rule, since `[study.kinds]` has no key for it. `Outcome.is_treatment`, the one decision of the record, the store, `classify` and the replay, scores such a kind only under its declared target, whatever rule the record's version has: the failure must name the declared target and the fault injector's snapshot must show that target held down in this attempt, and a record or a result that declares the kind with no target is never treatment. `build_record` refuses to write such a record, and `validate` refuses to read one (`treatment_kinds: 'provider_outage' is declared with no target`). Registering a kind again with the same answers is harmless, and with another answer to any of them is refused.

A study's `study.toml` is validated when it is loaded, before its plugin is imported, so a kind registered by the plugin, or by a platform only the plugin imports, is unknown to the conditions. A study that declares a platform's kind as a treatment lists the module that registers it in `[study] platforms`; the loader imports each listed module before it validates anything, and refuses one that cannot be imported, naming the file, the key, the module and the type of the exception, never its text. The same listed module can tell the core, when a campaign starts, the longest the platform's health check may stay deferred, read from the platform's own configuration (`declared_deferral_from_config(path)`, [HealthCheck](#healthcheck)); `[study] platform_config` names the configuration it is given, a file of the study directory (`platform.toml` beside study.toml when the key is left out). For the Onedata layer, that module is `expcore_onedata.sandbox.kinds`, which registers every kind the layer raises: `data_gate_integrity` and the fault injector's `provider_outage` and `injector_failure`, the ingest's `ingest_failure` and its hold, `ingest_held` (`charged=False`), and the mount instrument's `io_mode_integrity` (O49), a mount run whose effective I/O mode was not the declared one or could not be shown. A study without a platform declares its own kinds in `[study.kinds]`, as `name = { description = "...", treatable = true }`. They are held in the vocabulary only while the study is loaded and while the pipeline works on it (`expcore.errors.study_kinds`), so they do not outlast it, and a failed load leaves none behind; a platform's kinds stay for the whole process. The scopes are counted: two studies that overlap in one process each keep their kinds and can see the other's for the overlap, which can only widen validation, and declaring one name differently while they overlap is refused, naming the kind. A study's kind that is a core kind or a platform's is refused; so is a platform registering a kind of the study in scope. Neither key is part of the factor schema (study.toml's hash is in the campaign fingerprint, as before), so a study that uses neither keeps its fingerprint and schema digest. [`study-guide.md`](study-guide.md#treatment-kinds) shows both keys.

A condition that names a kind outside the vocabulary is refused as unknown, with the treatable kinds as the choices; a known kind that is not treatable is refused as one that can never be a treatment, with the reason its registration gave; a kind that requires a target and is named without one is refused as declared with no target, with the table that is accepted.
